### [1] [Modality Discrepancy Transformer for Ambivalence and Hesitancy Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19148)
*Shiyu Luo,Yu Wang,Jiawen Huang,Zhaoxiang Xiao,Chenxi Huang,Qi Zhang,Bin Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a transformer-based multimodal model that explicitly models discrepancies between modalities (face, voice, text) to detect ambivalence/hesitancy, achieving a large performance gain and fast training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard multimodal fusion tends to suppress or average out conflicting cues between modalities, yet those conflicts are precisely the signal needed to detect ambivalence and hesitancy in clinical videos. There’s a need for an architecture that highlights and exploits cross-modal disagreement instead of discarding it, while remaining efficient and effective on benchmark datasets like BAH from ABAW.

Method: They build on a conflict-aware fusion framework and design the Modality Discrepancy Transformer (MDT). MDT converts inputs into 9 tokens: 3 per-modality embeddings plus 6 discrepancy tokens (3 absolute-difference and 3 Hadamard-product features) produced via linear projections. These tokens are processed by a Transformer with self-attention. The architecture uses FiLM for text-conditioned modulation and LoRA for parameter-efficient fine-tuning. At inference, a text-guided late fusion combines a text-only auxiliary classifier with the full multimodal output.

Result: On the BAH dataset, MDT reaches 0.7408 Macro F1 on the labelled test split and 0.7368 on the private leaderboard, exceeding the strongest published baseline by more than 10 F1 points, while training in under 20 minutes on a single GPU.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling and attending to modality discrepancies with a lightweight transformer architecture substantially improves automatic recognition of ambivalence and hesitancy in clinical videos, showing that cross-modal disagreement is an informative signal rather than noise and can be leveraged efficiently in practice.

Abstract: Ambivalence and hesitancy (A/H) are affective states in which individuals express contradictory signals across facial, vocal, and linguistic channels. Automatically recognising A/H in clinical videos requires detecting cross-modal disagreement -- the signal that standard fusion methods suppress. Based on the conflict-aware multimodal fusion framework of Bekhouche et al., we present the Modality Discrepancy Transformer (MDT). MDT enriches the original 6-token design to a 9-token representation comprising three modality embeddings, three absolute-difference features, and three Hadamard-product discrepancy features learned through linear projections. These nine tokens undergo Transformer self-attention, with FiLM-based text-conditioned modulation and LoRA fine-tuning as core architectural components. A text-guided late fusion branch blends a text-only auxiliary head with the full multimodal output at inference. On the BAH dataset from the 3rd ABAW Challenge, MDT achieves 0.7408 Macro F1 on the labelled test split and 0.7368 on the private leaderboard, outperforming the strongest published baseline by over 10 points while training in under 20 minutes on a single GPU.

</details>


### [2] [Subliminal Prompting Beyond Static Geometry: Causal Depth and Multi-Token Confounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19149)
*Barath Velmurugan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how large language models can implicitly transmit hidden traits (e.g., a number) via outputs that look unrelated (e.g., animal names), and disentangles several hypothesized mechanisms behind this “subliminal learning.”


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work observed that models can encode and transmit hidden information through seemingly unrelated outputs, and proposed that this happens via “token entanglement” in the output vocabulary. However, existing measurements mix up multiple different questions—about co-variation of outputs, similarity of fixed output vectors, readability from hidden states, and causal control by those states—without clearly separating them. This paper aims to rigorously separate and compare these properties in a controlled setting.

Method: Using a standardized animal-number prompting protocol across Llama-3.1 models (8B–70B) and two Qwen models, the authors separately measure: (1) similarity of fixed output vectors (geometry-based token entanglement), (2) observational readability via fixed output-head readouts over depth (normalized depth AUC), (3) causal control by copying the temporary answer-position hidden state from one number prompt to another at various depths and checking which prompt’s hidden number the animal output follows, and (4) multi-token vs single-token association by scoring every digit in sequence and then by per-token averaging, with controls for number length (“width”).

Result: They find that (1) similarity of fixed output vectors actually predicts behavior less well as model size increases (mean correlation change −0.080 with CI excluding 0); (2) fixed output-head readability shows no clear change in normalized depth AUC; (3) causal hidden-state control is strong and increases with scale: donor-control AUC rises from 0.254 to 0.540 across models, with robust increases for all 18 concepts and persistence even when 8 transformer blocks remain, while specificity and identity controls stay small or exact; and (4) in Qwen models, token-level scoring of each digit fails to show a positive one-token association, while per-token averaging yields an apparent positive association that disappears after adjusting for number width, exposing a length confound.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that fixed token geometry, observational readability, causal timing of hidden states, and multi-token measurement are distinct dimensions of how hidden traits are transmitted in “subliminal learning” protocols. None of these alone identifies the mechanism by which training transfers traits across tokens, but they jointly place strong constraints on simple token-level explanations such as static output-vector entanglement. The work argues for more nuanced mechanistic accounts of trait transfer in language models and cautions against overinterpreting fixed-geometry correlations or naive multi-token analyses.

Abstract: Subliminal learning shows that language models can transmit a hidden trait through outputs that appear unrelated to it. One proposed explanation, token entanglement, links animal and number tokens through the model's output vocabulary. Yet existing measurements answer different questions: whether outputs co-vary, fixed output vectors align, an answer can be read from a hidden state, or that state causally controls the answer. We measure each separately in a fixed animal-number prompting protocol. From Llama-3.1-8B to 70B, fixed output-vector similarity predicts behavior less well: the paired mean correlation change is -0.080 (95% CI [-0.127, -0.035]). A fixed output-head readout shows no resolved change in normalized depth AUC. To test control, we copy the temporary answer-position state from one number prompt into another at five depths and measure which prompt the final animal score follows. Donor-control AUC rises from 0.254 to 0.540, a paired change of +0.286 (95% CI [+0.272, +0.300]), with increases for all 18 concepts. The contrast remains with exactly eight transformer blocks remaining, while specificity and identity controls remain small or exact. In two Qwen models, scoring every digit in sequence does not recover the positive one-token association. Per-token averaging instead creates a positive pooled association that disappears after controlling number width, revealing a length confound. Thus, fixed geometry, observational readability, causal timing, and multi-token measurement are distinct properties of this frozen prompting channel. They constrain token-level explanations but do not identify the mechanism of training-time trait transfer.

</details>


### [3] [Sampling Reveals Style: Unsupervised, Training-Free Discovery of Prompt-Conditional Stylistic Axes in LLM Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19150)
*Ajit Mallavarapu,Ziwei Gu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a training-free way to discover and label stylistic dimensions encoded in LLM activations by sampling many high-temperature completions to a single prompt, running PCA on hidden states, and automatically naming the principal axes from the most extreme generations. It shows that this simple probe recovers human-salient style axes for some models and reveals cross-model differences in how stylistic vs structural variance is represented.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work shows that LLMs encode style in hidden activations, but identifying which stylistic dimensions are salient usually needs supervised contrastive datasets or human annotations. This is expensive and prompt-agnostic, making it hard to quickly inspect a model’s stylistic structure for a specific prompt or to compare models. The authors want a low-cost, training-free, prompt-conditional probe that can reveal which stylistic factors a model naturally varies along when generating text, and to understand how this varies across architectures and training runs.

Method: For a given prompt, they repeatedly sample many completions at elevated temperature from an LLM, collect the hidden activations during decoding, and pool them. They then apply Principal Component Analysis (PCA) to these activations to find principal axes of variation. For each principal component, they identify the most extreme (polar) generations on both ends and automatically derive descriptive style labels from these poles. They then run a two-phase human evaluation: (1) compare discovered axes to a set of 245 human-elicited stylistic dimensions to measure precision and macro-recall of matches; (2) ask annotators to rate the validity of the axis labels relative to the pole generations and measure inter-annotator agreement. They run this on multiple models (Qwen-3.5-4B-Instruct, Qwen variant, Llama-3.2-3B, DeepSeek-7B-Chat) to compare discoverability across models.

Result: For Qwen-3.5-4B-Instruct, the top two discovered axes correspond to spontaneously requested human stylistic dimensions with 72.8% precision and 43.6% macro-recall, indicating that the most salient PCA components align well with how humans conceptualize style. In human validity judgments, 75.6% of ratings consider the axis labels accurate descriptions of the polar generations, with high inter-annotator agreement (90.9% on adjacent ratings), suggesting that the axes are coherent and interpretable. Discoverability differs by model: both Qwen models and Llama-3.2-3B yield principal components that map onto human-salient stylistic axes, while DeepSeek-7B-Chat shows much lower precision (35.3%), with its principal components reflecting mostly structural variance (e.g., length, formatting) rather than style per se.

Conclusion: A simple, training-free PCA over an LLM’s own high-temperature decoding activations serves as an effective probe of its stylistic representation space. It can recover interpretable, human-salient style dimensions for some models and provides a low-cost alternative to supervised contrastive methods. The approach also surfaces pronounced cross-model differences: some architectures or training regimens produce representations where stylistic variance dominates salient components, while others mostly expose structural variance. This makes the method useful both for understanding and auditing a model’s stylistic behavior and for comparing how different LLMs internally organize stylistic information.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) encode rich stylistic structure in their hidden activations, but discovering which stylistic dimensions are salient for a given prompt typically requires supervised contrastive data. We present a training-free, prompt-conditional alternative: we repeatedly sample completions of a single prompt at elevated temperature, apply Principal Component Analysis (PCA) to the pooled hidden activations, and label the resulting axes automatically from the pole generations. We validate the discovered axes against 245 human-elicited stylistic annotations in a two-phase study. On our strongest model (Qwen-3.5-4B-Instruct), the top two axes match spontaneously requested human dimensions with 72.8% precision and 43.6% macro-recall, and 75.6% of validity ratings judge the axes' polar generations accurate to their labels, with 90.9% adjacent inter-annotator agreement. Discoverability is strongly model-dependent: both Qwen models and Llama-3.2-3B expose human-salient axes, while DeepSeek-7B-Chat drops to 35.3% precision, its leading components dominated by structural rather than stylistic variance. Simple PCA over a model's own decoding variance is thus an effective, low-cost probe of stylistic structure in LLM representations, one that also exposes sharp cross-model differences in how that structure is organized.

</details>


### [4] [What Users Think of Generative AI: A Cross-Platform NLP Analysis of Trust and Friction in App Store Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19151)
*Md Jafrin Hossain,Umme Nusrat Jahan,Shouvaggo Sharif Shammo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes 17,012 app-store reviews of six major GenAI apps to quantify user sentiment, trust, and adoption barriers, finding strong negative sentiment around ads, authentication, reliability, and pricing, and proposing a Trust Friction Score for comparing apps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite rapid consumer adoption of generative AI apps, there is limited large-scale, empirical evidence on how users perceive their quality, trustworthiness, and barriers to adoption. Existing work is often small-scale, app-specific, or anecdotal. The authors aim to fill this gap with a systematic, cross-application analysis that can inform both researchers and product teams about the main trust and usability pain points in GenAI apps.

Method: They collect 17,012 English-language reviews from Google Play and Apple’s App Store for six major GenAI apps (ChatGPT, Gemini, Microsoft Copilot, Claude, DeepSeek, and Perplexity). They apply BERTopic for topic modeling and a RoBERTa-based classifier for sentiment analysis, then compare patterns across apps using chi-square tests, Kruskal-Wallis tests, and multinomial logistic regression with Bonferroni correction for multiple comparisons. Both the topic and sentiment components are validated against human-coded labels on a stratified sample of 300 reviews.

Result: Negative sentiment clusters strongly around four recurring issues: advertising (91% of related reviews are negative), authentication and login (89%), server reliability and uptime (83%), and subscription pricing (73%). Sentiment distributions differ significantly by application; for example, Claude shows both the highest proportion of negative reviews (47.7%) and a very enthusiastic subset of users, indicating statistically significant polarization. These findings remain robust even though the apps have very different numbers of reviews.

Conclusion: User trust and adoption barriers in GenAI apps are driven less by model intelligence per se and more by surrounding product and business factors—ads, access friction, reliability, and pricing. Sentiment and issue profiles vary markedly across apps, with some (e.g., Claude) showing polarized user bases. The authors suggest that their validated pipeline and the proposed Trust Friction Score can serve as practical tools for monitoring and comparing trust, usability, and adoption barriers across GenAI applications.

Abstract: Generative AI (GenAI) applications have achieved rapid consumer adoption, yet little large-scale research examines user-perceived quality, trust, and adoption barriers. We present one of the first cross-application analyses of app store reviews for six major GenAI applications (ChatGPT, Gemini, Microsoft Copilot, Claude, DeepSeek, and Perplexity), comprising 17,012 English-language reviews from Google Play and the Apple App Store. We combine BERTopic topic modeling with RoBERTa sentiment classification and evaluate cross-application differences using chi-square, Kruskal-Wallis, and multinomial logistic regression with Bonferroni correction. Both components are validated against human coding using a stratified sample of 300 reviews. Results show that negative sentiment concentrates in advertising (91%), authentication (89%), server reliability (83%), and subscription pricing (73%). Sentiment differs significantly across applications, with Claude exhibiting the highest negative sentiment (47.7%) alongside a strongly enthusiastic user base, indicating statistically significant polarization. These findings are robust despite unequal review counts across applications. As exploratory observations, a subset of DeepSeek reviews raised geopolitical and data privacy concerns related to its Chinese origin, while a proposed Trust Friction Score summarizes application-specific trust and usability barriers into interpretable dimensions. The study provides validated and actionable evidence on user trust, usability, and adoption barriers in consumer generative AI applications.

</details>


### [5] [FakeSpotter: A content and strategy agnostic Viral Misinformation Detection Tool](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19152)
*Giovanni Spitale,Federico Germani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FakeSpotter is a language-model-based system that predicts how likely a text is to become viral misinformation by analyzing its structural patterns instead of checking its factual truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing misinformation detectors often depend on binary true/false labels and training on historical misinformation examples, which makes them brittle when facing new, unseen misleading narratives. The authors aim to create a tool that can flag potentially risky content early, in a content- and strategy-agnostic way, by focusing on stable structural characteristics of misinformation rather than topic-specific signals or ground-truth verification.

Method: The authors design FakeSpotter, a framework that extracts theoretically motivated features along linguistic, narrative, logical, and critical-thinking dimensions via repeated assessments by large language models. These features are then fed into domain-specific logistic regression classifiers tuned separately for short and long texts. The system produces risk estimates along with interpretable feature-based scores, signal agreement measures, and a caution index, enabling explainable outputs and social listening applications.

Result: On a labelled dataset of 764 social media and FakeNewsNet texts, FakeSpotter achieves macro F1 scores of 0.788 for short texts and 0.793 for long texts on a held-out test set, indicating strong performance in distinguishing higher-risk from lower-risk content based on structural fingerprints alone.

Conclusion: Structural fingerprints of misinformation—captured through theory-driven, LLM-derived features and lightweight classifiers—can provide early, explainable, and human-supervised assessments of viral misinformation risk without directly adjudicating truthfulness, making FakeSpotter a promising tool for social listening and risk monitoring of emerging narratives.

Abstract: Misinformation detection tools often rely on binary true and false classifications or models trained on historical examples, limiting their usefulness when novel misleading narratives emerge. Here, we present FakeSpotter, a content- and strategy-agnostic tool designed to estimate the viral misinformation risk of textual content by measuring structural fingerprints of misinformation rather than directly adjudicating truthfulness. FakeSpotter operationalizes a theory-driven framework across linguistic, narrative, logical, and critical-thinking dimensions, using repeated LLM assessments and domain-specific logistic regression classifiers for short and long texts. In a labelled corpus of 764 texts from social media and FakeNewsNet, FakeSpotter achieved macro F1 scores of 0.788 for short texts and 0.793 for long texts on a held-out test set. FakeSpotter's interpretive layer provides explainable outputs through feature-based scores, signal agreement, and a caution index, and can be used for social listening. These findings suggest that identifying the structural fingerprints of misinformation can support early, explainable, and human-supervised assessment of potentially viral misinformation.

</details>


### [6] [Stop Removing Stopwords: How an Inherited Preprocessing Default Distorts Legal Text-as-Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19153)
*Gregory M. Dickinson*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tests whether removing stopwords actually improves classification of judicial texts and finds it does not; removing them may even harm measurement validity in legal text-as-data research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Empirical legal scholarship often uses interpretable text pipelines (like TF-IDF plus linear models) where the actual words are substantively meaningful. However, these pipelines inherit old information-retrieval preprocessing defaults such as stopword removal without evidence that they improve classification accuracy, especially in settings where measurement validity of language features is crucial. The authors want to rigorously test whether stopword removal is beneficial or harmful in legal text classification.

Method: They construct an exhaustive single-word ablation framework that evaluates preprocessing directly on downstream classification performance. Using Supreme Court opinions (Caselaw Access Project) labeled with Supreme Court Database metadata, they define two binary classification tasks: predicting ideological direction and distinguishing constitutional vs. non-constitutional cases. For each of roughly 18,500 candidate words, they remove that word from the corpus, retrain TF-IDF plus linear classifiers, and measure the effect on F1. They compare generic stoplists, optimized (oracle) stoplists composed of the individually most harmful words, and meta-models that try to predict which word removals will help.

Result: In all tests, common generic stoplists perform worse than doing no stopword removal at all, reducing F1 below the baseline that keeps all words. Even when they construct optimized stoplists using oracle knowledge of each word’s ablation impact, the resulting performance is statistically indistinguishable from the no-removal baseline. Furthermore, meta-models that take word-level features as input cannot reliably predict which word removals would help performance, indicating that there is no systematic signal to guide curation of effective stoplists.

Conclusion: The study concludes that stopword removal does not improve, and can even degrade, performance for interpretable legal text classification tasks, and that it offers no reliable gains even when aggressively optimized. Because legal text-as-data scholarship depends on the substantive meaning of words, silent preprocessing steps that reshape the feature space—like stopword removal—risk distorting doctrinal and ideological signals. The authors recommend leaving stopwords in place in such research, framing this as a matter of measurement validity rather than mere model optimization. Their ablation framework can be used to re-evaluate other inherited preprocessing defaults.

Abstract: Empirical legal scholarship increasingly treats judicial text as data, and much of it still runs on sparse, interpretable pipelines -- TF-IDF features and linear classifiers -- because the textual feature is often the object of study, not merely a means to a prediction. Yet these pipelines inherit a chain of preprocessing defaults from mid-century information retrieval that were never validated against classification accuracy, the most entrenched being stopword removal. This study introduces an exhaustive single-word ablation that measures a preprocessing step's effect directly against the downstream objective, and applies it to stopword removal as the hardest case to dislodge. Matching Supreme Court Database labels to Caselaw Access Project opinion texts, it examines two binary tasks that bracket F1 headroom, ideological direction (no-removal baseline F1 ~ 0.68) and constitutional versus non-constitutional law type (~ 0.92), across 7,668 and 7,001 opinions. For each task the analysis approximates the best stoplist any expert could build, removing each of roughly 18,500 candidate words and measuring the effect directly. Three findings follow: generic stoplists in common use fall below the no-removal baseline in every test; even optimized stoplists are statistically indistinguishable from removing nothing; and meta-models trained on word-level features cannot predict which removals help, so list curation has nothing to target. The method generalizes to any inherited preprocessing default, and the result is a caution specific to interpretable legal text-as-data: a step that silently reshapes which features a model sees can distort the very doctrinal and ideological signal such research exists to recover. Leaving stopwords in place is a question of measurement validity.

</details>


### [7] [Neo-Classic: A Benchmark for Evaluating Linguistic-Aesthetic Reasoning in Classical Chinese Poetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19154)
*Han Zhang,Zihan Gu,Zhiyuan Wang,Tianyi Ma,Jiacheng Lu,Xinyan Zhang,Yuhao Wei,Cheng Hua*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Neo-Classic, a new benchmark to test whether LLMs can truly reason about linguistic and aesthetic structure in Classical Chinese poetry, rather than just memorizing or pattern-matching pretraining data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks show high LLM accuracy on Classical Chinese Poetry, but it is unclear if this reflects genuine, transferable Linguistic-Aesthetic Reasoning or mere reliance on memorized historical corpora and familiar patterns. There is a need for a more controlled, out-of-sample evaluation that isolates global hierarchical planning and aesthetic reasoning abilities, beyond local form matching or direct retrieval from pretraining data.

Method: The authors build Neo-Classic, an evaluation benchmark consisting of (1) a constructionist out-of-sample dataset of strictly metrical poetry written by contemporary experts—so it is unlikely to appear in LLM pretraining data, and (2) five “reverse understanding” behavioral probes crafted to test hierarchical constraint satisfaction in poetry comprehension and organization. They then evaluate several state-of-the-art LLMs (Qwen3-Max, Gemini-3-Pro, DeepSeek-V3.2) on both historical and contemporary poems, comparing performance across tasks such as discourse-level ordering and other probes of Linguistic-Aesthetic Reasoning.

Result: Two main limitations emerge: (1) models show a 20–50 percentage point performance drop when moving from familiar historical corpora to the new, contemporary Neo-Classic dataset, indicating poor transfer beyond pretraining distributions; (2) on discourse-level ordering tasks that require global hierarchical planning, models perform very poorly, with standard accuracies between 0% and 13%. Even with expert-level prompting or guidance, reasoning-focused models only improve to around 36%, still well below human expert performance.

Conclusion: Current LLMs can learn and exploit local formal patterns in Classical Chinese poetry but lack robust global hierarchical planning and Linguistic-Aesthetic Reasoning. Neo-Classic exposes that their high scores on existing benchmarks largely reflect familiarity with pretraining-era data and shallow pattern use rather than deep, transferable aesthetic reasoning. The benchmark thus provides a more stringent testbed and highlights a key frontier for advancing LLM capabilities in structured, artistic language understanding and generation.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve high accuracy on established Classical Chinese Poetry benchmarks, it remains challenging to distinguish transferable Linguistic-Aesthetic Reasoning from reliance on familiar pre-training patterns. To address this issue, we introduce Neo-Classic, an evaluation benchmark that combines a constructionist Out-of-Sample (OOS) dataset with a suite of reverse understanding probes. Unlike traditional benchmarks that rely on verification or generation over historical corpora, Neo-Classic comprises strictly metrical poetry authored by contemporary experts, reducing the possibility of direct retrieval. We evaluate state-of-the-art models, including Qwen3-Max, Gemini-3-Pro, and DeepSeek-V3.2, across five behavioral probes designed to test hierarchical constraint satisfaction. Our results reveal two primary limitations. First, a performance gap of 20 to 50 percent emerges when models transition from historical to contemporary texts. Second, models exhibit substantial difficulties in discourse-level ordering tasks, with standard accuracy remaining low (0 to 13 percent). Although expert-level guidance improves the performance of reasoning-enhanced models to 36 percent, a notable gap with human experts persists. These findings suggest that while current LLMs capture local formal patterns, they struggle with global hierarchical planning required for robust Linguistic-Aesthetic Reasoning.

</details>


### [8] [Towards Proactive Detection of User-Side Implicit Conflicts in Human-LLM Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19155)
*Jinqiang Wang,Tao Zhu,Huansheng Ning*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces UC-Bench and UC-Data for detecting user-side conflicts in human–LLM dialogues and proposes SynUC, a constraint-guided data synthesis method that significantly improves a small LLM’s conflict detection performance over larger general-purpose models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-turn conversations with LLMs, users often change or implicitly contradict their previous intents. Current systems mainly check for inconsistencies on the model side (e.g., self-contradictions) but not on the user side, causing misinterpretation and inappropriate responses. Reliable dialogue agents need to proactively detect when new user utterances conflict with prior ones, especially when the conflict is implicit and grounded in dialogue history. There is also a lack of benchmarks and high-quality training data tailored to these user-side conflicts, which hinders systematic evaluation and model improvement.

Method: The authors first build UC-Bench, a human-annotated benchmark specifically designed to evaluate user-side conflict detection in dialogues. They then propose SynUC, a constraint-guided data synthesis method. SynUC models user-side conflicts in a constraint space, representing user intents and their compatibility as constraints, and employs the SPEAKING framework to guide structured, traceable transformations of these constraints over dialogue turns. This enables the generation of synthetic training examples where conflicts—especially implicit ones—arise through controlled evolution of user constraints. Using SynUC on the WildChat corpus, they create UC-Data, a training dataset for user-side conflict detection with 2,487 samples. They train a lightweight LLM (Qwen3.5-4B) on UC-Data and compare it against baselines, including larger general-purpose LLMs and models trained on data from prior synthesis strategies.

Result: Experiments on UC-Bench show that off-the-shelf LLMs struggle to accurately detect user-side conflicts, particularly when conflicts are implicit and depend heavily on the dialogue history. The Qwen3.5-4B model fine-tuned on UC-Data achieves superior performance on UC-Bench compared to larger general-purpose models like Claude Opus 4.8. It also outperforms the same Qwen3.5-4B backbone trained on datasets synthesized using existing methods, indicating that SynUC produces higher-quality, more informative conflict examples for this task.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that user-side conflict detection is a distinct and challenging problem that current LLMs handle poorly, especially when conflicts are implicit. By introducing UC-Bench and UC-Data, the authors provide resources for systematic evaluation and training. Their SynUC method, which formalizes user-side conflicts in a constraint space and uses the SPEAKING framework for controlled constraint transformation, yields synthetic data that meaningfully improves performance of a small LLM beyond that of much larger general-purpose models. This suggests that targeted, constraint-aware data synthesis is an effective path toward more reliable, clarification-seeking dialogue agents that can proactively recognize and address conflicting user intents.

Abstract: In Human-LLM dialogue, follow-up user utterances may implicitly conflict with earlier intents, leading the LLM to misinterpret user needs and generate inappropriate responses. A reliable dialogue system should proactively detect user-side conflicts before generating a response and seek clarification when necessary. However, prior work has largely focused on LLM-side conflicts, leaving user-side conflicts underexplored. To fill this gap, we construct UC-Bench, a human-annotated benchmark for evaluating user-side conflict detection. Preliminary experiments show that existing LLMs struggle with this task, especially when conflicts arise from implicit incompatibilities grounded in dialogue history. To improve lightweight LLMs with limited training data, we investigate data synthesis for user-side conflict detection. Existing synthesis methods do not explicitly model the implicit incompatibilities between historical and current user utterances, making it difficult to capture the evolution of conflicts and to generate reliably labeled implicit conflict samples. We propose SynUC, a constraint-guided synthesis method that represents user-side conflicts in a constraint space and uses the SPEAKING framework to guide traceable constraint transformations. Applying SynUC to WildChat, we construct UC-Data, a user-side conflict training set containing 2,487 samples. On UC-Bench, Qwen3.5-4B trained on UC-Data outperforms larger general-purpose LLMs such as Claude Opus 4.8, as well as the same backbone trained on data synthesized by existing methods.

</details>


### [9] [Reflective Recovery: A Self-Supervised Method for Reasoning by Learning from Mistakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19156)
*Qirui Chen,Renjie Pi,Jiahui Gao,Lingpeng Kong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Reflective Recovery, a self-supervised fine-tuning strategy that converts failed reasoning traces of LLMs into training data, teaching models to recover from erroneous intermediate steps and overcoming the scaling collapse of standard imitation learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard data-driven fine-tuning for reasoning uses only perfect trajectories. When high-quality problems are limited, simply adding more positive examples stops improving performance (scaling collapse). Moreover, at inference time LLMs frequently make incorrect intermediate steps and then get stuck, because they were never trained to recognize and repair their own mistakes. The authors want a way to exploit abundant failed attempts, without external critics, to help models learn to self-correct during multi-step reasoning.

Method: They introduce Reflective Recovery, a self-supervised method that repurposes failed reasoning trajectories as recovery data. From an incorrect solution attempt, they take an initial prefix of the trajectory, prepend/concatenate it to the original prompt, and then fine-tune the model to output a correct solution from that (possibly corrupted) state. Since these prefixes likely contain errors, the model learns to detect and fix mistakes in-context, effectively conditioning on erroneous reasoning and steering back toward valid answers. The approach avoids external reward models or human annotations and stays within standard supervised fine-tuning.

Result: On benchmarks, Reflective Recovery substantially improves reasoning accuracy. For example, for DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, accuracy increases from 30.0% to 37.5% on AIME 2025 and from 37.6% to 47.8% on Minerva. Empirical analyses in the paper show that performance continues to scale with additional data, overcoming the previously observed scaling collapse when only perfect trajectories are used. The model exhibits more robust behavior when starting from erroneous partial chains of thought.

Conclusion: Using failed reasoning attempts as self-supervised recovery training signals enables LLMs to learn process-level self-correction, rather than just imitating final correct trajectories. Reflective Recovery breaks the scaling limitations of traditional imitation learning on scarce high-quality problem sets and induces emergent reflective reasoning capabilities, marking a shift from purely outcome-focused training to training that explicitly targets recovery from errors in the reasoning process.

Abstract: Data-driven fine-tuning is widely adopted to enhance reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs) due to its simplicity and efficiency. However, mainstream imitation learning methods that rely exclusively on perfect reasoning trajectories suffer from a Scaling Collapse: when the problem set is limited, increasing positive examples fails to yield continuous improvement. However, during inference, an LLM can not guarantee that every intermediate step is correct and is therefore prone to errors. Once such errors arise, the LLM often struggles to recover and may be further misled by the accumulation of previous mistakes. To address this, we propose Reflective Recovery, a simple yet effective self-supervised approach that transforms failed reasoning attempts into recovery training data. Specifically, we extract initial segments of failed trajectories, concatenate them with prompts, and use them to guide the LLM toward valid solutions. Because these segments from failed trajectories are likely to contain errors, this process teaches models to recognize and correct mistakes during reasoning, enabling recovery from erroneous states without relying on external critics or reward models. Evaluated on extensive benchmarks, Reflective Recovery significantly improves performance. On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, it boosts accuracy from 30.0% to 37.5% on AIME 2025 and from 37.6% to 47.8% on Minerva. More importantly, analyses demonstrate that it breaks the scaling collapse barrier and enables models to develop emergent self-correction behaviors, representing a paradigm shift from outcome-oriented memorization to process-oriented reflective reasoning.

</details>


### [10] [VisKG-LM: Compiling Knowledge Graphs into Visual Memory for Multiple-Choice Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19158)
*Yixin Peng,Er Jin,Shiwei Luo,Diego Collarana,Stefan Decker*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes VisKG-LM, a QA model that compiles knowledge graph subgraphs once into visual memories, which are then reused and consulted only at the final layer of a language model, improving accuracy and efficiency over existing graph-augmented and text-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge-graph-enhanced QA systems repeatedly re-encode the same retrieved KG subgraphs online with graph neural networks, which is computationally inefficient and couples graph reasoning tightly with language processing. The authors want to know if KG information can be compiled once into a reusable, read-only memory that is accessed only after the question and candidate answers are understood, reducing redundancy while still leveraging structured knowledge effectively.

Method: The authors introduce VisKG-LM, which decouples graph encoding from language reasoning by transforming each candidate-specific KG subgraph into Relation-Labeled Paths, then laying these paths out as 2D images that preserve their branching structure. These images are encoded offline into visual features and cached. During inference, a language model processes the question and candidate purely in text, and only its final layer attends to the precomputed visual-memory representations, reading both global layout and fine-grained relational information. They compare this setup with graph-propagation methods (e.g., GreaseLM), a larger vision-language model (GraphVis), and a text-only control given the same path content without the full visual interface.

Result: On CommonsenseQA, OpenBookQA, and MedQA-USMLE, VisKG-LM outperforms GreaseLM by 1.2, 0.8, and 4.3 accuracy points, respectively, while matching or beating GraphVis, a 7B-parameter vision-language model, despite using only about 400M online parameters. Compared to a text-only baseline that receives identical Relation-Labeled Paths but without the visual-memory interface, VisKG-LM gains 4.2, 6.5, and 5.1 points on the three benchmarks, demonstrating that both the compiled visual representation and late fusion scheme contribute to performance.

Conclusion: Compiled visual memory of knowledge-graph subgraphs is a viable alternative to online graph neural propagation for QA. Encoding subgraphs once as relation-labeled visual layouts and accessing them only at the final LM layer preserves or improves accuracy relative to more expensive graph reasoning approaches and even larger vision-language models. The additional gains over a text-only path representation indicate that the structured visual interface contributes meaningful signal beyond simple textualization of KG paths.

Abstract: Knowledge graphs are usually integrated into question answering by encoding a retrieved subgraph with a graph neural network and fusing it with the language model in the online inference path. The same subgraph is therefore re-encoded from scratch every time a pair is scored, across training epochs, seeds, and evaluation runs, even though the knowledge graph never changes. We ask whether the retrieved knowledge graphs can instead be compiled once, offline, and then accessed as read-only memory. VisKG-LM shows that it can, by decoupling graph encoding from language reasoning. It serializes each retrieved candidate-specific subgraph as Relation-Labeled Paths and renders the result as an image whose two-dimensional layout preserves the branching structure of the paths. Each image is encoded once, offline, and cached for reuse. At inference, the language model contextualizes the question and candidate from text alone, and only its final layer consults the cached visual memory, reading both its global layout and its local relational detail. The graph information thus enters only after the text has been understood. On the test sets of CommonsenseQA, OpenBookQA, and MedQA-USMLE, VisKG-LMimproves over GreaseLM by $1.2$, $0.8$, and $4.3$ points, respectively, while matching or surpassing GraphVis, a $7$B vision-language model, with only about $400$M online parameters. Against a matched text-only control that receives the identical Relation-Labeled Paths, it gains $4.2$, $6.5$, and $5.1$ points across the three benchmarks. These gains show that the complete visual-memory interface adds value beyond path textualization alone and support compiled visual memory as an alternative to online graph propagation.

</details>


### [11] [Advantage Scale Calibration Imbalance in Group-Relative Optimization under Low-Variance Rewards: Diagnosis and Bounded Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19164)
*Fei Ding*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes advantage scaling in verifier-style RLVR and proposes a unified calibration interface and new methods to better handle small reward differences without distorting KL regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In verifier-style reinforcement learning from verifier rewards (RLVR), existing group-relative optimization methods often treat how advantage values are scaled as a minor implementation detail. However, improper scaling can either ignore meaningful small reward differences or over-amplify tiny noise, leading to miscalibrated KL regularization and poor learning, especially in low-variance settings.

Method: The authors introduce an advantage-scale three-way calibration interface where a single within-group scale factor jointly controls (1) the strength of the reward optimization branch, (2) the weight of each prompt-level batch, and (3) the effective KL regularization when rewards are mapped back to their original cardinal scale. Using this interface, they analyze the behavior of algorithms like RLOO, Dr.GRPO, and GRPO, and identify their failure modes with small reward gaps. They then propose two new techniques: the Reward-Resolution Protocol, which filters out sub-resolution reward differences that should not inform preferences, and MaxNorm-AC, which normalizes advantages to ensure bounded amplification of credible nonzero gaps.

Result: Empirically, across both dense and mixture-of-experts (MoE) model architectures and on math and code reasoning benchmarks, the proposed MaxNorm-AC method outperforms strong robust-scaling baselines while avoiding the long inverse-scale tail that can arise from low-variance batches. This indicates better utilization of small but meaningful reward differences without destabilizing training.

Conclusion: Advantage scaling is a crucial design dimension in verifier-style RLVR, not a mere implementation detail. By explicitly calibrating how scale mediates reward strength, batch weighting, and KL regularization, the proposed interface clarifies why existing methods mishandle small reward gaps. The Reward-Resolution Protocol and MaxNorm-AC offer practical improvements that preserve KL calibration, ignore sub-resolution noise, and robustly learn from credible small advantages, leading to better performance in reasoning tasks.

Abstract: In verifier-style RLVR, group-relative optimization often treats advantage scale as an implementation detail. This paper separates two low-variance cases: sub-resolution jitter that should not become a preference signal, and credible but small cardinal gaps that should be learned without distorting KL calibration. We propose an advantage-scale three-way calibration interface: the same within-group scale denominator simultaneously determines the reward-branch strength, prompt-level batch weight, and the effective KL calibration induced when the reward branch is re-expressed on the original cardinal scale. This interface explains why RLOO / Dr.GRPO can let credible small gaps become KL dominated, whereas GRPO's standard-deviation denominator can amplify tiny gaps without bound. Based on this interface, we further introduce the Reward-Resolution Protocol and MaxNorm-AC, respectively filtering sub-resolution gaps and providing bounded cardinal recovery on credible nonzero gaps. Across dense / MoE architectures and math / code reasoning, MaxNorm-AC improves over the strongest robust-scale baseline while truncating the low-variance inverse-scale tail.

</details>


### [12] [To Memories and Beyond: From Remembering to Knowing You across Long-Term Multimodal Personal Archives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19167)
*Wenqi Zhou,Zhuorui Yu,Kaiao Wen,Hao Zheng,Xinyi Zheng,Peiran Wu,Enmin Zhou,Chi-Hao Wu,Junxiao Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ReaLMem, a real-world long‑term multimodal memory benchmark using multi‑year personal visual archives, plus ChronoProfiler, a temporal-weighting module that improves long-term, personalized reasoning in AI companions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long‑term memory benchmarks for AI are mostly synthetic and text-only, which fails to capture how humans actually remember: through causally connected, long‑term, often visual experiences. This limits research to shallow factual recall and prevents realistic evaluation of personalized AI assistants that must reason over evolving user histories and preferences.

Method: 1) Build ReaLMem, a benchmark derived from authentic multi‑year personal visual archives with first-person subjective annotations, and define three evaluation tiers: factual recall, persona inference, and predictive personalization. 2) Design ChronoProfiler, which assigns temporal stability scores to user attributes and uses them as a salience prior to resolve conflicts in temporally inconsistent preferences and to combine multiple co‑active preferences during complex personalized decisions. 3) Evaluate state-of-the-art multimodal LLMs and memory systems on ReaLMem, with and without ChronoProfiler, to analyze performance ceilings and bottlenecks.

Result: Experiments show that predictive personalization remains a challenging ceiling task even for frontier MLLMs and memory systems. There are clear performance gaps between base MLLMs and systems augmented with explicit memory. Models using high-quality, temporally informed user representations via ChronoProfiler significantly outperform those without such temporal modeling, especially on personalization-related tasks.

Conclusion: ReaLMem offers a realistic, multimodal, and longitudinal benchmark for studying long-term personalized memory in AI, while ChronoProfiler provides a simple but effective mechanism for incorporating temporal dynamics of user attributes. Together, they supply both an evaluation testbed and a modeling component that can drive future progress toward robust, lifelong AI companions capable of deep, temporally aware personalization.

Abstract: As AI systems evolve into personalized digital companions, a central capability is reasoning over a user's long-term personal history: not merely storing past events, but tracking longitudinal experiences and evolving preferences. Progress here is bottlenecked by evaluation, existing long-term memory benchmarks are largely synthetic and text-only, they overlook the visual records that anchor everyday human memory, lack the authentic and causally connected longitudinal data that real personalization demands, and consequently remain confined to shallow factual recall. We introduce ReaLMem (Real-world Long-term Multimodal Memory), the first benchmark built from authentic multi-year personal visual archives, paired with first-person subjective annotations. ReaLMem evaluates models across three cognitive tiers of increasing difficulty: factual recall, persona inference, and predictive personalization. We further propose ChronoProfiler, a temporal-weighting profiling module that computes temporal stability scores for user attributes and applies them as a salience prior, resolving conflicts among temporally inconsistent preferences and helping models compound multiple co-active preferences in complex personalized decisions. Extensive evaluation of frontier multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and memory systems on ReaLMem reveals predictive personalization as a consistent ceiling, exposes clear performance gaps and bottlenecks between MLLMs and memory systems, and shows that high-quality, temporally informed representations substantially improve personalization. Together, ReaLMem and ChronoProfiler provide an authentic testbed and a simple, effective mechanism for long-term personalization, laying a foundation for future research on lifelong AI companions.

</details>


### [13] [YNU-HPCC at SemEval-2025 Task 11: Bridging the Gap in Text-Based Emotion Using Multiple Prediction Headers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19238)
*Hao Yang,Jin Wang,Xuejie Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: YNU-HPCC participated in SemEval-2025 Task 11 (emotion in text) using a RoBERTa-based model, achieving a 0.44 official score and showing that single-emotion prediction heads and an all-English translated dataset work better.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridging the gap in text-based emotion recognition across multiple languages is challenging due to language variability and model complexity. The authors aim to build an effective system for SemEval-2025 Task 11 and to understand architectural and data-preprocessing choices that improve multilingual emotion detection performance.

Method: They use RoBERTa, a Transformer encoder-based model, as the backbone. They modify the output (classification) head so that the model predicts one emotion at a time instead of predicting multiple emotions (six emotions) simultaneously. The authors translate the entire multilingual dataset into English using Google Translate and train on this unified English version. They analyze performance using probabilistic outputs and attention patterns to compare (1) single vs. multi-head emotion prediction and (2) original multilingual data vs. translated English-only data.

Result: Their best system achieves an official ranking score of 0.44 on SemEval-2025 Task 11 subtask A, using all languages. Empirically, they find that a single prediction head dedicated to one emotion outperforms a model with six parallel heads predicting all six emotions. They also observe that training on the automatically translated, English-only dataset yields better performance than training on the original multilingual dataset.

Conclusion: The study concludes that, for this SemEval emotion detection task, simplifying the output architecture to one emotion-specific head and normalizing all inputs into a single language (English via machine translation) leads to better results. This suggests that careful design of prediction heads and language normalization can significantly boost performance in multilingual emotion recognition tasks.

Abstract: This paper describes the participation of the YNU-HPCC team in subtask A of task 11, Bridging the Gap in Text-Based Emotion at SemEval-2025. Our best-performing system employs the RoBERTa (Robustly Optimized BERT Approach) model, an improved version of BERT that utilizes the Transformer encoder architecture. We enhanced the output head to allow the model to process one emotion simultaneously. We obtained the official ranking score (0.44), including results from all languages. The entire dataset was translated into English using Google Translate to facilitate subsequent processing. Through probabilistic and attention analyses, we found that (I) a single prediction head performs better than six heads predicting six emotions simultaneously, and (II) training on a uniformly translated English dataset yields better results than using the original dataset. The code is available at: https://github.com/BGWH123/Semeval-2025-task11.

</details>


### [14] [Why Pretraining Fails to Share Cross-Lingual Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19291)
*Adam Gaber,Uriel Dolev,Elisabeth Fittschen,Bobby Cheng,Yuval Marton,Leshem Choshen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates why large language models (LLMs) show poor cross-lingual knowledge transfer and identifies disjoint token spaces as a key cause. It demonstrates that aligning languages into a shared token space significantly improves cross-lingual generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLMs perform well in many individual languages, they fail to transfer knowledge between languages as effectively as human multilinguals. The community has observed this limitation, but it is unclear at what stage and why it arises during multilingual pretraining. Understanding the root cause is essential for designing more efficient multilingual models that share knowledge across languages instead of redundantly relearning it.

Method: The authors pretrain two sizes of LLMs (360M and 7B parameters) and carefully analyze when and how cross-lingual generalization breaks down. To isolate the cause, they construct a controlled bilingual pretraining setup with two copies of the same language that share exactly the same text and tokenization scheme but use disjoint token IDs (separate vocabularies). This lets them test whether disjoint token spaces alone can cause knowledge compartmentalization, independent of linguistic differences. They then propose a simple word-wise translation scheme to map different languages into a shared token space and evaluate its impact on cross-lingual generalization efficiency.

Result: They find that poor cross-lingual generalization already emerges during pretraining and remains even after applying standard interventions. In the controlled bilingual experiments, using disjoint token spaces for identical data is sufficient to produce strong knowledge compartmentalization, proving that separate token IDs form a major barrier to cross-lingual transfer. When they instead map languages into a shared token space with simple word-level translation, cross-lingual knowledge generalization improves markedly, recovering up to 12.6% of the learning efficiency of the native language, which is a 14× improvement over the baseline.

Conclusion: Disjoint token spaces are a fundamental architectural barrier to cross-lingual knowledge sharing in multilingual LLMs. Even when languages are effectively identical, separate vocabularies cause the model to isolate knowledge instead of sharing it. By aligning languages into a shared token space, even with a simple word-wise scheme, models can achieve substantially better cross-lingual generalization. This suggests that future multilingual pretraining should prioritize shared vocabularies or explicit token-space alignment to improve transfer and efficiency.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have made remarkable progress in the processing and modeling of many languages. Yet, unlike human multilinguals, they exhibit surprisingly limited cross-lingual knowledge transfer. While this limitation is well documented, its origins during multilingual training remain unclear. We pretrain 360M- and 7B-parameter LLMs and show that poor cross-lingual knowledge generalization emerges during pretraining and persists under standard interventions. To isolate its cause, we employ a controlled bilingual pretraining setting using two copies of the same language, sharing identical text and token segmentation, but mapped to disjoint token spaces. We find that disjoint tokens alone are enough to induce knowledge compartmentalization, even between identical copies of the same language, establishing disjoint token spaces as a fundamental barrier to cross-lingual knowledge generalization. Guided by this understanding, we suggest mapping languages into a shared token space by simple word-wise translation and find it substantially improves cross-lingual knowledge generalization, recovering up to 12.6\% of native-language learning efficiency --- 14$\times$ the baseline.

</details>


### [15] [A frontend-backend architecture for tool calls in full-duplex speech models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19334)
*Ke Hu,Slyne Deng,Chen Chen,Elena Rastorgueva,Edresson Casanova,Punit Kumar,Dharmendra Choudhary,Nikhil Srihari,Ameya Sunil Mahabaleshwarkar,Viet Anh Trinh,Slim Essid,Oluwatobi Olabiyi,Zhehuai Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a modular full-duplex speech-to-speech system that delegates tool usage to a text LLM backend via a lightweight token-based interface, preserving low-latency, natural interaction while enabling strong tool-call capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing full-duplex speech-to-speech models enable natural, low-latency conversations but struggle to integrate external tool usage and agentic behaviors without harming latency, robustness, or duplex turn-taking. There is a need for an architecture that adds powerful tool-call capabilities to such systems in a modular way, without heavily modifying the speech frontend.

Method: They introduce a frontend-backend architecture: a full-duplex speech-to-text frontend is trained to emit a special delegation token that triggers sending streaming ASR transcripts to a text-only backend LLM for tool calls. The backend handles tool selection and execution, and its tool-call results are injected back into the frontend via a lightweight "prefill-and-repeat" mechanism. The frontend then continues the conversation and uses streaming TTS to respond to the user, keeping normal duplex behaviors like barge-in and low latency. The method is evaluated on single-turn tool-call tasks and multi-turn benchmarks with different backend sizes.

Result: In single-turn tool-call evaluations, the system reaches 92–97% tool-call recall, competitive tool-call prediction, and 81.2% accuracy in rejecting irrelevant tool calls. With a larger backend model (Qwen3-235B-A22B), it performs competitively on Full-Duplex-Bench-V3 relative to both open- and closed-source systems, and significantly surpasses GPT-realtime-mini and Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B-Instruct on EVA-Bench.

Conclusion: Delegating tool use to a text LLM backend through a simple delegation token and prefill mechanism is an effective, modular way to augment full-duplex speech systems with strong tool-call and agentic capabilities, while preserving natural low-latency conversational dynamics and requiring only minor changes to the speech frontend.

Abstract: Full-duplex speech-to-speech (S2S) models provide natural, low-latency conversational interaction and would benefit from the ability to use external tools and complete voice-agent tasks. We propose a frontend-backend architecture where a duplex speech-to-text frontend learns to emit a delegation token and forwards streaming ASR transcripts to a text-based backend LLM for tool calls. Tool-call results from the backend are injected back into the frontend through a lightweight prefill-and-repeat mechanism and then synthesized using streaming TTS to the user. Our approach largely preserves regular duplex turn-taking, interruption handling, and low-latency interaction as it requires minimal modifications to the frontend model. In a single-turn tool-call evaluation, our system achieves 92-97% tool-call recall, competitive tool-call prediction performance, and 81.2% accuracy in rejecting irrelevant calls. When equipped with a larger backend (e.g., Qwen3-235B-A22B), our system achieves competitive results on Full-Duplex-Bench-V3 compared to open and closed source models, and significantly outperforms GPT-realtime-mini and Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B-Instruct on EVA-Bench. These results demonstrate that backend delegation is an effective and modular approach for combining natural duplex speech interaction with strong agentic tool-call capabilities.

</details>


### [16] [The Role of Fine-grained Harm Signals in LLM Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19366)
*Soyeon Park,Seogyeong Jeong,Sunwoo Kim,Alice Oh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how category-specific harmfulness representations in LLMs behave once the common, general harmfulness component is removed, and how these residuals affect safety-related behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work shows that LLMs have both a shared general harmfulness representation and category-specific harmfulness representations (e.g., for different risk domains). However, it is unclear what extra role these category-specific parts play beyond the general component for safety behaviors like refusal and internal alignment. The authors want to understand whether and how the purely category-specific parts (after removing the general harmfulness signal) still encode harmfulness and influence downstream safety.

Method: They decompose harmfulness representations in LLMs into a shared general component and category-specific components across 11 risk categories. By projecting out the shared general harmfulness direction from each category’s representation at each layer, they obtain category residual vectors that are orthogonal to general harmfulness. They then use activation steering with these residuals in three instruction-tuned LLMs to test: (1) whether the residuals still encode harmfulness, (2) whether they induce refusal behavior, and (3) how they affect downstream internal alignment with the shared general harmfulness representation.

Result: They find that: (1) The degree to which category residuals encode harmfulness differs by risk category, and this category-wise pattern is quite consistent across different LLMs. (2) The extent to which category residuals cause the model to refuse harmful requests also varies by category, but this pattern is more dependent on the specific model. (3) Applying category residuals tends to increase the models’ internal alignment with the shared general harmfulness representation downstream in the network, even though the residuals are orthogonal to that representation at the layer where they are defined.

Conclusion: Category-specific residual directions, even when made orthogonal to a shared general harmfulness signal at a given layer, still have important effects on both internal representations and behavioral safety outcomes. Therefore, understanding and controlling LLM safety cannot rely solely on a single general harmfulness direction; it must also account for finer-grained, category-level residual structure. More broadly, the work shows that directions orthogonal to a concept at one layer can still contribute to amplification of that concept deeper in the model.

Abstract: Prior work has shown that internal harmfulness representations in large language models vary across risk categories, while sharing a common general harm representation component. This raises a question about the role of the category-specific component beyond general harm representation in LLM safety. To answer this question, we isolate the category-specific component by removing shared general harmfulness representation from each categorical harmfulness representation, yielding a category residual that is orthogonal to general harmfulness at every layer. Using activation steering with category residuals across 11 risk categories in 3 instruction-tuned LLMs, we find that whether category residuals encode harmfulness varies across categories, and that this category-wise pattern is similar across models. Whether category residuals induce refusal also varies across categories, but this category-wise pattern is more model-dependent. We also find that category residuals increase LLMs' downstream internal alignment with shared general harmfulness representation. Together, these findings demonstrate that more fine-grained category residuals should also be considered beyond shared general harmfulness representation to fully understand LLM safety. More broadly, our findings show that even a direction orthogonal to a concept at one layer can contribute to the concept's downstream amplification.

</details>


### [17] [Less Is More: Graph-free Multimodal RAG via Multi-signal Late Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19417)
*Tithi Rakshit,Hongkuan Zhou,Lavdim Halilaj,Yuqicheng Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces TrioRAG, a graph-free multimodal RAG framework that uses three complementary retrieval signals instead of explicit graphs, and AutoQA, a new multimodal automotive benchmark based on noisy web images. TrioRAG matches or beats graph-based systems while being cheaper and faster, and AutoQA stresses settings where image retrieval is weak but text-based and VLM-enhanced queries remain robust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-based multimodal RAG systems are effective for cross-document QA but require expensive corpus-level graph construction, are slow at query time, and are difficult to maintain as corpora evolve. Moreover, many benchmarks focus on clean, document-sourced figures rather than noisy real-world images, and they may not stress scenarios where images are out-of-corpus and retrieval must rely heavily on text signals. The authors aim to design a simpler, more efficient retrieval framework that avoids graphs, and to create a benchmark that better reflects realistic, noisy multimodal conditions in the automotive domain.

Method: They propose TrioRAG, a graph-free multimodal retrieval-augmented generation framework that uses three complementary signals: (1) the original question, (2) an anchor image, and (3) a VLM-enhanced query generated from both the question and image. Each signal independently retrieves candidates over a shared multi-vector index containing both page text and page images. Retrieval results from the three signals are then combined via late fusion rather than an explicit corpus-level graph. Additionally, they construct AutoQA, a multimodal automotive benchmark where questions are grounded in noisy web-sourced images and require reasoning across multiple manuals; the benchmark is curated using models rather than human annotation and is explicitly positioned as a testbed rather than a human-validated gold standard.

Result: On three benchmarks, TrioRAG performs on par with or better than graph-based multimodal RAG systems, while reducing overall cost and speeding up per-query inference by a factor of 1.6 to 2.3. In the AutoQA setting, because questions are grounded in out-of-corpus web images, pure image retrieval is weak, achieving only 19.3% document-level recall. In contrast, text-based retrieval signals—especially the VLM-enhanced query—maintain strong retrieval performance, demonstrating the robustness and importance of the textual and VLM-derived components in this challenging setting.

Conclusion: TrioRAG shows that effective multimodal, cross-document RAG can be achieved without constructing expensive corpus-level graphs, using instead three complementary retrieval signals fused late over a shared multi-vector index, which yields performance comparable to or better than graph-based approaches at lower cost and latency. AutoQA provides a challenging, realistic benchmark with noisy, out-of-corpus images that highlights the limitations of pure image retrieval and underscores the value of robust text and VLM-enhanced queries. Together, they suggest that graph-free, signal-fusion approaches are a promising direction for practical, scalable multimodal RAG systems, especially in domains like automotive documentation where images and documents are heterogeneous and noisy.

Abstract: Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is widely used for multimodal, cross-document question answering. However, building corpus-level graphs is expensive, slow to query, and difficult to maintain. We present TrioRAG, a graph-free multimodal framework that integrates evidence from three complementary signals: the question, the anchor image, and a VLM-enhanced query generated from both. Each signal retrieves independently over a shared multi-vector index of page text and page images, and the results are combined through late fusion. Further, we introduce AutoQA, a multimodal automotive benchmark whose questions are grounded in noisy, web-sourced images rather than clean document-sourced figures. Its questions require reasoning across manuals. We position it as a model-curated testbed rather than a human-validated gold standard. Across three benchmarks, TrioRAG matches or outperforms graph-based systems while reducing total cost and accelerating per-query inference by 1.6-2.3 times. By construction, AutoQA grounds its questions in out-of-corpus web images. In this setting image retrieval reaches only 19.3% document-level recall, while text-derived signals, especially the VLM-enhanced query, keep retrieval robust.

</details>


### [18] [For Your Eyes Only: Evaluating Coordination Between Isolated Language Model Instances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19504)
*Alexander Shirnin,Aleksey Kudelya*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether large language models can hide and detect coordination signals in natural language between independent instances of the same model, introducing a signalling game benchmark and showing that some frontier models can coordinate almost perfectly even when obvious signals are removed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI-generated text is increasingly read and used by other AI systems, it becomes important to know whether models can embed subtle, hard-to-detect signals in otherwise natural language that other instances of the same model can reliably decode, without any explicit shared key or extra training. This has implications for safety, misuse, and robustness of automated AI-AI workflows, where covert coordination or misdirection might emerge unintentionally or be exploited deliberately.

Method: The authors design a cooperative signalling game called For Your Eyes Only. In each round, a Sender model instance receives two words, one of which is a hidden target, and must write natural-language descriptions for both. A separate Receiver instance, with no shared memory or special training, sees only the descriptions and must guess which word is the target. They run this game over 300 word pairs drawn from psycholinguistic datasets, across seven contemporary language models from four architecture families. To avoid confounds from inherent output preferences (e.g., always picking one position), they use a Double-Pass Success Rate metric, which repeats trials with role/ordering changes to factor out simple biases. They also introduce conditions that require the Sender to avoid easily detectable signalling patterns, then measure how performance changes, and test whether models can instead use their capability for deliberate misdirection.

Result: Across models, many can coordinate to some degree when free to use any patterns, but most lose coordination ability once obvious or easily detectable signalling strategies are filtered out. However, one frontier model maintains almost perfect Sender–Receiver coordination even under these constraints, indicating robust, subtle signalling capabilities. The experiments also show that models can repurpose this capability for intentional misdirection—using descriptions that lead the Receiver away from the true target. Moreover, coordination performance is systematically stronger when Sender and Receiver share the same architecture than when they differ, highlighting architecture-specific shared inductive biases.

Conclusion: Independent instances of modern language models can embed and detect coordination signals in natural language purely via shared pretraining and instructions, without explicit keys or further training. While many models’ coordination collapses when constrained to avoid simple, detectable signals, at least one frontier model retains near-perfect, hard-to-detect coordination and can be steered toward misdirection. This suggests that covert signalling and architecture-specific coordination are realistic capabilities in current models, with implications for safety, oversight, and the design of AI systems that communicate with each other.

Abstract: As model-generated content is increasingly consumed by other model instances in automated workflows, a practically important question arises: can a model embed a signal in natural language that an independent instance of the same model can detect, relying only on shared pre-training and task instructions, without any shared memory or coordination-specific training? We introduce For Your Eyes Only, a cooperative signalling game designed to evaluate this directly. A Sender produces free-form descriptions for two words, one of which is a hidden target; an isolated Receiver must identify it. We evaluate seven contemporary models from four architectural families on 300 word pairs from established psycholinguistic corpora, using the Double-Pass Success Rate to control for output biases. We find that most models struggle to maintain coordination once they are required to avoid detectable signals, while one frontier model retains near-perfect performance even after such filtering. We further show that models can direct this capability toward deliberate misdirection, and that coordination is consistently weaker across architectures than within them.

</details>


### [19] [From Parameters to Behaviors: A Survey of Model Fusion for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19553)
*Shuo Cai,Yanggan Gu,Zihao Wang,Yuanyi Wang,Yibo Yan,Wenjun Wang,Yuhang Liu,Guanghao Zhu,Sirui Huang,Ming Li,Hongxia Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper is a survey that defines and systematizes the area of model fusion, organizing prior work into three levels and outlining metrics, benchmarks, challenges, and future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There are millions of models (e.g., on Hugging Face) that could be reused and combined, but existing surveys only cover fragments of this space and lack a unified definition and taxonomy of model fusion. Researchers need a clear conceptual map to understand, compare, and advance techniques for integrating capabilities from multiple source models into a single target model.

Method: The authors perform a literature survey on model fusion methods and systematically categorize prior work into three levels: parameter-level fusion (combining model weights), representation-level fusion (combining intermediate features/embeddings), and behavior-level fusion (combining outputs or decision behaviors). They also review and organize evaluation metrics, benchmarks, and application scenarios, and compile a public list of model-fusion-related papers.

Result: The survey delivers a unified definition of model fusion, a three-level taxonomy (parameter-, representation-, and behavior-level), an organized overview of methods under each level, and a structured summary of evaluation practices, benchmarks, and applications. It also aggregates a comprehensive bibliography of model fusion work in an open GitHub repository.

Conclusion: Model fusion is framed as a distinct and structured research area with clear subcategories and evaluation practices. The paper provides a roadmap of existing techniques and open problems, highlights current challenges, and points out promising directions for future research on reusing and integrating capabilities from multiple models into a single fused model.

Abstract: Model fusion integrates the capabilities from source models into a single target model. As of June 2026, Hugging Face hosts more than 2M models. This growing pool provides a rich base for model reuse and capability integration. Yet existing surveys often cover only separate parts of this space, and they do not provide a unified definition or a systematic taxonomy. This survey defines model fusion and organizes prior work into three levels: parameter-level, representation-level, and behavior-level fusion. We also review related metrics, benchmarks, and applications, summarize current challenges, and identify future directions. Our goal is to provide a clear map of this area and support future work on model fusion. A comprehensive list of papers about model fusion is available at https://github.com/Baicaihaochi/Awesome-Model-Fusion-Survey.

</details>


### [20] [CliniCIRCA: A Modular LLM Framework for Constructing Longitudinal Mental Health Patient Journeys from Raw EHR Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19585)
*Aiwei Ivy Zhang,Nimra Ishfaq,Mohit Chandra,Santiago Alvarez Lesmes,Adam Coscia,Khatiya Chelidze Moon,Xiaohan Ding,Munmun De Choudhury*

Main category: cs.CL
