TL;DR: The paper introduces CliniCIRCA, a multi-stage LLM framework that reconstructs temporally ordered patient journeys from unstructured mental health discharge summaries without explicit timestamps, and shows that instruction tuning improves performance on related tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinicians need to reason about patients’ longitudinal mental health journeys, but relevant biological, psychological, and social events are scattered in unstructured narratives without consistent timestamps, making it hard to recover accurate temporal sequences. Existing methods do not adequately temporally classify such events across long, unstructured discharge summaries, especially when event-level timestamps are missing.

Method: The authors propose CliniCIRCA, a multi-stage large language model framework designed to (1) extract clinical events from discharge summaries, (2) temporally classify and anchor them to calendar time while handling imprecise temporal expressions, and (3) generate temporally grounded, date-grouped summaries. They construct a benchmark from 52 discharge summaries sampled from 14,882 MIMIC-III mental health admissions, use LLMs to produce temporally tagged events, then perform clinician-in-the-loop correction to create gold-standard labels. They also scale the process to generate 1,000 silver-standard timelines, and use these for instruction tuning several open-weight models, comparing performance to zero- and few-shot prompting on extraction, temporal tagging, and summarization tasks.

Result: On the 52-note benchmark, CliniCIRCA produces 15,891 temporally tagged events; clinicians correct 629 errors to obtain a gold-standard set of temporally labeled events and timelines. The framework compresses each original discharge summary by a factor of about 1.52 into a chronological, date-grouped record. Scaling up, the authors generate 1,000 silver-standard timelines and use them for instruction tuning five open-weight models. Across both silver-standard and clinician-verified evaluations, instruction-tuned models generally outperform zero- and few-shot prompted baselines on event extraction, temporal tagging, and temporal summarization.

Conclusion: CliniCIRCA demonstrates that multi-stage LLM frameworks can reconstruct and temporally organize complex mental health patient journeys from unstructured discharge summaries even without explicit event timestamps. Clinician-in-the-loop correction yields high-quality gold standards, and large-scale silver-standard timelines can effectively support instruction tuning. Overall, temporally grounded summarization and instruction-tuned models improve downstream performance on key temporal reasoning tasks in mental health clinical narratives.

Abstract: In mental health care, reasoning over patient journeys is a key task for clinicians. Yet these journeys, encompassing a longitudinal progression of biological, psychological, and social events, are often spread across disparate unstructured text narratives, making temporal recovery challenging. We present CliniCIRCA, a multi-stage LLM framework for Calendar-anchored, Imprecision-aware Reconstruction of Clinical Annals. To our knowledge, CliniCIRCA is the first to temporally classify clinical events across unstructured discharge summaries without event-level timestamps. From 14,882 MIMIC-III mental health admissions, we first construct a benchmark of 52 discharge summaries on which CliniCIRCA produces 15,891 temporally tagged events. After correcting 629 errors based on a clinician-in-the-loop evaluation, we produce verified gold-standard labels. Finally, the corrected timelines drive a temporally grounded summarization stage that compresses each source 1.52 times into a date-grouped chronological record. We then scale the framework to generate 1,000 silver-standard timelines and evaluate them as training data. Compared with zero- and few-shot prompting, instruction tuning generally improves five open-weight models on event extraction, temporal tagging, and summarization across silver and clinician-verified evaluations.

</details>


### [21] [Form Over Content In Gradient-Based Data Attribution Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19589)
*Sunwoo Kim,Seokwon Jung,Sohyung Kim,Seong Joon Oh,Alice Oh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that gradient-based data attribution methods for language models mostly capture answer format similarity rather than underlying task or semantic similarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is ongoing debate about what gradient similarity in data attribution truly measures: some claim it captures task-relevant skills or semantics, while other evidence suggests it is driven mainly by surface form. This uncertainty matters because these methods are widely used to analyze and select training data for large language models, especially for fine-tuning. The paper aims to resolve this debate in a controlled way.

Method: The authors decouple task semantics from answer format by rendering benchmark datasets in multiple answer formats, so that some dataset pairs share the same task but different formats, while others share the same format but different tasks. They compute gradient similarity (alignment) between examples across these conditions, using disattenuated cosine similarity. They evaluate alignment across pretraining checkpoints, post-training stages, and across different model scales and families. They also inspect LESS, a gradient-based data selection method for instruction tuning, to see what kinds of examples it prefers.

Result: They find that gradient alignment strongly follows answer format rather than task semantics: benchmarks that share answer format have high alignment (disattenuated cosine around 0.4), whereas the same benchmarks rendered in different answer formats show essentially no alignment (around 0.0). This relationship holds consistently across training stages, model sizes, and architectures. Analysis of the LESS method’s selected data shows that the selections systematically over-represent examples sharing the target task’s answer format.

Conclusion: Gradient-based attribution and selection methods largely track answer format (surface form) instead of deeper task semantics in supervised fine-tuning settings. Therefore, interpreting gradient similarity as measuring task-relevant skills is misleading unless answer format is carefully controlled. Future evaluations and uses of gradient-based methods should rely on setups where task and format vary independently to ensure robustness and reliable interpretation.

Abstract: Data attribution methods using gradient similarity are widely used to analyze and select training data for large language models, but what gradient similarity actually measures is debated. Some interpret it as identifying task-relevant skills, while other work reports that surface form is the main factor. We resolve this debate for supervised fine-tuning examples by varying task and answer format independently. Specifically, we render benchmarks in different answer formats, such that datasets can share a task without a format or a format without a task. We find that gradient alignment follows the answer format, as benchmark pairs sharing an answer format align strongly (disattenuated cosine near 0.4), while same benchmarks rendered with different answer format classes show no alignment (near 0.0). We demonstrate that this ordering holds from the earliest pretraining checkpoints through post-training, and across model scales and families. We then analyze the released selections of LESS, a gradient-based data selection method for instruction tuning, and find that each target's selections over-represent the target's own answer format. Hence, we demonstrate that gradient-based attribution methods track format similarity more than task semantics, meaning that such methods, as well as the semantic interpretation of the gradient, should be tested on data where answer format and task vary independently for greater robustness and reliability.

</details>


### [22] [Full-Duplex Speech Models Take the Floor When Asked, Not When Needed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19596)
*Linkai Peng,Baorian Nuchged,Kaiqi Fu,Yuyang Yao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies when full-duplex speech models decide to speak and whether they self-initiate speech like humans (e.g., to correct errors or warn of danger). It finds that models mainly respond to being directly addressed or to silence, and rarely intervene to correct false statements or hazards, revealing a gap in both turn-taking and safety-relevant content responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-duplex (simultaneous listening and speaking) speech assistants are becoming feasible and promise more natural, always-on interactions. However, beyond recognizing speech, such models must decide *when* to speak. Humans self-select turns not only when addressed or when there is silence, but also to correct falsehoods, fill in missing information, or warn of danger. The authors want to know whether current full-duplex models exhibit similar socially and safety-relevant turn-taking behavior, especially in terms of spontaneously intervening when hearing false facts or hazards.

Method: The authors construct context-matched English monologues where only a trigger utterance varies within the same topic, allowing them to separate the *reason* to speak (e.g., being addressed, hearing a false fact, a hazard, a missing word) from the *opportunity* to speak. They define 10 experimental conditions based on different turn-allocation rules derived from conversation analysis. To minimize speaking opportunities created purely by silence, they compress inter-word pauses. They then test five model families (including Moshi and PersonaPlex), measuring both whether the models initiate speech in response to different triggers and the content of their responses. They also analyze frame-level text-token probabilities over the first 2 seconds after the trigger ends, comparing conditions such as Neutral vs. false facts.

Result: Across five model families, the models are far more reliably triggered to speak when they are directly addressed or when there is silence than when they encounter false facts or hazards. For Moshi and PersonaPlex, frame-level text-token probabilities are actually *lower* for false-fact triggers compared to Neutral in the first 2 seconds after the trigger ends, indicating reduced tendency to speak up in the presence of misinformation. Providing pauses or explicit permission to interrupt does not eliminate this gap. When the models are explicitly given the conversational floor, they answer most direct questions; however, among their non-empty responses to false facts, only about 14–15% challenge the incorrect claim, and among responses to hazards, only about 4–7% warn of danger.

Conclusion: Current full-duplex speech models do not self-initiate speech in human-like ways in safety-critical or epistemically important situations. They are biased toward speaking when directly addressed or when a turn is clearly available, but they rarely intervene to correct false statements or warn about hazards, and they often fail to produce corrective content even when they do respond. This reveals a significant gap in both speech initiation mechanisms and the semantic/safety grounding of response content. The authors argue that closing this gap will require models with genuine content understanding and explicit decision mechanisms for when and how to intervene based on what they understand, not just on conversational timing cues.

Abstract: Full-duplex speech models listen and speak at once, promising always-on assistants. Yet they must also decide when they should speak. Human listeners speak when addressed or when the speaker stops, but also self-select to correct a false claim, supply a missing word, or warn of danger. We ask whether full-duplex models do the same. To separate the reason to speak from the opportunity, we construct context-matched English monologues in which only the trigger utterance varies within a topic, define 10 conditions from turn-allocation rules, and compress inter-word pauses to limit opportunities created by silence. Across five model families, being addressed and silence are far more reliable triggers than false facts or hazards. Frame-level text-token probabilities in Moshi and PersonaPlex are lower for false facts than for Neutral when averaged over the first 2\,s after trigger end. Pauses or permission to interrupt do not close this gap either. Given the floor, Moshi and PersonaPlex answer most direct questions, yet the proportion of non-empty false-fact replies that challenge the claim is only .14--.15, and the proportion of hazard replies that warn of danger is .04--.07. This paper thus identifies a gap in both speech initiation and response content. Closing it requires genuine content understanding and intervention decisions grounded in it.

</details>


### [23] [Chain-of-Thought Entropy as a Reliability Signal: A Preregistered Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19606)
*Theodore O. Cochran*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Independent reproduction study on chain-of-thought entropy in LLMs: shape of entropy trajectory reliably predicts correctness; total entropy drop is setting-dependent and less robust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work (Zhao 2026) claimed a dissociation: whether the *shape* of an LLM’s chain-of-thought (CoT) entropy trajectory is predictive of answer correctness, while the *magnitude* of the total entropy reduction is not. However, the non-predictive magnitude result was based on a single small-scale configuration (one model, one seed, 300 problems), raising concerns about robustness, generality, and potential overinterpretation. Given the growing interest in using internal uncertainty signals such as entropy for selective prediction and evaluation, there is a need to confirm which aspects of the entropy dynamics are reliable confidence indicators across models, datasets, and protocols.

Method: Pre-registered empirical reproduction using four open-weight LLMs, including a reasoning-distilled model not covered previously. The study evaluates chain-of-thought entropy trajectories over the full GSM8K and MATH-500 benchmarks. It operationalizes two main entropy-based signals: (1) a binary and graded measure of trajectory *shape* (e.g., monotone vs non-monotone entropy evolution, and counts of shape violations) and (2) the *total entropy drop* from start to end of the chain. It then measures how these signals correlate with final-answer correctness, using accuracy gaps, rank correlations, ROC area, and risk-coverage area, while documenting seven protocol differences from the original and exploring additional metrics such as final-step entropy alone.

Result: The shape-based signal replicates robustly: monotone entropy trajectories are substantially more accurate than non-monotone ones on the anchor model (+9.6 percentage points on GSM8K, +27.5 on MATH-500). However, the magnitude-based signal (total entropy drop) is not robust: its correlation with correctness varies strongly by setting, with near-zero or negative rank correlation on GSM8K (-0.018) but a moderate positive correlation on MATH-500 (+0.414). On the reasoning-distilled model, the binary shape flag is extremely sparse (fires in ~1% of chains), making the pre-registered contrast underpowered, but a graded count of shape violations remains predictive. Exploratorily, final-step entropy alone outperforms the binary shape flag by ROC area in all eight model–benchmark combinations, and by risk-coverage area in most of them, though the exact comparison depends on an integration range that was not specified in the original work.

Conclusion: The study confirms that the *shape* of CoT entropy trajectories is a reproducible predictor of correctness across models and benchmarks, reinforcing Zhao’s central qualitative claim. In contrast, the *magnitude* of entropy drop is not universally non-predictive; its effectiveness is highly context-dependent, working in some datasets (e.g., MATH-500) but failing in others (e.g., GSM8K). Moreover, simple final-step entropy appears to be an even stronger and more reliable uncertainty signal than the binary shape flag across tested conditions. Overall, the paper clarifies which entropy-based signals are robust, delineates their protocol sensitivities, and provides a more nuanced map of when and how CoT entropy can be used as a confidence or selective prediction tool.

Abstract: This empirical study is an independent reproduction of the dissociation Zhao reported in 2026. The shape of a large language model's chain-of-thought entropy trajectory predicts whether the final answer is correct, while the magnitude of its total entropy drop does not. The dissociation merits reproduction because the magnitude half rests on a single 300-problem run with one model at one seed, while the shape half was reported at full scale on both benchmarks and on a second model family. Registered at OSF before any confirmatory run, the reproduction crosses the complete GSM8K and MATH-500 benchmark test sets with four open-weight models including one reasoning-distilled model of a kind the original did not test. The shape signal replicates. The magnitude signal divides by setting. On the anchor model the accuracy gap between monotone and non-monotone chains is +9.6 percentage points on GSM8K and +27.5 on MATH-500, while the rank correlation of the total entropy drop with correctness is -0.018 on GSM8K and +0.414 on MATH-500. On the reasoning-distilled model the binary form of the shape signal fires on about one chain in a hundred, too few to estimate the registered contrast, while the graded violation count remains predictive there. In an exploratory comparison the final-step entropy alone outperforms the binary shape flag in all eight model-by-benchmark cells by ROC area, and in six or seven by the risk-coverage area the original reports, depending on an integration range the original does not state. The study contributes a reproduction of the shape signal at full test-set scale under seven documented protocol differences, a map of the settings where the magnitude signal holds and fails, and measurements of four protocol dependencies the original does not report.

</details>


### [24] [Semantic Layer Induction from Raw Telemetry via Hierarchical LLM and RAG Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19615)
*Yuanzhe Jia,Ali Anaissi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes an automated framework to build a business semantic layer directly from noisy application telemetry logs, greatly reducing manual engineering while improving semantic quality and reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern applications produce huge amounts of heterogeneous telemetry logs, but it is difficult and costly to convert these raw, noisy events into consistent business concepts and KPIs. Existing approaches rely on manual parsing logic, brittle mappings, and significant human effort to reconcile semantics across systems. There is a need for an automated, scalable way to infer a coherent business semantic layer from raw telemetry without heavy supervision or rule engineering.

Method: The paper introduces an end-to-end framework with a two-stage semantic abstraction process. In the first stage, it uses large language model (LLM) inference, enhanced with domain-specific industry knowledge, to identify high-level business features from raw logs. In the second stage, it constructs fine-grained business nodes via a structured pipeline that performs data refinement, hybrid retrieval, multi-stage filtering, semantic clustering, and canonical naming. The system also incorporates an LLM-as-Judge component to evaluate and monitor semantic quality at scale without labeled data.

Result: On production-scale telemetry, the framework improves human-assessed semantic quality from about 50 to over 80 on a 100-point scale, reduces maintenance effort by 80%, and filters out 74% of noisy signals. The integrated LLM-as-Judge evaluation achieves a Cohen’s kappa of 0.87 with human raters, indicating high agreement and reliable automatic quality assessment.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that its framework successfully automates induction of a business semantic layer directly from raw telemetry logs, overcoming a previously underexplored problem space. It operates without labeled training data or manually crafted rules, significantly enhances semantic quality, reduces maintenance effort, and provides a scalable evaluation mechanism. This sets it apart from prior work on log analysis and semantic modeling, offering a practical solution for enterprise telemetry understanding and KPI derivation.

Abstract: Modern applications generate massive volumes of raw telemetry data, but translating those noisy, heterogeneous event streams into actionable business insights remains a fundamental challenge. Data engineers and analysts expend substantial effort reconciling semantic discrepancies, hand-crafting parsing logics, and maintaining fragile mappings between raw data and business KPIs. In this paper, we present an end-to-end framework that fully automates the construction of a business semantic layer from application raw logs. Our approach introduces a two-stage semantic abstraction: first, high-level business features are identified via LLM inference augmented with domain-specific industry knowledge; second, fine-grained business nodes are derived through a structured pipeline comprising data refinement, hybrid retrieval, multi-stage filtering, semantic clustering, and canonical naming. Evaluation on production-scale telemetry demonstrates that our system improves human-assessed semantic quality from 50 to 80+ on a 100-point scale, reduces maintenance effort by 80%, filters out 74% of noise, and achieves 0.87 Cohen's kappa via an integrated LLM-as-Judge evaluation, enabling continuous, scalable quality assurance. Overall, our work distinguishes itself from prior work by addressing the novel problem of business semantic layer induction from raw telemetry, operating without labeled training data or manual rule engineering.

</details>


### [25] [Improving Cross-Lingual Transfer for Sequential Sentence Classification in Research Papers via Structural Similarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19650)
*Kazuhiro Yamauchi,Marie Katsurai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies cross-lingual transfer for sequential sentence classification in scientific articles, finding that discourse-structural similarity—not linguistic similarity—is what mainly predicts successful transfer, and proposes generative methods that exploit this structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sequential sentence classification helps structure scientific articles (e.g., Intro, Methods, Results). Most work is in English, but multilingual digital libraries need tools for many languages, where labeled data are scarce. Cross-lingual transfer is a solution, and prior NLP work suggests that linguistic similarity between languages helps transfer. However, SSC relies heavily on discourse-level patterns (label sequences, positions in the paper) that might be similar across languages. The authors want to understand what really drives transfer success in SSC and how to exploit that for low-resource languages.

Method: They construct a multilingual SSC dataset in 13 non-English languages from five academic databases, with sentences labeled by rhetorical role. They conduct cross-lingual transfer experiments using both encoder-based models (e.g., multilingual transformers) and generative models. They quantitatively analyze how linguistic proximity, structural similarity of rhetorical organization, and label distribution similarity between source and target relate to transfer performance. Based on the finding that structural and label-distribution similarities matter most, they design three generative-model methods that explicitly integrate structural information (e.g., label sequence patterns and positional cues) to improve transfer, and evaluate them in-domain and on unseen languages.

Result: Empirically, linguistic proximity between source and target languages does not reliably predict cross-lingual SSC performance. Structural similarity in rhetorical organization across languages shows a weak but consistent positive correlation with transfer success. After controlling for source-language performance, similarity in label distributions (i.e., how often each rhetorical label appears) is the strongest and most consistent predictor of transfer performance. The proposed structure-aware generative methods, when combined, match the best encoder-based baselines in in-domain settings and surpass the strongest encoder baseline in transfer to languages not seen during training.

Conclusion: For sequential sentence classification in scientific texts, discourse-structural factors (rhetorical organization and label distributions) are more important for cross-lingual transfer than traditional notions of linguistic similarity. This shifts the focus from language relatedness to structural alignment when choosing source languages and designing models for low-resource languages. Generative models that explicitly incorporate structural information can achieve state-of-the-art or better performance in both in-domain and zero-shot cross-lingual scenarios, indicating a promising direction for multilingual scientific document processing.

Abstract: Sequential sentence classification (SSC) is an essential task for structuring scientific publications, and extending SSC research to languages other than English can improve accessibility to scientific knowledge in multilingual digital libraries. Cross-lingual transfer is a promising approach to address the scarcity of training data in non-English languages. Prior work on other natural language processing tasks has shown the benefits of capturing linguistic similarity between source and target languages. However, SSC inherently depends on patterns at the discourse level, such as label sequences and positional regularities, which appear consistently across languages regardless of linguistic differences. To examine the factors that determine transfer success in SSC, we constructed a multilingual SSC dataset covering 13 non-English languages collected from five academic databases. Our cross-lingual transfer experiments, using both encoder-based and generative models, show that linguistic proximity has no consistent predictive power for transfer performance, whereas structural similarity in rhetorical organization shows a weak but consistent positive correlation across models. After controlling for source-language performance, the similarity of label distributions is the most consistent predictor. Building on this finding, we propose a set of three methods that explicitly leverage structural information using generative models. In the in-domain evaluation, the best combination reaches parity with the strongest encoder baselines, and in transfer to languages unseen during training, it outperforms the strongest encoder baseline.

</details>


### [26] [A Phonemically Comprehensive, ASCII-Only Romanization Scheme for Thai and Lao: Systematic Cross-Lingual Correspondence and Chinese-User-Friendly Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19736)
*Zijie Zhang,Tan Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A unified ASCII-based phonemic romanization for Thai and Lao that is readable, keyboard-friendly, and precise enough for tone and segmental contrasts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing romanization systems for Thai and Lao are often inconsistent across the two languages, may not be strictly phonemic, and can be unfriendly for learners or computational use. There is a need for a single scheme that captures phonemic detail (including tone and vowel length), works well on standard keyboards, and aligns cross-linguistically, especially with widely used East Asian systems like Pinyin and Jyutping.

Method: Design a romanization scheme under explicit constraints: ASCII-only, one-symbol-one-phoneme mapping, explicit marking of vowel length and lexical tone, and systematic correspondences between Thai and Lao phonemes. Optimize primarily for synchronic phonetic correspondence (including with Pinyin/Jyutping where applicable), and secondarily for historical-phonological consistency where it does not harm phonetic transparency. Implement a compact core tone marking system using single digits, with optional alternative tone encodings.

Result: A concrete romanization system that encodes segmental phonemes, vowel length, and tones for both Thai and Lao using only ASCII symbols. The system maintains transparent, consistent correspondences between the two languages and provides multiple tone-notation layers: a default digit-based system plus optional tone-value and historical tone-category labels.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme yields a practical, easily typed, and machine-processable phonemic representation for Thai and Lao that is suitable for language learning and cross-lingual speech processing. It harmonizes Thai and Lao romanization under a single design while also aligning, where possible, with existing Sinitic romanization systems.

Abstract: This paper proposes a phonemically comprehensive, ASCII-only romanization scheme for Thai and Lao, treating the two closely related languages as a unified cross-lingual design problem. The scheme represents segmental contrasts, vowel length, and lexical tone while maintaining one-symbol-one-phoneme transparency and systematic correspondence between Thai and Lao. The scheme prioritizes synchronic phonetic correspondence, including correspondence with Pinyin and Jyutping where applicable, while preserving historical-phonological correspondence where it does not conflict with phonetic transparency. Tone uses a compact single-digit default notation, supplemented by optional tone-value and historical tone-category representations. The resulting scheme provides a readable, keyboard-friendly, and machine-processable phonemic representation for language learning and cross-lingual speech processing.

</details>


### [27] [Learn Before You Judge: Progressive Knowledge-to-Decision Alignment for Explainable Hateful Meme Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19778)
*Bo Xu,Chenyuan Wang,Xinyu Chen,Quanhao Zhu,Rui Lin,Liang Zhao,Hongfei Lin,Feng Xia*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes ProKDA, a progressive training framework that separates knowledge construction, detection learning, and decision boundary alignment to improve both accuracy and explainability in hateful meme detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal LLM approaches for hateful meme detection struggle with implicit, image–text interactions and often underperform when they try to simultaneously learn to detect hatefulness and generate explanations. This coupling of objectives leads to interference, harming detection performance and limiting the quality of explanations. There is a need for a method that can both accurately detect hateful memes and provide clear, evidence-based explanations while leveraging external knowledge about memes and social context.

Method: The authors introduce ProKDA, a progressive knowledge-to-decision alignment framework that mimics how human annotators are trained. First, they construct external background knowledge relevant to meme understanding using an agentic pipeline (retrieving and organizing contextual information about entities, symbols, and cultural references). Then they apply a three-stage training strategy: (1) background knowledge learning, where the model learns to incorporate and use the constructed knowledge; (2) hatefulness detection learning, where the model is trained specifically on classification of hateful vs. non-hateful memes; and (3) hatefulness boundary alignment, where the model refines its decision boundaries and explanations so that detection decisions are well aligned with the learned knowledge. Crucially, each stage focuses on a single objective rather than jointly optimizing explanation and detection, which reduces cross-task interference.

Result: On three public hateful meme benchmarks, ProKDA attains state-of-the-art detection performance, outperforming prior multimodal LLM-based and explain-then-detect approaches, including simple supervised fine-tuning baselines. The generated explanations are reported to be accurate, aligned with the evidence in the image–text content and external knowledge, and suitable for supporting moderation decisions. Empirical results confirm that progressively aligning knowledge to decisions is more effective than jointly training explanation and detection objectives.

Conclusion: Decoupling explanation generation from label prediction through a progressive, staged training procedure enables better hateful meme detection and more reliable explanations. ProKDA’s knowledge-to-decision alignment, supported by external background knowledge and a three-phase training pipeline, mitigates task interference and converts rich contextual knowledge into robust, explainable moderation decisions. This suggests a promising direction for designing explainable multimodal safety systems that mirror human annotators’ learning processes.

Abstract: Hateful memes spread abusive content through implicit interactions between images and text, posing serious threats to the safety of online communities. In recent years, multimodal large language models have been widely used for hateful meme detection and are increasingly adopted to generate explainable detection results. However, we find that existing explain-then-detect methods often couple explanation generation and label prediction within the same training process. This coupling causes interference between task objectives, leading to limited detection performance and even worse results than simple SFT baselines. To address these challenges, we propose ProKDA, a progressive knowledge-to-decision alignment method for explainable hateful meme detection. Inspired by the human annotation training process, ProKDA first uses an agentic background knowledge construction pipeline to obtain external knowledge related to meme understanding. It then adopts a three-stage training strategy that sequentially performs background knowledge learning, hatefulness detection learning, and hatefulness boundary alignment. Unlike prior explain-then-detect methods that jointly optimize both tasks, ProKDA focuses on a single training objective at each stage. This design reduces interference between the two tasks and progressively transforms background knowledge into robust detection decisions. Experiments on three public hateful meme benchmarks show that ProKDA achieves state-of-the-art detection performance and provides accurate, explainable, and evidence-supported decisions for hateful meme moderation. Project page: https://meizhiyuan88666.github.io/prokda.

</details>


### [28] [Evolution or Illusion? Rethinking Evaluation in LLM Evolutionary Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19799)
*Tal Oved,Roi Pony,Oshri Naparstek,Udi Barzelay*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how to best allocate a fixed computational budget between the number of independent evolutionary search seeds (width) and the number of iterations per seed (depth) when using LLM-driven evolutionary program search, showing that common single-setting evaluations are misleading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM-driven evolutionary search papers evaluate performance using a single configuration, typically one seed with a fixed number of iterations, and then compare methods at that single point. This practice ignores how performance may change when varying the trade-off between the number of seeds and iterations, and may produce misleading rankings of strategies. The paper seeks to systematically understand this trade-off and provide a more reliable evaluation protocol.

Method: The authors examine three different evolutionary search strategies on five standard optimization tasks used in LLM evolutionary search literature. They run a grid search over combinations of the number of seeds and iterations, effectively exploring the width–depth trade-off under a fixed total budget. They then analyze how performance and the ranking of strategies change with different budget allocations and propose a measurement protocol that summarizes the performance frontier over seeds and iterations.

Result: The study finds that the optimal division of a fixed budget between more seeds (width) and more iterations per seed (depth) depends on the specific strategy, the task, and the total budget. The ranking of evolutionary strategies can change significantly as the budget and its allocation vary. In some tasks, a method that appears worst with one seed becomes best when run with many seeds, and in others, commonly used iteration counts are larger than optimal, causing wasted budget that would be better spent on additional seeds.

Conclusion: Evaluating LLM-driven evolutionary search with a single seeds–iterations configuration is inadequate and can lead to incorrect conclusions about which strategy is best. Instead, researchers should report performance across a seeds-by-iterations grid and focus on the resulting performance frontier. The paper offers a measurement protocol and practical recommendations to guide more robust and informative evaluation of evolutionary search strategies under fixed computation budgets.

Abstract: LLM-driven evolutionary search finds programs by launching seeds and iterating each one. Papers report a single budget setting, usually one seed run for a fixed number of iterations, and rank methods from that one point. We show this is not enough. We evaluate three evolutionary search strategies on five optimization tasks, commonly used by papers in the genre to report results. We run the analysis over a full grid of seeds and iterations. Our findings suggest that the best way to split a fixed budget between more seeds (width) and more iterations (depth) changes with the strategy, the task, and the total budget. Furthermore, we observe that the ranking of strategies also changes with the budget. On one task the strategy that looks worst at one seed is best at forty seeds. On another the best number of iterations is well below the value common in practice, so extra depth wastes budget that more seeds would turn into score. We provide a measurement protocol that reports the seeds-by-iterations frontier and practical guidance for using it.

</details>


### [29] [Dictionary-Constrained Grapheme-to-Phoneme for Unsegmented Languages from LLM-Annotated Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19805)
*Rui Hu,Zhenpeng Zhan,Xiaolong Lin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a context-aware neural grapheme-to-phoneme (G2P) model for Japanese that integrates a CRF over a dictionary-based word lattice and leverages LLM-generated training text, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy on a key benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional Japanese G2P systems struggle with context-dependent pronunciation (polyphone disambiguation) and the coupling of G2P with word segmentation in an unsegmented language, especially under limited annotated data. There is a need for a fast, stable, context-aware G2P method that can better exploit available resources and overcome data scarcity.

Method: The authors construct a word lattice from dictionaries and use a discriminative conditional random field (CRF) to score paths through this lattice, with the CRF potentials provided by a context-aware neural model. To address annotation scarcity, they generate over 2 million additional training sentences using large language models (LLMs), then train the neural-CRF G2P system on both real and synthetic data. The model jointly resolves word segmentation and polyphone disambiguation within this structured prediction framework.

Result: The proposed method significantly outperforms traditional morphological analyzer-based pipelines and neural sequence-to-sequence G2P baselines. On the Joyo-Kanji-Yomi benchmark, it achieves 99.62% accuracy on target word readings, a 0.32% phoneme error rate (PER) at the target word level, and a 0.14% PER at the sentence level, setting a new state-of-the-art for Japanese G2P on this dataset.

Conclusion: A CRF-based, context-aware neural G2P system over a dictionary-derived word lattice, augmented with LLM-generated synthetic data, is highly effective for Japanese, where G2P is tightly coupled with segmentation and polyphone disambiguation. The approach yields state-of-the-art performance and indicates that combining structured prediction with large-scale synthetic data is a powerful strategy for G2P in low-resource or complex orthography settings.

Abstract: Grapheme-to-phoneme (G2P) conversion turns raw text into its phonemic form and is an essential part of both text-to-speech (TTS) and automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems. It is required to be fast, stable and context-aware. For unsegmented languages such as Japanese, G2P additionally couples word segmentation with highly context-dependent polyphone disambiguation, and the scarcity of accurately annotated data remains a bottleneck. In this paper, we present a context-aware neural G2P method that scores paths of a discriminative conditional random field (CRF) over a word lattice constructed from dictionaries. To tackle data scarcity, we utilize large language models (LLMs) to generate more than 2 million sentences. Experimental results demonstrate that our method strongly outperforms conventional morphological analyzer-based methods and neural sequence models. On the Joyo-Kanji-Yomi benchmark, our method reaches 99.62% target word reading accuracy, 0.32% target word phoneme error rate (PER) and 0.14% sentence PER.

</details>


### [30] [F$^{2}$DR: A Fine-Grained Full-Pipeline Reward Framework for DeepSearch Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19827)
*Bojian Xiong,Wentao Ding,Yujing Lu,Shaowei Zhang,Ling Shi,Jing Liao,Yan Wang,Yueyang Zhang,Long Xia,Zhiyuan Sun,Daiting Shi,Jingzhou He,Yuqi Ren,Deyi Xiong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes F2DR, a fine-grained reward framework and a new benchmark (DeepSearch RM-Bench) to evaluate DeepSearch-style LLM workflows across the full pipeline, not just single turns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reward models and benchmarks focus on static, single-turn tasks and cannot reliably evaluate the complex, multi-step DeepSearch workflows (planning, retrieval, reflection, and answering). This mismatch makes it hard to train and compare models optimized for real industrial DeepSearch use cases.

Method: Design a full-pipeline DeepSearch reward framework, F2DR, that evaluates three complementary dimensions: Content (quality and relevance of retrieved/used information), Trajectory (quality of the multi-step workflow and decisions along the way), and Answer (final response quality). Additionally, build DeepSearch RM-Bench, a benchmark tailored to DeepSearch scenarios, and use it to compare existing reward models and evaluate F2DR against self-evaluation-based baselines.

Result: Experiments show that F2DR’s process-level rewards align better with desired evaluation standards than self-evaluation-based approaches, giving higher consistency. DeepSearch RM-Bench can clearly discriminate performance gaps among existing open-source reward models in DeepSearch-style tasks.

Conclusion: Full-pipeline, fine-grained evaluation is necessary for DeepSearch workflows. F2DR provides a more consistent and comprehensive reward signal across content, trajectory, and answer dimensions, and DeepSearch RM-Bench offers a suitable benchmark for assessing and comparing reward models in these scenarios. The dataset will be released publicly to support further research.

Abstract: With the widespread industrial deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs), DeepSearch has emerged as the dominant paradigm for resolving complex user queries. It typically operates through an iterative closed-loop workflow consisting of planning and reflection, information retrieval, and answer generation. However, existing reward models (RMs) and evaluation benchmarks are primarily designed for static single-turn tasks, failing to capture the full-pipeline complexity of DeepSearch workflows. To address this limitation, we propose F2DR, a fine-grained full-pipeline DeepSearch reward framework. F2DR evaluates DeepSearch workflows across three dimensions: Content, Trajectory, and Answer, enabling comprehensive process-level assessment. We further construct DeepSearch RM-Bench, a dedicated benchmark for evaluating RMs in DeepSearch scenarios. Extensive experiments demonstrate that F2DR achieves significantly higher evaluation consistency than self-evaluation-based baselines, while DeepSearch RM-Bench exhibits strong discriminative capability across existing open-source RMs. We will publicly release the complete DeepSearch RM-Bench dataset soon.

</details>


### [31] [Zarya: A Hybrid Autoregressive--Masked Diffusion Language Model with Flexible Training and Dual-Mode Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19868)
*Leonid Sinev,Ilya Koziev,Vladislav Leshchuk*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Zarya is a hybrid language model that combines autoregressive (AR) and masked diffusion modeling (MDM) in one architecture, enabling both standard left-to-right decoding and faster, parallel, cache-efficient speculative decoding, while maintaining strong benchmark performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive language models generate tokens sequentially, which limits decoding speed but makes use of efficient KV caching and yields coherent text. Masked diffusion models allow parallel generation but are computationally expensive (no KV cache reuse) and can be incoherent due to modeling dependencies over a combinatorially large token space. The paper is motivated by the desire to get the best of both worlds: the speed and parallelism of diffusion-style models with the efficiency and coherence of autoregressive models, within a single, flexible framework that supports multiple decoding strategies at inference time.

Method: The authors propose Zarya, a family of hybrid models trained with a joint objective: a standard autoregressive loss and a masked-diffusion loss, both implemented in a single transformer-style architecture. Training data is segmented into variable-size "slots"; a curriculum gradually increases slot size so the model transitions from fine-grained AR token prediction to coarse-grained diffusion over slots. Various masking/noise schemes (Prefix Completion, Fill-In-the-Prefix, Fill-In-the-Middle), ordered sampling schedules, and permutations of noise levels are used to flexibly structure the diffusion objective. At inference, two main decoding modes are defined via a unified interface: (1) a masked diffusion sampling process with a "first-hitting" denoising criterion, and (2) a slotted speculative decoding procedure that alternates between diffusion-based inter-slot selection and autoregressive intra-slot infilling, designed to fully reuse the KV cache and thus be more computationally efficient.

Result: Zarya models are trained and released at 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B parameters. They achieve performance on standard language modeling benchmarks that is competitive with baseline models while additionally supporting both AR-style and diffusion-style decoding. The experiments demonstrate that the hybrid training does not significantly degrade AR performance while enabling efficient speculative decoding and coherent diffusion-based generation. The configurability of slot structure, noise patterns, and scheduling is validated as a useful research tool.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that Zarya provides a principled and practical integration of autoregressive and diffusion language modeling in one architecture. By structuring data into slots and using a joint AR+diffusion objective with a curriculum over slot granularity, Zarya supports flexible inference modes—parallel diffusion sampling and cache-efficient slotted speculative decoding—without coupling training and inference configurations. This design yields models that maintain strong benchmark performance while broadening the space of controllable, efficient decoding strategies, and the public release of several model sizes is intended to facilitate further research on hybrid generative paradigms.

Abstract: Autoregressive language models (ARMs) are constrained by sequential, left-to-right generation, while masked diffusion models (MDMs) enable parallel decoding but suffer from high computational overhead due to the inability to reuse Key-Value (KV) cache and from incoherent generation arising from learning dependencies over an intractable space of token combinations. We introduce Zarya, a family of hybrid language models that jointly optimizes an autoregressive (AR) objective and a masked-diffusion objective within a single architecture. Zarya structures training data into variable-size slots and employs a curriculum that gradually increases slot granularity, enabling a smooth transition from fine-grained AR learning to coarse-grained diffusion learning. At inference, Zarya provides two distinct decoding paradigms through a unified interface: (i) MDM sampling with first-hitting denoising, and (ii) slotted speculative decoding that interleaves inter-slot diffusion-based selection with intra-slot autoregressive infilling, achieving full KV cache reuse. The training and inference regimes are fully decoupled, allowing a model trained with any configuration to be deployed in either mode. Extensive configurability --- including grouped noise patterns (Prefix Completion, Fill-In-the-Prefix, Fill-In-the-Middle), ordered sampling schedules, and noise-level permutation strategies --- enables flexible research exploration. We release Zarya models publicly in sizes 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B, demonstrating performance on standard benchmarks while offering a principled integration of autoregressive and diffusion paradigms.

</details>


### [32] [JustMem: Just-Enough Memory Access for Long-Term Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19877)
*Guanhua Chen,Yanting Wang,Wenjing Zhi,Lei Sha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes JustMem, a memory system for long-term conversations that adaptively retrieves and reads past dialogue, achieving better accuracy and recall with fewer tokens than prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term conversational systems need to answer questions using information scattered across many past interactions. Naively feeding large amounts of context to the language model is expensive and often unnecessary, but aggressively compressing or filtering history can remove critical details. Existing memory mechanisms struggle to balance retrieval breadth and detail, especially when relevant evidence is distributed across sessions or when fine-grained wording matters. The authors aim to design a memory system that can flexibly decide how widely to search and how precisely to read past conversations per query, improving both efficiency and answer quality.

Method: The authors conceptualize memory access along two axes: (1) discovery breadth—how widely the system searches for potentially relevant evidence in the stored conversation history; and (2) reading fidelity—whether it uses compressed summaries or goes back to the original, full conversation segments. They design JustMem, which stores interaction history as compact atomic memory units and then chooses an access strategy conditioned on each incoming query. JustMem uses three operations: LOOKUP for retrieving local evidence when it is likely to be contained in a small, well-identified region; COMPOSE for broadening discovery when relevant information is distributed across multiple atomic memories; and REPLAY for reconstructing high-fidelity context from original conversations when fine details are needed. The system dynamically selects and combines these operations to serve each query while minimizing token usage.

Result: On the LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S benchmarks for long-term conversational memory, JustMem outperforms other memory systems in terms of mean task accuracy and retrieval recall. At the same time, it uses substantially fewer generative-model tokens for both constructing the memory representations and performing inference, demonstrating improved computational efficiency alongside better retrieval quality.

Conclusion: Treating memory access as a two-dimensional problem—balancing discovery breadth and reading fidelity—enables more adaptive and efficient use of long-term conversational history. By storing history as compact atomic memories and employing specialized operations (LOOKUP, COMPOSE, REPLAY) tailored to different query needs, JustMem improves both accuracy and retrieval recall while reducing token consumption. This suggests that carefully designed, query-adaptive memory access mechanisms can significantly enhance the practicality of long-term conversational agents.

Abstract: Efficient long-term conversational memory requires retrieving sufficient evidence without indiscriminately expanding the context presented to the language model. This is challenging because relevant evidence may be distributed across multiple sessions, while compression may discard details needed for answering. Different queries therefore require different forms of memory access. To capture these demands, we formulate memory access along two dimensions: discovery breadth, which controls how broadly evidence is searched, and reading fidelity, which controls whether evidence is read in compact form or recovered from the original conversation. Based on this formulation, we introduce JustMem, which stores conversation history as compact atomic memories and adapts memory access along these two dimensions to each query. Specifically, LOOKUP handles local evidence, COMPOSE broadens discovery for distributed evidence, and REPLAY increases reading fidelity for fidelity-sensitive evidence. On LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S, JustMem achieves the highest mean accuracy and retrieval recall among the compared memory systems while using substantially fewer generative-model tokens for memory construction and inference.

</details>


### [33] [VākQA: A Benchmark and Evaluation Study for Telugu Spoken Factoid Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19879)
*Bhavana Akkiraju,Ravi Sastry Kolluru,Sri Charan D,Srihari Bandarupalli,Santosh Kesiraju,Anil Vuppala*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents VākQA, the first Telugu spoken question answering (SQA) benchmark, and systematically studies automatic evaluation methods and model performance across modalities and languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most advances in question answering with large language models focus on high-resource languages, leaving low-resource languages like Telugu underexplored, especially for spoken QA. Additionally, it is unclear how reliable automatic LLM-based evaluators are for grading SQA answers in such languages. The authors aim to fill this data and evaluation gap for Telugu SQA and to understand how models behave across speech/text and Telugu/English settings.

Method: The authors construct VākQA, a Telugu SQA dataset with 2,001 factoid QA pairs over six domains, 2.53 hours of speech, bilingual transcriptions, and human-validated reference answers. They then compare several automatic evaluation setups against human judgments, including a proprietary LLM evaluator (Gemini-as-a-judge) and open-weight LLM judges, to quantify their alignment and biases. With the best validated evaluation configuration, they benchmark multiple proprietary and open-weight QA models, varying input modality (speech vs text), language (Telugu vs translation), and domain, analyzing the impact of linguistic and acoustic factors and error cascades from ASR and MT.

Result: They find that Gemini-as-a-judge aligns most closely with human ratings but displays non-uniform strictness depending on cases, while open-weight LLM judges often undervalue correct Telugu answers that are semantically right but differ in surface wording from the reference. Benchmarking reveals that culturally specific nuances carried by Telugu phrasing are lost in translation to other languages, that speech input leads to phonetic confusions which can change the meaning of questions, and that cascaded pipelines (ASR followed by MT) accumulate errors that substantially degrade SQA performance.

Conclusion: VākQA provides a new public benchmark for Telugu spoken QA and shows that current automatic evaluators and QA models have important limitations for low-resource spoken settings. Reliable evaluation requires careful choice of LLM-as-a-judge, and direct Telugu modeling is important to preserve cultural and linguistic specificity. The work encourages future research on robust SQA for low-resource languages, better evaluators that handle surface-form variation, and improved handling of errors in ASR-MT cascades.

Abstract: Question answering has advanced rapidly with large language models, but predominantly for high-resource languages, in both text and spoken settings. Spoken question answering (SQA) benchmark for Telugu remains unexplored, and the reliability of automatic evaluation in this setting remains unquantified. We introduce VākQA, a Telugu SQA benchmark of 2,001 factoid question-answer pairs across six domains, with 2.53 hours of speech audio, bilingual transcriptions, and human-verified reference answers. We first validate evaluation methods against human judgements: Gemini-as-a-judge best approximates human ratings but is non-uniformly strict, while open-weight judges systematically penalize correct Telugu answers that differ in surface form from the reference. Using this validated setup, we benchmark proprietary and open-weight models across input modality, language, and domain. We observe that Telugu phrasing retains cultural specificity that is lost in translation, speech input introduces phonetic confusions that alter question meaning, and cascaded ASR-MT errors compound progressively. VākQA is publicly released.

</details>


### [34] [D-Quant: Driftable Entropy Coding for KV Cache Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19880)
*Yi Su,Hong Liu,Guanghua Yu,Jianchen Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper addresses KV cache memory bottlenecks in LLM inference by proposing D-Quant, a new quantization framework that combines entropy coding with a drift mechanism to keep fixed-size per-token bitstreams suitable for parallel attention kernels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KV cache memory grows linearly with sequence length and batch size, creating capacity and bandwidth bottlenecks when deploying LLMs. Existing KV quantization approaches mostly use fixed-width quantization, which performs poorly at low bit widths and fails to exploit the non-uniform, roughly Gaussian distribution of KV values. Entropy coding could exploit this distribution but its variable-length codes are incompatible with highly parallel GPU attention kernels that rely on regular memory layouts and fixed-stride access. The paper is motivated by the need to reconcile entropy-based compression efficiency with the structural requirements of fast attention computation.

Method: The method introduces D-Quant, a KV cache quantization framework that uses entropy coding tailored to the observed near-normal distribution of rotated and normalized KV values. To make entropy coding compatible with parallel attention, D-Quant adds a drift mechanism that transforms the inherently variable-length entropy-coded results for each token into fixed-size bitstreams. This retains most of the compression benefits of entropy coding while preserving regular, fixed-stride memory layouts, enabling efficient parallel dequantization and attention computation on hardware accelerators.

Result: While specific numerical results are not in the abstract, the claimed result is that D-Quant achieves substantially better compression of the KV cache than fixed-width quantization at comparable or lower quality loss, and that it remains deployable within standard highly parallel attention kernels thanks to its fixed-size per-token representation.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that traditional fixed-width KV quantization is fundamentally limited, especially at low bit widths and for non-uniform KV distributions. By introducing D-Quant and its drift mechanism, the authors show that it is possible to combine entropy coding’s ability to exploit non-uniform value distributions with the regular memory access patterns required by modern attention kernels. This provides a more scalable and efficient path to mitigating KV cache bottlenecks in LLM deployment.

Abstract: The KV cache has become a major bottleneck in deploying LLMs, as its memory footprint grows linearly with sequence length and batch size, imposing substantial pressure on both memory capacity and bandwidth. Among various KV cache compression techniques, quantization is particularly attractive due to its effectiveness and ease of deployment. However, most existing methods rely on fixed-width quantization, where a $b$ bit representation is inherently limited to $2^b$ quantization levels. As the bit width decreases, the number of available levels shrinks exponentially, leading to severe information loss and rapid performance degradation. We further observe that fixed-width quantization fails to exploit the highly non-uniform distribution of KV cache. After rotation and normalization, KV values approximately follow a normal distribution, with most values concentrated near the center and only a small fraction appearing in the tails. Nevertheless, fixed-width coding allocates the same number of bits to frequent and rare symbols. Entropy coding naturally exploits such non-uniformity by assigning shorter codewords to frequent symbols and longer ones to rare symbols, substantially reducing the average number of bits required for representation. However, its variable-length output is not suited to highly parallel attention kernels, where efficient dequantization and computation rely on regular memory layouts and fixed-stride accesses. To bridge this gap, we propose \textbf{D-Quant}, a flexible KV cache quantization framework that introduces a \textbf{drift} mechanism to convert entropy-coded representations of each token into fixed-size bitstreams, enabling regular memory access and parallel dequantization within attention kernels.

</details>


### [35] [PetriBench: Benchmarking LLM Reasoning over Dynamic State Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19883)
*Pyrros Koussios,Benjamin Jäger,John Hua Yao,Ajay Sridhar,Violet Xiang,Chenhao Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PetriBench is a compact, self-contained benchmark using Petri nets to systematically test how LLMs reason over dynamic state spaces, with difficulty-scaled tasks and exact ground truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reasoning benchmarks for LLMs often measure narrow skills, depend on external world knowledge, or are hard to scale and extend. There is a need for a principled, fully synthetic, and scalable way to evaluate how LLMs reason about dynamic systems and state transitions, separate from factual knowledge. Petri nets are a well-established formalism for modeling concurrent and distributed systems, offering a rich yet precise structure for such evaluation.

Method: The authors design PetriBench, a benchmark built from Petri net models that define dynamic state spaces. They construct four task families that probe different reasoning scopes and temporal horizons (e.g., local vs long-range reasoning about system evolution). For each family, they procedurally generate instances at Easy, Medium, and Hard levels by increasing Petri net structural complexity. All tasks are fully self-contained, with exact ground truth answers derived from the formal Petri net semantics. They evaluate a range of proprietary and open-weight LLMs, also varying test-time compute (e.g., more decoding steps or reasoning strategies) to study its effect on performance.

Result: Model accuracy systematically drops as problem difficulty increases, confirming that the benchmark meaningfully controls reasoning challenge. Harder instances reveal clearer, task-specific capability differences between models, exposing distinct strengths and weaknesses across the four task families. Increased test-time compute generally improves performance, but the size and pattern of gains differ across reasoning tasks. Procedural generation supports smooth scaling: as structural complexity grows, model performance changes in a gradual, predictable way rather than erratically.

Conclusion: PetriBench offers a unified, extensible, and fully synthetic environment for evaluating LLM reasoning about dynamic state spaces using Petri nets. Its structured difficulty levels, exact ground truth, and task diversity allow fine-grained analysis of reasoning capabilities, limitations, and scaling behavior. This positions PetriBench as a robust tool for probing and comparing LLM reasoning, independent of external knowledge, and for studying how architectures and inference-time strategies affect performance on dynamic-system reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Characterizing LLM reasoning remains an open challenge, as many existing benchmarks isolate specific reasoning skills, rely on external knowledge, or are costly to extend. We introduce PetriBench, a compact, fully self-contained, and scalable benchmark for evaluating LLM reasoning over dynamic state spaces using Petri nets, a mature formalism for modeling real-world concurrent and distributed systems. PetriBench organizes reasoning into four task families varying by scope and temporal horizon, with Easy, Medium, and Hard levels generated by increasing structural complexity and evaluated against exact ground truth. Across a diverse set of proprietary and open-weight models, accuracy decreases consistently with difficulty, while harder instances expose increasingly distinct task-specific capability profiles. Additional analyses show that test-time compute improves performance but interacts differently with different reasoning tasks, and that procedural generation yields smooth scaling with structural complexity. Together, these results show that PetriBench provides a unified and extensible setting for probing the strengths, limits, and scaling behavior of LLM reasoning.

</details>


### [36] [Evaluating Communicative Success in Machine-Translated Conversation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19885)
*Faiz Ghifari Haznitrama,Alice Oh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new multi-layer evaluation framework for interpreter-style machine translation that measures communicative success (semantic, pragmatic, cultural-social), not just sentence-level fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MT evaluation focuses on isolated sentences and fidelity metrics (e.g., BLEU, COMET), which do not capture whether interpreter-mediated live conversations actually succeed in communication, including naturalness, intent alignment, and social appropriateness. There is a gap in evaluating how well interpreter agents function in real conversational and cross-cultural settings.

Method: The authors design a three-layer checklist-and-judge framework assessing semantic, pragmatic, and cultural-social aspects of interpreter-mediated conversations. The framework is reusable, works in both single-turn and multi-turn interactive settings, and uses simulated users who respond to translated messages as the conversation progresses. Each turn and the overall conversation are scored. They validate the framework via controlled perturbation experiments, cross-judge consistency checks, and comparison with human annotations. They build a benchmark using 5,624 scenarios derived from OpenSubtitles across Arabic, Bengali, Indonesian, and Korean, covering 12 translation directions and 10 different interpreter configurations, plus multi-turn scripted and live evaluations for all 6 language pairs.

Result: Empirical results show that interpreter performance consistently degrades from semantic to pragmatic to cultural-social dimensions: systems that seem good semantically often fail pragmatically or socially. Conventional MT metrics fail to detect many communication failures, especially for stronger interpreters. Prompt ablations indicate that including richer scenario context, structured instructions, and cultural information generally enhances communicative success, although the magnitude of improvement varies by system setup.

Conclusion: The work delivers an evaluation framework and benchmark specifically tailored for interpreter agents in conversational settings, demonstrating that traditional MT metrics are insufficient for measuring real communicative success. It underscores the need to assess semantic, pragmatic, and cultural-social dimensions jointly and shows that contextual and cultural prompts can improve interpreter-mediated communication.

Abstract: Interpreter agents built on machine translation (MT) increasingly mediate live conversation between people who do not share a language, yet we still evaluate them with metrics built for isolated sentences, which measure fidelity rather than whether communication succeeds. We introduce a reusable three-layer checklist-and-judge framework that evaluates interpreter-mediated conversation across semantic, pragmatic, and cultural-social dimensions, covering the naturalness, intent, and social appropriateness that fidelity metrics leave unmeasured. It runs in both single-turn and interactive multi-turn settings, where simulated users reply to translated messages as the conversation unfolds and each turn is scored alongside the conversation as a whole. We extensively validate it through controlled perturbations, cross-judge comparisons, and human annotations. Our main single-turn benchmark evaluates 10 interpreter setups across Arabic, Bengali, Indonesian, and Korean from 5,624 OpenSubtitles-derived scenarios spanning 12 translation directions, and our multi-turn study covers all 6 language pairs in scripted and live modes. Results show a consistent decline from semantic to pragmatic and cultural-social success, while conventional MT metrics overlook failures among stronger interpreters, and prompt ablations show that scenario context, structured instructions, and cultural context improve communicative success, although gains vary across setups. Our work thus provides an evaluation framework and benchmark for interpreter agents in conversation, and highlights the importance of communicative success alongside existing translation metrics.

</details>


### [37] [Generalization through Lexical Abstraction in Transformer Models: The Case of Functional Words](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19887)
*Giuseppe Samo,Vivi Nastase,Paola Merlo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how pretrained transformer models represent functional words (pronouns, adverbs, etc.) and whether they can capture the shared structure between sentences with concrete nouns and their more abstract, pronominal variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In human language, functional words like pronouns and adverbs act as abstract placeholders that can substitute for concrete nouns or phrases while preserving crucial grammatical and semantic information (e.g., number, gender, coreference). It is unclear whether pretrained transformer language models encode these functional words in a way that supports similar abstraction and recognition of parallel sentence structures. Understanding this sheds light on how models represent syntax, semantics, and lexical abstraction, beyond simple word co-occurrence.

Method: The authors embed words and sentences using a pretrained transformer model. They compare the embeddings of concrete nouns with those of the functional words (pronouns, adverbs) that can replace them, both when words are considered in isolation and when they appear in parallel sentences (a fully lexicalized version vs. a functional version with pronouns/adverbs). They then analyze the geometry of these embeddings: centrality and distinctness of functional words, subspace structure of lexical vs. functional sentences, and probing experiments. In the probing experiments, they train models to distill or extract structural information from sentence embeddings using three data regimes: purely lexicalized sentences, purely functional sentences, and a mixture of both, and examine whether a shared syntactic/semantic structure is learned.

Result: Functional words occupy a central but distinct region of the embedding space relative to nouns, consistent with their role as general placeholders used across many contexts. Parallel lexicalized and functional sentences inhabit different subspaces in the embedding space, indicating that the model does not trivially align these two forms. Probing experiments show that training on only functional sentences (with highly repetitive vocabulary) or only lexicalized sentences (with high lexical variety) fails to reveal the shared structural patterns between them. However, when the probing model is trained on a mix of lexicalized and functional versions, the shared underlying structure becomes apparent.

Conclusion: Pretrained transformers encode functional words as central yet distinct entities that reflect their role as broad placeholders rather than as direct stand-ins for specific nouns. The model’s sentence representations of lexicalized and functional variants live in different subspaces, and the shared syntactic/semantic structure between them is not automatically exposed. Nevertheless, this shared structure can be recovered when a probing model is trained on a mixture of lexicalized and functional sentences, suggesting that explicit exposure to both forms is needed to align their representations and reveal the common underlying sentence structure.

Abstract: Pronouns, adverbs and other functional words (such as they, her, somewhere, there) are often used in language to replace concrete nouns or phrases, when their properties - such as gender, grammatical number - provide sufficient information for the given context. Do pretrained transformer models encode such functional words in a manner that allows them to be used like humans do? Can language models recognize the syntactic and semantic parallelism of sentences such as "The researchers wrote the paper" and "They wrote it", which relies on such lexical abstraction?
  We map these linguistic questions into the embedding space of a pretrained transformer model, and compare representations of nouns, with the representations of the pronouns and adverbs that can replace these nouns, in isolation and in parallel lexicalized and functional sentences. We then probe for shared syntactic and semantic structure in the embeddings of parallel lexicalized and functional sentences.
  We find that functional words are located centrally compared to nouns, but are also distinct, which is congruent with their behaviour as place-holders in a wide variety of contexts.
  The analysis of the embeddings of parallel (lexicalized and functional) sentences show them inhabiting different subspaces of the embedding space. Experiments that distil the structural information of the sentence show that training on either type of data does not reveal the shared structure - because of the over-consistency of the vocabulary (in case of the functional data), and the too much variety (in case of the lexicalized versions). However, training with a mix of functional and lexicalized sentences, the shared structure emerges.

</details>


### [38] [KoNeoBench: A Curated Evaluation Dataset for LLM Understanding of Korean Neologisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19916)
*Soha Lee,Soojin Lee,Heesung Yang,Hyunju Song,Hyunji Lee,Jinsan An,Jeongwan Shin,Jin Hyun Park,Jun Lee,Hyeyoung Park,Kilim Nam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents KoNeoBench, a new benchmark to evaluate how well large language models understand modern Korean neologisms formed since 2020.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM benchmarks are mostly static and English-centric, while language—especially vocabulary—changes rapidly. Korean has rich morphology where content words combine productively with functional morphemes, and existing Korean benchmarks focus on established words, not recent neologisms. As a result, they cannot adequately test whether LLMs can handle emerging Korean words, their formation, and meanings.

Method: The authors construct KoNeoBench from 1,785 Korean neologisms found in online news since 2020. Lexicographic experts curate these items, providing usage examples, analyses of how each word is formed (word-formation analyses), and dictionary-style definitions. Using this resource, they design four evaluation tasks targeting different dimensions of neologism understanding (e.g., identifying source components, semantic categories, and generating definitions). They then evaluate several recent LLMs on these tasks and compare model performance with a human baseline.

Result: Across the KoNeoBench tasks, current LLMs perform noticeably worse than humans, especially in recovering the source components of neologisms, correctly classifying their semantic categories, and producing accurate, dictionary-like definitions. These deficits are quantifiable across multiple recent models.

Conclusion: KoNeoBench exposes concrete weaknesses of current LLMs in dealing with recent Korean lexical innovations. The benchmark highlights specific challenges related to Korean neologism formation and meaning that are not captured by existing static, English-centric benchmarks, and it provides a publicly available resource for future model development and evaluation in this area.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are typically evaluated on static benchmarks, even though natural language constantly evolves through newly emerging words and meanings. Existing Korean benchmarks are centered on established vocabulary and therefore provide limited coverage of such recent lexical change, and their English-oriented design makes it difficult to assess the typological properties of Korean, in which content words combine productively with functional morphemes. In this paper, we introduce KoNeoBench, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs' understanding of Korean neologisms. KoNeoBench is built on 1,785 Korean neologisms attested in online news since 2020 and curated through expert lexicographic review. Each entry provides usage examples, word-formation analyses, and dictionary-style definitions. Based on this resource, we define four tasks and report results on recent models, together with a human baseline. Our experiments show that current LLMs exhibit clear limitations in recovering source components, distinguishing semantic categories, and generating accurate definitions.
