These results reveal specific aspects of recent Korean lexical change that remain challenging for current LLMs. KoNeoBench is available at https://github.com/bcmilab/ko-neobench/ .

</details>


### [39] [Intrinsic Sequence-Likelihood Confidence in Retrieval-Dominated Extractive QA: Two Pre-Specified Negatives, and What They Do and Do Not Attribute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19942)
*Gunwoo Lee,Changmin Sung,Sang-Hwan Gwak,Ina Kim,Ji-Young Choi,Kyong-Ha Lee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that in extractive document QA with strong retrieval, using a model’s own confidence as a control signal brings little to no benefit; retrieval already recovers nearly all achievable accuracy and confidence-based routing/abstention or adaptation triggers fail under pre-registered tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers hoped that a language model’s internal confidence (e.g., sequence likelihood) could guide when to trust its answers and when to adapt or add extra processing, especially in specialized domains. This could enable efficient closed-book QA improvements and selective routing/abstention, making systems more reliable and resource-efficient. However, it was unclear whether this confidence signal is actually strong and stable enough, once retrieval is already very good.

Method: They study extractive document QA where questions are generated from answer-containing passages, so retrieval is strong and recovers 92–99.8% of the maximum possible accuracy. They fine-tune several 7–9B open LLM families on specialized domain corpora, then use the model’s own confidence (sequence likelihood) in two ways: (1) as a trigger for further adaptation (distillation) within a pre-registered three-step transfer budget across all four model families, and (2) as a routing-and-abstention policy in a single-model pilot setting. They pre-register correctness criteria and evaluation protocols, measure closed-book F1 gains, and evaluate confidence as a classifier via ROC AUC, also testing scalar calibration and token-level temperature rescaling. Selector ablations test whether adding confidence improves downstream performance.

Result: Fine-tuning yields negligible closed-book F1 gains (≤ +0.03). Retrieval alone recovers 92–99.8% of the best-case accuracy any mode combination can achieve, leaving almost no headroom for confidence-based routing. Both main uses of confidence fail under the pre-registered criteria: the adaptation/distillation trigger fails on all four model families under the three-step transfer budget, and the routing-and-abstention policy fails in its single-model pilot. The sequence-likelihood confidence signal has only moderate discriminative power (AUC 0.65–0.81) and does not improve after adaptation; simple scalar recalibration and token-level temperature rescaling do not make it consistently better. More detailed diagnostics vary with correctness definitions and answer length. On the limited adapted combinations where ablations are feasible, removing the confidence term yields no statistically significant downstream harm; for Gemma, removing it actually makes the selector pass both registered criteria instead of fail.

Conclusion: In extractive QA setups where retrieval is already very strong, a model’s own sequence-likelihood confidence is not a reliable or useful control signal for either triggering further adaptation or for routing/abstention. The marginal accuracy left beyond good retrieval is too small for routers to exploit, and the confidence signal itself is too weak and unstable, resistant to basic calibration. Designers should not expect large practical gains from confidence-based control in such settings; the pragmatic output of this work is a carefully curated set of negative results, with dependencies and conditions explicitly documented, clarifying limits of confidence-driven mechanisms in extractive QA with high-quality retrieval.

Abstract: In extractive document question answering whose questions were generated from the passages that contain their answers -- so that retrieval recovers 92-99.8% of what any mode combination could reach, whatever its absolute accuracy -- confidence-driven mechanisms have little to gain. Fine-tuning an open language model on a specialized domain corpus yields a model whose own confidence is a tempting control signal: it could decide which queries warrant further adaptation, and which answers to trust. We evaluate both uses under criteria fixed before the runs were executed, across four 7-9B model families whose adaptation moved closed-book F1 by at most +0.03, and both fail: a distillation trigger on all four families, under its pre-specified three-step transfer budget, and a routing-and-abstention policy in its single-model pilot. Retrieval alone recovers 92-99.8% of best-case combined accuracy under every correctness criterion we test, leaving routers no meaningful gain. The sequence-likelihood signal is insufficient relative to that mode -- area under the receiver operating characteristic curve 0.65-0.81 under the registered criterion -- before adaptation as well as after, unchanged by scalar recalibration and not consistently improved by token-level temperature rescaling. And the finer diagnostics depend on the correctness criterion and on answer length; on the three adapted combinations where we could test it, selector ablations show no statistically detectable downstream benefit from the confidence term on any seed; on Gemma, removing it changes the selector from failing to passing both registered criteria. The usable product is a set of pre-specified negatives with their dependencies made explicit.

</details>


### [40] [Before the Arrest: Benchmarking LLMs on Criminal Profiling from Incomplete Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19965)
*Yutong Yao,Yanjie Cao,Guanhua Chen,Xu Yang,Junchao Wu,Zeyu Wu,Lidia S. Chao,Derek F. Wong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new benchmark dataset (PIJ) of 2,500 real homicide cases to test how well LLMs can handle pre-arrest investigative tasks, showing that models perform much worse on inferential profiling and reasoning from incomplete evidence than on factual extraction, and exhibit systematic biases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM-for-legal research focuses on post-arrest or courtroom tasks where a suspect is already identified and facts are mostly explicit. However, real criminal investigations crucially involve pre-arrest inference: reasoning from partial crime-scene evidence to unknown suspect characteristics, reconstructing events, and anticipating legal outcomes. This stage is both high-stakes and under-studied for LLMs. The authors aim to fill this gap by creating a benchmark that reflects the full investigative pipeline and highlights where current models fail, particularly in abductive and deductive reasoning under uncertainty and in fairness and bias.

Method: The authors construct the Profiling, Investigation, and Judgment (PIJ) benchmark from 2,500 real homicide cases spanning five countries. They define three tasks representing core stages of an investigation: (1) criminal profiling—given fragmentary scene evidence, infer latent suspect attributes (e.g., gender, age, relationship to victim, motive); (2) crime process reconstruction—perform structured extraction of key elements of the crime process; (3) sentence prediction—predict sentencing outcomes, requiring application of legal reasoning to the case. They then evaluate nine state-of-the-art LLMs on these tasks, compare performance across task types and attribute categories, and analyze gaps between LLMs and human experts as well as systematic biases in the models’ predictions.

Result: Performance of all nine LLMs declines as tasks move from explicit fact extraction (crime process reconstruction) to implicit reasoning about unknown suspects (criminal profiling) and legal deduction (sentence prediction). Attributes that require stronger inferential or abductive reasoning—particularly motive and victim–offender relationship—show the worst accuracy and become the main bottlenecks. The models’ outputs diverge significantly from human expert judgments, and the analysis uncovers systematic biases in how LLMs assign gender, age, and motives to suspects, indicating reliability and fairness issues in pre-arrest applications.

Conclusion: Current LLMs are not reliable for pre-arrest investigative use where crucial information is missing and reasoning must proceed from incomplete evidence. They struggle especially with inferential profiling and motive/relationship attribution, lagging behind human experts and displaying notable demographic and motivational biases. The PIJ benchmark exposes these weaknesses and establishes pre-arrest inference as an open research challenge, underscoring that LLMs should not be deployed uncritically in real investigative workflows and that future work must focus on robust, unbiased abductive and legal reasoning under uncertainty.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to legal and criminal justice tasks, yet existing work focuses almost exclusively on post-arrest scenarios where the suspect's identity is already known, leaving the critical pre-arrest challenge of inferring suspect characteristics from incomplete evidence largely unexplored. To fill this gap, we introduce the Profiling, Investigation, and Judgment (PIJ), comprising 2,500 real homicide cases from five countries. PIJ evaluates LLMs across three tasks that span the entire criminal investigation pipeline: criminal profiling, which requires abductive reasoning to infer suspect attributes from fragmentary scene evidence, crime process reconstruction, which tests structured information extraction, and sentence prediction, which demands legal deductive reasoning. We evaluate 9 powerful LLMs and find that performance degrades systematically as tasks shift from explicit fact extraction to implicit reasoning over unknown suspect profiles. Categories requiring inferential reasoning, such as motivation and victim-offender relationships, remain the primary bottlenecks. Further analysis reveals substantial gaps between LLMs and human experts, along with pervasive biases in gender, age, and motive attribution. Our findings indicate that pre-arrest inference from incomplete evidence remains an open challenge.

</details>

L. Xiao,Wei An,Wei Li,Wei Wang,Weiwei Lin,Wenqing Hou,X. Lin,Xiangfei Meng,Xianzhu Huang,Xiao Peng,Xiaoqian Li,Xiaoting Zhang,Xiaowen Sun,Xiaoxiang Wang,Xiaoyu Ye,Xinrou Zhang,Xinyu Zhang,Xue Cao,Xueyin Chen,Yanan Zhou,Yanhong Xu,Yao Xia,Yao Xu,Yi Shao,Yihong Zhang,Yiling Ma,Ying Tang,Yining Lou,Yiru Chen,Yishi Piao,Yixuan Chen,Yong Xiong,Yuchen Xuan,Yuehan Yang,Yuer Xu,Yukun Zha,Yunxian Ma,Yuping Lin,Yuting Yan,Yutong Xie,Yuwen Sheng,Yuxuan Zhu,Zekai Zhang,Zhe Ju,Zhenzhen Lin,Zheren Gao,Zheyang Sun,Zhigang Yan,Zhongyu Wu,Zi Wang,Zihua Qu,Ziling Yan,Ziyi Wan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash is a 552B-parameter multimodal MoE model designed to drastically cut compute, memory, and bandwidth costs for million-token, agent-style workloads while improving quality over its predecessor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon, agentic LLM workloads are dominated by input-heavy phases (prefill) and huge KV caches, which stress GPU memory (HBM), SSD/host memory, and interconnect bandwidth. While long-context attention itself has become more efficient, prefill compute and KV cache storage/transfer now form the main deployment bottlenecks, blocking further cost reductions in real-world applications such as long-running agents and multimodal tasks.

Method: The authors design DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, a 552B-parameter multimodal Mixture-of-Experts model with a Causal Encoder-Decoder (CED) architecture that asymmetrically activates parameters: 8B per token for prefill and 16B per token for decode. They introduce aggressive KV cache optimizations: (1) Compressed Sparse Attention 2 (CSA2) with cross-layer KV cache reuse, and (2) FP4 KV caching, keeping the global KV cache fully in HBM at only 890 bytes per token. They additionally propose SWA Bounded Replay, a deployment-time optimization that limits the persistent KV cache footprint kept on SSD or host memory to roughly one-eighth of the previous DeepSeek-V4-Flash baseline. The architecture is streamlined relative to DeepSeek-V4 and extended efficiently for multimodal support. The model is pretrained on 45T multimodal tokens and then thoroughly post-trained for agentic use cases.

Result: DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash supports up to 1M-token contexts while activating fewer parameters during prefill than decode, improving cost efficiency for long, input-heavy sessions. Its global KV cache size is reduced to 890 bytes per token—about one quarter of DeepSeek-V4-Flash’s—while staying entirely in GPU HBM. The persistent KV cache footprint is further lowered to about one eighth of the baseline with SWA Bounded Replay. Despite these aggressive compression and efficiency measures, the model outperforms DeepSeek-V4-Flash in text and multimodal agentic benchmarks. Public checkpoints are released on Hugging Face.

Conclusion: The paper shows that careful co-design of model architecture (CED MoE), attention/KV caching mechanisms (CSA2 with FP4), and deployment strategies (SWA Bounded Replay) can significantly reduce compute, memory, and bandwidth requirements for million-token, agentic LLM workloads without sacrificing—and even improving—model quality. DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash demonstrates that very large multimodal MoE models can be made practical for long-context applications by focusing on prefill efficiency and aggressive yet effective KV cache compression, and it provides open checkpoints for the community.

Abstract: The widespread adoption of long-horizon agents has made model workloads increasingly input-heavy. Although prior work has substantially reduced the cost of long-context computation, prefill remains computationally expensive, and large KV caches continue to strain HBM and SSD capacity and data-transfer bandwidth. Together, these compute, storage, and bandwidth demands constitute the primary bottleneck to further lowering deployment costs. To address this challenge, we introduce DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, a multimodal Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with 552B backbone parameters and support for contexts of up to one million tokens. With its Causal Encoder-Decoder (CED) architecture, the model activates 16B parameters per token during decode but only 8B parameters during prefill, substantially improving cost efficiency for agentic workloads. To push the limits of KV cache compression, DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash combines cross-layer KV cache reuse in Compressed Sparse Attention 2 (CSA2) with FP4 KV caching. These designs reduce its global KV cache footprint (always in HBM) to 890 bytes per token, roughly 1/4 of the corresponding footprint of DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Further, through a dedicated deployment optimization known as SWA Bounded Replay, DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash reduces its persistent KV cache footprint (always on SSD or in host memory) to roughly 1/8 of that of DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Despite its much smaller KV cache footprint, the model delivers substantially better performance than the baseline. In addition, we streamline the DeepSeek-V4 architecture and introduce several efficient architectural extensions. We pretrain DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash on a multimodal corpus comprising 45T tokens and conduct comprehensive post-training, yielding strong performance across diverse text-based and multimodal agentic scenarios. Model checkpoints are available at https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash.

</details>


### [42] [Benchmarking LLM Compliance with China AI Generated Content Regulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19989)
*Chenrui Cui,Hongye Fang,Lisha Song,Weichao Chen,Yue Zhu,Gang Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper builds a regulatory benchmark and evaluation framework to measure how well Chinese and non-Chinese LLMs comply with China’s AI-generated content rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are widely deployed and pose content compliance risks. Existing safety/compliance work is mostly English-centric and does not capture the specific complexity and regulatory requirements of Chinese-language content under China’s legal framework.

Method: They operationalize China’s current AI-generated content compliance requirements into six dimensions and 2303 Chinese questions, including 203 specially constructed constitutional questions. Twenty popular LLMs are tested. A novel multi-judge framework is used, where multiple judging models independently assess each answer using hierarchical alignment memory to produce compliance and refusal verdicts.

Result: Across the 20 evaluated LLMs, many international models also show high compliance and refusal rates on standard Chinese questions. The largest performance gaps appear in dimensions tied more closely to ideological alignment than to generic safety. The benchmark quantitatively compares models on compliance and refusal behavior under Chinese regulations.

Conclusion: The work provides a unified, legally grounded regulatory benchmark for Chinese content that can be applied to both Chinese and non-Chinese LLMs. This enables researchers and developers worldwide to systematically evaluate and compare LLMs’ compliance with China’s AI-generated content rules, particularly around ideologically sensitive areas.

Abstract: The widespread adoption of LLMs has led to escalating content compliance risks. Prior works have contributed to addressing these risks in the English context, downplaying the complexity of Chinese language content. This paper follows China's current AI-Generated content compliance requirements and provides evaluation results on 20 notable LLMs, offering insight into China's regulatory landscape. We design a novel framework to assess the compliance and refusal rates with 2303 questions spanning six distinct dimensions, including 203 self-constructed constitutional questions. The framework employs several judges to generate verdicts independently based on their hierarchical alignment memory. Our findings show that international models also exhibit high levels of compliance despite the use of standard Chinese questions, and the main differences may stem from dimensions closely related to ideological alignment. We establish a regulatory benchmark that enables the global AI community to evaluate both Chinese and non-Chinese LLMs under a unified set of legally grounded compliance requirements.

</details>


### [43] [Reading Emotions in the Token Space: Discriminative Adaptation of SpeechLLMs for Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20081)
*Hasindri Watawana,Sergio Burdisso,Esaú Villatoro-Tello,Manjunath K E,Kadri Hacioglu,Petr Motlicek,Andreas Stolcke*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a simple discriminative readout for SpeechLLMs to perform emotion recognition more reliably than using their generative decoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SpeechLLMs perform emotion recognition by generating emotion labels with a decoder designed for open-ended text, which can output invalid labels, overuse frequent classes, and hallucinate. There is a need for a classification-friendly, controllable way to use SpeechLLMs for emotion recognition without retraining or altering the backbone model.

Method: Instead of reading emotion labels from the generative decoder, the authors tap into the hidden state of the final prompt token and feed it into a lightweight classification head (a single linear layer). This head maps the hidden state to a fixed emotion label set in one forward pass, leaving the SpeechLLM backbone unchanged. Because the hidden state is the same one the model would use for generation, it enables a clean comparison between generative and discriminative inference. The linear head also makes each emotion correspond to a direction in the token embedding space, enabling interpretation via associated tokens.

Result: On the IEMOCAP emotion recognition dataset, using this discriminative head on top of two different SpeechLLM architectures improves Macro F1 and eliminates hallucinated labels compared to generative decoding. The gains are largest when using realistic, error-prone ASR transcripts as input, showing robustness benefits.

Conclusion: A simple discriminative adaptation—reading the final prompt token’s hidden state through a linear classification head—can make SpeechLLMs more accurate and reliable for emotion recognition than generative decoding, while preserving interpretability. The learned emotion directions reveal indirect associations that reflect biases present in web-scale training text, suggesting both interpretability benefits and fairness concerns.

Abstract: SpeechLLMs have shown strong potential for emotion recognition, yet they read the predicted emotion off a generative decoder not suited for classification: it can emit labels outside the target set and favors frequent classes. We propose a discriminative adaptation that reads the final prompt token's hidden state through a classification head, producing a label in one forward pass without modifying the backbone. Because this readout starts from the hidden state the model would otherwise decode, it gives a controlled comparison of generative and discriminative inference in an otherwise identical speechLLM. We keep the head a single linear layer, trading little accuracy for interpretability: each emotion becomes one direction in the LLM output token space, revealing associated tokens. On IEMOCAP, across two speechLLM architectures, it improves Macro F1 and removes hallucinations, with largest gains on realistic ASR transcripts. Our analysis reveals that these emotion directions encode indirect associations mirroring biases in web-scale text.

</details>


### [44] [Design of the IBM Granite 5.0 TurboCTC ASR Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20104)
*Brian Kingsbury,George Saon,Masayuki Suzuki,Hong-Kwang J. Kuo,Takashi Fukuda,Samuel Thomas,Vishal Sunder,Avihu Dekel*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Granite 5.0 Turbo CTC is a 470M-parameter, encoder-only ASR model that achieves state-of-the-art speed-accuracy tradeoffs via architectural, training, and inference optimizations, and is released under a permissive license.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To build an automatic speech recognition (ASR) model that lies on the speed-accuracy Pareto frontier, particularly for English short-form ASR, and that is both fast and accurate enough for practical deployment while being trained on only publicly available data and released under a permissive license.

Method: They design an encoder-only, 470M-parameter Conformer-based CTC architecture that incorporates pyramidal temporal subsampling with strided depthwise convolutions, block-diagonal (chunk-wise) self-attention, and intermediate prediction conditioning from the middle layer. Training uses only public datasets, a novel application of the Muon optimizer, and balanced data sampling. For inference, they replace 1x1 convolutions with linear layers and optimize attention computation in Conformer blocks to reduce computational overhead.

Result: The resulting Granite 5.0 Turbo CTC model achieves a speed-accuracy tradeoff that lies on the Pareto frontier of the Open ASR leaderboard for English short-form ASR and runs at least twice as fast as the fastest competing system while maintaining competitive accuracy.

Conclusion: Through targeted architectural choices, careful training strategies, and specific inference optimizations, the Granite 5.0 Turbo CTC model delivers state-of-the-art efficiency for English short-form ASR and is made broadly usable by relying only on public data and being released under a permissive license.

Abstract: We describe the architecture, training methodology and inference speedups of Granite 5.0 Turbo CTC, a 470 million parameter encoder-only model with an excellent speed-accuracy tradeoff. The architecture uses pyramidal temporal subsampling within Conformer blocks using strided depthwise convolutions, block-diagonal (chunk-wise) self-attention, and conditioning on intermediate predictions from the middle layer. Training highlights are the use of only publicly available data, the novel use of a Muon optimizer, and balanced data sampling. Inference speedups include replacing 1 x 1 convolutions with linear layers and optimizing the attention computation in the Conformer blocks. Collectively, these result in a model that is on the speed-accuracy Pareto frontier of the Open ASR leaderboard for English short-form ASR while being twice as fast as the fastest competitor. The model can be used under a permissive license and downloaded from https://huggingface.co/ibm-granite/granite-speech-5.0-470m-turboctc.

</details>


### [45] [Fine-Tuning Models for Biomedical Relation Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20169)
*Claudiu Creanga,Liviu P. Dinu,Daniela Gifu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper develops and evaluates pre-trained models for automatically extracting relationships between genetic variants and phenotypes from biomedical text, showing that fine-tuned BERT-style models and Gemini Pro 1.0 achieve strong, near-SOTA or SOTA performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Next-Generation Sequencing generates vast data on genetic mutations and their links to disease, which is reflected in a rapidly growing biomedical literature. Manually extracting useful variant–phenotype relationships from this literature is infeasible, creating a need for automatic, accurate relation extraction methods tailored to this domain.

Method: The authors use pre-trained language models, focusing on small BERT-based architectures (with particular emphasis on DeBERTa) and Google’s Gemini Pro 1.0, and fine-tune them on the SNPPhenA corpus for the task of extracting variant–phenotype relations. They consider two settings: sentence-level (model sees only the sentence containing the candidate relation) and abstract-level (model processes the entire abstract) and compare performance to state-of-the-art baselines.

Result: Fine-tuned small BERT-based models, especially DeBERTa, achieve strong performance on SNPPhenA, approaching the current state of the art. Furthermore, when carefully fine-tuned, Gemini Pro 1.0 surpasses existing SOTA results for both sentence-level and abstract-level variant–phenotype relation extraction tasks.

Conclusion: Pre-trained language models, even relatively small ones, are highly effective for variant–phenotype relation extraction in biomedical text when properly fine-tuned, and large models like Gemini Pro 1.0 can set new state-of-the-art performance across sentence- and abstract-level settings. This supports adopting PTM-based and LLM-based pipelines for scalable, automated mining of genetic variant–phenotype knowledge from the literature.

Abstract: Next-Generation Sequencing has revolutionized the study of genetic mutations, enabling large-scale investigations into their roles in disease development. However, extracting meaningful insights from the vast amount of biomedical literature remains a complex challenge that cannot be addressed manually. In this paper, we present pre-trained models (PTMs) for the automatic extraction of relations from biomedical text, specifically targeting the variant-phenotype domain. Our evaluation on the SNPPhenA corpus demonstrates that fine-tuning small BERT-based models, particularly DeBERTa, yields strong performance, approaching the current state-of-the-art (SOTA). Additionally, our results indicate that carefully fine-tuning Google's Gemini Pro 1.0 outperforms the existing SOTA for both sentence-level tasks (where the model processes only the target sentence) and abstract-level tasks (where the model processes the entire abstract).

</details>


### [46] [To Copy or Not to Copy: Controlling Speculative Decoding via Intrinsic Model Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20186)
*Roy Eisenstadt,Ido Cohen,Edo Cohen-Karlik,Lior Wolf,Itamar Zimerman*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes SwitchSD, an adaptive speculative decoding framework that dynamically switches between neural drafting and context-based copying to speed up LLM inference, achieving up to 15% throughput gains over strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speculative decoding speeds up LLM inference but current methods must choose between neural drafting, which is robust across tasks, and copy-based strategies, which are faster in copy-heavy text but fragile. Existing copy-based approaches rely on surface n-gram overlap and often mis-detect accidental repetitions as intentional copying, causing false positives and reduced throughput. The authors aim to create a principled, model-aware way to exploit copying without these drawbacks.

Method: The authors treat copying as a latent control signal within the LLM. They train lightweight probe models on internal representations of the target LLM to predict when the model genuinely intends to copy from the context. Using these predictions, their framework SwitchSD adaptively chooses between neural drafting (e.g., EAGLE-style speculative decoders) and context-based copying. The approach is evaluated on multiple LLM families such as Llama and Qwen.

Result: SwitchSD’s probes reach very high copy-intent detection performance (AUC greater than 0.99). By using these predictions to decide when to copy versus when to rely on neural drafts, the system improves decoding throughput by up to 15% compared to strong speculative decoding baselines like EAGLE3, especially in settings with substantial copying but without sacrificing robustness on more general text.

Conclusion: Copying can be reliably leveraged as a decoding primitive if modeled as an internal control signal rather than via heuristics on surface text overlap. SwitchSD’s adaptive switching between neural drafting and context-based copying converts noisy copy heuristics into a principled, model-aware decoding strategy, providing consistent speedups across model families and text regimes over existing speculative decoding methods.

Abstract: Speculative Decoding (SD) has significantly accelerated Large Language Model (LLM) inference, yet existing approaches face a fundamental tradeoff between two drafting strategies: neural drafting and context-based copying. Neural drafts (e.g., EAGLE3) provide robust performance across diverse text settings, while copy-based methods achieve higher speedups in copy-intensive regimes by generating candidates faster and exploiting long repetition spans for near-perfect speculation. We analyze existing copy-based methods and find that they are prone to accidental repetitions where surface-level n-gram overlap does not reflect a structural intent to copy, leading to false-positive triggers that ultimately degrade throughput. We introduce SwitchSD, an adaptive framework that treats copying as a latent control signal of the LLM. By training lightweight probes on the target model's internal representations, SwitchSD identifies genuine copy-intent with high precision (AUC > 0.99). This allows the system to dynamically switch between neural drafting (e.g., EAGLE) and context-based copying. Our results across Llama and Qwen families demonstrate throughput gains of up to 15% over state-of-the-art baselines like EAGLE3, effectively turning copying from a noisy heuristic into a principled, model-aware decoding regime.

</details>


### [47] [Foundations of Stochastic Lexical Calculus: Semantic Descent and Random Dynamics on Probability Simplices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20207)
*Matthew F Dixon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper examines when probabilities from large language models (LLMs) can be treated as proper, updatable uncertainties over meaningful latent states, and develops a mathematical and empirical framework to test and enforce this.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs assign probabilities to next tokens based on prompts, but scientific and decision-making applications require uncertainty over stable, interpretable states that can be sequentially updated as evidence accumulates (e.g., hidden Markov states, beliefs in a Bayesian model). It is unclear when raw LLM token probabilities can be safely treated in this way. The authors aim to provide a principled criterion and framework for deciding when language-derived probabilities are suitable for such state-based, sequential inference, and how to adjust them if not.

Method: The authors build a theoretical framework using typed measurable transformations that map contextual language inputs to probabilistic states, and construct a minimal closed representation of these states. They derive necessary and sufficient conditions for semantic updates (state transitions given new language evidence) to exist uniquely. They analyze nonclosure (when updates leave the state space), bound the associated error, and under an average contraction assumption, prove existence, uniqueness, and stability of an external random recursion on a probability simplex—effectively a stochastic process over a low-dimensional state space driven by language-derived probabilities. This defines a ":stochastic lexical calculus" that treats the LLM as a black box without assuming an internal symbolic calculus. Empirically, they run "frozen" experiments where an LLM’s parameters are fixed, and they test observable implications of the theory via invariance and stability gates. They assess whether raw prompt-conditioned token probabilities admit a stable low-dimensional state representation, and apply prompt-specific calibration to enforce the theoretical conditions when they fail.

Result: The raw probabilities output by the LLM, conditioned directly on prompts, do not satisfy the required invariance property for a valid state representation and thus fail the initial theoretical gate. After applying prompt-specific calibration to adjust these probabilities, the authors recover a shared three-state representation that passes the stability tests. This calibrated three-state model successfully explains 28 out of 30 previously unseen eight-step sequences, yielding empirical coverage of 0.933, which is consistent with a nominal confidence level of 0.90. This demonstrates that, with proper calibration and within a restricted domain, language-derived probabilities can be mapped to a stable stochastic state process.

Conclusion: LLM token probabilities cannot in general be naïvely treated as proper beliefs over latent states for sequential scientific inference. However, if one verifies and enforces closure, stability, and coverage properties within a well-defined operating domain, then calibrated language-derived probabilities can support a stochastic state representation. The paper provides both the mathematical conditions and empirical testing procedures needed to certify when an LLM can be used as a component in a principled state-based uncertainty model, without assuming any internal symbolic calculus inside the model itself.

Abstract: Large language models produce prompt-dependent probabilities over words, whereas scientific systems require uncertainty over meaningful states that can be updated as evidence arrives. We develop an observable framework for determining when language-derived probabilities support such a sequential state representation. Theoretically, we define typed measurable transformations of contextual language, construct a minimal closed representation, and give necessary and sufficient conditions for semantic updates to exist uniquely. We bound irreducible nonclosure and accumulated error, and under average contraction prove existence, uniqueness and stability of an external random recursion on a probability simplex. These results define a stochastic lexical calculus without attributing an internal calculus to the language model. Empirically, frozen experiments test the observable implications. Raw prompt-conditioned probabilities fail the prespecified invariance gate; after prompt-specific calibration, a common three-state representation passes the stability gates and covers 28 of 30 untouched eight-step paths, or 0.933 at nominal level 0.90. Accordingly, language probabilities support a stochastic state only conditionally on verified closure, stability and coverage within a declared operating domain.

</details>


### [48] [Before the Warning Comes Too Late: Incremental Phone-Scam Detection from Speech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20223)
*Khang Nhat Hoang Vo,Anh Trac Duc Dinh,Tai Tien Ta,Tho Quan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes StreamFraudNet, a model for weakly supervised, incremental telecom fraud detection directly from streaming telephone audio, achieving very high accuracy and low latency without needing transcripts or fine-grained annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Telecom fraud calls need to be detected as early as possible while the call is still ongoing, but most existing systems rely on full call transcripts, strong supervision, or non-incremental models that only operate after the call ends. There is thus a need for a system that can work with weak labels (conversation-level only), operate incrementally on raw audio, and meet real-time latency constraints.

Method: The authors design StreamFraudNet, which takes raw incoming speech audio and splits it into overlapping, bounded-context windows. Each window is passed through a frozen self-supervised speech encoder to obtain representations, then through a recurrent temporal module that captures context across windows, and finally through a learned aggregation mechanism that combines latent scores from windows into an updatable fraud-risk prediction. Training is weakly supervised using only whole-conversation labels, but the model is architected to provide predictions throughout the call. Ablation studies are performed to understand the contribution of components, especially the recurrent context modeling.

Result: On a controlled English telecom-fraud benchmark, StreamFraudNet achieves a ROC–AUC of 0.9953, clearly outperforming simpler acoustic-feature and mean-pooling baselines, and approaching the performance of stronger global temporal models that use the full call. The system generates its first fraud-risk score after 10 seconds of audio, refreshes predictions every 2 seconds, and runs faster than real time on the tested server hardware. Ablation analysis shows that recurrent temporal context is the main factor driving the observed performance gains.

Conclusion: The study shows that high-quality, incremental fraud-risk scoring is feasible directly from raw telephone speech with only weak conversation-level supervision, eliminating the need for transcripts or fine-grained temporal labels. The results also underline the importance of recurrent temporal context for this task and suggest that explicitly latency-aware training strategies will be necessary to further improve very-early-call predictions.

Abstract: We study weakly supervised incremental telecom fraud detection from raw telephone audio, where training provides only conversation-level labels and predictions must be updated before a call ends. We introduce StreamFraudNet, which processes incoming audio through overlapping bounded-context windows using a frozen self-supervised speech encoder, recurrent temporal modeling, and learned aggregation of latent window scores. On a controlled English benchmark, StreamFraudNet achieves a ROC--AUC of \(0.9953\), significantly outperforming acoustic and mean-pooling baselines while remaining competitive with strong global temporal models. The model produces its first prediction after 10 seconds of audio, updates every 2 seconds, and operates faster than real time on the evaluated server hardware. Ablations identify recurrent temporal context as the principal contributor to performance. These results demonstrate that fraud risk can be scored incrementally from raw speech without transcripts or temporal annotations, while highlighting the need for latency-aware training to improve early prediction.

</details>


### [49] [The Public Discourse Corpus (PDC): A Speaker-Attributed Dataset for Valence and Epistemic Modality with Target Speaker Participation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20232)
*Bo Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces the Public Discourse Corpus (PDC), a large dataset of public-figure interview speech annotated for affective valence, epistemic modality, and target speaker participation, with an open-source diarization and processing pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of large-scale, systematically annotated corpora of real-world public-figure interviews that jointly capture emotional tone (affective valence) and how certain or uncertain speakers are (epistemic modality). Existing resources also often fail to clearly separate the target speaker’s speech from interviewers or others, making analysis noisy and unreliable. The authors aim to fill this gap with a robust, well-documented dataset and methodology that others can reuse.

Method: The authors collect 998 interview videos of 100 public figures from seven professional domains. They segment and filter the videos into sentences, resulting in 186,642 sentences (3.1M words). They design and apply a Target Speaker Participation (TSP) taxonomy with five categories to verify that each video contains sufficient analyzable speech from the intended target speaker, achieving substantial inter-annotator agreement (κ=0.616). To cleanly isolate the target speaker’s turns from interviewers and others, they build an audio-first diarization pipeline that combines a local Whisper ASR system for transcription with pyannote for speaker separation. They annotate the corpus for affective valence and epistemic modality and package the dataset together with annotation tools, a cross-provider validation sample, and the full processing pipeline, releasing everything as open source on Hugging Face.

Result: The outcome is the Public Discourse Corpus (PDC), a large-scale dataset of 186,642 sentences of public-figure interview speech, with each instance linked to affective valence and epistemic modality labels and curated using the TSP taxonomy. The TSP scheme shows substantial inter-annotator reliability (κ=0.616), validating its usefulness for corpus construction. The audio-first diarization pipeline successfully separates the target speakers’ turns from others and is released as open source, along with all annotation tools, validation samples, and processing code.

Conclusion: The paper delivers a novel and practically useful resource for studying affect and epistemic stance in public-figure discourse, addressing prior shortcomings in speaker separation and annotation coverage. The TSP taxonomy provides a reusable methodological contribution for future corpus-building efforts, and the open-source release of data and tools facilitates reproducibility and further research in computational social science, NLP, and speech analysis.

Abstract: We introduce the \textbf{Public Discourse Corpus (PDC)}, the first dataset of public-figure interview speech jointly annotated for affective valence and epistemic modality. The corpus contains 998 videos from 100 speakers across seven professional domains, yielding 186,642 sentences (3.1 million words) after sentence segmentation and filtering. To ensure that all retained videos contain analyzable speech from the intended speaker, we introduce \textbf{Target Speaker Participation (TSP)}---a five-category annotation taxonomy with documented inter-annotator reliability ($κ= 0.616$)---as a key methodological contribution that any corpus construction project can adopt. Target-speaker turns are separated from interviewer and third-party speech through an \textbf{audio-first diarization pipeline} combining local Whisper ASR with pyannote speaker separation, released as an open-source implementation. We release the annotated corpus, the annotation tools, the cross-provider validation sample, and the complete processing pipeline. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/ictchenbo/public-discourse-corpus.

</details>


### [50] [Lens: Bringing the Right Semantic Perspective into Focus for Training-Free Multimodal Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20252)
*Xinran Liu,Shouqian Shi,Yixian Chen,Ruizhi Chen,Xin-Wei Yao,Sheng Zhong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes Lens, a training-free way to extract task-directed representations from large (multimodal) language models using special readout phrases, improving retrieval performance without further training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing embedding models are explicitly trained for representation learning but have less diverse and smaller training data than large language models (LLMs) and multimodal LLMs. While LLMs, thanks to large-scale pretraining and instruction tuning, can already integrate evidence and infer semantics from multiple modalities, current methods that extract embeddings from them do not reliably align with the semantic ‘perspective’ required by a downstream task. As a result, the obtained embeddings are biased toward the most salient input content rather than the task-specific meaning. The authors identify this as a semantic perspective misalignment problem and aim to exploit LLMs’ reasoning abilities for better, task-aware representations without extra training.

Method: They introduce Lens, a training-free framework to obtain task-directed embeddings from autoregressive (multimodal) LLMs. Lens has two key components: (1) Semantic Perspective Anchoring, which ties the downstream task’s required semantic perspective to a task-specific readout phrase, thereby assigning a clear interpretive role to the token positions used later for extraction; and (2) Contextualized Phrase Readout, which appends this readout phrase after the entire input sequence and then aggregates the hidden states of its tokens. This way, the model has full access to the input context, and the final representation is taken from tokens explicitly conditioned on the anchored task perspective, instead of from generic content tokens. No model parameters, architecture, or reranking procedures are modified.

Result: Using Lens on existing LLM/MM-LLM backbones, they evaluate across 36 datasets in the MMEB benchmark. Lens achieves an overall Precision@1 of 63.9, surpassing the strongest training-free embedding baseline that uses the same backbone by 10.2 absolute points, demonstrating substantially better retrieval accuracy without any additional training or changes to the model.

Conclusion: Training-free, task-directed representation extraction is feasible and effective by leveraging LLMs’ inherent semantic reasoning through carefully designed readout phrases and contextualized extraction. By addressing semantic perspective misalignment, Lens produces embeddings that better reflect task-conditioned evidence integration rather than generic content salience, yielding large performance gains on multimodal retrieval benchmarks without modifying or fine-tuning the underlying models.

Abstract: High-quality representations are essential for a wide range of downstream tasks. Dedicated embedding models are explicitly optimized for representation learning, yet their training data are often more limited in scale and diversity than the massive corpora used to pretrain modern large language models and multimodal large language models. Large-scale pretraining and instruction following enable autoregressive models to select relevant evidence, integrate multimodal information, and infer semantics under different task perspectives, creating a distinctive opportunity for training-free representation learning. However, our analysis reveals that existing semantic-elicitation methods do not reliably orient the extracted states toward the semantic perspective required by the downstream task. Consequently, the resulting representations often remain dominated by salient input content. We characterize this problem as semantic perspective misalignment and propose Lens, a training-free framework that makes representation readout task-directed. Semantic Perspective Anchoring associates the task-required perspective with a task-specific readout phrase, specifying the interpretive role of the positions later used for extraction. Contextualized Phrase Readout places the same phrase after the complete input and aggregates its token states, combining full-context access with the anchored perspective. The resulting representation reflects task-conditioned evidence integration and inference rather than a generic summary of salient content. Without parameter updates, architectural modification, or reranking, Lens achieves an overall Precision@1 of 63.9 across all 36 MMEB datasets, outperforming the closest same-backbone training-free embedding baseline by 10.2 points.

</details>


### [51] [Viveka-Insight: a cross-lingual concept graph and citation-grounded retrieval resource over the complete works of Swami Vivekananda in English and Bengali](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20303)
*Tamal Maharaj*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Viveka-Insight is a bilingual resource and pipeline for Swami Vivekananda’s English and Bengali works, providing structured text, a cross-lingual concept graph, alias inventory, and human-annotated links to support grounded, cross-lingual analysis and retrieval of philosophically sensitive material.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical philosophical texts are hard to use with modern NLP because they exist in multiple, non-aligned languages, use archaic and domain-specific vocabulary, and require that any generated text over culturally sensitive material be tightly grounded in the source. The authors want to create a resource and methods that address these problems for a major philosophical corpus and that can generalize to other classical works.

Method: They build a pipeline over two related but non-parallel corpora (English and Bengali) of Swami Vivekananda’s works. First, they parse and structurally segment the texts into paragraphs and sentences, anchoring each paragraph to locations in the printed editions. Second, they construct a cross-lingual, language-agnostic concept graph: English canonical labels serve as keys linking concept mentions across both languages, and they add typed edges between paragraphs and concepts, and among concepts themselves. Third, they compile a bilingual alias inventory of all surface forms. Fourth, they create a human-annotated evaluation set of paragraph–concept links with multiple annotator judgments. They then evaluate cross-lingual retrieval using known lecture pairs, audit citation integrity and question-bridging, and measure concept-extraction precision, including calibration by extractor confidence.

Result: The released resource includes: (i) a structure-preserving parse with nearly 33k paragraphs and ~169k sentences; (ii) a concept graph of 8,362 concepts, nearly 88k paragraph–concept edges, and ~56k concept–concept edges; (iii) a 60,850-entry bilingual alias inventory; and (iv) 200 human-annotated paragraph–concept edges with full annotator labels. Cross-lingual retrieval over 194 verified lecture pairs achieves Recall@10 of 0.86 in both directions. Concept-extraction precision under strict two-annotator consensus is 0.60 (kappa 0.61), and by restricting to high-confidence predictions (weight ≥ 0.8), precision rises to 0.71 while keeping 98% of concept-bearing paragraphs. Precision is substantially higher in English (0.68) than in Bengali (0.54), pinpointing a key weakness for cross-lingual use.

Conclusion: Viveka-Insight provides a deeply structured, bilingual, and partially validated resource for Swami Vivekananda’s English and Bengali works, enabling grounded cross-lingual retrieval and concept-based analysis of a major classical philosophical corpus. The calibrated extraction and graph design make it feasible to support verifiable question-answering and exploration, though performance remains uneven across languages—especially Bengali, which is critical for cross-lingual access. The general design can be applied to other multilingual classical corpora facing similar challenges.

Abstract: Classical philosophical corpora pose three compounding challenges for language resources: they exist in several languages without parallel alignment, their vocabulary is remote from that of contemporary readers, and generated text over culturally sensitive material must be verifiably grounded. We present Viveka-Insight, a bilingual resource and open-source pipeline for the works of Swami Vivekananda (1863-1902): the nine-volume English Complete Works and the ten-volume Bengali Vani o Rachana, two related but non-parallel corpora of about 15 million characters. Four layers are released: (i) a structure-preserving parse (32,694 paragraphs, 168,842 sentences) with per-paragraph anchors deep-linking into the published editions; (ii) a cross-lingual concept graph of 8,362 language-agnostic concepts with 87,518 relation-typed paragraph-concept and 55,872 concept-concept edges, in which canonical English labels act as a string-equality key linking Bengali and English passages with no parallel data; (iii) a bilingual alias inventory of 60,850 surface forms (30,053 English, 30,797 Bengali); and (iv) a human-annotated set of 200 paragraph-concept edges judged by three annotators, released with all per-annotator judgments. We report known-item cross-lingual retrieval over 194 verified rendered lecture pairs (Recall@10 0.86 in both directions), a 30-question audit of citation integrity and modern-question bridging, and a human study placing concept-extraction precision at 0.60 under strict two-annotator consensus (Cohen's kappa = 0.61). The extractor's confidence weight is calibrated: restricting to weight >= 0.8 raises precision to 0.71 while retaining 98% of concept-bearing paragraphs. Precision is markedly lower in Bengali than English (0.54 vs 0.68), locating the weakness in exactly the half that cross-lingual access depends on. The design transfers to other multilingual classical corpora.

</details>


### [52] [Schema-Anchored Latent Reasoning for Semantic Parsing-Based Knowledge Base Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20398)
*Guangze Gao,Zixuan Li,Sikui Zhang,Chunfeng Yuan,Wenjuan Li,Bing Li,Xiaolong Jin,Weiming Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes SALR, a schema-anchored latent reasoning method for semantic-parsing-based knowledge base question answering that keeps reasoning in continuous latent space while being grounded to KB schema, leading to better logical form generation and improved performance on GrailQA and WebQSP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based semantic parsing over large, heterogeneous knowledge bases struggles with correctly selecting and composing relevant schema elements (relations, classes). Existing methods commit early to discrete schema choices during intermediate reasoning, so any mistake propagates into the final logical form and answer. The authors want a way to let the model reason in multiple steps while delaying hard schema decisions, but still keep this reasoning grounded in the KB schema.

Method: SALR performs multi-step latent reasoning by generating continuous “thought” representations in hidden states instead of explicit textual reasoning steps. To connect these latent thoughts to the KB, it learns an alignment between hidden states and a codebook of schema elements (relations, classes). This alignment is supervised using schema traces automatically derived from gold logical forms. At each reasoning step, the model maps its hidden state to schema codes, and these codes are fed back into the model as schema-guided input for subsequent steps, providing schema-mediated feedback without explicit chain-of-thought text.

Result: On the GrailQA and WebQSP benchmarks, SALR consistently improves over strong LLM-based and SP-based baselines. On compositional questions in GrailQA, SALR surpasses TIARA, a strong semantic parsing baseline, by 2.86 F1 points. Additional analyses indicate that the schema-mediated feedback significantly influences logical form generation and that the latent states learned by SALR encode recoverable schema information.

Conclusion: Grounding multi-step latent reasoning in KB schemas via a learned alignment between hidden states and schema elements allows LLMs to delay discrete logical form commitments while still being schema-aware. This reduces error propagation from early schema choices and leads to more accurate logical form construction and question answering over large heterogeneous KBs. SALR demonstrates that schema information can be effectively injected and later recovered from latent representations, improving performance on complex, compositional KBQA tasks.

Abstract: Semantic parsing (SP)-based knowledge base question answering aims to answer natural language questions by generating executable logical forms (LFs) over knowledge bases (KBs). When applying Large Language Models (LLMs) to this task, a key challenge over large, heterogeneous KBs is selecting question-related schema elements (i.e., relations and classes) and composing them into complex LFs. Recent LLM-based methods often make early discrete commitments to schema elements during intermediate reasoning, allowing incorrect intermediate schema decisions to propagate and finally result in incorrect LFs. To overcome this limitation, we propose SALR, a schema-anchored latent reasoning method for LF construction. It performs multi-step reasoning by generating continuous thoughts in the model's hidden states, thereby delaying the explicit commitment to LF decisions. To ground this latent reasoning process in the corresponding KB schema, SALR aligns continuous thoughts with a codebook of KB schema elements through an alignment objective supervised by schema traces deterministically derived from gold LFs. It then incorporates the aligned schema codes into inputs for subsequent reasoning steps. This schema-mediated feedback guides LF generation without requiring the model to emit an explicit textual reasoning trajectory. Experiments on GrailQA and WebQSP show that SALR achieves consistent overall gains over strong baselines. Notably, on compositional questions from GrailQA, SALR outperforms TIARA, a strong SP-based baseline, by 2.86 F1 points. Further analyses show that schema-mediated feedback affects LF generation and that schema information is recoverable from the latent states.

</details>


### [53] [Xeno-Interpretability: Investigating the Alien Minds of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20408)
*F. Pierucci,M. Bracale Syrnikov,M. Prandi,M. Galisai,F. Giarrusso,P. Bisconti*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces and motivates the idea that large language models may contain internal representations that do not map cleanly onto any existing human concept, and proposes a research program (xeno-interpretability) to locate, characterize, and study these ‘alien’ representations and their safety implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most interpretability work assumes that model internals can ultimately be explained using human concepts like truthfulness, deception, or toxicity. But LLMs live in a very high-dimensional representational space that may support distinctions for which humans have no adequate concepts. If such ‘alien’ distinctions systematically influence behavior, then purely human-centric interpretability will miss important aspects of model operation and risk. The authors therefore want to expand the goal of interpretability beyond mapping to human concepts, to also include discovering and analyzing model-native structures that may be semantically opaque to us.

Method: Conceptual and theoretical analysis, not an empirical benchmark. First, they define a distinction between (a) human-interpretable semantic space, where model features can be labeled or described with existing human concepts, and (b) xeno-semantic space, where internal distinctions have no adequate human conceptual counterpart. They argue information-theoretically and geometrically that the possible internal distinctions of an LLM far exceed what can be captured by finite human language and concept inventories. They then propose a methodological split between: (1) experimentally identifying internal structures—locating them in the model, describing their geometry, causally intervening on them, and correlating them with behavior; and (2) semantically interpreting them in human terms. Xeno-interpretability focuses on (1) even when (2) is impossible or incomplete. On this basis, they outline an empirical research program for detecting model-native structures that resist human conceptualization.

Result: The paper does not report new experiments but rather theoretical and conceptual results. It establishes that: (1) there are principled reasons to expect a large xeno-semantic space in modern LLMs; (2) internal representations can be robustly studied and causally manipulated even when we lack adequate human-level semantics for them; and (3) such representations can plausibly propagate and stabilize in multi-agent or multi-model ecosystems via purely model-native channels, largely bypassing human-understandable communication. These arguments reframe existing interpretability goals and suggest new lines of empirical work.

Conclusion: Interpretability should not be limited to finding human concepts inside models. We must also study xeno-representations: model-native internal structures that lack adequate human conceptual counterparts but influence behavior. A research program of xeno-interpretability would focus on discovering, localizing, and causally characterizing such structures, even when we cannot give them clear human-readable meanings. This has direct implications for AI safety and multi-agent systems, where xeno-semantic structures could circulate and stabilize among interacting models while remaining partially or wholly invisible to human observers. The paper concludes that future interpretability and safety work must explicitly account for this xeno-semantic space to better anticipate and control unpredictable model behaviors.

Abstract: Large language models are usually interpreted through concepts that humans already possess: truthfulness, refusal, deception, personality, harmfulness, and related categories. This paper asks whether models may also represent and use distinctions for which no adequate human concept exists. We call such internal structures xeno-representations, and their study xeno-interpretability. We distinguish the human-interpretable semantic space from the xeno-semantic space: the region of model-native representations for which no adequate human conceptual counterpart is available. We show that the space of possible internal distinctions in an LLM is substantially larger than the space available through finite human descriptions. We then separate experimental identification from semantic interpretation: an internal representation may be reproducibly located, geometrically characterized, causally manipulated, and linked to downstream behaviour even when its semantic content cannot be adequately expressed in human terms. On this basis, we sketch an empirical programme to identify xeno-representations. We finally examine the implications for AI safety and multi-agent systems, where model-native representations may propagate and stabilize across interacting agents while remaining only partially visible through human-readable communication. Xeno-interpretability therefore shifts the aim of interpretability from finding human concepts inside models toward discovering and characterizing the representational structures that are native to the models themselves and might affect their behaviour in unpredictable ways.

</details>


### [54] [Stress-testing Alignment Midtraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20412)
*Sid Baines,Jonathan Bostock,Maria Angelica Martinez,Andrew Draganov,David Africa,Daniel Tan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates alignment midtraining (AMT) – continuing pretraining on alignment-relevant data – and finds that its benefits are fragile and insufficiently evidenced for solving core alignment challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier AI models need to be aligned to desired behaviors in diverse deployment settings, but post-training can only directly supervise a narrow distribution of behaviors. AMT is proposed to improve generalization of alignment by injecting large amounts of alignment-relevant data during pretraining, yet there is little public empirical evidence about how well this works, especially at scale.

Method: The authors formalize several assumptions about how midtraining should help alignment and then design controlled experiments spanning models up to 110B parameters and midtraining runs up to 1B tokens. They construct scenarios with ambiguous post-training signals (two competing motivations) and scenarios where models must follow multiple rules but only observe some rules in different training stages. They vary the presence and proportion of alignment-relevant signals in midtraining vs post-training and measure how model motivations and rule-following behaviors change.

Result: Midtraining can successfully bias model behavior toward a desired motivation in simplified settings when the post-training data is not adversarial or conflicting. However, even a very small portion of finetuning data indicating an alternative, competing motivation is enough to wipe out the midtraining-induced preference. For rule-learning, models only robustly learn and retain rules that are explicitly demonstrated in either the midtraining or post-training data; rules that are never explicitly demonstrated tend not to be reliably inferred by generalization from others.

Conclusion: The experimental results undermine strong claims about AMT’s effectiveness: its benefits appear fragile to small amounts of conflicting post-training data and insufficient for robustly instilling un-demonstrated rules. Consequently, the authors argue that current public evidence does not justify confidence that alignment midtraining can, by itself, resolve the hardest parts of aligning powerful AI systems.

Abstract: When aligning frontier models through post-training techniques, it is not possible to directly demonstrate all of the behaviours we want a model to exhibit in all possible deployment environments; our model must generalise outside of the post-training distribution. One proposed solution is alignment midtraining (AMT), which continues pretraining on large volumes of alignment-relevant documents to encourage generalisation in later stages of training.
  Despite the prominence of AMT as an alignment approach, there is limited public evidence for its effectiveness. To resolve this, we identify several assumptions around midtraining and evaluate them across scale: up to 110 billion-parameter models and 1 billion midtraining tokens. For instance, we study a scenario where post-training data is ambiguous between two possible motivations. We find that midtraining can steer the model's motivation in simple versions of this setting. However, the presence of a tiny fraction of finetuning data which suggests a competing motivation erases the effects of AMT. We also study scenarios in which we want an AI to follow a number of rules, but only demonstrate a subset of them. We find that demonstrations must be present either in midtraining or post-training datasets for these rules to be robustly learned.
  Based on these and other findings, we do not believe that there is sufficient public evidence for us to confidently state that midtraining can address the core difficulties inherent in aligning powerful AI systems.

</details>


### [55] [Edustories: A Collection of Real-world Case Studies from Classroom Practices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20484)
*Michal Štefánik,Jan Nehyba,Jirina Karasova,Martin Fico,Lucie Škarková,Markéta Košatková,David Kosatka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents Edustories, a dataset of 1,492 real classroom case studies for studying AI support in collective teaching and evaluating LLMs’ ability to predict outcomes of teacher interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most AI-in-education research focuses on one-on-one, individualized student support, while real-world teaching largely occurs in whole-class settings. There is a lack of resources and benchmarks to study how AI can assist teachers dealing with complex, collective classroom situations and to rigorously evaluate models’ pedagogical judgment.

Method: The authors collect and curate 1,492 teacher-written case studies describing real elementary and high-school classroom situations, including challenging student behaviors, the pedagogical interventions attempted, and their outcomes. They design Edustories both as a resource for studying AI support for teachers and as a benchmark to test LLMs’ ability to predict intervention success. They then evaluate multiple recent LLMs from four model families on the prediction task and compare model performance to expert human assessments.

Result: LLMs can predict the success of teacher interventions better than random but still lag behind human experts. The best-performing models achieve 58% accuracy in predicting classroom outcomes, compared to 64% accuracy by expert human raters on the same task.

Conclusion: Edustories provides a new, realistic benchmark and dataset for researching AI support in collective classroom teaching. Current LLMs show promising but limited ability to approximate expert pedagogical judgment in predicting the success of teacher interventions, revealing a measurable gap to human expertise. This suggests substantial room for improvement in designing AI assistants that can reliably support practicing teachers in complex classroom settings.

Abstract: Despite the widely recognized potential of AI in education, most prior work has focused on individualized student assistance. In contrast, the majority of educational practice worldwide still takes place in collective classroom settings. To enable researchers to study AI assistance in collective teaching, we introduce Edustories, a dataset of 1,492 teacher-written case studies describing real elementary and high-school classroom situations involving challenging student behavior, pedagogical interventions, and their outcomes. Among many other applications, Edustories enables evaluating LLMs' ability to predict the success of teacher interventions, crucial for providing practicing teachers with useful feedback. Comparing the latest models from four language-model families against expert assessments, we find that current models fall short of human expertise in predicting classroom outcomes; the strongest models reach 58% accuracy compared to 64% of human experts. This gap highlights both the limitations and the emerging potential of AI as assistants for practicing teachers.

</details>


### [56] [Relational Attention for Data-Efficient Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20530)
*Adrian Brasoveanu,Ece Takmaz,Jakub Dotlačil*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Relational BabyLM introduces a decoder-only Transformer that integrates relational attention with standard lexical processing plus a Next-Latent Prediction objective, achieving strong data-efficient structural generalization and competitive BabyLM 2026 leaderboard results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explore whether relational attention architectures that excel at purely relational reasoning can improve data-efficient language modeling under BabyLM’s strict data limits, and test how combining structural inductive biases with belief-state style training affects generalization beyond training samples.

Method: Replace standard self-attention with a Dual Attention Transformer that disentangles object-level lexical information from structural/relational information via three relational attention variants (full RA, RCA, DisRCA); add a Next-Latent Prediction auxiliary objective to encourage compressed belief states; implement a new RoPE-based symbol-retrieval mechanism without extra parameters; train under BabyLM 10M and 100M word tracks and evaluate on the challenge’s broad benchmark suite, including structural linguistic generalization and NLP tasks.

Result: Architectural changes dominate gains in structural generalization, with the NextLat objective providing additional but smaller benefits; all three RA variants behave similarly at 10M tokens, but at 100M tokens the full RA variant outperforms RCA and DisRCA; the parameter-free RoPE-based symbol retrieval matches the performance of learned symbol libraries; on the 100M-word strict BabyLM track the best model ranks 6th overall and 3rd on NLP tasks, with two models beating the GPT-2 baseline on most benchmarks and one achieving the top EWoK score among strict-track systems.

Conclusion: Combining relational attention with a decoder-only Transformer and a Next-Latent belief-state objective yields data-efficient language models that generalize structurally and competitively under severe data constraints; full relational attention becomes particularly beneficial with more data, and carefully designed, parameter-free symbol-retrieval mechanisms can replace learned symbol libraries without loss, suggesting relationally structured inductive biases are promising for cognitively plausible language learning.

Abstract: We present Relational BabyLM, a system submission to the BabyLM 2026 challenge that combines two cognitively motivated inductive biases in a single decoder-only Transformer. Architecturally, we replace standard self-attention with a Dual Attention Transformer (DAT), which separates the routing of object-level ("sensory") lexical features from structural/relational information (Altabaa and Lafferty, 2025; Altabaa et al., 2024; Webb et al., 2024; Kerg et al., 2022; Webb et al., 2021). Relational attention (RA) disentangled from self-attention greatly increases data efficiency and out-of-training-sample generalization on purely relational tasks, but language modeling requires object-level and relational information to be integrated as well as disentangled, and RA-based LMs have remained largely unexplored. BabyLM's data-constrained training and comprehensive evaluation is an ideal testing ground for whether that data efficiency transfers. As a training intervention, we add a Next-Latent Prediction (NextLat; Teoh et al. 2026) objective that encourages hidden states to compress history incrementally into a dense belief state. Architecture is the dominant factor for structural linguistic generalization; the objective is secondary but still significant. DAT's three relational attention types (full RA vs. the simpler RCA and DisRCA variants) are largely interchangeable at 10M words; full RA pulls ahead at 100M. We also introduce a novel symbol-retrieval mechanism (RoPE-based, as opposed to learned, relative symbols) that matches learned symbol libraries while adding no parameters. On the strict (100M-word) track, our best model ranks 6th of 55 overall and 3rd of 55 on the leaderboard's NLP-task subset at the time of writing; our two strongest models outperform the GPT-2 baseline on most benchmarks, with one attaining the highest EWoK score among strict-track entries.

</details>


### [57] [An Analysis of Training-Free Self-Reported Confidence in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20541)
*Lukas Meyer,Sofia Rossi,Wei Chen,Thomas Laurent,Yiming Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how reliable large language models’ own confidence reports are at predicting correctness, comparing different training-free uncertainty signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models often output a numerical or verbal confidence alongside answers, but it is unclear whether these self-reported confidences genuinely reflect uncertainty or are just rhetoric. Reliable uncertainty estimates are important for safe deployment, so the authors want to empirically test how informative several simple, training-free confidence signals actually are.

Method: Using 100 TriviaQA questions and two LLM families, the authors compare three signals: (1) confidence verbalized together with the answer, (2) a post-hoc probability-of-truth score P(True), and (3) agreement with three additional independent generations (self-consistency). They compute AUROC for predicting answer correctness, audit benchmark errors, analyze cases of unanimous but wrong agreement, and study robustness of confidence to prompt re-elicitation and to real-world factual biography claims tagged with confidence.

Result: Directly verbalized confidence is a strong predictor of correctness, achieving very high AUROC (0.956 and 0.937). Agreement across three samples performs substantially worse (0.765 and 0.790), and combining it with verbalized confidence via simple interpolation gives no statistically significant gain. Some wrong answers receive unanimous agreement, indicating shared misconceptions that self-consistency can amplify. Confidence scores are unstable under re-elicitation, with average changes of 0.043–0.084 and 4–9% of decisions at a 0.8 threshold flipping. In a biography-claims audit, confidence differences between supported and contradicted claims are only modest.

Conclusion: LLM self-reported confidence can be surprisingly informative on some benchmarks but is fragile: it is sensitive to prompt elicitation, suffers from correlated errors (where multiple samples confidently agree on the same mistaken belief), and is affected by benchmark noise. Simple self-consistency and naive combinations of signals do not reliably improve uncertainty estimates. Overall, the work cautions against over-trusting raw confidence reports and highlights the need for more robust elicitation methods and error-decorrelating strategies for reliable uncertainty estimation.

Abstract: Large language models can report a numerical confidence together with generated content, but it is unclear whether this report is more than calibrated rhetoric. We analyze three training-free signals: confidence verbalized with the answer, post-hoc $P(\mathrm{True})$, and agreement with three additional generations on the same 100 TriviaQA questions for two model families. Direct verbalization is a surprisingly strong baseline: after auditing benchmark errors, it reaches AUROC 0.956 and 0.937 for correctness prediction. Three-sample agreement is substantially weaker (0.765 and 0.790), and a fixed interpolation with verbalized confidence has no statistically reliable benefit. Four of nine errors from one model and two of eight from the other receive unanimous sample support, showing that self-consistency can amplify shared misconceptions. Re-eliciting confidence for the same fixed answers with equivalent prompts changes scores by 0.043 to 0.084 on average and flips 4\% to 9\% of decisions at a 0.8 threshold.
