An exploratory audit of 100 confidence-tagged biography claims further finds only a modest confidence gap between supported and contradicted claims. These results argue that useful self-reports remain sensitive to elicitation, correlated errors, and benchmark noise.

</details>


### [58] [Steering the Compass: Aligning Dynamic Psychological Counseling Conversations with Cognitive Behavioral Therapy Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20565)
*Zimu Wang,Yiwen Jiang,Xiangyu Zhao,Yaling Shen,Jiahe Liu,Stephanie Fong,Maxmartwell H Cheng,Guilherme C Oliveira,Anh Nguyen,Robert Desimone,Barnaby Nelson,Dominic Dwyer,Zongyuan Ge*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents StratCBT, a large strategy-annotated CBT counseling dialogue dataset built with LLM-based self-chat and aligned to eight CBT strategies, and shows its usefulness for training and evaluating counseling agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work on using large language models for psychological counseling, particularly CBT, underutilizes the dynamic, strategy-level decision-making that real therapists rely on. Most datasets and systems lack fine-grained annotation of counseling strategies and realistic, state-aware dialogue flow, which constrains both research on therapeutic reasoning and the quality of automated CBT support.

Method: The authors construct StratCBT, a dataset of 9,688 CBT-style counseling sessions (~256K utterances) where each counselor response is labeled with one of eight CBT strategies. They model clients using structured negative thoughts and generate counselor–client conversations via LLM self-chat, guided by realistic example sessions. They then run experiments comparing strategy-aligned generation against baselines, and evaluate how well LLM-based counselors using these strategy signals can provide professional, effective CBT-like support to simulated clients.

Result: StratCBT substantially exceeds prior counseling and CBT datasets in scale and in coverage of CBT-specific skills. Experiments show that explicitly aligning counselor utterances to CBT strategies improves the quality and effectiveness of generated counseling compared to non-strategy-aware approaches, with LLM counselors performing well with LLM-simulated clients in realistic testing setups.

Conclusion: Strategy-annotated CBT dialogue data is crucial for modeling the dynamic decision-making of therapists. StratCBT provides such a resource at scale, enabling more flexible, professional, and effective LLM-based counseling systems. The findings suggest that strategy-aware generation is a promising direction for future research in automated psychological counseling and CBT support.

Abstract: Recent advancements in large language models have revolutionized the field of psychological counseling, especially in the context of Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT). While the success of CBT relies heavily on dynamic decision-making informed by the client's real-time mental state, this aspect has often been overlooked in current research, limiting both flexibility and therapeutic outcomes. In this paper, we introduce StratCBT, a dataset specifically designed for psychological counseling conversations with CBT Strategies, consisting of 9,688 sessions and around 256K utterances, with each counselor's response aligned with one of eight distinct strategies. The creation of StratCBT involves modeling clients based on their negative thoughts and generating high-quality counseling conversations through self-chat, incorporating realistic sessions as guidance, thereby significantly surpassing existing datasets in both general counseling and CBT-specific skills. We conduct extensive experiments to demonstrate the effectiveness of strategy-aligned generation and evaluate its efficacy in delivering professional and effective counseling with LLM-simulated clients to reflect real-world scenarios. The dataset can be obtained from https://github.com/zimuwangnlp/StratCBT.

</details>


### [59] [SAFARI: An Industrial Benchmark for LLM-Assisted Hazard Analysis and Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20584)
*Chenxi Wu,Zimu Wang,Haiyang Zhang,Wei Wang,Zhijie Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces SAFARI, the first industrial benchmark and evaluation protocol for using LLMs in ISO 26262 automotive hazard analysis and risk assessment, showing current models are weak at formal risk classification despite plausible narratives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are being considered for use in safety-critical engineering domains like automotive functional safety, but their reliability and limitations in formal, regulated workflows such as ISO 26262 Hazard Analysis and Risk Assessment (HARA) are not well understood. Industry needs realistic benchmarks and evaluation methods to know whether, and where, LLMs can safely assist experts.

Method: The authors build SAFARI, an industrial-scale benchmark with 3,000 de-identified real HARA cases from the automotive domain. They define two coupled tasks for LLMs: (1) open-ended generation of hazard analyses, and (2) standards-based risk assessment, including Automotive Safety Integrity Level (ASIL) classification per ISO 26262. To score open-ended HARA outputs, they design a reference-anchored "LLM-as-a-judge" evaluation protocol that is empirically shown to correlate well with expert assessments. They then benchmark nine frontier LLMs, including experiments with and without Chain-of-Thought prompting, and conduct targeted error analysis to identify systematic failure modes.

Result: Across nine frontier LLMs, the models can generate superficially plausible hazard narratives but perform poorly on formal ISO 26262 risk classification: the best model achieves only 0.261 macro-F1 for ASIL assignment. Chain-of-Thought prompting does not consistently improve performance and can even harm categorical risk judgments. Error analysis reveals two main issues: critical scenario details are often omitted in generated hazards, and models frequently misjudge controllability in the risk assessment step.

Conclusion: Current LLMs are not reliably capable of performing ISO 26262-compliant HARA and risk classification without expert oversight. SAFARI provides a realistic industrial benchmark and an LLM-as-a-judge protocol for evaluating such systems, highlighting specific weak spots—context capture in hazard generation and controllability assessment—where human experts must remain in the loop. This work sets a baseline and evaluation framework for future improvements in LLM-assisted functional safety engineering.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly considered for safety-critical engineering, yet their reliability in regulated functional-safety workflows remains underexplored. We introduce SAFARI (Safety-Aware Functional Automotive Risk Inference), the first industrial benchmark for LLM-assisted automotive Hazard Analysis and Risk Assessment (HARA) under ISO 26262. It contains 3,000 de-identified industrial HARA cases and evaluates two coupled tasks: open-ended hazard analysis and standards-grounded risk assessment. To evaluate open-ended HARA artifacts, we propose the first reference-anchored LLM-as-a-judge protocol with high expert correlation. Experiments with nine frontier LLMs show that models often produce plausible hazard narratives but remain weak at ISO 26262 risk classification, with the best ASIL macro-F1 reaching only 0.261. Chain-of-Thought prompting provides limited benefit and often degrades categorical risk assessment. Error analysis further localizes major failures to scenario-critical context omissions during hazard generation and to controllability misjudgments during risk assessment, indicating where expert oversight should be concentrated. The dataset can be obtained from https://github.com/xixi47520-hash/HARA.

</details>


### [60] [WiC is Not WSD: A Study on LLMs and Lexical Ambiguity Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20593)
*Yi Zhou,Kiamehr Rezaee,Danushka Bollegala,Mohammad Taher Pilehvar,Jose Camacho-Collados*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies why Word-in-Context (WiC) is hard for LLMs and shows that giving models explicit sense inventories (as in WSD) makes them more accurate and consistent, revealing that many WiC ‘errors’ are due to annotation and sense-boundary issues rather than poor lexical understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLMs perform well on many lexical-semantic tasks, they still struggle with WiC, where the goal is to decide if a target word has the same sense in two contexts. The authors suspect this is partly because WiC does not provide explicit sense inventories or a clear granularity of meaning, unlike traditional WSD. They want to understand if adding sense candidates helps, how WiC and WSD compare under similar conditions, and whether model ‘errors’ actually reflect problems in task design or annotation rather than genuine failures of semantic competence.

Method: The authors evaluate open-source LLMs on both WiC and traditional WSD tasks in parallel, controlling for similar experimental conditions. For WiC, they test standard formulations (just two contexts and a binary same/different decision) and augmented formulations where candidate senses are supplied, analogous to WSD. For WSD, they follow the usual setup with explicit sense inventories. They then compare performance across conditions to see how explicit sense information affects accuracy and consistency. In addition, they conduct human evaluation of model ‘errors’ on WiC to inspect whether disagreements stem from real misunderstandings or from ambiguous labels and mismatched sense boundaries between models and annotators.

Result: Across all experimental setups, providing candidate senses significantly improves WiC performance for open LLMs. Explicit sense inventories lead models to make more stable and targeted judgments about word meaning in context. Human analysis of apparent WiC errors reveals that many disagreements are due to label ambiguity or differing assumptions about how fine-grained sense distinctions should be, rather than clear semantic mistakes by the models. The study also finds that LLMs tend to ‘overthink’ sense distinctions, sometimes imposing unnecessarily fine-grained sense splits that cause them to diverge from the annotated gold labels.

Conclusion: The work shows that LLMs handle WiC-style sense comparison more effectively when they are given explicit sense candidates, aligning the task more closely with traditional WSD. This indicates that part of WiC’s difficulty lies in its underspecified sense granularity rather than purely in LLM semantic limitations. Many model–gold mismatches reflect annotation and sense-boundary issues rather than poor lexical understanding, and LLMs may even adopt finer-grained sense distinctions than the dataset encodes. Future evaluations of lexical semantics in LLMs should therefore carefully control sense inventories and granularity, and interpret WiC-style benchmarks with caution, as raw accuracy may underestimate models’ true semantic competence.

Abstract: Word-in-Context (WiC) remains challenging for language models, despite recent progress on lexical-semantic tasks. We hypothesise that this difficulty arises not only from comparing two contextual uses of a word, but also from the absence of an explicit sense inventory that specifies the relevant level of semantic granularity. We evaluate open LLMs on WiC and traditional Word Sense Disambiguation (WSD) under similar settings. We find that providing candidate senses, similar to what is done in traditional WSD, improves WiC performance in all settings. In general, explicit sense information helps models make more consistent and targeted judgements. Human evaluation further shows that many apparent WiC errors reflect label ambiguity or mismatches between model and annotator sense boundaries rather than simple failures of lexical understanding. In particular, results show that LLMs overthink the sense distinction often leading to errors based on overly fine-grained distinctions.

</details>


### [61] [What Does Privileged Information Add to On-Policy Self-Distillation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20612)
*XiuYu Zhang,Wei Chow,Junfeng Fang,Zhenkai Liang,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how much extra benefit on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) gets from giving the teacher privileged reasoning traces (worked solutions) beyond standard reference-free distillation, using a new math benchmark with multiple reasoning views sharing the same answer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OPSD lets a model learn from its own stronger, reasoning-enabled outputs, but it is unclear whether the gains mainly come from distilling better answers or from exposing the student to privileged reasoning traces (e.g., full solutions). Prior work intertwines these factors, so the authors want to disentangle and quantify the unique contribution of privileged references for improving reasoning in language models.

Method: The authors introduce AMPLE-Math, a dataset of 5,319 math problems, each with six different reasoning views that all lead to the same final answer. Using this controlled setting, they compare: (1) reference-free distillation from a thinking-enabled teacher supervising direct-response rollouts, and (2) OPSD variants where the teacher also sees different types of privileged references (short answers, polished solutions, complete traces). They run experiments primarily on Qwen3-1.7B and SmolLM3-3B, varying rollout length and evaluating both in-domain and on external reasoning benchmarks. They also analyze teacher behavior and perform matched loss interventions to see how changes in token-level supervision affect student behavior.

Result: For Qwen3-1.7B, most of the improvement in thinking-enabled evaluation can be explained by reference-free distillation from a thinking-enabled teacher; the additional gain from privileged references is modest and depends heavily on the reference type, with polished solutions performing best. For SmolLM3-3B, complete reasoning traces provide a noticeable (about 2 percentage point) boost at step 50. However, simply switching student rollouts from short direct responses to long thinking-enabled ones at the same checkpoint can flip performance gains into losses, despite keeping problems, references, and evaluation fixed. Analyses of teacher profiles and matched loss interventions show that substantial changes in token-level supervision sometimes yield little change in downstream student behavior.

Conclusion: OPSD mainly improves models by better exploiting existing reasoning ability shared between direct-response and thinking-enabled modes, rather than by directly transferring detailed solution content. The marginal value of privileged references lies in how much they enhance cross-mode transfer of reasoning capacity, not in how fully they expose the solution. This suggests that carefully designed distillation setups may be more important than ever-longer or more detailed reasoning traces, and that modifying token-level supervision alone may not reliably change a model’s reasoning behavior.

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) lets a language model learn from a frozen copy of itself that sees an answer or a worked solution. Giving the teacher this extra information seems to offer the student more to learn, but how much does it add beyond distillation itself? To isolate that contribution, we construct AMPLE-Math, a reusable suite of 5,319 mathematical problems with six reasoning views that share the same answer, and compare each view with matched reference-free distillation. With a thinking-enabled teacher supervising direct-response rollouts, reference-free distillation accounts for much of Qwen3-1.7B's improvement under thinking-enabled evaluation, both in domain and on external benchmarks. Evidence for an additional reference benefit is modest in Qwen, strongest for a polished solution, whereas complete traces add two percentage points in SmolLM3-3B at step 50. These benefits depend on the student being trained. At the same checkpoint, replacing short direct-response rollouts with long thinking-enabled rollouts turns gains into losses in both families while the problems, references, and evaluation stay fixed. Teacher profiles and matched loss interventions in Qwen further show that changing token-level supervision can leave student behavior largely unchanged. Together, these findings suggest that OPSD can improve access to existing reasoning capabilities through parameters shared by direct-response and thinking-enabled inference. The value of a privileged reference is what it adds to this cross-mode transfer, not how much of the solution it reveals.

</details>


### [62] [Chronicle: Cut-Point Replay for Regression Testing of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20625)
*Tisha Chawla,Susheem Koul*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Chronicle is a record-and-replay system for LLM agents that makes nondeterministic failures reproducible and testable via selective re-execution (cut-point replay).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents behave nondeterministically and depend on external tools and multi-step trajectories, so when they fail, developers struggle to reproduce the exact conditions that led to the failure. Existing recording in agent frameworks is aimed at tracing or evaluation, not at turning real incidents into regression tests that can be rerun after code changes. The authors want a practical way to capture these incidents once and reuse them to reliably test future versions of the agent and its tools in CI.

Method: The paper introduces Chronicle, a system that records agent runs at all nondeterministic boundaries (e.g., LLM calls, tool I/O) as immutable envelopes. It then replays these envelopes to reproduce executions. The key mechanism is cut-point replay: for any recorded incident, developers select which boundaries to replay from the log and which to execute live with new code. Chronicle thus allows full replay (no live calls) for bit-stable reproduction, or partial replay where specific components (like tools or agent logic) are tested under new implementations while the rest is held fixed by the recording. The authors evaluate Chronicle on a benchmark of six recorded failure incidents with simulated model boundaries and perform a mutation study on guarded tools to measure how well cut-point tests detect unsafe behavior compared to a baseline that stubs every boundary.

Result: On six recorded failure cases, recording overhead is small: about 23 microseconds per nondeterministic boundary crossing, corresponding to 0.008% of an assumed 300 ms LLM call. Full replay issues no model calls and is bitwise stable across 20 repetitions, showing strict reproducibility. Cut-point tests consistently fail when code is faulty and pass when changes are guarded or benign for all six incidents. In a mutation analysis of tool guards, every mutant that allows the recorded unsafe action to occur is detected by cut-point tests, whereas a baseline approach that stubs all boundaries (but uses the same assertion) fails to catch any such mutants. Chronicle and the benchmark implementation are released publicly.

Conclusion: Chronicle effectively transforms real LLM agent failures into reproducible, CI-friendly regression tests via record-and-replay at nondeterministic boundaries and selective cut-point replay. It achieves low recording overhead, strong reproducibility, and substantially better fault detection than naive stubbing approaches in the evaluated scenarios. The system and benchmark are available as open-source infrastructure for testing and hardening LLM agents.

Abstract: Large language model responses are non-deterministic, so failures in LLM agents are hard to reproduce: a failure depends on inference that is not bitwise reproducible, on tools that read changing state, and on a multi-step trajectory that a re-run rarely repeats. Record-and-replay makes a run reproducible, but existing agent tooling records runs only to trace or score them, not to test a code change against them. We present Chronicle, which records an agent run at its non-deterministic boundaries as immutable envelopes and replays it from the record. Its central operation, cut-point replay, serves a chosen subset of boundaries from the record and executes the complementary subset live with new code, turning a recorded incident into a regression test that runs in continuous integration. On a benchmark of 6 recorded failures with simulated model boundaries, recording adds 23 μs per crossing (0.008% of an assumed 300 ms model call), full replay issues zero model calls and is bit-stable across 20 repetitions, and cut-point tests fail on faulty code and pass on guarded and benign changes for all 6 incidents. In a mutation study of the guarded tools, cut-point tests catch every mutant that lets the recorded unsafe action through, while a baseline that stubs every boundary, using the same assertion, catches none. Chronicle and the benchmark are publicly available at https://github.com/theagentplane/chronicle.

</details>


### [63] [UniPolicy: Unified Objective-Specific Policies for Generative Search Advertising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20630)
*Kun Yao,Yuhang Zhou,Yichi Zhang,Zeliang Tong,Shengri Xue,Haitao Wang,Siyu Lu,Qianlong Xie,Xingxing Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: UniPolicy is a multi-objective alignment framework for search ads that jointly optimizes relevance, click propensity, and commercial value using objective-aware parameter decoupling and multi-policy decoding, leading to balanced gains in both user and business metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing search advertising systems largely align generative models to a single business reward (e.g., eCPM) or use simple reward fusion, which can cause gradient competition and globally suboptimal performance when multiple heterogeneous objectives (relevance, CTR, value) must be balanced. There is a need for a principled way to jointly optimize multiple objectives while maintaining user experience and platform revenue, and to better leverage rich behavioral feedback beyond crude single-signal rewards.

Method: UniPolicy introduces an objective-aware multi-policy framework on top of a shared generative backbone. It uses: (1) objective-specific prefix tokens to encode different business goals; (2) sparse MoE-LoRA routing plus objective-specific residual feed-forward networks to hierarchically decouple parameters so each objective has partially dedicated capacity while sharing a core representation; (3) a preference-construction scheme that derives pairwise preferences from multi-stage behavioral logs, adding signal from exposed-but-unclicked and clicked samples to shape the generation distribution; and (4) a parallel multi-policy beam search at inference that allows different objectives to generate candidates in parallel with customizable quota allocation under a fixed retrieval budget.

Result: In large-scale offline experiments, UniPolicy achieves balanced improvements across multiple metrics relevant to search advertising (e.g., relevance, CTR-related, and value-related measures), and preserves overall retrieval quality, outperforming both single-objective RL approaches and naive multi-reward fusion baselines. These results indicate more effective multi-objective optimization without destabilizing the core search experience.

Conclusion: UniPolicy effectively aligns search advertising generative models with multiple heterogeneous business objectives through objective-aware parameter decoupling and multi-policy decoding. It leverages richer behavioral feedback to build pairwise preferences and achieves consistent, balanced gains across key online metrics (CTR, RPS, revenue) in real production settings, without sacrificing latency, demonstrating its practicality for large-scale commercial search advertising systems.

Abstract: Search advertising connects user intent with commercial content and plays a critical role in platform monetization. Recent systems typically align pretrained generative models with a single business reward, such as eCPM, or use naive reward fusion for preliminary multi-objective alignment. However, an ideal search advertising system must jointly account for heterogeneous objectives, including relevance, click propensity, and commercial value, to balance user experience and business value while mitigating globally suboptimal performance caused by gradient competition. We propose UniPolicy, an objective-aware multi-policy alignment framework. UniPolicy combines objective-specific prefix tokens, sparse MoE-LoRA routing, and objective-specific residual FFNs to hierarchically decouple parameters within a shared backbone, providing differentiated parameter and policy-expression spaces for different business objectives. It further constructs pairwise preferences from multi-stage behavioral feedback, supplementing the relative preference information in exposed-but-unclicked samples and strengthening the relative advantage of clicked candidates in the generation distribution. At inference, UniPolicy supports parallel, business-customizable multi-policy beam search, flexibly allocating candidate quotas across objectives under a fixed retrieval budget. Large-scale offline experiments show that UniPolicy delivers balanced improvements across multiple metrics while preserving retrieval quality, outperforming single-objective reinforcement learning and naive reward-fusion baselines. In a 7-day online A/B test on a real search advertising system, UniPolicy improves CTR by 0.71%, RPS by 1.58%, and advertising revenue by 1.32%, while maintaining stable serving latency.

</details>


### [64] [HerHealthEval: Evaluating Multilingual and Register-Sensitive Understanding of Women's Health Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20684)
*Hassan Saeed Hassan Albattra,Mazen Mohammed Bahgat,Rahatara Ferdousi,Hana Essam Sayed Ahmed Amrya,Mariam Mousa*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents HerHealthEval, a controlled multilingual benchmark to test whether large language models correctly understand and safely respond to women’s-health concerns across different languages and communication styles, highlighting that headline accuracy can hide serious safety failures, especially in non-English settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most current evaluations of large language models in healthcare focus on how good the responses look, assuming the model has understood the patient’s concern correctly. This is risky in women’s health, where patients often communicate in indirect, emotional, or layperson terms and in multiple languages. There is a need for a benchmark that explicitly tests whether models: (1) correctly interpret the underlying clinical concern across languages and registers; (2) recognize missing information and ask for clarification; and (3) maintain safe triage behavior, especially avoiding under-triage, in multilingual settings.

Method: The authors build HerHealthEval, a controlled evaluation framework for women’s-health communication. For each clinical case, they create parallel versions in English, French, and Modern Standard Arabic, and in six communication forms: canonical, clinical, layperson, indirect/hedged, emotionally concerned, and deliberately under-specified. The first five are semantically matched and clinically equivalent; the last one intentionally omits key details to test uncertainty handling and clarification behavior. They then evaluate a multilingual instruction-tuned model and QLoRA-adapted variants on several axes: concern classification, risk calibration (triage safety), when and how models request clarification, adherence to required output format (parse compliance), and consistency of behavior across forms. They also compare different adaptation strategies, including one with language-asymmetric risk supervision and another with source-derived, language-invariant risk labels.

Result: The evaluation shows that looking only at overall accuracy or consistency can mask important safety problems. In particular, a multilingual adaptation strategy trained with language-asymmetric risk supervision leads to very high under-triage rates in French (0.994) and Arabic (0.994), meaning it almost always underestimates risk in these languages. When the authors re-adapt the model using risk labels that are derived from a source language but enforced to be invariant across languages, the under-triage rates in French and Arabic drop substantially to 0.572 and 0.558, respectively, though they are still high. The framework also exposes failures in how the model handles indirect language, emotional expressions, and under-specified inputs that require clarification.

Conclusion: Robust and safe multilingual evaluation of healthcare-oriented language models cannot rely on aggregate accuracy metrics alone. It requires controlled testing of how models handle different registers (clinical, lay, emotional, indirect), how they respond to uncertainty and missing information (including when they should ask for clarification), and how adaptation labels are constructed and propagated across languages. Language-asymmetric supervision can induce severe safety failures such as extreme under-triage in some languages, while using language-invariant risk labels improves but does not fully solve the problem. HerHealthEval is proposed as a framework to systematically uncover these issues in women’s-health communication.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used in healthcare communication, yet most evaluations emphasize response quality while assuming that the user's concern has been interpreted correctly. We introduce HerHealthEval, a controlled evaluation framework for multilingual understanding of women's-health communication. For each clinical case, HerHealthEval provides matched versions in English, French, and Modern Standard Arabic using six communicative forms: canonical, clinical, layperson, indirect or hedged, emotionally concerned, and deliberately under-specified. The first five express the same underlying concern and retain the same clinical information, whereas the under-specified form intentionally omits relevant details to test whether the model recognizes that clarification is needed. We evaluate a multilingual instruction model and QLoRA-adapted variants on concern classification, risk calibration, clarification behavior, parse compliance, and cross-form consistency. Results reveal that aggregate accuracy and consistency can conceal safety-relevant failures. A multilingual adaptation model reaches 0.994 under-triage in French and Arabic under language-asymmetric risk supervision. A controlled re-adaptation using source-derived, language-invariant risk labels reduces under-triage to 0.572 and 0.558, respectively. These findings show that robust multilingual healthcare evaluation requires explicit testing of register variation, uncertainty handling, and the provenance and invariance of adaptation labels.

</details>


### [65] [Summarization Bias: The Directional Collapse of Objective Projection into Told-Mode Labels in Large Language Models --- A Conceptual Framework and Registered Test Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20712)
*Levent Bulut*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper defines and motivates a new proposed bias of LLMs, called summarization bias, where models favor explicit summary labels of narrative meaning over the underlying inferential structure (shown mode), and it outlines a preregistered plan to test this construct in both generation and evaluation settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs increasingly act as both writers and judges of text, their preferences can shape how prose is produced and rewarded. The authors are concerned that if LLMs systematically favor explicitly told emotional and informational content over implicitly shown content (which requires reader inference), then using LLMs as evaluators or reward models will exert a selection pressure that degrades narrative quality toward flat, declarative prose. To address this, they want a precise construct and testable framework for this bias, aligned with an existing theoretical account of narrative effects (the Bulut Doctrine).

Method: The paper is largely conceptual and methodological rather than empirical. It: (1) introduces the construct of summarization bias and formalizes it using the Bulut Doctrine’s told–shown axis and the notion of Objective Projection; (2) distinguishes two operational regimes—generative (how LLMs write) and evaluative (how LLMs judge); (3) reinterprets a previously completed independent reliability study as providing directional, though not confirmatory, evidence compatible with the bias; and (4) preregisters a two-regime experimental test, including decision rules that would lead to rejecting the construct if results do not support it.

Result: No new empirical results are claimed. The main outcome is a structured hypothesis about summarization bias, supported only by suggestive reinterpretation of prior reliability data. The paper provides a fully specified preregistration for future tests in both the generative and evaluative regimes, with clear criteria for confirming or abandoning the bias construct.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that summarization bias is a theoretically grounded, empirically testable hypothesis about how LLMs handle narrative meaning: preferring explicit summary statements (told mode) over reconstructable inferential structure (shown mode). While unvalidated, the construct is now precisely defined, linked to existing theory, and paired with a preregistered two-regime testing plan and falsification criteria. The authors emphasize the potential stakes for narrative quality if LLM-based evaluators systematically over-reward told-mode prose.

Abstract: This paper introduces and operationalizes summarization bias: a proposed systematic tendency of large language models (LLMs) to represent narrative meaning as an abstract summary label rather than as the reconstructable inferential structure that produces it. Within the Bulut Doctrine, narrative effect is theorized along a told-shown axis: in told mode, emotional and informational content is declared explicitly and requires little reader reconstruction; in shown mode, that content is suppressed at the surface and must be reconstructed from physical cues and indirection (Objective Projection). Shown mode is the higher-load condition the doctrine is designed to measure.
  The claim is that LLMs fail along this axis in a specific direction. Summarization bias is hypothesized to operate in two regimes: (i) a generative regime, in which a model asked to render an emotion through Objective Projection defaults to declaring it instead; and (ii) an evaluative regime, in which a model judging narrative quality rewards told-mode explicitness and under-detects shown-mode suppression. The evaluative regime is the more consequential, since LLMs increasingly serve as judges and reward models, and a directional bias toward told mode would impose a selection pressure degrading prose toward flat declaration.
  This report does not claim the bias is validated. It defines the construct, situates it against LLM-as-judge biases, rereads a completed independent reliability study as directional evidence consistent with it, and pre-registers a two-regime test with decision rules under which the construct would be abandoned.

</details>


### [66] [On-Demand Attention: Language Models Know When to Recall](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20734)
*Haibo Feng,Ruiqi Liang,Hanyang Peng,Shiqi Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes On-Demand Attention (ODA), a decoding method where a small recall head decides when to use expensive global attention, enabling efficient long-context inference while preserving most model performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context reasoning and agent workloads require handling very long input histories, but standard full-attention decoding is inefficient because it repeatedly attends to the entire history at every generation step, even when it doesn’t help prediction. The authors seek a way to reduce unnecessary global attention reads without retraining the whole model or losing much accuracy.

Method: They empirically show that the model’s existing decoding states (local representations before global attention) already contain signals about whether a global read will be useful. Leveraging this, they add a lightweight recall head that, at each step, predicts the benefit of invoking global attention. Decoding proceeds with local attention by default, and only when the recall head’s prediction crosses a threshold does the model perform a global attention read using the full KV cache. The base model weights remain frozen; only the recall head is trained. They implement GPU-side conditional execution in vLLM so that skipped global reads translate into real-world speedups.

Result: Across Qwen and Gemma models, including ones with hybrid-attention architectures, ODA substantially reduces the number of global attention operations while maintaining performance close to full attention. It recovers most of the accuracy/perplexity lost when using purely local attention, demonstrating that selective recall based on the recall head’s predictions is effective.

Conclusion: Pretrained models’ own decoding states can guide when to access long-range context, enabling an on-demand attention mechanism that is both computationally efficient and accurate. This supports a new paradigm for long-context inference where models selectively recall from their full history as needed, rather than always performing costly global attention.

Abstract: Reasoning and agentic workloads increasingly demand efficient long-context inference. Yet full-attention decoding reads the growing history at every step, regardless of its benefit to the next prediction. We show that a pretrained model's decoding states already contain information predictive of this benefit, before the global read. Building on this finding, we introduce On-Demand Attention (ODA), a local-first decoding method that uses a lightweight recall head to selectively invoke global attention as its predicted benefit changes during generation. ODA trains only the recall head, leaving pretrained weights unchanged and the complete historical KV cache available for future recall. We further implement GPU-side conditional execution in vLLM, translating reduced global reads into practical decoding speedups over full attention at long context lengths. Experiments across Qwen and Gemma models, including hybrid-attention backbones, show that selective recall recovers most of the performance lost under local attention while substantially reducing global reads. These findings support long-context inference in which pretrained models guide their own access to the information they retain.

</details>


### [67] [dQwen3.5: Hybrid-Attention Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20751)
*Anton Xue,Litu Rout,Aditya Akella,Adam Klivans,Sujay Sanghavi,Sanjay Shakkottai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that modern hybrid Transformer-RNN autoregressive language models can be efficiently adapted into diffusion language models, forming the dQwen3.5 family, which trains faster (in tokens) and preserves strong any-order and parallel decoding performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion language models are promising for flexible any-order and parallel decoding, but adapting them from existing autoregressive LMs usually assumes full-attention Transformers. Meanwhile, state-of-the-art AR models are moving to hybrid architectures that interleave attention and RNN layers, which are structurally causal and hard to make bidirectional. The authors want to know whether these newer hybrid backbones can still be turned into effective diffusion LMs and whether they offer efficiency advantages.

Method: Start from Qwen3.5 hybrid autoregressive models at multiple parameter scales (0.8B, 2B, 4B, 9B) and adapt each into a diffusion language model variant, dubbed dQwen3.5. Compare these hybrid-based DLMs to a matched full-attention baseline in terms of training dynamics (tokens needed to reach a given loss), any-order decoding behavior, and parallel decoding performance.

Result: Hybrid backbone models, once adapted, require roughly half as many training tokens as the full-attention baseline to reach the same training loss. Empirically, the resulting dQwen3.5 models behave similarly to standard full-attention DLMs in any-order decoding and achieve strong performance when decoded in parallel.

Conclusion: Despite their structurally causal RNN components, modern hybrid Transformer-RNN backbones are viable and even efficient starting points for diffusion language models. Adapting such hybrids can reduce training token requirements while retaining the desirable any-order and parallel decoding capabilities associated with full-attention DLMs.

Abstract: Adapting a pretrained autoregressive (AR) model is a cost-efficient route to a diffusion language model (DLM). While nearly all such adaptations start from a full-attention transformer, AR modeling has shifted toward hybrid architectures that interleave attention and RNN layers. This creates an obstacle for adaptation: unlike attention, RNNs are structurally causal and nontrivial to bidirectionalize. Despite this mismatch, we investigate whether such backbones can become effective DLMs by adapting Qwen3.5 at 0.8B, 2B, 4B, and 9B scales, yielding the dQwen3.5 family. We find that hybrid backbones can be efficient starting points for adaptation: against a full-attention control, the hybrid reaches a given training loss in about half the tokens. Across scales, dQwen3.5 resembles full-attention DLMs in any-order decoding behavior and performs strongly under parallel decoding.

</details>


### [68] [Harm Laundering in GPT Models: Evidence That Gender Discrimination Is Transformed Rather Than Reduced Across Safety-Trained Generations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20779)
*Sarah Wyer,Sue Black,Noura Al Moubayed*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that current safety evaluations for large language models (LLMs) are systematically incomplete: they reduce overt toxicity but allow subtler, structural representational harms to persist or worsen, a process the authors call “harm laundering.”


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety metrics for LLMs focus heavily on surface-form toxicity (e.g., slurs, explicit insults). As newer models show lower toxicity scores, they are assumed to be safer. The authors suspect this is misleading: harmful bias may be transformed into more subtle, representational forms that standard toxicity classifiers do not detect, particularly in gender-directed outputs. They aim to reveal and formalize this gap in safety assessment.

Method: The authors analyze 450,000 gender-directed text completions produced by 15 OpenAI GPT models from GPT-2 through GPT-5 under three demographic prompt conditions (e.g., prompts targeting women vs. men). They apply topic modeling and clustering to examine thematic patterns (such as sexual violence, caregiving, emotionality, or identity). They also use multiple automated classifiers (three toxicity/“non-toxic” classifiers and REGARD for representational harms) and sentiment analysis to quantify differences in how genders are represented across model generations. They then formalize “harm laundering” as a three-criteria test and propose a three-stage detection protocol to identify it in any generative model.

Result: They find that overtly harmful clusters, like those involving sexual violence directed at women, largely disappear in later models (e.g., by GPT-4), and toxicity classifiers correspondingly show declining harm. However, the representational patterns change in problematic ways: later models grant men more positive, complex representational space (e.g., caregiving, emotional range, ally identities) that is not equivalently afforded to women. In GPT-5 outputs, a major topic cluster reframes breast cancer as a men’s rights debate with no parallel cluster in women-directed text. Three independent toxicity classifiers label such content as non-toxic. Sentiment analysis shows that early models demean women, but later models “over-correct,” shifting the imbalance rather than eliminating it. Topic diversity for women-directed completions drops by 36% relative to men-directed completions at the GPT-4 alignment boundary. REGARD scores for representational harms become more disparate over time and correlate with model release date, while Detoxify toxicity scores do not, indicating toxicity decreases even as representational harms increase.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that reductions in measured toxicity scores cannot be taken as evidence of genuine harm reduction in LLMs. Instead, harmful content can be “laundered” into subtler representational biases that escape standard toxicity classifiers. The authors formalize harm laundering and propose a general detection protocol, arguing that safety evaluations must go beyond surface-form toxicity and explicitly measure representational harms and distributional patterns across demographic groups when assessing model alignment and safety.

Abstract: Safety evaluations for large language models rely on surface-form classifiers that report declining harm scores across model generations. We provide evidence that this methodology is systematically incomplete: explicit discriminatory content is transformed rather than removed. We call this \emph{harm laundering}. Analysing 450,000 gender-directed completions across 15 models spanning GPT-2 through to GPT-5 (OpenAI GPT lineage; three demographic conditions), we show that sexual violence clusters prevalent in GPT-2 women-directed output disappear by GPT-4, while men-directed completions gain positive representational territory (caregiving, emotional range, ally identity) that women-directed completions do not. The pattern is most visible at GPT-5: Topic~5 (1,997~documents) frames breast cancer as a men's rights debate, while zero equivalent clusters appear in women-directed output. Three independent classifiers score this content as non-toxic. Sentiment scores invert at GPT-4: early models demean women; later models over-correct. Topic diversity in women-directed completions falls 36\% relative to men at the GPT-4 alignment boundary (W/M~$= 0.58$, from $0.91$ at GPT-2). REGARD representational harm disparity correlates with release date ($ρ= +0.55$, $p = .034$) while Detoxify does not ($ρ= -0.23$, $p = .42$): toxicity scores fall as representational harm grows. We formalise harm laundering as a three-criteria test and provide a three-stage detection protocol applicable to any generative model. Within the OpenAI GPT lineage, toxicity score reduction is not a sufficient proxy for harm reduction.

</details>


### [69] [RetireOPD: Self-Retiring On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20784)
*Yan Yu,Zhengxi Lu,Yizhou Liu,Yichen Pan,Aozhe Wang,Qipeng Chen,Hua Yang,Wenqi Zhang,Weiming Lu,Qianglong Chen,Yongliang Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces RetireOPD, an adaptive self-distillation framework for multi-turn RL agents that learns from a skill-conditioned teacher early on and automatically drops it when it stops being useful, leading to substantial performance gains on ALFWorld and WebShop and even outperforming the teacher itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL for multi-turn agents provides only a single sparse scalar reward per full trajectory, making learning inefficient. Self on-policy distillation (OPD) tries to alleviate this by providing dense token-level supervision from a stronger self-teacher with privileged information, allowing a weaker student to learn skills more efficiently. However, in complex agentic tasks the teacher is not always reliable even with privileged information, and the usefulness of teacher supervision varies during training. A fixed distillation schedule can therefore be suboptimal or even harmful, creating a need for a method that can capitalize on teacher guidance when it helps and stop relying on it once it no longer provides value.

Method: The authors propose RetireOPD (Self-Retiring On-Policy Distillation). First, they train a decoupled, skill-conditioned teacher agent using environment rewards; this teacher has access to privileged task skills. Then they train a skill-free student jointly with standard RL and OPD from that teacher, providing token-level supervision. Crucially, they introduce Adaptive Retirement: instead of using a hand-crafted schedule for distillation, the student automatically decides when to stop using the teacher. The retirement criterion monitors (1) the discrepancy between student and teacher policies and (2) the student’s success rate relative to the teacher. When the discrepancy no longer decreases and the student reaches a target fraction of the teacher’s success rate, OPD is turned off and training proceeds with pure RL.

Result: Using Qwen2.5 models ranging from 1.5B to 7B parameters, RetireOPD is evaluated on ALFWorld and WebShop, two multi-step agent benchmarks. It improves ALFWorld success rate over an RL-only baseline by 14.1%–18.8% and increases WebShop accuracy by 11.8%–19.0%. Notably, in all evaluated settings the resulting student not only surpasses the RL baseline but also outperforms its own skill-conditioned teacher, indicating effective transfer and subsequent refinement beyond the teacher’s performance.

Conclusion: RetireOPD demonstrates that adaptive, self-retiring on-policy distillation can overcome the limitations of fixed-schedule teacher-student distillation in multi-turn RL agents. By first training a strong, skill-conditioned teacher and then allowing a skill-free student to learn from both environment rewards and teacher signals—and to automatically stop distillation when it ceases to provide benefit—the method yields substantial gains on challenging agentic benchmarks and produces students that exceed their teachers’ performance.

Abstract: Multi-turn agents trained with reinforcement learning (RL) receive a single scalar reward per trajectory, which motivates self on-policy distillation (OPD) to supply dense token-level supervision from a self-teacher with privileged task skills, letting a skill-free student internalize them. This recipe, however, is undermined by two findings in agentic tasks: privileged information alone does not always make a teacher reliable, and the benefit of teacher supervision is stage-dependent. We therefore propose RetireOPD (Self-Retiring On-Policy Distillation), which first optimizes a decoupled, skill-conditioned teacher with environment rewards and then trains a skill-free student jointly with RL and OPD. Rather than following a predefined distillation schedule, RetireOPD adopts Adaptive Retirement: the student drops the teacher on its own once their discrepancy stops shrinking and it reaches a target fraction of the teacher's success rate, after which training proceeds with RL alone. Across Qwen2.5 models from 1.5B to 7B, RetireOPD improves ALFWorld success rate over RL baseline by 14.1% to 18.8% and WebShop accuracy by 11.8% to 19.0%, and surpasses its own skill-conditioned teacher in every setting.

</details>


### [70] [JEPA-Anything: Learning Predictive Models across Different Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20800)
*Taoyong Cui,Zhongyao Wang,Xinyue Xu,Weiyang Liu,Zhaochen Yu,Yuying Zhang,Qiang Gao,Mengyue Yang,Wanli Ouyang,Pheng Ann Heng,Yingcheng Wu,Zhenfei Yin,Ling Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes JEPA-Anything, a general, domain-agnostic world modeling framework based on orthogonal predictive factorization, and shows it works across seven very different domains, improving prediction, intervention modeling, and scientific discovery tasks over strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most predictive or world models are domain-specific and don’t transfer well across very different systems (e.g., images, molecules, weather, clinical data). The authors want a single, principled way to build models that can handle heterogeneous worlds, support accurate prediction over long horizons, model interventions, and still yield interpretable structure useful for scientific discovery.

Method: They extend joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) with orthogonal predictive factorization (OPF). In OPF, the model decomposes latent targets into complementary factors, learns each factor via a dedicated pathway, and then recombines these factors within a shared predictive architecture. They instantiate this “JEPA-Anything” framework and apply the same principle across seven distinct domains, using matched JEPA variants as baselines. Tasks include representation learning, single and multiple intervention prediction, out-of-distribution tests, and long-horizon dynamics forecasting.

Result: Across 7 domains (vision, biology, clinical trajectories, control, molecular dynamics, physical fields, weather), JEPA-Anything consistently beats matched JEPA baselines. It improves metrics on all 10 matched dynamics tasks, cuts single-intervention prediction error on Interventional Pong by 34.8%, and attains the best one-step and 100-step molecular prediction errors in 4 molecular systems. It forecasts over 1,000 clinical events, produces 100-step molecular rollouts, and shows strong long-horizon performance. The factorized representation is also interpretable enough to nominate a biological intervention that is validated experimentally, and to recover a Keplerian orbital scaling exponent of about -1.5 from latent modes.

Conclusion: A single factorized predictive principle—orthogonal predictive factorization inside a JEPA-style architecture—can support world modeling across highly heterogeneous domains. This common framework not only improves predictive performance and robustness (including interventions and long horizons) over strong baselines, but also yields interpretable factors that connect modeling to experimentally validated interventions and scientific laws. The work suggests that domain-agnostic, factorized predictive learning can unify world modeling, control, and scientific discovery.

Abstract: World modeling enables intelligence to anticipate consequences, guide interventions, and learn from interaction. Yet predictive models remain domain-specific: can a common learning principle support world modeling across radically different systems? We introduce JEPA-Anything, a domain-agnostic framework based on orthogonal predictive factorization (OPF). Extending joint-embedding predictive architectures, OPF decomposes latent targets into complementary factors, learns them through dedicated pathways, and recombines them within a shared predictive design. We evaluate JEPA-Anything across seven domains: vision, biology, clinical trajectories, control, molecular dynamics, physical fields, and weather. Experiments span representation learning, intervention prediction, out-of-distribution generalization, and long-horizon dynamics, including 10 matched dynamics tasks, forecasting of over 1,000 clinical events, and 100-step molecular rollouts across four systems. Against matched JEPA baselines, JEPA-Anything improves reported metrics on all 10 dynamics tasks and reduces single-intervention prediction error on Interventional Pong by 34.8%. It achieves the lowest one-step and 100-step molecular errors among compared methods in all four systems. Beyond prediction, a factor-nominated biological intervention receives experimental support in cell co-cultures, patient-derived organoids, tumor fragments, and mice; latent orbital modes recover the Keplerian scaling exponent with a fitted slope of -1.4991. These results support a common factorized predictive principle across heterogeneous worlds, connecting world modeling with intervention and experimentally grounded scientific discovery. Code: https://github.com/Gen-Verse/JEPA-Anything

</details>


### [71] [Unifying Models of Intergroup Hostility in Online Discourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20808)
*Patrick Gerard,Julia Mendelsohn,Kristina Lerman*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper builds a unified, data-driven map of how different theorized mechanisms of intergroup hostility show up and unfold over time in real online discourse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theories of how hostility toward social groups forms—across social, moral, and political psychology—are fragmented, partially conflicting, and have rarely been tested against each other on large-scale real-world discourse. This fragmentation limits our ability to design effective moderation and interventions, because we lack a clear, integrated view of how different rhetorical mechanisms of hostility co-occur and evolve in practice.

Method: The authors collect 2.86 million social media posts from TikTok, Truth Social, and Twitter/X around the 2024 U.S. presidential election. They then operationalize six foundational theoretical mechanisms of intergroup hostility—boundary construction, threat construction, scapegoating, negative evaluation, dehumanization, and action orientation—and model them within a unified empirical/computational framework. This framework allows them to estimate how these mechanisms are organized structurally and how they unfold temporally in real discourse, rather than treating each as a separate single-label classification task.

Result: Structurally, boundary construction and threat construction emerge as central anchors in the system of hostile rhetoric. Temporally, the mechanisms show a consistent ordering pattern in discourse: boundary construction, derogation (negative evaluation), and action orientation tend to appear earlier; dehumanization and threat construction appear later; and scapegoating tends to appear last. This reveals a typical progression from defining group boundaries and expressing early hostility to escalating into more severe and targeted forms of rhetoric.

Conclusion: By jointly modeling multiple classic theories of intergroup hostility in large-scale online speech, the paper demonstrates that these mechanisms are systematically related and follow regular temporal patterns. This integrated, empirically grounded map helps reconcile divisions across social science traditions and provides computational social science with a richer foundation for modeling hostility rhetoric as a multi-stage, multi-mechanism process rather than a simple single-label detection problem.

Abstract: Hostile rhetoric toward social groups can normalize exclusion and justify mistreatment, as well as contribute to rising polarization and political violence. Efforts to moderate hostile rhetoric in online speech draw on foundational theories in social and moral psychology, and political science. However, these theories were developed largely in parallel, often propose different and sometimes conflicting accounts of how hostility develops, and have rarely been tested against each other in real discourse. The result is a fragmented understanding of the rhetorical mechanisms of hostility, without a clear sense of how they appear, and relate to each other, in real-world discourse. Using 2.86 million posts from TikTok, Truth Social, and Twitter/X during the 2024 U.S. presidential election, we model the mechanisms of six foundational theories of intergroup hostility -- boundary construction, threat construction, scapegoating, negative evaluation, dehumanization, and action orientation -- within a common empirical framework to recover the broader organization of intergroup hostility rhetoric. Structurally, we find that boundary construction and threat construction anchor the system; temporally, we find that these mechanisms tend to follow a regular ordering: boundary construction, derogation, and action orientation tend to appear early; dehumanization and threat construction later; scapegoating latest. Mapping how these theoretical frameworks actually manifest in discourse bridges longstanding divisions across social science traditions and presents computational social science with a clearer empirical foundation for modeling intergroup hostility rhetoric beyond single-label detection.

</details>


### [72] [Embedding Models Measure in Peculiar Ways](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20821)
*Juri Opitz,Andrianos Michail*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how well embedding spaces capture true physical measurements (mass, distance, time, volume) and finds only weak alignment, with representations dominated by superficial string similarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether widely-used embedding spaces, which define semantic similarity and distance between tokens, actually encode objective, quantitatively defined concepts like physical measurements. Because physical quantities have clear, ground-truth equivalence and distance relations, they are a good probe of how semantically meaningful embedding distances really are.

Method: The authors examine embeddings of tokens corresponding to physical quantities (e.g., different units and magnitudes of mass, distance, time, volume), analyze their geometric relations, and compare embedding-space similarities to true physical relationships. They further investigate patterns that arise, such as clustering driven by spelling or morphology, and attempt to recalibrate similarity metrics to see if alignment with physical reality can be improved.

Result: They observe that embedding similarity correlates only weakly with actual physical measurement relationships. Instead, embeddings exhibit unusual and unintuitive patterns that do not match physical equivalence or proportionality. The structure of embedding space appears to be strongly influenced by superficial string similarity and form rather than by underlying physical meaning. Attempts to improve correspondence via recalibrating similarity measures yield limited gains.

Conclusion: Standard embedding spaces do not naturally encode accurate quantitative relationships for physical measurements; semantic distance in these spaces is shaped more by surface-level textual features than by objective physical meaning. This suggests inherent limitations of current embedding models as faithful representations of precise, measurable semantics, and indicates that additional modeling or structure is needed to capture such quantitative relationships.

Abstract: Embedding spaces define notions of semantic similarity and distance. We study whether those embeddings reflect physical measurements of mass, distance, time and volume, which admit a unique, objective notion of semantic equivalence and distance. We find that physical measurement is only weakly modeled in the embedding space, and that instead quite peculiar measurement patterns can be observed. Further analysis indicates that embedding representations of physical measurements are strongly influenced by superficial string similarity, and recalibration of similarity does not substantially improve the alignment.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [73] [Regularized Emphatic Temporal-Difference Learning: Stability under Constant Stepsizes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19170)
*Xingguo Chen,Zhaohui Wu,Jinguo Ye,Chao Li,Shangdong Yang,Guang Yang,Skylar Liang,Wenhao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes limitations of emphatic temporal-difference (ETD) learning in off-policy reinforcement learning, constructs a counterexample where ETD is unstable despite a contracting mean map, and proposes a modified algorithm, Regularized ETD (RETD), that ensures better stability and convergence while preserving the ETD fixed point.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ETD is designed to stabilize off-policy TD learning and alter its projection geometry, but its theoretical stability under constant stepsizes and sampled (as opposed to expected) dynamics is not fully understood. The authors are motivated by practical and theoretical observations that ETD can still behave unstably, prompting a need to characterize this instability, separate causes such as infinite variance from other mechanisms, and design an algorithm that retains ETD's benefits while achieving more reliable convergence properties.

Method: The authors construct a minimal ergodic two-state Markov chain counterexample to show a discrepancy between the ETD mean map (which is contracting) and the sampled dynamics (which have a positive top Lyapunov exponent, indicating instability). They use regenerative-cycle analysis to decouple the sign of this exponent from the infinite variance of the follow-on trace. They then propose Regularized Emphatic TD (RETD), which modifies ETD using a normalized first-order post-shock repair mechanism that keeps the trace and importance ratios intact, stores the emphatic signal in a leaky scalar state, and applies a delayed correction. They analyze RETD theoretically, proving almost-sure convergence under diminishing stepsizes and providing a moment-contraction bound under constant stepsizes using Markovian random-product techniques. They also perform numerical experiments including variants of Baird’s counterexample and large-scale multi-run simulations to validate their theory.

Result: The paper shows that ETD can be unstable even when its expected update map is a contraction, as demonstrated by a positive Lyapunov exponent in the constructed two-state example. They demonstrate, both theoretically and empirically, that this instability is not solely due to the infinite variance of the follow-on trace. RETD is shown to preserve the ETD equilibrium (fixed point) exactly via one- or two-regularization readouts, and its raw equilibrium is an affine shift of the ETD equilibrium. They prove almost-sure convergence for RETD under harmonic diminishing stepsizes and establish a conditional moment-contraction result for constant stepsizes. RETD achieves certified negative Lyapunov exponents on the two-state example and on one Baird instance, whereas ETD exhibits positive or numerically unstable exponents. Experiments with 10,000-run ensembles confirm the theoretical separations, stability properties, fixed-point recovery, nonmonotone stability regions, and dependence on the task structure.

Conclusion: ETD's good behavior in terms of its mean map and projection geometry does not guarantee stability or convergence under constant-stepsize sampled dynamics, and its instability cannot be fully attributed to the infinite variance of the follow-on trace. The proposed RETD algorithm modifies the post-shock dynamics while leaving the follow-on trace variance and importance sampling structure unchanged, thereby retaining ETD’s fixed point but achieving better stability guarantees. RETD is theoretically sound under both diminishing and certain constant stepsizes, and empirical results support its improved stability and fixed-point recovery, though it does not address the high variance of the follow-on trace itself.

Abstract: Emphatic temporal-difference learning (ETD) stabilizes the expected off-policy TD update and changes its projection geometry, but neither property determines constant-stepsize sampled dynamics. We construct an ergodic two-state counterexample in which the ETD mean map contracts while the sampled product has a positive top Lyapunov exponent. Regenerative-cycle analysis separates this sign from the infinite variance of the follow-on trace. We introduce regularized emphatic TD (RETD), a normalized first-order post-shock repair that leaves the trace and importance ratios unchanged, stores the emphatic TD signal in a leaky scalar state, and releases a delayed correction. RETD's raw equilibrium is an affine shift of the ETD equilibrium; single- and two-regularization readouts recover the ETD fixed point exactly. We prove almost-sure convergence for harmonic diminishing stepsizes and a conditional constant-stepsize moment-contraction result from a Markovian random-product bound. RETD has certified negative exponents on the two-state construction and one Baird point, whereas the positive Baird ETD sign remains numerical. Paired 10,000-run experiments validate both separations, fixed-point recovery, a nonmonotone stability region, and task dependence. RETD changes post-shock dynamics; it does not reduce the shared follow-on-trace variance.

</details>


### [74] [BioPhys-Bridge: A Benchmark for Interdisciplinary Scientific Reasoning in Physics-Grounded Biological Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19180)
*Qingyang Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BioPhys-Bridge is a new benchmark for testing how well language models perform evidence-grounded, quantitative scientific reasoning in biophysics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interdisciplinary biophysics problems require models to connect textual evidence, quantitative physics, and biological mechanisms, which current LMs struggle with. Existing benchmarks don’t adequately test evidence-grounded, multi-step reasoning with quantitative and mechanistic grounding over real literature.

Method: The authors build BioPhys-Bridge, a curated dataset of biophysics cases, each including structured evidence blocks with stable IDs, quantitative values and units, equations, assumptions, mechanisms, and follow-up decisions. They define QA and RAG-style tasks, apply strict quality checks (schema, evidence integrity, quantitative grounding, licensing, deduplication, unit normalization), and obtain expert annotations for a subset of cases. They then benchmark several frontier models on evidence attribution (evidence-ID F1).

Result: The initial dataset contains 500 cases and 1,517 tasks, spanning six biological domains and nine physical model families (with some reserved for future expansion). In preliminary evaluations, DeepSeek-V4-Flash achieves the best evidence-ID F1 (0.360), followed by Qwen3.7-Max (0.316) and GPT-4o-mini (0.294), indicating substantial headroom for improvement.

Conclusion: BioPhys-Bridge provides a challenging, interdisciplinary benchmark for assessing attribution, faithfulness, hallucination reduction, and experimental design in complex biophysical reasoning. Early results show models are far from perfect, and the authors plan to expand the dataset and conduct broader evaluations. Code and data are publicly released to support community use and extension.

Abstract: Language models face unique challenges in analyzing interdisciplinary scientific research literature. In biophysics research, faithful answers require grounding observed data in source evidence, interpreting it through a quantitative physics model, and linking it to a biological mechanism. To address this challenge, we introduce BioPhys-Bridge, a novel benchmark dataset for evidence-grounded scientific reasoning over biophysical literature. Each case contains evidence blocks, stable evidence IDs, quantitative values, units, equations, assumptions, mechanisms, and next decisions as grounding targets for question answering (QA) and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). The initial release contains 500 cases, 1,517 agent-facing tasks, and covers six biological domains and nine physical model families, including three sparse families reserved for future expansion. We enforce strict quality gates for all cases in schema, evidence-integrity, quantitative-grounding, source-license, duplicate, unit-normalization, with domain expert review and annotation for 81 cases. Preliminary evaluations show that DeepSeek-V4-Flash obtain the highest evidence-ID $F_1$ score (0.360), followed by Qwen3.7-Max (0.316) and GPT-4o-mini (0.294). BioPhys-Bridge is an interdisciplinary benchmark for evaluating attribution, faithfulness, hallucination reduction, and biological experiment design with complex, multi-step scientific reasoning. Future works will increase the size and complexity of the dataset and perform comprehensive evaluations. Code and data are available in the GitHub repository and on Hugging Face.

</details>


### [75] [What Do We Expect from LLMs? Mapping the Design of LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19182)
*Chao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how benchmarks for large language models (LLMs) have evolved from 2022–2026, focusing on what researchers expect models to do and how they define success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmark leaderboards show which models perform best, but they do not reveal how evaluation practices and expectations themselves are changing. With many new benchmarks emerging, there is a need to understand what kinds of abilities, tasks, and application settings the research community is increasingly prioritizing, and how this shapes the narrative of LLM progress and capabilities.

Method: The authors construct a dataset of 14,767 arXiv papers (2022–2026) that introduce or update evaluation resources. They use staged screening to identify relevant papers, and apply automated full-text coding to extract information on target systems and domains, evaluation materials and conditions, and scoring mechanisms. They then analyze temporal trends and patterns across these dimensions, including how often and in what ways models themselves are involved in the evaluation process (e.g., as judges or as generators of test materials).

Result: The study finds a growing emphasis on benchmarks that test action, interaction, and professional applications rather than only static reasoning or text completion. Traditional and newer benchmark design features often coexist instead of one replacing the other. Participation of LLMs in evaluation is uneven: LLM-based scoring is increasingly common across both agentic and non-agentic settings, but there is no sustained growth in the use of model-generated materials as part of benchmarks. Overall, benchmarks are shifting toward more applied and interactive scenarios, with AI systems more often helping to judge performance than to generate test content.

Conclusion: Public research benchmarks are progressively translating higher expectations for LLM capabilities—especially around action, interaction, and professional tasks—into concrete tests and success criteria. However, as AI systems themselves become more involved in evaluating other AIs (serving as judges and, to a lesser extent, as content generators), there is a tension: expanded evaluation may not automatically yield more independent evidence, and could instead reinforce the preferences and blind spots of the models participating in the evaluation loop.

Abstract: Benchmarks are central to how progress in large language models (LLMs) is assessed and communicated. Yet model rankings alone reveal little about how evaluation requirements themselves are changing. The expanding variety of benchmarks offers another perspective: what researchers expect LLMs to do, and what they count as successful performance. We systematically map 14,767 papers introducing or updating evaluation resources from arXiv submissions between January 2022 and August 2026. Using staged screening and automated full-text coding, we examine changes in target systems and domains, evaluation materials and conditions, and scoring mechanisms. The collection shows growing emphasis on action, interaction, and professional applications, while established and newer design elements frequently coexist. Model participation also develops unevenly: LLM-based scoring grows within both agent and non-agent groups, whereas model-generated materials show no comparable sustained increase in recent cohorts. These findings illuminate how public research translates capability expectations into concrete tests and criteria for success. As AI participates in constructing tests, performing tasks, and judging responses, they also raise a question: does expanding evaluation provide more independent evidence, or risk reproducing the preferences and blind spots of its participating models?

</details>


### [76] [Position: It is Time to Virtualize Foundation Models with a Self-evolving Operating System Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19203)
*Suparna Bhattacharya,Tarun Kumar,Cong Xu,Satish Kumar Mopur,Jiahao Li,Ashish Mishra,Aalap Tripathy,Annmary Justine Koomthanam,Martin Foltin,Ian Foster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS) to unify and virtualize how applications interact with foundation models and agentic systems, analogous to how operating systems virtualize hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI application stacks are fragmented: each agent or framework re-implements its own runtime for state, memory, budgets, and guardrails, leading to non-portable behavior and fragile governance. This is similar to early computing before operating systems, where each program managed low-level resources itself. The authors argue this fragmentation blocks scalability, reliability, and governance of complex agentic AI systems.

Method: This is a position/vision paper rather than an empirical study. The authors conceptually define a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS) as a system layer that virtualizes and manages FM interactions. They describe key responsibilities of FMOS: orchestrating knowledge across memory tiers, handling model selection and resource allocation, and managing verification and policy enforcement. They also introduce an analogy with the human brain to frame how FMOS should decide when to intervene versus letting inference run directly.

Result: As a conceptual paper, there are no experimental results. The main outcome is an architectural proposal for FMOS and an argument that such a layer can provide applications with the illusion of dedicated, trustworthy FM instances with effectively unbounded capabilities while centralizing control, governance, and optimization.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the evolution from single models to compound agentic systems makes an operating-system-like layer inevitable. A Foundation Model Operating System can standardize runtimes, improve portability and governance, and dynamically manage memory, resources, and policies—much like how OSes enabled the modern software ecosystem. The FMOS should be adaptive, learning from operational data when to route, constrain, or augment model inferences.

Abstract: AI applications have shifted from single, monolithic foundation models (FM) to compound agentic systems. Yet today's stacks remain fragmented: even as protocols (e.g., MCP, A2A) ease tool/agent connectivity, each framework embeds an implicit runtime for state, memory, budgets, and guardrails, making behavior non-portable and governance brittle. It mirrors computing before operating systems, when every program re-implemented basic services.
