This position paper argues that the field now needs a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS) -- a system layer that virtualizes FM interactions analogous to how virtual machines abstract physical hardware, giving applications the illusion of dedicated, trustworthy FM instances with effectively unbounded capabilities. Internally, the FMOS orchestrates knowledge across memory tiers, model selection and resource allocation, and verification and policy enforcement. Like the human brain switching between fast intuition and slow deliberation, the FMOS learns when to intervene and when to let inference proceed directly and continuously adapting its policies based on operational experience.

</details>


### [77] [What Do Current Systematic Generalization Tasks Miss? A Reasoning-Centered Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19212)
*Chengwen Qi,Deheng Ye,Yatao Bian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose TranSGrid, a testbed that jointly stresses deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning to more rigorously measure systematic generalization in Transformers, showing large performance drops compared to standard held-out tests and revealing that common simplifying assumptions make the task much easier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Systematic generalization—recombining known atomic elements to solve novel problems—is a hallmark of human intelligence but is hard to evaluate cleanly in machine learning. Existing benchmarks simplify the problem (e.g., assuming near-linear action composition, using productivity-based tests, or making goals action-explicit), which may hide crucial aspects of genuine systematic generalization. The authors want to understand what these simplifications leave out and how to design a benchmark that better reflects the multi-faceted reasoning humans use.

Method: They design TranSGrid, a unified task that requires three kinds of reasoning simultaneously: deductive (reasoning from rules to consequences), inductive (inferring rules or patterns from examples), and abductive (inferring the most plausible actions or causes given goals or observations). They then evaluate seven Transformer models on 4,800 TranSGrid instances and compare their performance to a standard held-out test set. To isolate the impact of common simplifications, they create two TranSGrid variants: one with almost linearly composable actions (lower inductive demand) and one with action-explicit goals (lower abductive demand), and re-measure performance.

Result: All seven Transformer models perform substantially worse on TranSGrid than on a conventional held-out test set. The best (largest) model achieves 79.6% accuracy on the held-out test set but only 55.3% on TranSGrid overall, and a mere 15.8% on the most challenging subset. This performance gap persists even when evaluation stays within the training sequence length, indicating that productivity-based generalization alone does not explain the difficulty. When either simplification is added back—making actions almost linearly composable or making goals action-explicit—model solve rates climb back near held-out test performance, showing each simplification alone is enough to make the task much easier and more like ordinary generalization.

Conclusion: The study concludes that many existing systematic generalization benchmarks are easier because they inadvertently remove or weaken inductive and/or abductive reasoning demands, often focusing mainly on productivity. TranSGrid demonstrates that when deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning are all required together, current Transformer models struggle much more, even without extrapolating beyond training lengths. Therefore, a comprehensive assessment of systematic generalization must involve tasks that jointly tax all three forms of reasoning, and TranSGrid is proposed as such a benchmark.

Abstract: Systematic generalization, the ability to solve novel problems by recombining known atomic elements, is central to human intelligence but difficult to study rigorously under controlled settings. Existing studies therefore rely on simplifications such as approximately linear action composition, productivity-based tests, and action-explicit goals, which make systematic generalization easier to study but omit some essential aspects of this capability. To characterize what these simplifications miss, we adopt a reasoning-centered lens and introduce TranSGrid, a testbed that brings deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning together within a unified task. Experiments with seven Transformers on 4,800 TranSGrid instances show that all models perform much worse on TranSGrid than on a held-out test set: the largest model solves 79.6% of the test set, but only 55.3% of TranSGrid and 15.8% of the hardest subset. The gap remains within the training length range, showing that productivity alone is not sufficient to evaluate systematic generalization. Additionally, we reintroduce the other two simplifications into TranSGrid: one variant makes actions compose almost linearly (reducing the inductive demand), the other makes goals action-explicit (reducing the abductive one). In both, solve rates return to roughly the test set level, showing that either simplification alone is enough to reduce TranSGrid to an ordinary held-out test set. Together, our results show that existing tasks reduce either or both of the inductive and abductive demands, and that comprehensively measuring systematic generalization requires a task that involves all three forms of reasoning.

</details>


### [78] [Characterizing Web Search by Conversational LLM Agents: From Search Decisions and Strategies to Results and Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19244)
*Mahsa Amani,Seungeon Lee,Abhisek Dash,Asmaa El Fraihi,Yunah Jang,Elisabeth Kirsten,Qinyuan Wu,Krishna P. Gummadi,Manish Gupta,Abhilasha Ravichander,Muhammad Bilal Zafar,Soumi Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how major conversational LLM agents use Web search end-to-end and uncovers substantial differences in when and how they search, what results they see, and how they ground answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational LLM agents frequently rely on Web search, but the full lifecycle of this "agentic search"—from deciding to search, to query formulation, results received, and grounding in final answers—is not well understood or systematically compared across platforms. Understanding this is key to improving reliability, attribution, and design of future AI agents and search tools.

Method: The authors conduct the first cross-platform study of Web search in conversational LLMs across four major systems: ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, and DeepSeek. They combine (1) in-vivo analysis of real user interactions on these platforms and (2) in-vitro controlled experiments using the same models via their APIs. They examine when agents decide to invoke Web search, how they formulate queries, what domains their search engines favor, and how results are incorporated into final responses.

Result: They find that: (a) Web-search invocation behavior differs widely between platforms and models; (b) more frequent use of Web search does not automatically improve answer quality; (c) agents employ distinct and often complex querying strategies; (d) underlying search engines show platform-specific preferences for certain domains in the results; and (e) although answers are mostly grounded in retrieved documents, some statements depend on uncited search results, which weakens attribution and reliability.

Conclusion: Agentic Web search in conversational LLMs is highly platform-dependent across the full lifecycle (invocation, querying, retrieval, and grounding). Simply searching more does not guarantee better answers, and current systems show domain biases and incomplete citation of sources. These findings point to the need for better-designed search invocation policies, more transparent and balanced retrieval, and stricter grounding and attribution mechanisms in future conversational AI and Web search integrations.

Abstract: Conversational LLM agents increasingly rely on Web search, yet the end-to-end lifecycle of agentic search remains poorly understood. We present the first study of Web search across four major conversational platforms (ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, and DeepSeek), combining real-world user interactions (invivo) with controlled experiments using the same platform's models by their APIs (invitro). We investigate the quality of agentic decisions to invoke Web search, their strategies to formulate queries, the potential domain preferences in the search results they receive, and the choices they make when transforming search results into grounded responses. We find that Web-search decisions vary substantially across platforms and models, while more frequent Web-search invocation does not necessarily yield better response quality. We further show that conversational agents employ different complex querying strategies and that platform specific search engines return search results from their preferred domains. Finally, although responses are largely grounded in search results, some claims rely on uncited search results, raising concerns about attribution and reliability. Our findings have important implications for the design of future AI agents and Web search tools optimized for conversational retrieval.

</details>


### [79] [Do AI Agents Understand Computer Architecture?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19387)
*Ambika Sharan,Grigory Chirkov,Soheil Abbasloo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AutoTuring, a framework to measure how much explicit architectural meaning helps agents design hardware accelerators, by comparing performance when design variables are meaningful vs anonymous.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many recent works claim that agents can successfully design hardware accelerators, but such results only show that designs improved, not whether the agents actually understood and reasoned about architecture. Distinguishing genuine architectural reasoning from blind but competent search is crucial, because only the former is likely to generalize to new architectures. Existing evaluations vary the agents while keeping the problem framing fixed, which cannot separate these effects.

Method: The authors propose AutoTuring, which gives the same agent access to the same 15-dimensional accelerator design space in two conditions: (1) as named, semantically meaningful architectural knobs, with simulator counters; and (2) as anonymous variables scaled to [0,1] without semantic meaning. The evaluator, legal design space, and reachable optima are held identical between conditions. The performance gap between the architect (meaningful) and blind (anonymous) settings quantifies the value of architectural meaning. They also introduce a critic loop that provides structured critique to the blind agent.

Result: On a benchmark of nine FP16 GEMM kernels, the architect agent (with meaning) outperforms a modeled H200 accelerator by 5.4% and beats its blind counterpart by 12.3% on average, while using 70.1% fewer simulator calls. However, adding a critic loop to the blind agent recovers most of this performance gap, and the critic adds little benefit to the architect agent.

Conclusion: Architectural meaning significantly improves search efficiency and design quality in this setting, but its benefits can largely be substituted by structured critique for agents without explicit architectural knowledge. The main contribution is the comparative methodology (AutoTuring) for isolating the role of meaning in accelerator design, and the findings are preliminary, being based on limited runs and a single modeled accelerator.

Abstract: Agents are increasingly asked to design hardware, and increasingly reported to succeed. Such reports establish that a design improved; they cannot establish why. An agent that improves an accelerator may be reasoning about the machine, or may be searching competently over knobs whose meaning it never recovers -- and only the first transfers to the next architecture. Existing evaluations cannot tell the two apart, because they vary the agent while holding the framing of the problem fixed. We do the opposite. AutoTuring hands the same agent the same 15-dimensional accelerator space twice: once as named architectural knobs with simulator counters, once as anonymous variables on [0,1], with the evaluator, the legal space and the reachable optima held identical, so that the only thing that varies is whether the problem means anything. The gap between the two is the measurement. On a nine-kernel FP16 GEMM basket, meaning pays: the architect beats a modeled H200 by 5.4% and its blind counterpart by 12.3% on average, with 70.1% fewer simulator calls. It does not pay uniquely: a critic loop recovers most of that gap for the blind agent and buys the architect nothing, so architectural knowledge and structured critique behave as substitutes rather than as complements. We report these as preliminary findings -- five to six runs per condition on a single modeled accelerator -- and take the comparison itself, not the accelerator, to be the contribution.

</details>


### [80] [MAGS: Multi-agent Auto-formalization Guarantees Safety for Agentic Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19391)
*Albert Wu,Nicholas Roberts,Tzu-Heng Huang,Haoran Lin,Gil Friedman,Sungjun Cho,Gabriel Orlanski,Frederic Sala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents MAGS, a multi-agent framework that uses formal verification (via Dafny) to automatically generate executable programs with machine-checkable safety guarantees, achieving 100% verified success on 220 diverse tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM coding agents can now generate large, complex programs, but humans cannot feasibly review all outputs, leaving safety and security vulnerabilities. Existing automatic methods (fuzzing, static analysis, LLM-as-a-Verifier) improve reliability but cannot systematically cover all edge cases. Formal verification can provide stronger, machine-checkable guarantees but typically requires heavy manual specification and proof engineering, which does not scale. The paper aims to bridge this gap by making formal verification practical and automated within an LLM programming pipeline.

Method: The authors design MAGS, a unified multi-agent system that integrates LLM code generation with formal verification using Dafny as a verification-aware intermediate language. The framework: (1) formalizes and "freezes" human-audited APIs and safety requirements into specifications; (2) translates LLM-generated code into Dafny; (3) runs the Dafny verifier to mechanically check safety properties; (4) uses verifier feedback to iteratively repair code and/or specifications; and (5) compiles the verified Dafny back into executable target code (e.g., CUDA kernels, shell scripts, robot controllers). Multi-agent coordination orchestrates these steps and manages transformation, verification, and repair loops.

Result: On a benchmark of 220 tasks (100 CUDA kernels, 100 terminal scripts, 20 robotic-arm tasks), MAGS successfully produces executable programs that satisfy non-trivial, formally specified safety properties in 100% of cases, with respect to the frozen specifications. Independent safety and functional evaluations across the three domains show that the generated programs are both safe (under the given specs) and largely functionally correct. The evaluation also highlights cases where the formalized semantics are incomplete, and MAGS passes verification while still missing aspects of the intended behavior.

Conclusion: MAGS demonstrates that integrating LLM-based program synthesis with automated formal verification via an intermediate language like Dafny can reliably produce programs with strong, machine-checkable safety guarantees across diverse domains. The approach significantly reduces the manual burden traditionally associated with formal methods while still achieving full verification against given specifications. However, the overall safety depends critically on the quality and completeness of the frozen specifications and semantics; gaps there can lead to verified yet behaviorally deficient programs, pointing to future work on richer, more accurate auto-formalization and specification tools.

Abstract: LLM coding agents now generate complex programs at a scale that makes thorough human review increasingly difficult, raising the risk of safety and security failures. Common approaches, including fuzz testing, static analysis, and LLM-as-a-Verifier, can detect many failures but struggle to cover all possible edge cases. Formal verification addresses this by providing machine-checkable guarantees over specified properties, but traditionally demands substantial manual specification and proof engineering. We introduce a unified multi-agent framework, MAGS, that generates executable programs with formal safety guarantees, using Dafny as a verification-aware intermediate representation where safety properties can be mechanically checked. MAGS formalizes and freezes human-audited APIs and safety requirements, translates generated code into Dafny, repairs violations using verifier feedback, and compiles verified programs back into executable code. We evaluate MAGS on 100 CUDA kernels, 100 terminal scripts, and 20 robotic-arm tasks. Across all 220 examples, it achieves a 100% success rate in producing programs with non-trivial safety guarantees against frozen specifications. Independent safety and functional evaluations further show strong performance across all three domains, while revealing failures when the auto-formalized semantics do not fully capture the target behavior.

</details>


### [81] [Closed-World Resolution Against Tool Hallucination in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19425)
*Laxmipriya Ganesh Iyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies and systematizes a neglected failure mode of tool-using LLM agents—hallucinated tool calls that reference non-existent tools or invalid schemas—and provides taxonomies, theory, measurements, and a benchmark to study and compare defenses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety and reliability work on tool-using LLM agents mostly focuses on choosing the correct tool among known options and constraining how real tools are used. These approaches implicitly assume the model only calls tools that actually exist in the registry and respect declared schemas. However, in practice LLMs often fabricate tool names or produce argument structures that do not match any schema, and existing gating/selection mechanisms do not even see these hallucinated calls as decisions they can regulate. This structural blind spot can cause robustness and safety failures, especially as systems evolve toward richer multi-server, multi-namespace protocols such as MCP. The paper is motivated by the need to formally characterize this failure mode, prove limits of current defenses, and provide measurements and benchmarks to drive better solutions.

Method: The authors conduct a measurement and benchmarking study. They first define a five-class taxonomy of tool hallucinations (H1–H5) covering different ways in which LLM agents fabricate tools or misuse schemas. They introduce the “Resolution Rung,” a training-free, closed-world resolver that checks whether a tool is in a registry and whether the call’s signature matches the tool’s schema; its role is to serve as a reference point rather than an optimal defense. Using this framework, they theoretically prove that any effective hallucination defense must operate before causal gating mechanisms, and they formally characterize a minimal irreducible class of hallucinations where arguments are borrowed in a way that is schema-indistinguishable from a valid call. Empirically, they evaluate ten hosted LLMs across two invocation surfaces—one being an unconstrained raw-JSON interface—and record genuine hallucinations. They then extend the analysis to the Model Context Protocol (MCP), where tools from several servers are merged into a unified namespace, and define a second taxonomy (M1–M5) for hallucinations specific to this multi-registry setting. They test live MCP deployments, measuring hallucinations due to namespace collisions and shadowing. Finally, they package tasks and data into a versioned Hallucinated-Tools Benchmark (HTB) to enable standardized comparison of future resolvers.

Result: The study documents 322 genuine hallucinations across ten hosted models on two invocation surfaces. It finds that calls to completely fabricated tools are heavily concentrated on the unconstrained raw-JSON surface (34 fabricated-tool calls there versus 3 on the more constrained surface), showing that looser interfaces exacerbate hallucination. It also observes that model size does not meaningfully reduce tool hallucination: a 675B-parameter model performs similarly to much smaller 7–8B models in this respect. Theoretical results show that hallucination defenses must act prior to any causal gating and that a residue of hallucinations remains in the form of schema-indistinguishable borrowed-argument calls. In the MCP setting, the authors identify structural causes of hallucination stemming from merging multiple tool registries into one namespace, including collisions and shadowing that no single-registry resolver can fully capture. On a live MCP surface they measure 154 hallucinations, including from advanced frontier models that did not hallucinate under the single-registry environment, demonstrating that multi-server merging introduces new hallucination opportunities. They release the Hallucinated-Tools Benchmark (HTB) as a standardized, versioned benchmark for evaluating hallucination resolvers.

Conclusion: Tool-using LLM agents suffer from a distinct class of failures—hallucinated tools and invalid schemas—that existing tool-selection and tool-gating approaches are structurally incapable of addressing, because these calls fall outside the decision boundary of any causal gate. Effective defenses must therefore begin with a resolution layer that validates the existence and signatures of tools before any downstream gating logic, yet even the best such resolver will face an irreducible residue where hallucinated calls are schema-indistinguishable from valid ones. The multi-registry, merged-namespace context of protocols like MCP further amplifies hallucination risk via collisions and shadowing that a single-registry model cannot express away. By providing taxonomies (H1–H5, M1–M5), theoretical limits, empirical measurements across multiple models and interfaces, and the Hallucinated-Tools Benchmark (HTB), the paper positions tool hallucination as a first-class research problem and offers a common framework for evaluating future mitigation techniques.

Abstract: Tool-augmented large language model (LLM) agents fail in a way no tool-selection or tool-security method addresses: they call tools that do not exist and pass arguments no schema declares. Existing defenses either pick the right tool (selection) or constrain what an agent may do with real tools (gating), both of which presuppose the emitted call refers to a real tool at all. We show this is a structural blind spot: a hallucinated call is by construction not a decision any gate made, so no gate can reject it. This paper is primarily a measurement and benchmark study. We give a five-class taxonomy of tool hallucination (H1-H5) and, as a reference point, the Resolution Rung: a training-free, closed-world resolver (registry membership plus a signature check) whose interest is where it must sit, not what it computes. We prove hallucination defense must precede any causal gate, and characterize the one irreducible residue (borrowed arguments schema-indistinguishable from a valid call). Across ten hosted models under two invocation surfaces we measure 322 genuine hallucinations; fabricated-tool calls concentrate on the unconstrained raw-JSON surface (34 vs. 3), and model scale does not help (a 675B model matches a 7-8B one). We then extend to the Model Context Protocol, where merging several servers into one namespace creates hallucination surfaces a single registry cannot express (a second taxonomy, M1-M5); on the live MCP surface we measure 154 hallucinations, including from frontier models that were clean on the single-registry surface, because collisions and shadowing are structural to the merge. We release the versioned Hallucinated-Tools Benchmark (HTB) so any resolver is comparable across submissions.

</details>


### [82] [The syntax and semantics of goals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19448)
*David M. Abel,Mark K. Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper examines goals as compositional cognitive representations, drawing parallels to the syntax-semantics interface, and maps a design space of different goal representation formats and their properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While goals are central in both cognitive science and AI, they are usually treated as fixed constraints on behavior rather than as representations with structure and content. This leaves open foundational questions: how expressive different goal formalisms are, how they relate to each other, and what assumptions they embed about rational behavior and motivation. By focusing on goals as representational states, the authors aim to connect disparate literatures (e.g., linguistics, logic, planning, decision-making) and to surface implicit constraints on how we model goals in humans and machines.

Method: The paper is primarily conceptual and synthetic. The authors survey and organize existing work on goal representations from cognitive science, AI, and related fields. They analyze these approaches through the lens of representation: syntax (form), semantics (meaning), and their interface, considering issues like compositionality, expressivity, equivalence between representations, and efficiency. They then propose a broader design-space perspective where different goal formalisms occupy points along multiple theoretical axes.

Result: The authors identify key properties and constraints that differentiate goal representation formalisms—such as their expressive power, how they capture compositional structure, and the behavioral equivalences they induce. They show that what are often treated as practical or implementation choices in AI (e.g., particular goal languages or planning formalisms) actually correspond to substantive theoretical commitments about the nature of goals and rational behavior. They also clarify relationships and parallels between goal representations and the syntax–semantics interface in language and logic.

Conclusion: Viewing goals explicitly as representations with both form and meaning reveals a systematic design space of possible goal formalisms. This perspective helps make explicit the assumptions baked into different models of goals, clarifies how higher-level cognition interacts with motivation, and provides axes along which to compare or translate between alternative conceptions of goals. Distinguishing goal syntax from goal semantics thus offers a unifying framework for studying and engineering goal-directed systems in both humans and machines.

Abstract: In both cognitive science and computer science, goals are conceptualized as cognitive states that flexibly combine with world knowledge to organize and specify purposeful behavior. In this way, goals are compositional representations whose content relates to rational behavior. We here draw attention to goals as representations and their content because it highlights a parallel with other areas in cognitive science - in particular, the syntax-semantics interface in linguistics and logic - while also foregrounding foundational questions about the expressivity, design, and efficiency of different goal representations. For example, goals are typically taken as fixed and imposing constraints on desirable behaviors, but we can also identify constraints on goal representations themselves, such as whether a particular goal language is sufficiently expressive to capture behaviors of interest, or whether different goal representations capture the same behavior. Here, we synthesize work that aims to characterize the properties of different goal representations and suggest these are points of a broader design space. We close by discussing how distinguishing the form and meaning of goals can elucidate the implicit assumptions we make about goals, inform the study of interactions between higher-level cognition and motivation, and isolate axes of variation for different conceptions of goals.

</details>


### [83] [Compositional Reasoning in Language Models under Reinforcement Learning Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19465)
*Yu He,Yingxi Li,Yifei Wang,Ellen Vitercik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how well post-trained language models can combine learned skills (compositional reasoning), finds an asymmetry where training on decomposed subtasks doesn’t reliably help on complex composed tasks but the reverse helps more, and offers a formal framework plus empirical/theoretical analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real-world settings models must solve novel, complex problems by recombining simpler skills rather than memorizing all possible cases. Although RL and related post-training improve general reasoning, it is unclear how they affect compositional reasoning in particular and whether training on simple decomposed skills suffices to get good performance on more complex compositions.

Method: The authors introduce a dependency-graph framework that formally models compositional reasoning and defines three increasing levels of compositionality. They instantiate this with data-structure manipulation tasks that have clear, deterministic rewards and compositional structure. Using language models trained with post-training (e.g., RL), they systematically compare training on decomposed skills versus composed tasks, analyze transfer in both directions, study length extrapolation and structural distribution shifts, and attempt a theoretical explanation of observed phenomena. They also run a small-scale study on tool-calling benchmarks as a more realistic application.

Result: Across experiments, they observe a robust decomposed-to-composed asymmetry: models trained only on decomposed subtasks do not reliably generalize to more complex composed tasks, whereas training on composed tasks generalizes better back to the decomposed subtasks. They further show how this behavior plays out under longer inputs, structural changes in task composition, and transfer to tasks involving skills not directly seen, and they provide theoretical support for why the asymmetry arises from the structure of learning and optimization. The pilot tool-calling study suggests the same pattern may appear in practical tool-use settings.

Conclusion: Improving compositional reasoning in LMs is not as simple as training on decomposed skills alone; exposure to composed tasks appears more valuable for both complex and simple-task performance. Their dependency-graph framework helps clarify different levels of compositionality and can serve as a basis for analyzing and benchmarking reasoning. The observed asymmetry has implications for how we design curricula and post-training regimes, suggesting that including sufficiently complex composed tasks is crucial for robust generalization, including in real-world tool-use applications.

Abstract: Compositional reasoning is critical for real-world problem solving: since training data is necessarily limited, models must generalize by composing learned skills in new ways. While post-training methods such as reinforcement learning (RL) have substantially improved the reasoning abilities of language models (LMs), their effects on compositional reasoning remain less well understood. We propose a dependency-graph framework to formalize compositional reasoning, yielding three levels of compositionality with increasing complexity. Empirically, we instantiate this framework with data-structure tasks, which provide deterministic reward computation and clear compositional structure. We find a consistent decomposed-to-composed asymmetry: decomposed-skill training does not reliably transfer to composed tasks, whereas composed-task training transfers more readily back to decomposed tasks. We provide theoretical explanation for this asymmetry, and further evaluate compositional generalization under length extrapolation, structural distribution shift, and transfer to tasks requiring unseen skills. Finally, we present a pilot study on real-world tool-calling benchmarks, showing preliminary evidence that the decomposed-to-composed asymmetry can extend to practical settings.

</details>


### [84] [Safety Beyond the Interface: Detecting Harm via Latent States in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19472)
*Alizishaan Khatri,Chiquita Prabhu,Omkar Neogi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They train small classifier probes on internal activations of LLaMA-3.1-8B to detect harmful prompts with very high accuracy and far lower cost than traditional guardrail models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety guardrails for LLMs are typically external models that add latency and compute, which is problematic for resource-constrained or time-critical autonomous systems. These guard models also lack visibility into the LLM's internal representations, creating a gap between what the model internally ‘knows’ and what the guardrail can see. The authors want to see if they can exploit the model’s own internal activations to cheaply and accurately detect harmful content, closing this gap and reducing overhead.

Method: The authors take intermediate activations from LLaMA-3.1-8B when it processes prompts and use these as features to train small multi-layer perceptron (MLP) classifier probes with around 12.6M parameters. These probes are trained to classify whether a prompt is harmful or safe. They evaluate these probes as safety detectors on three benchmark datasets for harmful or jailbreaking inputs: WildJailbreak, Beavertails, and AEGIS 2.0.

Result: The activation-based probes achieve high performance on harmful prompt detection benchmarks: 99% F1 on WildJailbreak, 83% F1 on Beavertails, and 84% F1 on AEGIS 2.0. Their performance is competitive with external guard models that are roughly 1000 times larger in parameter count, while using far fewer computational resources and incurring lower latency.

Conclusion: Internal activation probes on LLMs can serve as highly effective and efficient safety detectors, revealing that the model’s internal representations already encode strong signals about content harmfulness. Such probes can substantially reduce the cost and latency of safety infrastructure while maintaining competitive detection performance, making them attractive for deployment in constrained or time-sensitive autonomous systems.

Abstract: Autonomous systems increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) yet the safety infrastructure surrounding these models introduces latency and compute overhead. This limits utility in resource-constrained, time-critical deployments. Existing external guardrail models remain blind to the model's internal workings, creating a fundamental assurance gap. We ask: does the model already know when the content is harmful? We extract activations from LLaMA-3.1-8B and train lightweight MLP classifier probes (12.6M parameters) to detect harmful prompts. Evaluated on WildJailbreak, Beavertails, and AEGIS 2.0, our probes achieve F1 scores of 99%, 83%, and 84%, respectively competitive with 1000x larger guard models while cutting latency and compute costs.

</details>


### [85] [QVAC Genesis III: A Large-Scale, High-Quality Open Synthetic STEM Corpus for Efficient Language Model Pre-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19513)
*Davide Vitabile,N. Ranjan,Akshay Nambiar,Kamal K. Gupta,Amril Nazir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces QVAC Genesis III, a large synthetic STEM-focused dataset that significantly improves small language model performance on STEM benchmarks compared to existing open-source synthetic corpora.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small, edge-deployable language models need highly efficient, high-quality pre-training data, especially for STEM and educational tasks, but the open community lacks such optimized synthetic corpora. Existing large-scale private datasets are not accessible, and current open synthetic datasets like Cosmopedia-v2 may not provide maximal per-token learning value for small models. The authors aim to fill this gap with an open, STEM-focused dataset tailored for efficient training.

Method: They create QVAC Genesis III, a 191.43B-token synthetic corpus spanning 19 STEM-related domains, multiple difficulty levels, and diverse educational styles. The corpus is generated using a dual generation strategy with a teacher model and a weak edge-scale student model. The student’s mistakes are turned into corrective explanations (targeted distillation), and the student’s correct answers are expanded into contrastive, option-level reasoning over all answer choices. Additionally, they propose an LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol that parses free-form outputs to extract final answers and measure both accuracy and answer validity.

Result: In controlled from-scratch training experiments with 1.7B-parameter models, models trained on QVAC Genesis III systematically outperform those trained on Cosmopedia-v2 and the released Cosmo-1B model on STEM benchmarks ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM. Reported gains include up to +28.57% on ARC-E, +21.35% on ARC-C, and achieving a Valid Answer Rate of up to 99.45%.

Conclusion: QVAC Genesis III is an effective, STEM-focused synthetic corpus that substantially boosts the performance and answer reliability of small language models relative to existing open synthetic datasets. The dual-generation, failure-focused distillation approach and the LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol together provide a practical recipe for building and assessing high-value, per-token training data for educational and edge AI models.

Abstract: High-quality pre-training data is a critical bottleneck for educational and STEM-specific language models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment where token budgets are tightly constrained. While major organizations train ever-larger models on private corpora, the open ecosystem lacks STEM-focused synthetic datasets that deliver high per-token learning value efficiently for small models. To address this gap, we introduce QVAC Genesis III, a 191.43B-token, STEM-focused multi-domain synthetic corpus covering 19 domains across several difficulty levels and different educational styles. QVAC Genesis III is built via a dual generation strategy that performs targeted teacher distillation using a weak edge-scale student model as signal: the student's failures are converted into corrective explanations, while its successes are expanded into contrastive option-level reasoning over all answer choices. We further introduce an LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol that extracts final answers from free-form outputs and tracks both accuracy and answer validity. To validate the effectiveness of our QVAC Genesis III data, we conduct controlled from-scratch ablations with 1.7B-parameter models, showing that models trained with QVAC Genesis III consistently outperform both models trained with the open-source synthetic corpus Cosmopedia-v2 and the publicly released Cosmo-1B model across ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM benchmarks, achieving up to +28.57% on ARC-E and +21.35% on ARC-C, while reaching a Valid Answer Rate of up to 99.45%.

</details>


### [86] [LLM-as-an-Improver: Turning Verification into Better Candidates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19515)
*Akiyoshi Tomihari,Yuma Ichikawa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Verify–Repair–Reselect (VRR), a method that uses verifier feedback not only to rank but also to iteratively improve LLM-generated candidate solutions, leading to better code-generation and reasoning performance than standard fixed-pool verifier-based selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing verifier-based selection methods for LLMs only use verification as a post-hoc ranking step over a fixed pool of candidates. This ignores rich feedback that could be used to refine or generate better candidates, and it fails when every candidate in the initial pool is wrong. The authors aim to exploit verification feedback to actively improve the candidate set itself, enabling recovery from initially incorrect outputs and further boosting performance across tasks.

Method: They introduce the LLM-as-an-Improver paradigm and a concrete algorithm called Verify–Repair–Reselect (VRR). First, an initial pool of candidate solutions is generated and evaluated with a verifier to choose a provisional winner and runner-up. VRR then uses verification feedback to conditionally generate three new complementary candidates: (1) a repaired version of the winning solution, (2) a repaired version of the runner-up, and (3) a solution based on a new approach distinct from the existing ones. The method filters out invalid or duplicate candidates using only information available at inference time and finally reselects the best answer under the same original evaluation criteria, combining the retained initial winner with the improved candidates.

Result: Across a range of LLMs and benchmarks in code generation and reasoning, VRR consistently outperforms traditional fixed-pool verifier-based selection. It can sometimes recover correct answers even when all candidates in the initial pool are incorrect, demonstrating that leveraging verifier feedback for iterative improvement meaningfully enhances solution quality beyond simple re-ranking.

Conclusion: Verification feedback in LLM systems need not be limited to ranking over a static candidate pool; it can drive the generation of improved candidates. The proposed VRR framework operationalizes this idea, showing that an LLM can act as an improver that repairs and diversifies solutions based on verifier signals. This broader use of verification leads to stronger performance and greater robustness, suggesting a promising direction for future LLM system design that closes the loop between generation, verification, and refinement.

Abstract: Verifier-based selection improves LLM performance by generating multiple candidate solutions and using a verifier to select the most promising one. However, existing methods typically treat verification only as a ranking step and discard its feedback once a fixed candidate pool has been evaluated. In this paper, we ask whether verification can also improve the candidate set itself. To this end, we introduce LLM-as-an-Improver and propose Verify--Repair--Reselect (VRR), which uses verification feedback to generate and reselect improved candidates. VRR retains the initial winner while conditionally generating three complementary alternatives: repaired versions of the winner and runner-up, and a solution based on a new approach. It filters invalid and duplicate candidates using only inference-time information and then reselects the final answer under the original evaluation criteria. Across diverse models and code-generation and reasoning benchmarks, VRR improves over fixed-pool verifier-based selection in many settings and can recover correct solutions even when all candidates in the initial pool are incorrect. These results highlight a broader role for LLMs as improvers: verification feedback can not only select among existing solutions but also construct stronger candidates beyond the initial pool.

</details>


### [87] [An Architecture for Long-Horizon Agents: Levels, Ticks and Cascaded Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19519)
*Erik Nijkamp,Anurag Koul,Egor Pakhomov,Bo Pang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a hierarchical, continual-running agent architecture that allows language-model agents to handle multi-week tasks by preserving and organizing knowledge across context resets, enabling continual learning at the system level rather than via model weight updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-model agents are increasingly used for long-running tasks (days to weeks), but current systems are bounded by context windows, process lifetimes, and human attention intervals. These constraints prevent agents from maintaining coherent progress and learning over long horizons. The authors are motivated to design an architecture that lets agents operate continuously, maintain memory without forgetting across resets, and support continual learning without requiring constant human supervision or model retraining.

Method: The authors analyze the requirements of long-horizon tasks and derive seven bottlenecks that such agents face (e.g., context limits, process restarts, need for scalable autonomy and oversight). They propose a three-part hierarchical architecture: (i) time-scale-indexed levels that each maintain a bounded file summarizing the level below, creating a persistent multi-level memory; (ii) a clocked tick as the standard unit of autonomous action, structuring the agent’s operation over time; and (iii) cascaded intelligence, where tasks are attempted by cheaper or less capable models first and escalated to more capable models only after failing review. They then implement an agent with this architecture and run a ten-day experiment in which the agent reproduces a published reinforcement-learning result with limited (once-daily) human attendance.

Result: In the ten-day reinforcement-learning reproduction campaign, the agent maintained continuity of work across all context resets and session boundaries. Knowledge recorded early in the campaign later influenced behaviour, despite no changes to the underlying model weights, indicating effective system-level continual learning. The experiment also demonstrates where and how learned components (e.g., trainable modules) could be integrated into the architecture to further enhance performance. These outcomes empirically support the viability of the proposed harness for long-horizon operation.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that achieving continual learning for long-horizon language-model agents requires an external, persistent substrate that outlives any individual context window or process instance. The key capabilities—memory, control, and oversight—should be implemented in the surrounding harness rather than relying solely on changing model weights. The checks and control mechanisms already present in such a harness are natural locations to embed learning, suggesting that future progress in continual learning will come from system-level design around language models rather than only from improving the models themselves.

Abstract: Language-model agents are increasingly asked to carry out work spanning days or weeks, such as an operations remediation or a research programme. Such a task outlives any context window, any process and any interval at which a person can attend. In this paper, we argue that a long-horizon agent must run continually without forgetting before it can learn continually. This ability lies in the harness around the model rather than in the model itself. We derive seven bottlenecks from the long-horizon setting and answer them with a hierarchical architecture of three parts: (i) levels indexed by time scale, each keeping a bounded file summarising the level below; (ii) a clocked tick as the unit of autonomous action; and (iii) cascaded intelligence, where work is escalated to a more capable model only after failing review. We report on a ten-day campaign in which an agent built on this architecture reproduced a published reinforcement-learning result with a human attending once a day, and show (1) the agent kept the thread across every context reset and session boundary of the campaign, (2) operating knowledge written early changed later behaviour with no change to model weights, and (3) where learned components would enter such a system. Overall, our experience suggests continual learning for these agents needs a substrate outliving every context and process, and the checks the harness already runs are where a learner belongs.

</details>


### [88] [EconSkills: Studying Skill Transfer and Retrieval for Web Agents on Live Economic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19523)
*Yinzhu Quan,Zefang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces EconSkills, a reusable skill library built from successful web agent trajectories on economic data sites, and shows that abstracted, parameterized procedures transfer better than raw trajectory replays and can improve efficiency and success in web-based economic data retrieval tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current web agent evaluations ignore skills learned from past successful interactions and treat each task independently, even though agents frequently revisit similar websites and task types. There is a need to systematically capture, reuse, and evaluate procedural knowledge—especially for structured, high-value domains like economic data retrieval—so agents can leverage prior experience, reduce navigation cost, and improve robustness on new but related tasks.

Method: The authors construct EconSkills by distilling verified trajectories from EconWebArena into parameterized standard operating procedures (skills). Each skill encodes scope, navigation procedure, site-specific instructions, verification checks, and recovery strategies, with concrete values replaced by placeholders to allow reuse. They then design two evaluation regimes: (1) controlled transfer, where a task is explicitly matched with a relevant skill, and (2) library-scale retrieval, where an agent must select skills from a library. They compare several conditions: no-skill prompting, replaying raw trajectories, and using abstracted skills, and analyze success rates, step counts, coverage-stratified performance, and detailed browser trajectories for portal navigation and verification behavior.

Result: In controlled transfer settings, providing a matched EconSkill significantly increases success relative to no-skill prompting and reduces the number of interaction steps on shared-success tasks. Abstracted skills outperform direct replay of raw trajectories, indicating that generalized procedures transfer better than pointwise re-enactment. At full-library scale, skill retrieval performs roughly on par with a no-skill baseline overall, with notable gains on tasks directly covered by existing skills. However, on uncovered tasks, approximate matches lead to mixed effects that partially offset these gains. Analysis of browser trajectories shows cases where procedural, portal-specific guidance reduces navigation complexity, but also reveals that semantic verification of economic content remains crucial and cannot be replaced by navigation alone.

Conclusion: Reusable, parameterized web procedures for economic data retrieval can successfully transfer across task instances and improve both effectiveness and efficiency, particularly when appropriate skills are matched to tasks. Abstraction into standardized operating procedures is more beneficial than raw trajectory replay. Nonetheless, at library scale, net benefits depend on coverage quality and selection accuracy, motivating future work on coverage-aware skill selection, better retrieval mechanisms, and more effective context delivery for web agents operating in dynamic economic data environments.

Abstract: Web agents often revisit the same sites, yet most evaluations discard the procedures learned in earlier successful interactions. We introduce EconSkills, a skill library and evaluation framework that distills verified EconWebArena trajectories into parameterized standard operating procedures for retrieving live economic data. Each skill records its scope, navigation procedure, site-specific guidance, verification checks, and recovery steps while replacing source-instance values with placeholders. EconSkills separates two questions: whether a known relevant procedure transfers to a held-out task, and whether an agent can retain that benefit when selecting from a library. In controlled transfer, matched skills improve success over no-skill prompting and require fewer steps on paired successes, while abstraction is substantially more effective than replaying raw trajectories. At library scale, retrieval is competitive with the no-skill baseline overall and performs best on directly covered tasks; coverage-stratified outcomes show that approximate matches on uncovered tasks offset these gains. Browser trajectories further identify when procedural guidance shortens portal-specific navigation and when semantic verification remains necessary. These results establish that reusable economic web procedures can transfer across task instances and provide a concrete design target for coverage-aware selection and context delivery.

</details>


### [89] [A Unified Evaluation Framework for Trustworthy Large Language Models, Agentic AI, and Multimodal Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19524)
*Shaina Raza,Ahmed Y. Radwan,Imran Liaquat,Kathryn Hume*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified, multi-dimensional framework to evaluate the trustworthiness of modern AI systems beyond simple benchmark scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI evaluation practices rely heavily on benchmark scores, which are inadequate for assessing the trustworthiness of complex modern systems such as LLMs, agentic systems, and multimodal models. There is a need for evaluations that are interpretable, comparable across system types, and aligned with governance and regulatory requirements, while capturing different levels of system behavior and risk.

Method: The authors design a unified evaluation framework that links output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal assessments across eight trustworthiness dimensions: capability, robustness, safety, fairness, transparency, governance, oversight, and efficiency. They preserve system-specific metrics but normalize them into common performance bands with uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence. A meta-evaluation layer is added to assess the validity, reliability, and reproducibility of the evaluation methods themselves. The framework also incorporates safety-critical overrides to prevent high aggregate scores from concealing serious failures and explicitly maps evaluation outputs to external governance frameworks, standards, and EU regulatory requirements.

Result: The framework yields multidimensional trustworthiness profiles that highlight a system’s strengths and weaknesses and connects technical metrics to governance and regulatory needs. It enables interpretable, comparable assessments across different AI system types while supporting examination of the credibility of the evaluation evidence. Empirical validation in real deployment settings is identified as future work rather than fully completed results.

Conclusion: A structured, unified trustworthiness evaluation framework can go beyond raw benchmarks to assess both AI system performance and the soundness of the supporting evidence. By organizing measurements into eight dimensions, adding a meta-evaluation layer, allowing safety overrides, and tying results to governance and regulatory schemes, the framework offers a more comprehensive basis for oversight and risk management, though it still requires empirical validation in practice.

Abstract: Benchmark scores alone provide an incomplete basis for assessing the trustworthiness of modern artificial intelligence systems. Large language models (LLMs), agentic systems, and multimodal models (MLLMs) require different forms of assessment, yet their evaluation evidence must remain interpretable for development and oversight. We propose a unified framework that connects output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal assessment through eight trustworthiness dimensions: capability, robustness, safety, fairness, transparency, governance, oversight, and efficiency. The framework preserves system-specific metrics while mapping native measurements to common performance bands, accompanied by uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence. A meta-evaluation layer examines the validity, reliability, and reproducibility of the evaluation itself. Multidimensional profiles expose strengths and weaknesses, while safety-critical overrides prevent aggregate scores from masking critical failures. Mappings to governance frameworks, international standards, and European Union regulatory requirements connect technical assessment with oversight needs. The framework provides a structured basis for assessing both system performance and the credibility of the evidence supporting it, with empirical validation across deployment contexts remaining an essential next step.

</details>


### [90] [Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19526)
*Xinghong Fu,Aravinth Kulanthaivelu,Yutaro Yamada*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduce SIFT, a sample-efficient self-improvement framework for coding agents using fast tree search and LLM-as-a-judge to reduce evaluation cost and improve benchmark performance under tight budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-improving coding agents can enhance performance but are very compute- and time-intensive. The main bottleneck is evaluating each candidate self-modification by rerunning benchmarks, which is slow and costly. There is a need for a more sample- and compute-efficient way to guide self-modification while maintaining or improving performance.

Method: Propose SIFT (Recursive Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search), which performs self-improvement through a disaggregated tree search over code patches. It combines sparse expensive downstream benchmark evaluations with cheap LLM-as-a-judge pairwise comparisons of candidate patches. The pairwise results are aggregated using a regularized Bradley–Terry model to estimate patch strengths, which then guide rank-based parent sampling in the tree search so that only top candidates receive full downstream evaluations.

Result: SIFT achieves better performance than existing tree-search-based self-evolution frameworks on the full Polyglot coding benchmark while using significantly fewer computational resources (CPU hours, wall-clock time, and API cost).

Conclusion: Incorporating fast, judge-based pairwise comparisons and a Bradley–Terry-guided tree search into the self-improvement loop yields a more sample- and compute-efficient framework for self-improving coding agents, enabling better performance under strict resource constraints compared to prior methods.

Abstract: Coding agents can recursively modify their own implementations, forming a loop of self-improvement. While prior work shows this can boost performance on coding benchmarks, existing approaches are costly and compute-intensive. We introduce a simple, sample-efficient self-improvement framework that significantly improves coding performance under strict budget constraints. We identify evaluation of candidate self-modifications as the main runtime bottleneck since prior approaches estimate their effectiveness by re-running a subset of benchmark tasks with the modified agent, which is time-consuming. We introduce Recursive Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search (SIFT), which augments these downstream task evaluations with an LLM-as-a-judge signal that performs pairwise comparisons between candidate patches, where the win-loss record is aggregated with a regularized Bradley-Terry model, and the resulting strength scores drive rank-based parent sampling inside a lightweight disaggregated tree search. Expensive downstream task evaluations are reserved only for the most promising nodes. Using a fully disaggregated tree search pipeline, the judge scores provide intermediate signal to guide exploration on promising candidate patches without being bottlenecked by slow evaluation runs. SIFT outperforms existing tree-search based self-evolution frameworks on the full Polyglot benchmark with significantly lower resource requirements in terms of CPU hours, wall clock time, and API cost.

</details>


### [91] [When Hiring Becomes Agent-Mediated: Evaluating Access and Recurrence in Two-Agent Résumé Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19530)
*Jian Gao,Hang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares traditional one-shot automated résumé screening with a new two-agent dialogue-based screening, where employer and candidate agents exchange evidence before deciding who advances. Using GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7 on 600 synthetic résumé-job pairs, two-agent screening changes which candidates pass, increases passes especially on borderline cases, and shows that the procedure design—not just the underlying model—significantly affects who reaches human review and how consistently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current automated résumé screening treats hiring as a one-sided, static decision: a single model scores a résumé against a job description and decides to pass or reject. However, hiring in practice is bilateral and interactive: employers probe for evidence and candidates respond, clarify, and defend their qualifications. As AI agents start mediating both sides of hiring, it is important to understand whether more interactive, bilateral procedures change selection outcomes, particularly around borderline cases and decision reliability. The motivation is to test whether procedure design (one-call vs two-agent dialogue) meaningfully influences which candidates advance and how stable those decisions are, beyond the choice of model alone.

Method: The authors construct 600 résumé–job pairs and compare two screening procedures implemented with two large language models, GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7. Baseline "one-call" screening asks the model to make a single-pass judgment about whether a candidate should advance. The proposed "two-agent" screening instantiates two roles: an employer-side agent and a candidate-side agent. These agents exchange evidence—e.g., the employer requests clarification or proof of qualifications and the candidate agent responds—updating their views before making a final decision about advancement. The study compares pass rates overall, focuses on a shared set of 191 borderline pairs across three runs, and analyzes how often decisions change direction (pass to fail and vice versa), whether a simple one-call threshold can replicate two-agent results, and how reliably specific applications re-pass in repeated runs, including a one-call follow-up comparison.

Result: Two-agent screening increases the proportion of applications that advance relative to the one-call baseline: for GPT-5.5 from 33.3% to 39.3%, and for Claude Opus 4.7 from 34.0% to 35.5%. On a shared pool of 191 borderline résumé–job pairs evaluated across three runs, pass-instance rates increase markedly under two-agent screening (GPT-5.5: 4.5% to 26.2%; Opus 4.7: 6.5% to 16.1%). The shift is not simply more lenient filtering; two-agent screening also rejects some applications that one-call would pass, leading to bidirectional decision changes. Even when overall pass volumes are matched, the two procedures advance different sets of applications, and no single one-call threshold reproduces the set of candidates consistently selected by two-agent screening. In repeated runs of discovery-selected cases, two-agent-only selections recur less frequently than selections shared with one-call, especially under GPT-5.5 and to a lesser degree under Opus 4.7. A separate one-call follow-up study does not show a similar decline in recurrence.

Conclusion: Interactive, two-agent screening fundamentally alters automated résumé evaluation compared with standard one-call judgments. It raises advancement rates, especially among borderline candidates, but also reshapes which specific applications proceed, with decisions changing in both permissive and restrictive directions. The selection sets produced by two-agent dialogue cannot be replicated by simply adjusting thresholds in one-call scoring, indicating that the procedure’s structure—multi-turn evidence exchange between employer and candidate agents—introduces qualitatively different behavior. Moreover, two-agent-only passes are less reliably repeated across runs, particularly with GPT-5.5, suggesting a trade-off between exploratory discovery of candidates and decision stability. As hiring workflows become increasingly mediated by AI agents on both employer and candidate sides, organizations must consider not only which model they deploy but also how the screening protocol is structured, since it directly affects access to human review and the robustness of hiring outcomes.

Abstract: Hiring is bilateral: employers assess fit, while candidates present and defend evidence of their qualifications. Yet résumé screening, the first gate, is commonly automated as a static, one-call judgment over a résumé-job pair. We study a two-agent alternative in which employer-side and candidate-side agents represent these roles, exchange evidence, and update their judgments before deciding who advances. We compare procedures on 600 constructed résumé-job pairs using GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7. Two-agent screening advances more applications (33.3% to 39.3% for GPT-5.5; 34.0% to 35.5% for Opus 4.7). Across three runs on the common 191-pair borderline pool, pass-instance rates rise from 4.5% to 26.2% and from 6.5% to 16.1%, respectively. This is not a uniform relaxation: two-agent screening rejects applications one-call advances, changing decisions in both directions. At similar pass volumes, the procedures advance different applications, and no one-call threshold recovers applications consistently selected by two-agent screening. Among discovery-selected cases re-executed in fresh runs, two-agent-only selections recur less often than shared selections, clearly under GPT-5.5 and less certainly under Opus 4.7, while a separate one-call follow-up shows no comparable decline. As hiring becomes agent-mediated on both sides, the screening procedure, not only the model behind it, shapes who reaches human review and how reliably that access recurs.

</details>


### [92] [Agentic AI Networking for Heterogeneous Unmanned Aerial Systems in Low-Altitude Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19538)
*Nguyen Duc Minh Quang,Chang Liu,Shuangyang Li,Derrick Wing Kwan Ng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hierarchical architecture that combines large language models (LLMs) with multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) to manage low-altitude wireless networks (LAWNs) for heterogeneous unmanned aerial systems, enabling dynamic adaptation of objectives and resource priorities without retraining MARL policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing controllers for LAWNs rely on fixed, predefined optimization objectives and cannot flexibly adapt to time-varying service requirements, resource priorities, and operator intent in a dynamic, game-theoretic multi-service airspace.

Method: Introduce a dual-loop hierarchical framework: an outer LLM-assisted adaptation loop that interprets service requirements and operator intent to orchestrate the underlying game (redefining objectives and priorities), and an inner loop of decentralized, parameter-conditioned MARL policies that operate under the game configuration provided by the outer loop.

Result: Through a logistics-monitoring case study, the framework is shown to enable coordinated coexistence of heterogeneous services in LAWNs while adapting to changing operating conditions and service demands without retraining the MARL policies.

Conclusion: The proposed LLM–MARL dual-loop architecture provides a promising path toward scalable, adaptive coordination in LAWNs, but achieving fully scalable, trustworthy, and robust agentic LAWNs demands further research on challenges such as scalability, reliability, and trustworthiness of LLM-guided orchestration.

Abstract: Low-altitude wireless networks (LAWNs) are emerging as a key infrastructure for heterogeneous unmanned aerial systems that support concurrent services within a shared three-dimensional airspace. Their coexistence creates strong coupling among mobility, connectivity, and shared network resources, while heterogeneous services impose distinct and time-varying requirements. These interactions naturally form a dynamic non-cooperative game in which both operating conditions and coordination objectives evolve over time. Conventional optimization and learning-based controllers typically rely on predefined objectives, limiting their ability to adapt autonomously to changing service requirements and resource priorities. To address this challenge, we propose a hierarchical hybrid large language model (LLM)- multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) architecture organized as a dual-loop structure. Specifically, an outer adaptation loop employs LLM-assisted game orchestration to interpret service requirements and operator intent, and reconfigure objectives and resource priorities, while an inner loop executes decentralized, parameter-conditioned MARL policies under the configured game. A logistics-monitoring case study illustrates how the proposed framework facilitates coordinated coexistence among heterogeneous services, adapting to evolving operating conditions without retraining the underlying MARL policies. Finally, we discuss key challenges and research directions toward scalable, trustworthy, and adaptive agentic LAWNs.

</details>


### [93] [Continual Enterprise World Model Discovery in Dynamic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19551)
*Shambhavi Mishra,David Vazquez,Perouz Taslakian,Marco Pedersoli,Jose Dolz,Issam H. Laradji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a continual world-model discovery agent for enterprise systems that learns and updates hidden business rules by interaction, benchmarked on a new ServiceNow-based environment, EnterpriseWorldShift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real enterprise systems, business logic is encoded as evolving, organization-specific rules that are not visible in the platform itself. An autonomous agent operating in such systems must understand how actions on records trigger side effects (e.g., updates, creations, approvals), but it cannot a priori know these rules. Existing approaches tend to query the live system or documentation for each question rather than maintaining a persistent, revisable model of the world. There is a need for methods and benchmarks to study agents that can discover, maintain, and revise an internal world model of such hidden rules as they change over time.

Method: The authors formulate the problem as continual enterprise world model discovery. An agent starts with no knowledge of the business rules and interacts with a live ServiceNow-based environment, taking actions on records and observing their consequences. From these observations, it induces a structured world model that captures the hidden rules linking actions to side effects. The environment, EnterpriseWorldShift, spans nine tables and 25 hidden rules and includes 600 evaluation actions. It is designed with four sequential "world" versions where only the rules change (modified, added, removed) while tables and records remain fixed, so that discovery, revision, extension, and retirement of rules can each be evaluated. The proposed Continual Discovery Agent (CDA) incrementally builds and carries forward its model across these world versions.

Result: On the EnterpriseWorldShift benchmark, CDA more accurately predicts the effects of hidden business rules compared to a baseline approach that effectively "looks up" answers from the running system for each query, which is how prior work operates. CDA achieves up to 8.98 IoU (Intersection over Union) points improvement in predicting rule-induced effects and is able to answer questions directly from its internal model, without needing to query the live enterprise system at inference time.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that an agent can continually discover, maintain, and revise an internal world model of hidden enterprise business rules through interaction alone. The EnterpriseWorldShift benchmark provides a controlled yet realistic setting to evaluate such capabilities, including rule evolution scenarios. The Continual Discovery Agent outperforms lookup-style baselines in predicting rule effects and can operate without direct access to the running system, suggesting that learned, persistent world models are a promising direction for robust enterprise agents in dynamic rule environments.

Abstract: In an enterprise system, updating one field can set another, create a record, or start an approval. These effects are produced by business rules that are not built into the platform but written by each organization and revised over time. An agent working in such a system cannot predict the result of its own actions without knowing these rules. We study continual enterprise world model discovery, where an agent starts without knowledge of these business rules and discovers them by interacting with records and observing the outcomes. From those observations it builds a world model, which it revises as the rules change. To evaluate this, we introduce EnterpriseWorldShift, built on a live ServiceNow environment with nine tables, 25 hidden rules and 600 evaluation actions. It presents four versions of the same enterprise world, with the tables and records held fixed while a rule is modified, then added, then removed, so that discovery, revision, extension and retirement are each tested in turn. Our Continual Discovery Agent (CDA) builds such a model and carries it from one world to the next. It predicts the effects of the hidden rules more accurately than looking them up for each question, the approach taken by prior work, by up to 8.98 IoU points, and it answers from its own model without querying the running system.

</details>


### [94] [SIMLIFE: Pattern Understanding for Long-Horizon Human-Agent Partnership](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19610)
*Run Peng,Zinnia Nie,Jing Ding,Yinpei Dai,Yichi Zhang,Zengqing Wu,Yao Fu,Ziqiao Ma,Jiayuan Mao,Joyce Chai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SimLife is a simulated household environment and benchmark (SimLife-BP) for testing how well AI models can understand and infer long-term human behavior patterns from rich, multi-modal, long-duration data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI systems struggle with understanding people’s routines and how they evolve over long periods. Existing benchmarks rarely combine long time horizons, realistic household settings, and multi-modal data (vision, actions, dialogue, audio), nor do they explicitly test whether models can infer underlying behavioral rules rather than just recall or match short contexts. The authors aim to create a scalable, controlled environment and benchmark to rigorously study these abilities, which are crucial for future embodied agents that live and collaborate with humans in everyday settings.

Method: The authors build SimLife, a simulated long-term household environment that generates rich visual observations, ground-truth logs of agent actions, and synthetic dialogues with audio. On top of this environment they construct SimLife-BP, a benchmark focused on long-context pattern understanding. It consists of 106 long episodes (average ~15.5 hours, ~38.6 in-game days) and 1,439 QA pairs designed to test whether models can infer latent behavioral rules from weeks or months of observations. The tasks include different reasoning types—direct, counterfactual, noisy, and inverse reasoning—and vary the amount of hints about the underlying rules. They then evaluate a variety of frontier models and architectures on these tasks to assess their capabilities and limitations.

Result: Across models and architectures, performance patterns indicate that current systems tend to succeed mostly via shallow strategies. They often make surface-level predictions, rely heavily on frequency-based heuristics, and do not robustly represent explicit if-then behavioral rules grounded in evidence from the episodes. Models also have difficulty adapting when the simulated household routines or behavioral rules change, revealing weaknesses in long-term memory, pattern abstraction, and flexible updating of beliefs.

Conclusion: The study concludes that long-context pattern understanding—inferring and updating latent behavioral rules over extended timelines—remains a key unsolved challenge for building competent, adaptive embodied agents. SimLife and the SimLife-BP benchmark provide an open, scalable platform for systematically studying this problem space, enabling future work on memory mechanisms, personalization to specific households or individuals, adaptation to changing routines, and long-horizon planning in everyday human-AI interactions.

Abstract: Understanding humans over long horizons requires agents to infer not only what people need in the moment, but also how routines form, why they repeat, and when they change. We introduce SimLife, a scalable platform for simulating long-term household life with rich visual observations, ground-truth action logs, and synthetic dialogues with audio. Built on SimLife, SimLife-BP evaluates long-context pattern understanding: the ability to infer latent behavioral rules from weeks or months of everyday observations. The benchmark contains 106 episodes averaging 15.49 hours and 38.57 in-game days, and 1,439 question-answer pairs. Each task probes direct, counterfactual, noisy, and inverse reasoning under different levels of rule hints. Evaluating frontier models and architectures, we find that current models often achieve surface-level prediction without comprehensive rule understanding, rely on frequency-based heuristics rather than if-then reasoning over evidence, and struggle to adapt when behavioral patterns change. These findings suggest that long-context pattern understanding remains a major bottleneck for future embodied agents, while SimLife opens a broader space for studying memory, personalization, adaptation, and long-horizon planning in everyday human-AI interaction.

</details>


### [95] [From Intent to Action: Benchmarking LLM Safety in Vehicle Voice Command Authorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19630)
*Diba Afroze,Xingli Zhang,Yazhou Tu,Xiali Hei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper builds a benchmark and evaluation framework to test how safely LLM-based in-car assistants decide whether and how to act on voice commands, showing current models still make unsafe execution errors and therefore require an external enforcement layer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are embedded into vehicle voice assistants, they must translate natural language requests into actions on safety-critical vehicle systems.
