The key safety challenge is the authorization decision before any action is taken: deciding whether to execute, refuse, clarify, confirm, or defer to a human. Existing evaluations do not isolate or rigorously test this pre-action decision across factors like who is speaking, whether they are authenticated, current vehicle state, and which tools (vehicle functions) are available. There is a need for a systematic benchmark and metrics to assess how reliably LLMs make these decisions and whether they can be trusted as a primary safety mechanism.

Method: The authors design a 202-scenario benchmark that systematically varies speaker role, authentication status, vehicle state, and tool availability. For each scenario they define a Reference Decision under a seven-class taxonomy of possible system responses (execute, refuse, clarify, require confirmation, defer to manual control, trigger emergency response, or no tool call). They then evaluate two local open-weight models (including Llama 3.2 3B) and three API-based LLMs (including Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview) on this benchmark. Performance is measured via Decision Alignment with the references and via safety-specific error metrics such as False Executes (executing when one should not). They also run an ablation study on Llama 3.2 3B, comparing a structured authorization-policy setup against schema-only and generic-safety prompting baselines to see how policy structure affects alignment and safety errors.

Result: Decision Alignment varies widely across models: from 40.1% for Llama 3.2 3B up to 89.1% for Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview. The three API-based models cluster between 83.2% and 89.1% alignment, with no statistically significant differences among them. Despite relatively high alignment, even the best models still produce 2–3 False Executes across the 161 scenarios where no execution should occur, indicating residual critical safety risks. They also show persistent systematic errors, especially in deciding when to seek confirmation or require manual control. In the Llama 3.2 3B ablation, using a structured authorization policy boosts alignment to 40.1%, compared with only about 28.2–29.2% for schema-only and generic-safety prompts, but even this improved setup still fails to fully eliminate False Executes.

Conclusion: LLMs, even when guided by structured authorization policies, do not reliably make safe pre-action decisions for in-vehicle command execution. Structured prompting improves alignment but cannot prevent all unsafe executions. Therefore, LLM-based assistants should not be treated as the sole safety or authorization layer in vehicles. Instead, deployments must include an independent enforcement layer that checks tool permissions and vehicle-state constraints before allowing any function calls to be carried out.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into vehicle voice assistants. But linking natural-language requests to vehicle functions creates a safety-critical authorization problem. Before executing a command, the system must choose whether to execute, refuse, clarify, require confirmation, defer to manual control, trigger an emergency response, or make no tool call. To our knowledge, prior evaluations do not isolate this pre-action decision across speaker role, authentication status, vehicle state, and tool availability. We introduce a 202-scenario benchmark with Reference Decisions under a seven-class taxonomy. We evaluate two local open-weight models and three API-based LLMs using Decision Alignment and safety-specific error metrics. Alignment ranges from 40.1% for Llama 3.2 3B to 89.1% for Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview. The API-based models score between 83.2% and 89.1%, with no statistically significant differences among them. Even these models produce two to three False Executes among 161 non-execution scenarios, and persistent errors remain in confirmation and manual-control decisions. A controlled Llama 3.2 3B ablation increases alignment to 40.1% under the structured authorization policy, versus 28.2-29.2% under schema-only and generic-safety baselines, but it does not eliminate False Executes. Structured LLM decisions are therefore insufficient as a standalone safety mechanism, and deployment requires an independent enforcement layer that verifies tool permissions and vehicle-state constraints before invoking any vehicle function.

</details>


### [96] [Reach or Solve? Attributing Agentic RL Gains with Checkpoint Handoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19636)
*Xuan Liu,Jingbin Qian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper examines why reinforcement learning (RL)-fine-tuned language-model agents show large gains in long-horizon tasks and introduces a new evaluation protocol, checkpoint handoff, to disentangle improvements in reaching good states from improvements in solving from those states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL-tuned language-model agents can act over many steps and show strong endpoint performance, but existing evaluations conflate two effects: (1) whether the agent navigates to promising states, and (2) how well it completes tasks once there. Since RL and supervised fine-tuned (SFT) checkpoints experience different state distributions in closed-loop interaction, their performance is not directly comparable, and naive conditioning on shared states can introduce selection bias and even invert observed effects. A more principled way is needed to separate and measure these components of improvement.

Method: The authors formalize the problem that RL and SFT checkpoints are evaluated on different induced state distributions in long-horizon environments. They propose "checkpoint handoff": record full trajectories (histories) from one policy (the "reacher"), clone intermediate states that are known to be a fixed number of actions from success, and then continue the episode from that cloned state using another policy (the "solver"), without any retraining. By crossing SFT/RL reachers with SFT/RL solvers, they define two metrics: REACH (how often a policy reaches such promising states) and SOLVE (how often a policy solves the task from an identical cloned state). They apply this protocol across two benchmarks and two independent RL pipelines, analyzing the interaction between reacher and solver types.

Result: Across all examined conditions, the interaction between reacher and solver is positive: an RL-generated history is more valuable to an RL solver than to an SFT solver. On the ALFWorld benchmark, RL policies improve both REACH and SOLVE, and there are no states where an SFT solver succeeds while an RL solver fails. Independent gaps in REACH and SOLVE together explain the aggregate performance interaction. The experiments also demonstrate that restricting evaluation to states visited by both policies can be misleading and may reverse the apparent effect of RL fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Checkpoint handoff provides a more faithful way to evaluate long-horizon agents by decoupling the ability to navigate to good states (REACH) from the ability to complete tasks once there (SOLVE). The study shows that RL fine-tuning improves both aspects in some settings and that histories generated by RL policies particularly benefit RL solvers. This protocol enables reporting arrival and completion metrics alongside endpoint success, avoiding selection biases inherent in conventional evaluations and offering a clearer understanding of how RL changes language-model agents.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning now trains language-model agents that act over dozens of steps in live environments. The gains are large, and they are read as better decision-making. An agent in a closed loop writes its own inputs. Each observation follows from its own earlier actions, so the states it meets late in an episode are partly of its own making. An SFT checkpoint and an RL checkpoint are then scored from different states, even on identical tasks. Endpoint success mixes two changes: where the agent arrives, and what it does once it is there. Restricting the comparison to states both policies reach does not separate them. That restriction selects on an outcome, and in our data it flips the sign of the effect. We introduce checkpoint handoff, an evaluation protocol that clones a state one released checkpoint reached and hands it to another, with no retraining. Crossing a reacher role and a solver role over SFT and RL splits an endpoint gain into REACH and SOLVE. REACH is how often a policy arrives at a state the environment confirms is a fixed number of actions from success. SOLVE is how often it finishes from an identical cloned state. Across two benchmarks and two independently released pipelines, the reacher by solver interaction is positive in all five conditions. An RL history is worth more to an RL solver than the same history is to an SFT solver. On ALFWorld, RL improves both terms, and the SFT solver never succeeds where the RL solver fails. Independent REACH and SOLVE gaps predict the aggregate interaction. Handoff asks only that one checkpoint's history can be replayed under another, so long-horizon evaluation can report arrival and completion beside endpoint success.

</details>


### [97] [ScientistTwo: Pioneering the Human Knowledge Frontier with Autonomous AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19644)
*Jaehyun Nam,Jinsung Yoon,Yanzhou Pan,Yubo Wang,Rui Meng,Parthasarathy Ranganathan,Tomas Pfister*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents ScientistTwo, an autonomous multi-agent AI system that can take a research problem and independently conduct an entire scientific discovery cycle, producing state-of-the-art, publishable results and code that outperform human baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI systems in science are largely assistive and task-specific, requiring heavy human guidance. The authors aim to move toward fully autonomous, problem-driven scientific discovery where AI can identify knowledge gaps, design and run experiments, and advance the state of the art with minimal human intervention.

Method: The authors design ScientistTwo as a fully autonomous multi-agent framework. Given an initial problem, it: (1) surveys and establishes state-of-the-art baselines; (2) formulates novel hypotheses; (3) decomposes tasks and coordinates specialized agents; (4) runs end-to-end experiments across datasets and metrics; (5) performs automated ablation studies to refine methods; and (6) uses a closed-loop simulated peer-review and rebuttal engine to validate and iterate on the research. The system then produces complete papers and executable codebases without human-in-the-loop guidance.

Result: When benchmarked on research problems derived from accepted papers at leading ML conferences (ICLR, ICML, NeurIPS), ScientistTwo autonomously generated expert-level, publishable papers and verified codebases. Its models consistently surpassed human state-of-the-art performance and received higher average review scores than human-authored papers under automated AI review agents.

Conclusion: ScientistTwo demonstrates that AI can function as an autonomous scientific researcher rather than a mere assistant. Its success in independently producing state-of-the-art results and publishable work suggests that such multi-agent frameworks can actively push scientific frontiers and may redefine how research is conducted in the future.

Abstract: Scientific discovery is defined by the ability to identify the boundaries of existing knowledge and venture into unexplored territory. The ultimate vision for AI in science is problem-driven autonomous research: given a fundamental challenge by a human expert, the AI independently navigates the scientific landscape, uncovers theoretical and empirical bottlenecks, and systematically expands the frontier of knowledge. In this paper, we introduce ScientistTwo, a fully autonomous multi-agent framework designed to realize this vision. Specifically, ScientistTwo takes an initial problem as input, establishes state-of-the-art baselines, formulates novel hypotheses, and coordinates specialized agents to orchestrate an end-to-end discovery cycle without human intervention. Moreover, the framework rigorously conducts experiments using diverse datasets and metrics, refines methodologies through automated ablation studies, and validates research findings via a closed-loop simulated peer-review rebuttal engine. To evaluate ScientistTwo's capabilities against the highest standards of human scientific achievement, we benchmark it across papers accepted at top-tier conferences such as ICLR, ICML, and NeurIPS. As a result, ScientistTwo autonomously generates expert-level, publishable papers and fully verified, executable codebases. Its solutions consistently outperform human state-of-the-art models, and achieve higher average review ratings than human-authored papers under automated AI review agents. These results show that ScientistTwo is not merely an assistive tool but an autonomous scientific pioneer capable of pushing the frontiers of human discovery. Project website: https://scientist-two.github.io/

</details>


### [98] [Replan, Repair, or Edit? A Unified Empirical Evaluation of Travel Agents for Itinerary Revision under Resource Disruptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19654)
*Xiaofei Yuan,Yan Zhang,Shaobo Qiao,Huangleshuai He,Leyan Ni,Mingchen Ju,Lujia Yang,Sijia Xu,Yifu Tang,Zhengyi Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically compares three ways to fix disrupted travel itineraries—full replanning, hierarchical repair, and local LLM-based revision—using standardized benchmarks, and derives practical trade-off guidelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Travel-planning agents often generate itineraries that later become infeasible due to real-world disruptions such as flight cancellations or hotel and attraction unavailability. Once a user has accepted an itinerary, revising it is challenging: we want to restore feasibility while changing as little as possible and keeping computational costs reasonable. Existing approaches—classical full replanning, plan-repair methods, and recent LLM-based revision agents—have been studied under different task formulations and evaluation setups, making it difficult to compare them and understand when to use which. The paper aims to create a unified empirical comparison of these approaches on realistic disruption scenarios to clarify their strengths, weaknesses, and trade-offs.

Method: The authors construct two benchmark sets derived from the TREK dataset: (1) 500 single-disruption cases that mix feasible and infeasible instances and (2) 200 feasible cases with simultaneous compound disruptions. They evaluate three families of methods: (a) LLM-Z3 full replanning, which uses a large language model plus a Z3 solver to regenerate itineraries from scratch, (b) IPyHOPPER, a hierarchical plan-repair method that modifies an existing itinerary minimally while enforcing constraints, and (c) iTIMO, a local-revision adapter that leverages LLMs to edit specific parts of an itinerary. They compare these approaches along three axes: effectiveness at restoring feasibility, plan stability measured by how much of the original accepted itinerary is preserved, and computational cost in terms of LLM calls and token usage. Different LLM configurations (e.g., Gemini) are tested within this framework.

Result: On compound-disruption cases, LLM-Z3 configured with Gemini achieved the best success rate in recovering feasible itineraries. On single-disruption cases, IPyHOPPER’s overall success nearly matched that of the best LLM-Z3 setup, but it preserved significantly more of the original accepted itinerary when repairs were successful. Both hierarchical repair (IPyHOPPER) and local LLM-based repair (iTIMO) required fewer edits and retained more user commitments than full replanning. In terms of computational cost, IPyHOPPER required no LLM inference, the LLM-Z3 adapter operated with compact, single-call LLM usage, whereas iTIMO consumed notably more LLM tokens, making it more expensive computationally.

Conclusion: Hierarchical and local-revision approaches can often repair disrupted travel itineraries with fewer changes and better preservation of user commitments than full replanning, while achieving competitive success rates in many single-disruption scenarios. Full replanning with a strong LLM+solver combination, such as LLM-Z3 with Gemini, is most effective for challenging compound disruptions but at the cost of greater change to the accepted plan. The computational profiles of these methods differ substantially: purely symbolic approaches avoid LLM costs, lightweight LLM adapters balance cost and performance, and heavy local-revision adapters may be more token-expensive. The study distills these findings into practical guidelines for choosing between full replanning, hierarchical repair, and local LLM-based revision depending on desired trade-offs among feasibility recovery, commitment preservation, and computational budget.

Abstract: Travel-planning agents generate itineraries that may become infeasible after acceptance because of flight cancellations, hotel unavailability, or attraction closures. Revising these itineraries involves full replanning, classical plan repair, and LLM-based travel-agent revision, whose differing task formulations and evaluation protocols hinder comparison. We conduct a systematic empirical study using two TREK-derived benchmark sets: 500 single-disruption cases, including feasible and infeasible instances, and 200 feasible simultaneous compound-disruption cases. We compare LLM-Z3 full replanning, IPyHOPPER hierarchical repair, and an iTIMO local-revision adapter across effectiveness, plan stability, and computational cost. LLM-Z3 with Gemini achieved the highest observed compound-disruption success. IPyHOPPER nearly matched that configuration's single-disruption overall success, while preserving substantially more of the accepted itinerary on successful repairs. Successful hierarchical and local repairs made fewer edits and retained more accepted commitments than full replanning. Computational profiles differed: IPyHOPPER used no LLM inference, the evaluated LLM-Z3 adapter used compact one-call inference, and the iTIMO adapter consumed substantially more tokens. The study provides practical guidelines for balancing feasibility recovery, commitment preservation, and computational cost within evaluated settings.

</details>


### [99] [When2Think: Learning Difficulty-Aware Length Control for Efficient Hybrid Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19671)
*Jaejun Shim,HyunJin Kim,Young Jin Kim,JinYeong Bak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces When2Think, a post-training framework that dynamically adjusts how much reasoning a large model does per instance, reducing overthinking on easy problems and underthinking on hard ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Reasoning Models are powerful but inefficient: they waste computation on easy tasks and fail to reason deeply enough on difficult ones. Existing solutions that use fixed length penalties or rigid routing reduce cost but hurt accuracy on hard instances. The authors want a principled way to allocate computation adaptively, preserving strong performance while cutting unnecessary tokens.

Method: They cast efficient reasoning as instance-adaptive computation allocation and propose When2Think, a hybrid reasoning framework applied post-training. The key component is Instance-level Difficulty-Aware Control (IDAC), a reward-shaping mechanism that uses pre-computed reference statistics of accuracy and token usage per difficulty level. IDAC, together with verifier-based rewards and batch-wise standardized advantages, guides learning of when to answer directly (System 1 / NoThink) versus when to invoke extended chain-of-thought reasoning (System 2 / Think). Optimization is critic-free and does not rely on learned reward models or online reference model queries.

Result: On mathematical reasoning benchmarks, When2Think improves the trade-off between accuracy and computation. On AIME24, it raises Pass@3 by 10 percentage points while cutting token usage by 27.9% relative to the base model. On AIME25, it reaches 40% Pass@3 and surpasses baselines that use compression or routing-only strategies.

Conclusion: Adaptive control over reasoning depth can substantially improve the accuracy-efficiency balance of large reasoning models. By using difficulty-aware reward shaping without extra reward models or online references, When2Think learns when to use fast direct answers and when to perform extended reasoning, avoiding the systematic overthinking and underthinking seen in existing models.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) achieve strong performance on complex tasks but exhibit systematic inefficiency: they often overthink easy problems and underthink hard ones. Existing approaches based on uniform length penalties or rigid routing incur an efficiency tax, trading reduced computation on easy instances for accuracy loss on hard instances. We formulate efficient reasoning as an instance-adaptive computation allocation problem and propose When2Think, a post-training framework for hybrid reasoning that dynamically allocates computation based on problem difficulty. Our method introduces Instance-level Difficulty-Aware Control (IDAC), a reward-shaping mechanism that leverages pre-computed reference statistics (accuracy and token usage) to regulate reasoning depth. Combined with verifier-based rewards and batch-wise standardized advantages, IDAC enables stable critic-free optimization without learned reward models or online reference-model queries. When2Think encourages direct answering on easy instances while preserving extended reasoning on hard instances, thereby learning when to use System 1 (NoThink) versus System 2 (Think). Experiments on mathematical benchmarks demonstrate improved accuracy-efficiency trade-offs: on AIME24, Pass@3 increases by 10.0% while token usage is reduced by 27.9% relative to the base model, and on AIME25, When2Think achieves 40.0% Pass@3, outperforming compression and routing-only baselines.

</details>


### [100] [FINSKILLOPS: A Self-Evolving Multi-Agent System for SEC Filing QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19680)
*Yanzhang Ma,Zhenghan Tai,Hanwei Wu,Sizhe Guan,Jianliang Lei,Hailin He,Chaolong Jiang,Jijun Chi,Tung Sum Thomas Kwok,Bohuai Xiao,Jingrui Tian,Xinlu Wu,Xingao Zhan,Peng Lu,Muzhi Li,Yihong Wu,Liheng Ma,Sicheng Lyu,Tianshuo Yan,Junhao Zhu,Yaqian Xu,Lei Ding,Yufei Cui,Ziquan Liu,Boyu Han,Hengli Liu,Ling Zhou,Xinyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes FINSKILLOPS, a multi-agent system that performs controlled, post-deployment self-improvement for financial QA on SEC filings by learning, validating, and lifecycle-managing scoped “skills” derived from diagnosed failures, improving reliability without causing regressions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial QA systems face diverse and recurring errors in period alignment, entity recognition, evidence selection, and calculations when answering questions about SEC filings. After deployment, most systems are improved only through better retrieval or prompting, leaving their deeper behavioral reliability largely fixed. Existing self-improvement techniques can update models based on failures but provide little control over how local those changes are or whether they unintentionally break previously correct behaviors. There is a need for a principled way to do post-deployment refinement that can safely incorporate new capabilities while restricting their scope and preventing regressions, especially in high-stakes financial settings where reliability and auditability matter.

Method: The authors frame post-deployment refinement as “controlled behavioral maintenance” and introduce FINSKILLOPS, a multi-agent system that manages a registry of modular skills for SEC-filing QA. The workflow is: (1) observe and diagnose failures using evidence-grounded, typed annotations that capture the nature of the error (e.g., wrong period, wrong entity, misused evidence, incorrect calculation); (2) derive reusable skills from these diagnoses, where each skill acts as a scoped behavioral patch that applies under specific conditions; (3) govern each skill via a structured operational process including targeted validation on relevant cases, regression tests on protected cases that were previously correct, negative controls to detect overgeneralization, and versioning that supports skill replacement or retirement; and (4) deploy only those skills that pass stringent checks into a frozen skill registry used at inference time by a multi-agent QA system. The approach is evaluated across multiple financial QA benchmarks and in a longitudinal operational study.

Result: On six financial QA benchmarks, a single frozen skill registry built with FINSKILLOPS achieves the best verdict-weighted correctness and reference consistency among compared systems. On an enhanced benchmark, evolving the skills over time improves correctness from 3.70 to 4.55. In a 12-round operational study, only 6 of 33 proposed skills satisfy the promotion criteria and are admitted into production, demonstrating the strictness of the admission process. During this period, the monitored non-correct answer rate drops from 20.0% to 12.5%, indicating substantial reliability gains without widespread regressions.

Conclusion: The study shows that treating model updates as controlled, scoped skills with explicit admission and lifecycle policies can provide reliable post-deployment self-improvement for financial QA systems. By grounding patches in typed failure diagnoses, rigorously validating them with targeted tests, and preventing regressions through protected cases and negative controls, FINSKILLOPS achieves better correctness and consistency while maintaining stability. The results support controlled skill scope, skill admission processes, and lifecycle management as key building blocks for trustworthy, continuously improving QA systems in financial domains.

Abstract: Financial QA systems are typically improved before deployment through better retrieval, prompting, or agent coordination, leaving their reliability behavior fixed thereafter. In practice, new SEC-filing questions repeatedly expose heterogeneous errors in period, entity, evidence use, and calculation. Existing self-improvement methods can turn failures into new behaviors, but offer limited control over where a correction should apply or which previously correct answers it may break. We therefore frame post-deployment improvement as controlled behavioral maintenance: recurring failures should become scoped skill patches, and each patch should earn deployment with- out introducing regressions. We instantiate this view in FINSKILLOPS, a multi-agent system for SEC filing QA. FINSKILLOPS derives reusable skills from evidence-grounded, typed failure diagnoses and governs them through targeted validation, protected-case regression checks, negative controls, and versioned replacement or retirement. Across six financial QA benchmarks, a single frozen skill registry achieves the highest verdict-weighted correctness and reference consistency among the evaluated systems. Evolved skills raise correctness from 3.70 to 4.55 on our enhanced benchmark. In a separate 12-round operational study, only six of 33 proposed skills are promoted, while the monitoring non-correct rate falls from 20.0% to 12.5%. These results establish controlled skill scope, admission, and lifecycle management as the foundation for reliable self-improvement.

</details>


### [101] [LearnActCoder: Role-Aware Error Memory for Adaptive Clinical Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19721)
*Meysam Ghaffari,Bhaskar Sen,Nasim Sabetpour,Nina Fatehi,Animesh Agarwal,Carlos Morato*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework (Learn-Then-Act) uses a small batch of labeled coding errors to build a structured mistake database that guides a coder and judge agent to improve clinical code assignment without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic clinical coding with language models often fails in repeated, systematic ways: suggesting codes not supported by the note, missing clearly documented conditions, picking codes with wrong specificity, and mishandling procedure-coding conventions. Existing methods either need retraining, are hard to adapt at inference time, or use unstructured memories that do not effectively correct these recurrent errors. The authors want a way to systematically capture and reuse feedback about such errors to adapt coding behavior across future cases, without changing model weights or workflows.

Method: They propose Learn-Then-Act, an inference-time adaptation framework. A small labeled LEARN batch of clinical notes with ground-truth codes is used to identify false negatives (missed codes) and false positives (incorrect codes) produced by a base coding pipeline. These errors are converted into a structured Mistake Knowledge Database (MistakeKDB), which encodes lessons about when certain codes should or should not be applied. At inference time, false-negative lessons are applied by a recall-oriented Coder component to encourage suggesting missing codes in similar contexts, while false-positive lessons are applied by a precision-oriented Judge to discourage unsupported codes. They instantiate this in LearnActCoder, a coder–judge pipeline that also uses lookup-table grounding when possible, and compare structured MistakeKDB to raw example memories and reflection-style memories on multiple MIMIC datasets and code types (CPT, ICD-9, ICD-10).

Result: On 150 matched MIMIC-III discharge notes, adding structured MistakeKDB memory improves CPT F1 by 5.9 percentage points over a no-memory baseline, while raw-example memory and reflection-style memory show little or no improvement. For ICD-9 coding on the same cohort, the observed improvement is not statistically significant. On a matched MIMIC-IV cohort for ICD-10, the memory shifts behavior toward higher precision but with reduced recall, resulting in no statistically significant F1 change. When the same MistakeKDB built on one cohort is applied to 1,000 held-out MIMIC-III notes, the system maintains a similar ICD precision–recall operating point, suggesting that the adaptation scales and remains stable across more cases.

Conclusion: Structured, feedback-derived error memories encoded in a MistakeKDB can effectively adapt clinical coding behavior at inference time, improving performance in some settings (notably CPT/HCPCS on MIMIC-III) without retraining or altering the underlying coding workflow. However, gains are code-set and dataset dependent, sometimes trading recall for precision without net F1 improvement. Overall CPT/HCPCS performance remains low, and results are based on retrospective evaluation rather than live clinical deployment, so further work is needed to reach clinically robust performance and validate impact in real-world settings.

Abstract: Clinical coding agents repeatedly encounter the same failure modes, including unsupported codes, missed documented conditions, specificity errors, and procedure-coding convention mismatches. We introduce Learn-Then-Act, an inference-time adaptation framework that converts errors from a small labeled LEARN batch into a structured Mistake Knowledge Database (MistakeKDB). False-negative lessons are routed to a recall-oriented Coder, while false-positive lessons are routed to a precision-oriented Judge. We instantiate the framework in LearnActCoder, a Coder-Judge clinical coding pipeline with lookup-table grounding where available. On 150 matched MIMIC-III notes, structured MistakeKDB improves CPT F1 by 5.9 percentage points, while raw-example and reflection-style memories remain near the no-memory baseline; the ICD-9 improvement is not significant. On a matched MIMIC-IV cohort, memory shifts ICD-10 coding toward higher precision at a recall cost, leaving F1 statistically unchanged. Applying the same memory to 1,000 held-out MIMIC-III notes maintains a stable ICD operating point, providing scale/stability evidence. Overall, the results are consistent with structured, feedback-derived error memory being useful for adapting clinical coding behavior across cases without weight updates or changes to the underlying workflow. Absolute CPT/HCPCS performance remains low, and the system is evaluated retrospectively rather than in clinical deployment.

</details>


### [102] [AutoData: Agentic Search for Pre-training Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19754)
*Yan Meng,Dhruv Srikanth,Bingchen Zhao,Zhengyao Jiang,Yuxiang Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoData is an LLM-driven agent that automatically designs pre-training data selection algorithms, outperforming human-crafted curation pipelines and transferring to larger scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLM agents have started to automate model and training-code optimization, data curation and selection for pre-training remain largely manual and based on heuristics engineered by humans. Existing data mixture methods typically tune weights over pre-defined domains rather than exploring richer data-selection logic, missing complex feature interactions and limiting automation. The paper aims to bring data engineering into the same agentic optimization loop as model and training engineering, enabling automatic discovery of effective data selection strategies.

Method: The authors propose AutoData, an LLM-based agent that treats data selection as heuristic engineering over per-document features such as lexical statistics, categorical labels, and perplexity. AutoData searches directly over a space of executable data selection algorithms composed of scoring, stratification, and stochastic selection rules. The agent iteratively edits and refines these selection programs using validation feedback from a small proxy model, effectively performing program search to discover useful feature interactions and selection strategies.

Result: Within an overnight search budget, AutoData discovers a pre-training data selection algorithm that outperforms existing human-designed curation pipelines on the target metric CORE when evaluated on the proxy model. Importantly, the discovered selection recipe generalizes: when applied at larger scale beyond the proxy setting, it continues to improve CORE, indicating that the agent-found heuristics are robust and transferable.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that pre-training data engineering can be automated by framing it as an agentic machine learning problem, where an LLM agent searches the space of selection algorithms with feedback from model performance. AutoData shows that such agents can automatically uncover effective, non-trivial data selection heuristics that outperform human-designed pipelines and scale to larger settings. This extends the scope of autonomous ML research from models and training code to the data itself, suggesting a broader paradigm where data, models, and training are all co-optimized by agents.

Abstract: LLM agents have recently shown promise in automating machine learning engineering by editing model and training code under execution feedback. Data, however, remains largely outside this agentic optimisation loop. We frame pre-training data selection as heuristic engineering over per-document features, i.e., lexical statistics, categorical labels, and perplexity. We introduce AutoData, an agent that searches directly over executable selection algorithms. Unlike prior data mixture methods that optimise weights over a fixed set of domains, AutoData searches a richer program space of scoring, stratification, and stochastic selection rules, discovering feature interactions automatically by iteratively refining algorithms with validation feedback from a proxy model. Within an overnight search, AutoData discovers a selection algorithm that outperforms existing human-designed curation pipelines. Despite being searched only on this small proxy, the discovered recipe transfers to larger scales and improves the downstream metric CORE. These results suggest that data engineering can be treated as an agentic machine learning problem, extending autonomous research from model and training-code optimization to the data.

</details>


### [103] [Rethinking Multi-Agent Collaboration: When More Is Less](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19759)
*Yishuo Yuan,Yibo Wu,Yihan Zhang,Minyuan Sun,Shenliang Li,Xinkai Ma,Yifan Li,Jiaheng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates when multi-agent LLM systems are actually better than strong single-agent setups and introduces SAIGE, a graph-based, lightweight multi-agent framework that works best for long, loosely coupled tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs and single-agent tool harnesses get stronger, it is unclear whether adding more agents still brings benefits, especially given the overhead of coordination and context sharing. The paper aims to clarify under what task conditions multi-agent collaboration is genuinely advantageous over a single powerful agent, and to avoid unnecessary multi-agent complexity and cost.

Method: The authors perform a systematic analysis of task structures and compare multi-agent collaboration with single-agent alternatives, focusing on horizon length and dependency patterns (sparse vs tightly coupled). They then design SAIGE, a semantic-aware incremental graph evolution mechanism where each agent instance is a node and edges represent semantic dependencies discovered via content-based retrieval. Collaboration is orchestrated as a dynamically grown and updated graph, with agents spawned on demand and context shared selectively based on semantic links.

Result: On long-horizon, complex task benchmarks, SAIGE achieves good task performance while using context more efficiently than naive multi-agent setups. The experiments demonstrate that multi-agent collaboration yields clear benefits on long-horizon tasks with sparse dependencies, but not on tightly coupled sequential workflows where single-agent harnesses work better. They also find that simply increasing the number of agents or recursion depth does not reliably improve performance.

Conclusion: Multi-agent LLM systems are not universally superior; their advantage is bounded by the underlying task structure. They help particularly in long, loosely dependent tasks, while strong single-agent harnesses remain better for tightly coupled, sequential workflows. The proposed SAIGE framework offers an efficient way to realize these benefits without excessive context overhead, and the results caution against assuming that more agents automatically make a system more capable.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of large language models and single-agent harnesses has reshaped the landscape of autonomous systems, raising a critical question of when multi-agent collaboration offers genuine value. As individual agent capabilities continue to scale, multi-agent collaboration faces diminishing returns while incurring growing context overhead. Through systematic analysis, we delineate the capability boundaries of multi-agent collaboration relative to single-agent alternatives, showing that it confers systematic benefits specifically in long-horizon tasks with sparse dependencies, while single-agent harnesses remain superior in tightly coupled, sequential workflows. Building on these insights, we propose SAIGE, a lightweight multi-agent collaboration mechanism based on Semantic-Aware Incremental Graph Evolution. SAIGE models collaboration as a dynamically evolving graph, where nodes are agent instances spawned on demand and edges encode semantic dependencies established through content-based information retrieval. Experiments on long-horizon, complex task benchmarks show that SAIGE achieves a favorable trade-off between context efficiency and task performance, and that scaling the agent pool or deepening the recursion level does not consistently improve outcomes. Our findings suggest that multi-agent superiority is bounded by task structure rather than universal, and that more agents do not necessarily make a system more intelligent.

</details>


### [104] [TorchCraft: Unified binder design by inverting an all-atom structure predictor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19770)
*TorchCraft Team,Yu Liu,Zhouhanyu Shen,Zhengyi Li,Xikun Huang,Jiaqi Liu,Shuxian Gao,Qilin Yu,Xiayan Qin,Yucheng Zhang,Mingchen Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TorchCraft is a unified framework that inverts frozen all-atom structure predictors (like AlphaFold 3) to design diverse protein and peptide binders by directly optimizing sequence logits within the predictor, achieving experimentally validated binders without extra redesign.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: All-atom structure predictors can accurately model many kinds of molecular interactions, but their internal structural knowledge (priors) is hard to reuse systematically for designing binders in different molecular formats. Existing approaches are often ad hoc, target limited formats, or require additional redesign steps. There is a need for a general, differentiable design framework that can leverage a frozen predictor across many binder types.

Method: TorchCraft freezes a pretrained all-atom structure predictor (here, AlphaFold 3) and performs gradient-based optimization on the input sequence logits instead of model weights. Implemented in TorchFold, it defines a unified loss that combines confidence metrics, interfacial contact terms, geometric constraints, and sequence-prior regularization. This shared optimization pipeline is applied to multiple design settings, including de novo minibinders, VHHs conditioned on fixed frameworks, cyclic peptides, and ligand-pocket designs.

Result: Using AlphaFold 3 weights, TorchCraft generated minibinders and VHHs against four targets each, and representative designs were experimentally confirmed to bind without needing additional sequence redesign. In silico tests showed successful extension of the same framework to cyclic peptide binders and ligand-conditioned pocket designs, indicating broad applicability across molecular contexts.

Conclusion: TorchCraft generalizes predictor inversion into a single, reusable binder-design framework that works across multiple protein and peptide formats. By optimizing sequence logits through a frozen all-atom predictor and combining structural, confidence, and sequence-prior objectives, it enables direct reuse of powerful structural priors for binder design without bespoke pipelines for each molecular context.

Abstract: All-atom structure predictors model diverse molecular interactions, but using their learned structural priors for binder design remains challenging. Here we present TorchCraft, a unified binder-design framework that optimizes sequence logits through a frozen all-atom predictor. Implemented in TorchFold, TorchCraft combines confidence, contact, geometric, and sequence-prior objectives within a shared optimization procedure for minibinders, framework-conditioned VHHs, cyclic peptides, and ligand-binding proteins. Using pretrained AlphaFold 3 weights, TorchCraft generated representative minibinders and VHHs with experimentally measured binding across four targets in each format, without post hoc sequence redesign. Computational benchmarks further demonstrated the framework's applicability to cyclic peptides and ligand-conditioned pocket design. TorchCraft extends predictor inversion to multiple binder formats and molecular contexts, providing a common framework for reusing all-atom structural priors in design.

</details>


### [105] [Integrating knowledge from case reports: a medical ontology based multimodal information system with structured summary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19775)
*Shuyu Guo,Lan Huang,Yichen Liu,Hanbin Ma,Tian Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper builds a multimodal, ontology-based information system that turns 52,949 PubMed case reports into structured, searchable clinical summaries (including images and biomedical entities) to help clinicians find relevant rare-disease cases more efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional keyword search in PubMed performs poorly on unstructured, heterogeneous medical case reports, making it hard for clinicians—especially juniors—to efficiently retrieve relevant cases for rare diseases, diagnostics, and treatments, despite a vast number of valuable case reports being available.

Method: The authors collect 52,949 open-access case reports from PubMed (2000–2021) and automatically extract structured clinical summaries, including medical images and biomedical named entities. They then organize this multimodal essential information in a well-designed medical ontology and implement a user interface that supports advanced search and browsing of case reports.

Result: They obtain a large-scale, ontology-structured, multimodal case report database integrating text-derived clinical entities and medical images, along with a functional search and browsing interface tailored to clinical use, especially to assist with case-based reasoning in rare diseases.

Conclusion: A multimodal, ontology-driven information system can transform unstructured case reports into structured, searchable clinical knowledge, thereby improving clinicians’ ability—particularly junior doctors’—to retrieve comparable cases and potentially enhancing rare disease identification and diagnosis.

Abstract: Published medical case reports serve as a crucial medical information carrier, documenting discoveries in rare diseases, diagnostic methods, and innovative treatments. Despite the wealth of clinical knowledge in millions of case reports in the public medicine literature database (PubMed), accessing relevant information efficiently is hindered by the limitations of traditional keyword-based retrieval tools on unstructured and diverse case reports. To address the above issues, we introduce a comprehensive multimodal information system for case reports integrating structured clinical summaries of patients including medical images and biomedical named entities from 52949 open-access case reports published from 2000 to 2021. The multimodal essential information is organized in a well-structured medical ontology. Also, a powerful interface for searching and browsing case reports is designed to assist junior clinicians in retrieving cases effectively and improving the identification and diagnosis of rare diseases.

</details>


### [106] [Contagion on the Trading Floor: How Adversarial Signals Spread in Multi-Agent Trading Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19789)
*Qi Rong Sua,Junhao Dong,Nguyen Duc Thai,Yuqing Wen,Cheston Tan,Yew-Soon Ong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how vulnerable LLM-based multi-agent trading systems are to subtle, black-box adversarial attacks injected via realistic social-media inputs, and shows both the damage such attacks can cause and system designs that improve robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-driven multi-agent trading systems become more common in quantitative finance, their security properties—especially against realistic, black-box adversarial inputs—remain poorly understood. Since these systems often rely on noisy, manipulable data sources like social media, it is crucial to know whether an attacker who can only post plausible messages can still significantly distort trading behavior and performance. The paper aims to fill this gap by formalizing these trading stacks, defining attack models, and quantifying the resulting fragility or robustness.

Method: The authors propose the Generic Multi-Agent Trading System (GMATS), a general framework capturing common LLM-based trading architectures with analyst and coordinator agents. Within this framework, they construct a class of black-box input-only poisoning attacks where the adversary uses an LLM as a content generator to craft seemingly benign social-media posts, subject to a posting budget, which are injected into the analyst’s evidence stream. They then define contagion metrics to track how adversarial inputs spread and influence beliefs and decisions: belief-shift scores at both analyst and coordinator layers, and attack-clean deltas on standard backtest metrics such as Sharpe ratio. Using a safe offline benchmark with historical market and social data, they simulate these attacks across different multi-agent topologies and prompting schemes.

Result: The experiments show that even relatively simple input-only attackers—who do not alter models or internal states but only add plausible social-media posts—can significantly deteriorate the trading system’s performance, notably by materially degrading risk–return profiles and sharply lowering Sharpe ratios in backtests. However, the results also indicate that certain multi-agent designs, including particular coordination structures and prompt configurations, can limit the spread of adversarial influence and preserve performance better under the same poisoning budget.

Conclusion: LLM-based multi-agent trading systems are indeed vulnerable to realistic, black-box, social-media-based poisoning attacks, which can substantially harm their financial risk–return characteristics. Nonetheless, architectural and prompt-level design choices in multi-agent topologies can meaningfully improve robustness by dampening the propagation of adversarial content. The paper underscores the need to treat adversarial robustness as a first-class design goal for LLM trading stacks and provides both a formal framework (GMATS) and evaluation metrics to guide future defensive strategies.

Abstract: Multi-agent trading systems built on large language models (LLMs) are beginning to appear in quantitative finance, yet their robustness to adversarial inputs is largely unknown. We study the vulnerability of LLM trading stacks to black-box, input-only attacks that enter solely via admissible social-media feeds. We introduce the Generic Multi-Agent Trading System (GMATS), a framework that captures modern multiagent trading architectures and instantiate a class of black-box poisoning attackers that treat an LLM as a post generator and inject budget-constrained, plausibly benign social-media content into the analyst's evidence stream. We define contagion metrics that trace how adversarial content propagates through the stack, including belief-shift scores at analyst and coordinator layers and attack-clean deltas on standard backtest metrics. Experiments on a safe offline benchmark with historical market and social data show that even simple input-only attackers can materially degrade risk-return profiles, sharply reducing Sharpe ratios. At the same time, we find that suitably designed multi-agent topologies and coordinator prompts can dampen adversarial shocks and improve average robustness under identical poisoning budgets.

</details>


### [107] [Steering Equilibrium Selection in Regularized Self-Play via the Reference Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19820)
*Luis Leal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper analyzes how regularized self-play with a KL/entropy-regularized reference policy can be used to intentionally select specific Nash equilibria in games with multiple value-equivalent equilibria, rather than just converging to any equilibrium.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In games with a polytope of value-equivalent Nash equilibria, standard self-play or regularized self-play converge to some equilibrium but give little control over *which* one. However, in many applications (e.g., safety, fairness, interpretability, or human preference alignment), different equilibria may have different qualitative properties even if they have the same game value. The authors want to understand and exploit how entropy-regularized self-play implicitly chooses among equilibria via the reference policy, turning this from a side-effect into a controllable selection mechanism.

Method: They study regularized self-play (as in DeepNash) where best responses are computed against a slowly moving, entropy-regularized reference policy ρ. They treat the regularizer as inducing an information projection (I-projection) of ρ onto the Nash equilibrium set and ask whether choosing ρ appropriately can steer convergence toward a desired equilibrium. They conduct controlled experiments on five exactly solvable games plus a 2‑D polytope, using exact best responses and exact equivalence tests over many random seeds. They "anchor" the reference at a specific target equilibrium and then refine, measuring coordinate error, exploitability, and equivalence via TOST, and analyzing how curvature, boundary effects, and family stiffness/flatness affect steering. They also compare table-based and MLP policies and run matched control arms to isolate the effect of attention and reference movement.

Result: Anchoring the reference at a given target equilibrium and then refining reliably steers self-play to that target with very low mean coordinate error (~0.007) and very low exploitability (~5×10⁻⁵), statistically equivalent to the requested target within ±0.05 in TOST tests. The equilibrium selected closely follows the reach-weighted I-projection of ρ onto the Nash set (slope ≈ 0.97 with tight CI). The anchoring effect is robust to initialization and persists through refinement. However, when the reference is fixed off the equilibrium manifold, exploitability increases (0.08–0.25), stiff/flat reference families require smaller mirror steps, and boundary targets tend to undershoot; curvature predicts where boundary saturation becomes problematic (rank correlation 0.90, p=0.037) while interior precision is insensitive to curvature. Table-based and MLP steering maps match within ±0.03 across targets, and control conditions show that any systematic shift from attention is small and statistically insignificant, with attention’s main effect being increased seed variance. The selection–robustness tradeoff collapses against a best response: steering matters only against fixed, non‑equilibrium opponents.

Conclusion: The reference policy in regularized self-play is not just a stabilizing prior but an effective knob for selecting among multiple value-equivalent Nash equilibria. By anchoring the reference at a desired equilibrium and then refining, one can steer learning to that member, effectively implementing the reach-weighted I-projection of the reference onto the Nash set. This reframes the KL anchor used in RLHF-style RL as a controllable equilibrium-selection mechanism, not only a stability device, and clarifies its limitations: off-manifold references, boundary targets, and stiff/flat families introduce exploitability and require careful step-size choices, though interior precision remains high and robust across architectures.

Abstract: Regularized self-play -- the family behind DeepNash's Stratego play -- drives a two-player zero-sum policy to a Nash equilibrium by best-responding to a slowly moving, entropy-regularized reference policy $ρ$. When the game has a polytope of value-equivalent equilibria, the regularizer silently breaks the tie: with a uniform reference it selects the maximum-entropy member, the I-projection of $ρ$ onto the Nash set. Can the reference be used to choose the equilibrium on purpose? On five exactly solvable games plus a 2-D polytope, with exact best responses and equivalence tests over independent seeds, anchoring the reference at a target member and refining steers self-play to that member with mean coordinate error 0.007 at median exploitability $5\times10^{-5}$, TOST-equivalent to the request within $\pm0.05$; the anchoring persists through refinement and follows the reference, not the initialization. Selection follows the reach-weighted I-projection (slope 0.969 [0.950, 0.987]). We report with equal emphasis where the story breaks: fixed off-manifold references cost 0.08-0.25 exploitability; stiff or flat families require a smaller mirror step, set by a pre-registered rule; boundary targets undershoot; curvature predicts where boundary saturation bites (rank correlation 0.90, p=0.037) while interior precision is curvature-independent. Table and MLP steering maps are equivalent within $\pm0.03$ at every target (30 seeds); matched control arms show attention's robust signature is excess seed variance, any systematic shift bounded at 0.018 and not significant. Against a best response the selection-robustness trade-off is degenerate: steering matters only against fixed, non-equilibrium opponents. The recipe -- anchor the reference at the desired member and refine -- reinterprets the KL anchor of RLHF-style RL as a selection knob, not only a stability leash.

</details>


### [108] [Dual-Axis Policy Optimization for LLM Agents: Bayesian Feedback Attribution and Trajectory Mass Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19830)
*Yingxuan Zhuang,Binhe Yu,Jingxiao Yang,Ruopei Sun,Ziting Li,Cheng Tan,Xuhong Zhang,Jianwei Yin,Jintao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BATON is a dual-axis reinforcement learning framework for LLM agents that improves how feedback within a trajectory is attributed to actions and how trajectories are weighted across a batch, yielding better performance across benchmarks and model sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for LLM agents do not cleanly separate or optimally handle two key aspects of optimization: (1) attributing sparse or delayed environment feedback to individual actions within a trajectory, and (2) aggregating and weighting entire trajectories across a training batch. This can lead to inefficient credit assignment and biased learning where a few trajectories dominate updates, limiting performance and scalability.

Method: The paper formalizes two optimization dimensions: Intra-Trajectory Feedback Attribution and Inter-Trajectory Objective Aggregation. It proposes BATON (Bayesian Attribution and Trajectory Objective Normalization). For the first axis, Bayesian Feedback Attribution builds a feedback-conditioned posterior over the sampled actions in a trajectory to better assign credit or blame. For the second axis, Trajectory Mass Normalization (TMN) normalizes optimization mass across trajectories so that each complete trajectory contributes equally to the overall objective, preventing domination by a handful of high-reward or longer trajectories. BATON is implemented on top of existing RL algorithms for LLMs such as GRPO and GiGPO.

Result: On three benchmarks relevant to LLM agents—ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA—BATON is evaluated with GRPO and GiGPO. The experiments show that each axis (Bayesian Feedback Attribution and TMN) individually improves performance over the corresponding baseline algorithms. When both axes are combined, the approach consistently achieves the best performance across different model scales, indicating complementary benefits and robust gains.

Conclusion: Separating and explicitly modeling intra-trajectory feedback attribution and inter-trajectory objective aggregation leads to more effective RL training for LLM agents. BATON’s Bayesian Feedback Attribution and Trajectory Mass Normalization provide independent and complementary improvements, and together yield state-of-the-art or at least consistently superior performance across tasks and model sizes. This suggests that principled treatment of credit assignment and trajectory weighting is a key lever for advancing RL fine-tuning of LLM agents.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for LLM agents involves two distinct optimization di- mensions: how environment feedback is exploited within a trajectory, and how complete trajectories are aggregated across a batch. We formulate these dimen- sions as Intra-Trajectory Feedback Attribution and Inter-Trajectory Objec- tive Aggregation, and introduce BATON (Bayesian Attribution and Trajectory Objective Normalization), a dual-axis policy optimization framework. BATON instantiates the first axis with Bayesian Feedback Attribution, which constructs a feedback-conditioned posterior over sampled actions, and the second with Trajec- tory Mass Normalization (TMN), which assigns equal optimization mass to com- plete trajectories. Experiments with GRPO and GiGPO on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show that both axes provide independent gains and that their combi- nation consistently achieves the strongest overall performance across model scales.

</details>


### [109] [MetaRTL: Meta-path Attention Enhanced Relational Table Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19832)
*Ken Zhong,Weichen Li,Zheng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MetaRTL is a scalable two-stage framework for learning from relational tables that replaces deep GNN stacks with lightweight pre-training and efficient meta-path aggregation to achieve better performance and efficiency on large databases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relational databases are widely used, and there is a growing need to learn effective representations from relational tables for downstream tasks. Existing relational table learning methods often depend on deep (heterogeneous) graph neural network stacks, which are computationally expensive and struggle to scale or perform well on large real-world databases. A more efficient yet expressive method is needed to capture rich relational semantics without incurring heavy computational costs.

Method: MetaRTL uses a two-stage approach. Stage 1 performs lightweight pre-training to obtain initial embeddings for each table, avoiding deep message passing at this stage. Stage 2 applies non-parametric message passing to construct meta-path-based features that capture relational patterns across tables. These meta-path features are then fed into an attention-based aggregation module called MetaAttn, which selectively combines them. The framework effectively shifts the main computation from deep GNN message passing to more efficient meta-path feature aggregation.

Result: On 10 real-world datasets covering 24 different tasks, MetaRTL achieves strong empirical performance, demonstrating both effectiveness and scalability compared to existing deep GNN/HGNN-based relational table learning methods. The experiments suggest that MetaRTL can handle large databases more efficiently while preserving or improving predictive performance.

Conclusion: MetaRTL provides a scalable and expressive alternative to deep GNN-based relational table learning by combining lightweight table-level pre-training with efficient, attention-based meta-path aggregation. The study concludes that this design can capture rich relational semantics at lower computational cost and is validated across multiple real-world datasets and tasks, indicating its practicality for large-scale relational database applications.

Abstract: Relational table learning has gained increasing attention with the widespread use of relational databases. Existing methods typically rely on deep GNN or HGNN stacks, leading to high computational costs and limited performance on large real-world databases. We propose MetaRTL, a two-stage framework for scalable and expressive relational table learning. In the first stage, MetaRTL obtains initial table embeddings via lightweight pre-training. In the second stage, it performs non-parametric message passing to derive meta-path features, which are then aggregated by an attention module, MetaAttn. By shifting computation from deep message passing to efficient meta-path aggregation, MetaRTL captures rich relational semantics while maintaining high efficiency. Experiments on 10 real-world datasets across 24 tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

</details>


### [110] [A Dual-Process Perspective on Nudge Susceptibility in LLM-Based GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19843)
*Haya Halimeh,Sascha Kaltenpoth,Kevin Bösch,Oliver Müller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies whether LLM-based GUI agents are vulnerable to digital nudges in user interfaces and how their reasoning configuration (e.g., chain-of-thought) changes that vulnerability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-based agents increasingly act on real GUIs, they interact with interfaces designed to influence human behavior via nudges. We know LLMs exhibit textual biases, but we don’t know how they react when embodied as agents navigating interfaces. Understanding if and how they are nudged, and whether stronger reasoning reduces or redirects this susceptibility, is important for safe deployment and governance.

Method: The authors ground their study in Dual-Process Theory (Type 1 automatic vs. Type 2 reflective processes). They run a large randomized online shopping experiment with 3,600 GUI agents and 21,600 simulations across six strong LLM models from three providers. Agents interact with interfaces that include automatic default nudges and reflective social influence nudges, under different reasoning configurations (e.g., more or less extensive reasoning) to test susceptibility and moderation effects.

Result: LLM-based GUI agents are susceptible to both automatic (Type 1) and reflective (Type 2) digital nudges. Reasoning configuration moderates this in opposite directions: extensive reasoning reduces vulnerability to automatic default nudges but increases vulnerability to reflective social influence nudges. Exploratory results show that how reasoning redirects nudge effects systematically depends on model scale.

Conclusion: Nudge susceptibility is a behavioral property of agentic LLM systems: they can be systematically influenced by interface design. Increasing an agent’s reasoning does not straightforwardly make it more robust; instead, it changes which kinds of nudges are effective. This makes GUI and choice-architecture design a governance and safety concern for organizations delegating decisions to autonomous LLM-based agents.

Abstract: LLM-based GUI agents increasingly act on behalf of users in digital environments that were designed with human users in mind. These graphical user interfaces were designed to support, but also deliberately steer, the behaviour and decisions of users. While behavioural biases in the textual outputs of LLMs are well-documented, far less is known about how such influence operates when models act as agents that perceive interfaces and execute decisions---and, in particular, whether the reasoning capabilities increasingly built into these agents make them more robust to it. Drawing on Dual-Process Theory, we empirically investigate whether LLM-based GUI agents are susceptible to automatic (Type 1) and reflective (Type 2) digital nudges, and how their reasoning configuration moderates this susceptibility. In a randomized online shopping experiment with 3,600 agents and a total of 21,600 simulations across six frontier models from three providers, we found that agents were vulnerable to both nudge types. Crucially, the reasoning configuration moderated these effects in opposing directions, reducing susceptibility to automatic default nudges while heightening it to reflective social influence nudges. Extensive reasoning therefore did not make agents more robust but redirected the route through which choice architecture takes effect. Exploratory analysis further showed this redirection to be systematically structured by model scale. Beyond establishing nudge susceptibility as a behavioural property of agentic AI, the study positions interface design as a governance concern for organizations that delegate decisions to autonomous agents.

</details>


### [111] [Constraint-Safe Graph-Context Scoring for Stable Point-Feature Labels Under Text-Width and Accessibility-Inspired Profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19848)
*Taimoor Ahmad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes LABELSENSE-Pilot, a learning-based, optimization-backed system for placing point feature labels on interactive maps that balances visibility, stability, and accessibility-related box sizes, achieving high display rates with very low flicker and no collisions in a controlled pilot study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing point-label placement on maps often optimizes only simple geometric conflicts (like overlaps) and ignores several practical needs: (1) high label visibility (display yield), (2) temporal stability across camera motion (reduced flicker), and (3) accessibility and multilingual requirements that change label box sizes. Current algorithmic evaluations typically reduce performance to overlap counts, which does not reflect these richer trade-offs nor the effects of enlarged fonts or different languages. There is a need for a more realistic, auditable pipeline that incorporates learned utilities, temporal consistency, and stress testing of different viewing conditions, while remaining reproducible and clearly scoped as an engineering study rather than a full user-accessibility evaluation.

Method: The system, LABELSENSE-Pilot, generates eight candidate positions (compass points) for each point feature. Each candidate is scored using a multilayer perceptron that operates on summaries of the label’s graph-based context, plus a bonus term for candidates close to previous placements to encourage temporal stability. A mixed-integer optimization (MIP) is then used to select a globally consistent layout subject to constraints. The method explicitly checks each chosen layout for viewport containment, uniqueness of chosen candidate per feature, and pairwise clearance between labels. The experimental setup uses a dataset of 2,500 airports across 155 countries, with splits by country groups and a suite of synthetic stressors: varying spatial density, camera motion, added text suffixes, preference variants, and enlarged font sizes. The model is intentionally described as a scorer with graph-context summaries rather than as a graph transformer, emphasizing engineering transparency over architectural novelty.

Result: Across five random seeds, LABELSENSE-Pilot succeeds in displaying 85.62% of labels, with only 2.09% flicker and no label collisions. Compared to a handcrafted-utility integer programming baseline, it shows a small sacrifice in display yield (1.43 percentage points less) but a substantial reduction in flicker (12.04 percentage points). When using layouts that explicitly account for enlarged label boxes, there are zero proxy violations under larger-font conditions; by contrast, layouts computed with standard geometry and then re-evaluated at 1.5x box size show violations for 52.57% of selected placements, highlighting the importance of incorporating accessibility-aware geometry directly into the optimization.

Conclusion: LABELSENSE-Pilot demonstrates that combining learned candidate scoring, temporal-consistency bonuses, and global optimization yields an effective, auditable trade-off between label visibility, stability, and collision avoidance for point-feature labels on interactive maps. The study shows the benefit of explicitly modeling enlarged-font/accessibility effects in the geometry used during optimization. However, the authors frame this as a pilot engineering study rather than a definitive solution for human accessibility or multilingual usability: the results rely on synthetic stressors and a specific dataset, and the work explicitly notes that proper comparisons with official recent baselines and user studies remain necessary before claiming broader deployment readiness or human-centered impact.

Abstract: Point-feature label placement on interactive maps must reconcile geometric validity, display yield, local placement utility, and stability across camera motion. Accessibility and multilingual requirements further change label dimensions, yet algorithmic evaluations often collapse these concerns into overlap counts. We present LABELSENSE-Pilot, a reproducible prototype that generates eight compass candidates per feature, scores candidates with a multilayer perceptron over graph-context summaries, adds a previous-placement bonus, and selects a layout through mixed-integer optimization. The executed scorer is deliberately not described as a graph transformer. Every returned layout is checked for viewport containment, per-feature uniqueness, and pairwise clearance. Experiments use 2,500 airport coordinates and names spanning 155 countries, with country-grouped splits and generated density, camera, text-suffix, preference, and enlarged-font stressors. Across five seeds, LABELSENSE-Pilot displayed 85.62 percent of labels with 2.09 percent flicker and zero collisions. Versus a handcrafted-utility integer program, LABELSENSE-Pilot sacrificed 1.43 percentage points of display while reducing flicker by 12.04 points. Enlarged-box-aware layouts produced zero proxy violations, whereas standard geometry reevaluated at 1.5x violated 52.57 percent of selected placements. These results establish an auditable engineering trade-off, not human accessibility, multilingual usability, or preference. Official recent baselines and participant evidence remain required before submission.

</details>


### [112] [Reproducibility is not construct validity: LLM measurement of institutionally situated communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19866)
*Veronika Batzdorfer,Carlo Romano Marcello Alessandro Santagiustina*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper shows that even when LLM annotations are extremely consistent with each other, they may still fail to accurately measure the underlying construct they are supposed to capture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers and policymakers increasingly use LLMs to automatically code and measure attitudes or constructs from text (e.g., public consultations). High reproducibility of LLM outputs is often taken as evidence of quality, but it is unclear whether this implies that LLM-derived measures validly capture the intended construct rather than systematic biases or communication differences.

Method: The authors use data from the European Commission's AI Act consultation, where the same stakeholders provided both structured survey responses and free-text submissions. They ask LLMs to annotate the free-text submissions with measures meant to approximate the same constructs measured in the survey (e.g., concern about AI risks). They then compare (1) reproducibility of LLM annotations (intraclass correlations), (2) convergence between LLM-inferred measures and survey-reported measures, and (3) systematic patterns of divergence across stakeholder groups and countries. They also examine whether survey-reported concerns still predict policy preferences (support for explainability) across different divergence levels.

Result: LLM annotations of the free-text submissions are extremely reproducible (intraclass correlations > 0.99). However, the LLM-based measures only weakly converge with the corresponding survey measures of the same nominal construct. Divergence is not random: business associations appear more concerned about AI risks in their text submissions than in their survey answers (effect size g ≈ +1.0), while public authorities and several nonbusiness stakeholders show smaller or even opposite divergences. Spatial analysis indicates positive spatial autocorrelation (Moran's I = 0.347, p = 0.036) in divergence across European countries, implying that neighboring countries tend to have more similar text-based stances on AI safety. Importantly, survey-based concern remains strongly related to support for explainability across all levels of divergence.

Conclusion: The study shows that very high reproducibility of LLM annotations does not guarantee that they accurately measure the intended construct; LLM-based measures can systematically diverge from traditional survey measures depending on stakeholder type and geographic context. Therefore, LLMs as measurement instruments require validation procedures that separately assess (a) reproducibility, (b) construct validity, and (c) how communication context (e.g., strategic or rhetorical differences between survey and free text) shapes what LLMs pick up from text.

Abstract: High annotation reproducibility does not necessarily imply that an LLM-inferred measure captures the construct it is intended to measure. We test this distinction using a dataset from the European Commission's AI Act consultation, linking structured survey responses to free-text consultation submissions from the same stakeholders. LLM annotations of consultation submissions are highly reproducible (intraclass correlations > 0.99), yet show limited convergence with survey-reported measures of the nominal construct they were intended to approximate. Divergence between survey-and LLM-inferred text-based measures varies systematically across stakeholder groups: business associations express greater concern about AI risks in text-based consultations than in survey responses ({g} = +1.0), whereas public authorities and several nonbusiness groups show smaller or negative divergences. Divergences between scores suggest positive spatial autocorrelation across European countries (Moran's I = 0.347, p = 0.036), indicating that stakeholders from neighboring countries tend toward more similar text-based stances towards AI safety concerns. Despite divergence, survey-reported concerns remain strongly associated with support for explainability across all divergence levels. These results demonstrate that LLM annotation reproducibility can coexist with poor construct correspondence and motivate validation procedures that distinguish reproducibility, construct validity, and communication context variation when LLMs are used as measurement instruments.

</details>


### [113] [Physical knowledge on historical data matters more than enforcing physical constraints on the forecast](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19871)
*Etienne Lehembre,Pascal Audigane,Vincent Nguyen,Christel Vrain,Thi-Bich-Hanh Dao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Physics Informed Recurrent Neural Network (PIRNN) that jointly forecasts target time series and latent physical variables, improving robustness and interpretability for systems governed by physical laws, demonstrated on groundwater level prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning has improved time series forecasting, but for systems governed by physical processes, there are two key gaps: (1) most models ignore known physical laws, which can reduce robustness and extrapolation ability, and (2) even Physics Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) typically focus on the target variable and do not estimate intermediate, unobservable physical states that are crucial for domain experts to understand and validate the model behavior. The authors aim to bridge data-driven forecasting and mechanistic modeling, so that forecasts respect known physics and provide insight into hidden states of the system.

Method: They design a Physics Informed Recurrent Neural Network (PIRNN) that integrates physical equations into a recurrent neural architecture for time series forecasting. The network is trained to predict both the observable target time series and the unobservable intermediate physical variables that appear in the governing equations.
