The physical model can involve multiple equations, each with its own set of latent variables; PIRNN incorporates these as structural constraints and additional outputs. As a case study, they instantiate this framework using the Gardenia groundwater model, which represents groundwater dynamics via reservoir transfer equations combined with data assimilation. They perform experiments on 12 real-world groundwater datasets, benchmarking PIRNN against standard neural models and the Gardenia physical model, and conduct an ablation study to quantify the contribution of the physics-based components.

Result: On 5 out of 12 real groundwater level datasets, PIRNN achieves better forecasting performance than both baseline neural networks and the Gardenia physical model, indicating that the hybrid physics-informed design can provide accuracy gains in a substantial subset of cases. The ablation study shows that removing or weakening the physics-informed components degrades performance, highlighting their importance. Additionally, a domain expert evaluates the predicted latent physical variables and finds them physically coherent, suggesting that the model’s internal states have meaningful physical interpretation rather than being arbitrary latent features.

Conclusion: The proposed PIRNN can effectively combine physical knowledge with recurrent neural networks to jointly forecast observable targets and infer unobservable physical states. This yields more robust and interpretable forecasts, particularly valuable in domains like groundwater modeling where physical understanding and expert validation of internal states matter. The method is flexible enough to adapt to different physical models with multiple equations and latent variables. The empirical results and expert assessment support the claim that incorporating explicit physical background into time series forecasting improves both predictive performance and scientific interpretability.

Abstract: Time series forecasting has seen signicant advancements with the emergence of new deep learning models. However, forecasting time series in applications involving physical processes remains a major challenge. Despite the apparition of Physics Informed Neural Networks (PINN), recent models do not estimate unobservable intermediate physical variables, which are important for domain experts to understand the target behavior. To this end, we propose a Physics Informed Recurrent Neural Network (PIRNN) which predicts, along the target, unobservable variables on both historic data and forecast target. This approach enhances the model robustness and results interpretation using domain knowledge. Our method is easily adaptable to any physical model using several equations, each having its own set of unobservable variables, to describe it-self. As a case study, we incorporate physical equations used for groundwater levels predictions by the physical model called Gardenia. This model uses transfers equations between reservoirs, optimized with data assimilation, to simulate the evolution of groundwater levels. Evaluation includes several well known neural network models and the Gardenia model compared on twelve real world datasets. In addition, we study the impact of each component through an ablation study. Our model outperforms other models on ve out of the twelve datasets and our ablation study underlines the importance of having a physical background in our time series forecasting task. Finally, the coherence of the physical variables predicted by our neural network is assessed by a domain expert.

</details>


### [114] [TRACE: Accountable Agentic Retrieval for Source Discovery in Digital Archives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19897)
*Donghan Bian,Marie Puren,Florian Cafiero*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents TRACE, a training-free, agentic retrieval framework tailored for historical archives, achieving strong retrieval performance and cost-efficiency on French Third Republic political discourse corpora.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Historical archives are difficult for retrieval-augmented generation because they contain OCR noise, heterogeneous document types, and require high standards of source traceability for scholarly and institutional use. Existing sparse, dense, or graph-based retrieval methods often lack accountability, are expensive to train or maintain, and are not optimized for these archival constraints. The authors want a system that works well on large digitized historical collections while ensuring reliable, transparent source discovery.

Method: The authors design TRACE, a training-free, agentic retrieval framework that orchestrates retrieval steps rather than relying on new model training or heavy graph construction. TRACE is integrated into DECIDON, a project studying circulation of political discourse between parliamentary debates and the press in the French Third Republic. The system leverages corpus-aware agent design, combining multiple retrieval strategies and decision rules to locate, verify, and trace relevant historical sources across heterogeneous corpora (parliamentary debates and newspapers). They then evaluate TRACE on HistoriQA-ThirdRepublic, a benchmark of 1,752 French historical questions linked to digitized collections from the Bibliothèque nationale de France.

Result: On HistoriQA-ThirdRepublic, TRACE attains R@10 of 0.856 and MRR of 0.653, outperforming several baselines, including sparse, dense, graph-based, and other agentic RAG systems, with particularly large improvements on multi-hop and cross-corpus questions. The framework also demonstrates economic viability, with an estimated cost of about $0.02 per question under a default hosted inference setup, making it suitable for institutions lacking substantial GPU resources.

Conclusion: The study concludes that for large digital libraries and historical archives, a training-free, agentic retrieval framework that emphasizes retrieval accountability and corpus-aware design can outperform heavier training-based or graph-construction methods while remaining cost-effective. TRACE thus offers a practical, deployable solution for scholarly and institutional use cases that demand both high retrieval quality and transparent source traceability.

Abstract: Historical archives pose a difficult retrieval problem for retrievalaugmented generation systems: documents are OCR-degraded, heterogeneous across genres and sources, and require strong source traceability for scholarly and institutional use. We introduce TRACE, a training-free agentic retrieval framework designed for accountable source discovery over historical corpora. The system was developed in the context of DECIDON, an interdisciplinary project on the circulation of political discourse between parliamentary debates and the press during the French Third Republic, involving digitised historical collections and institutional use cases. The prototype is currently deployed internally within the project and accessible to 24 researchers across six partner institutions. We evaluate TRACE on HistoriQA-ThirdRepublic, a benchmark of 1,752 French historical questions over parliamentary debates and newspapers from 1887, with documents derived from Biblioth{è}que nationale de France digitised collections. TRACE achieves R@10 = 0.856 and MRR = 0.653, outperforming sparse, dense, graph-based, and agentic RAG baselines, with the largest gains on multi-hop and cross-corpus questions. At approximately $0.02 per question under the default hosted inference configuration, TRACE also remains economically feasible for heritage institutions, laboratories or companies that cannot rely on costly local GPU infrastructure. These results suggest that, for large digital libraries and archives, retrieval accountability and corpus-aware agent design can provide a practical alternative to heavier training-based or graph-construction approaches.

</details>


### [115] [From "Who Is This User?" to "What Does This Purchase Mean?": A Deployed Pipeline for Semantic User Profiling at Bank Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19928)
*Ryota Mitsuhashi,Tetsuro Morimura,Hirotake Ito*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a scalable way to infer user attributes from transaction histories by running LLM inference on recurring transaction patterns instead of on each user, achieving similar accuracy with far fewer LLM calls and deploying it at large bank scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Running large language models separately on every user’s full transaction history is too expensive when there are millions of users, because the inference cost scales linearly with user count. A more efficient approach is needed that maintains profiling quality while dramatically reducing the number of LLM calls.

Method: They reframe the problem from per-user to per-transaction-pattern attribute inference. The system has three phases: (1) Resolve cleans and normalizes item names, optionally using the web for grounding; (2) Profile applies a single LLM call to each frequent transaction pattern to output categorical labels, free-text attributes, and prevalence estimates for each attribute; (3) Tag clusters and structures the free-text attributes into a queryable database. This way, attributes are inferred at the level of patterns and then used for user profiling.

Result: On the Open e-commerce public dataset, the pattern-based profiling database achieves AUC statistically indistinguishable from an LLM that directly reads each user’s raw history across evaluated attributes. The per-attribute prevalence estimates also discriminate well between positive and negative users. In real-world deployment at a large Japanese bank, the approach profiles tens of millions of users and cuts the number of LLM inference targets by nearly three orders of magnitude compared with a per-user approach.

Conclusion: Pattern-level LLM inference is a practical and highly scalable alternative to per-user inference for profiling from transaction histories. It preserves predictive performance while dramatically reducing computational cost, and it can be deployed in large-scale industrial settings. The released code enables others to reproduce and extend the approach.

Abstract: Per-user LLM inference on transaction histories binds the inference budget linearly to user count, which becomes prohibitive at applied scale. We re-cast attribute inference from per-user to per-transaction-pattern. The pipeline runs in three phases: Resolve abstracts item names with optional web grounding, Profile infers attributes for each frequent pattern, and Tag clusters free-text attributes into a queryable database. In Profile, a single LLM call per pattern emits predefined categorical labels, free-text attributes, and per-attribute prevalence estimates. Because inference runs over patterns rather than users, the budget grows with the pattern count rather than the user count. On the public Open e-commerce corpus, the database is statistically indistinguishable from an LLM that reads each user's raw history directly in AUC across the evaluated attributes, and the prevalence estimates carry discriminative signal between positive and negative users. The pipeline is deployed at a major Japanese bank profiling on the order of tens of millions of users, with close to a three-order-of-magnitude reduction in LLM inference targets versus a per-user pipeline. The code is publicly available on https://github.com/CyberAgentAILab/profiling-agent-open-ecommerce.

</details>


### [116] [Beyond Depth Truncation: Controlled Evaluation of Depth Utilization in Recursive Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19934)
*Ha Van Dau,Thanh Tung Khuat,Nguyen Thanh Dung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper critiques the standard way of measuring whether depth-recurrent language models use their depth and proposes a new diagnostic protocol that cleanly separates different factors affected by depth truncation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Depth-recurrent language models reuse a small stack of layers multiple times, decoupling per-token compute from the total number of distinct parameters. Researchers want to know whether these models actually benefit from additional depth, and they typically test this by truncating depth at inference and examining performance degradation. However, this common metric is flawed because depth truncation simultaneously changes several aspects of the model, so its effect is misinterpreted. A better diagnostic is needed to understand what depth is really doing in such models.

Method: The authors analyze the standard depth-truncation evaluation and identify three distinct changes it induces: fewer block applications, less distinct computation, and an out-of-distribution residual stream for the readout head. They design the Depth Control Protocol (DCP), a set of diagnostic experiments composed of: (1) three positive controls, each isolating one factor while varying the others; (2) a negative control that applies the same interventions to standard dense transformers to ensure any observed behavior is specific to depth-recurrent architectures rather than the protocol itself; and (3) a controlled training intervention to test causal claims. A key experimental manipulation is to keep the total number of block applications fixed while forcing the model to execute only one distinct iteration, which is naturally supported in depth-wise weight-sharing models.

Result: Using DCP, the authors can separately attribute performance changes to the number of block applications, the amount of distinct computation, and the degree of distribution shift in the residual stream seen by the readout head. The protocol reveals that the conventional slope-from-truncation metric does not purely reflect changes in distinct computation depth and therefore can be misleading about how much models actually use their depth. The linchpin experiment—running the full application budget but with a single shared iteration—is only feasible in weight-sharing architectures and demonstrates that repeated application of identical blocks behaves differently from simply having fewer distinct layers.

Conclusion: The standard practice of inferring depth usage from performance under depth truncation is conceptually flawed because it conflates multiple changing factors into one scalar metric. The proposed Depth Control Protocol provides a more principled way to study depth utilization in depth-recurrent language models by experimentally disentangling these factors. This enables more reliable interpretations of how and why such models benefit from depth-wise recurrence and highlights the unique diagnostic affordances of weight-sharing transformer architectures.

Abstract: Depth-recurrent language models iteratively apply a small layer stack, decoupling per-token compute from distinct parameter count. To determine whether such a model genuinely utilizes its depth, both recurrence and layer-pruning literatures rely on a shared evaluation: truncating depth at inference time, plotting quality against retained depth fraction, and reading off the slope. While cheap and training-free, this metric suffers from an unexamined flaw: it extracts a single scalar from an intervention that alters multiple model properties simultaneously. Depth truncation concurrently reduces the number of block applications, decreases the volume of distinct computation performed, and pushes the readout head onto an out-of-distribution residual stream. The observed slope conflates all three factors, yet is conventionally interpreted as reflecting solely the second.
  We propose the Depth Control Protocol (DCP), a diagnostic suite that disentangles these three quantities. DCP comprises three positive controls that isolate each factor while varying the others, a negative control applying the identical interventions to dense transformers to ensure the effect is not an artifact of the measurement protocol, and a controlled training intervention to verify causality. The linchpin control, running the full budget of block applications while executing only a single distinct iteration, is strictly realizable only in depth-wise weight-sharing architectures, since in a dense network repeating a layer yields an entirely different model rather than the same model in an alternative configuration.

</details>


### [117] [MaSCoD: A Multi-Agent Framework for Structural-Context-Guided Candidate Causal Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19944)
*Yudai Nakada,Yuichiro Nishiura,Jin Michael Splichal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MaSCoD, a multi-agent LLM-based framework for causal discovery that explicitly aims to reduce premature omission of plausible causal relations by structurally organizing third variables and local patterns before judging direct edges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based causal discovery methods focus on generating candidate graphs but do not explicitly optimize for avoiding early omission of potentially correct causal relations. Prematurely discarding such relations can hurt recall and downstream inference, and there is little understanding of how context construction (what information is surfaced and organized) affects omission versus false positives. The authors want to treat structural pre-organization and omission control as first-class design objectives and to analyze how different information-supply phases impact recall/precision trade-offs across datasets and LLM backbones.

Method: They design MaSCoD, a multi-agent framework where separate agents first identify and organize candidate third variables and local structural patterns (Phase 1) before another stage performs direct-edge judgments between variable pairs using this structured context (Full setting). They compare this to a No Phase 1 setting where structural hypotheses are instead generated within the judgment step, as well as other baselines. Experiments are run on three benchmark datasets (Auto-MPG, DWD, Sachs) using GPT-5.4 as the main model and GPT-4o for replication. They conduct ablations where only some information components from Phase 1 are supplied, and perform stage-wise analyses (pre- and post-reconciliation) to see at which step edges are lost or retained.

Result: MaSCoD does not uniformly dominate baselines; its benefits depend on dataset and backbone. In all six dataset-backbone combinations, the Full setting (with explicit structural hypotheses before judgment) achieves higher mean Recall and F1 than No Phase 1, but at the cost of higher false-positive rates. Additional retention of true reference edges over all baselines occurs only in some settings (DWD with GPT-5.4 and Sachs with GPT-4o). Ablations reveal that providing both third-variable lists and structural patterns is not always better than providing only one, suggesting nuanced interactions between context components. For GPT-5.4, the recall advantage of Full over No Phase 1 appears already after the direct-edge judgment stage, and a later reconciliation step can actually remove some correct edges, particularly on Sachs.

Conclusion: Pre-organizing structural information—such as candidate third variables and local patterns—before LLM-based edge judgment is a meaningful lever for controlling omission in causal discovery. While this strategy generally improves recall and F1, it introduces more false positives and its advantages are context-dependent across datasets and backbones. The work argues that causal-discovery systems should explicitly design and evaluate how they construct and present context to the judgment model, considering context construction and utilization jointly rather than focusing solely on the judgment phase.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have been applied to causal discovery, but candidate-graph generation rarely treats premature omission of potentially relevant causal relations as an explicit design objective. We propose MaSCoD, a multi-agent framework that organizes candidate third variables and local structural patterns before direct-edge judgment. We evaluate MaSCoD on Auto-MPG, DWD, and Sachs using GPT-5.4 as the primary backbone and GPT-4o for replication. MaSCoD exhibits a dataset- and backbone-dependent retention-selectivity profile rather than uniform superiority. Across all six dataset-backbone settings, Full, which supplies structural hypotheses before direct-edge judgment, achieved higher mean Recall and F1 than No Phase 1, which instead constructs them within the judgment procedure, while also increasing false-positive rates. Additional reference-edge retention over all evaluated baselines was observed on DWD with GPT-5.4 and on Sachs with GPT-4o, rather than uniformly across settings. Partial ablations showed that supplying both information components did not always outperform supplying only one. For GPT-5.4, stage-wise analysis showed that the Full-No Phase 1 retention gap was already present after direct-edge judgment, while reconciliation introduced additional reference-edge loss for Full on Sachs. These findings support structural pre-organization as an explicit design and evaluation target for omission control and motivate evaluating context construction jointly with its utilization in judgment.

</details>


### [118] [Not All AI Agents Are Equal: Characterizing Resource and Performance Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19947)
*Wonmi Choi,Minuk Park,Zhixiong Niu,Yongqiang Xiong,Chuck Yoo,Gyeongsik Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how LLM-based AI agents consume and contend for resources (CPU, memory, disk I/O, and remote LLM latency) across different tasks, and proposes scheduling/CPU-allocation optimizations that significantly reduce latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents use remote LLM APIs together with local tools running in containers. Their performance and cost are affected by intertwined factors: network/LLM latency, local CPU, memory, disk I/O, and concurrency. Existing agent systems largely ignore these resource dynamics and simply run tools/containers naively, which leads to inefficient resource usage and unnecessary latency, especially under concurrent multi-request, multi-task workloads. The authors are motivated to understand and systematically characterize these dynamics and then exploit them to optimize agent serving.

Method: The authors empirically analyze resource usage and latency behavior of LLM-based agents on three representative tasks: retrieval-augmented question answering, web search, and software coding. They run multiple requests and tasks concurrently, measure end-to-end latency, and profile resource usage (CPU, disk I/O, memory, and remote LLM latency) for different tools and tasks. They then identify patterns and bottlenecks, showing that tasks differ greatly in their resource sensitivity and that the same tool can exhibit different dynamics depending on context. Building on these insights, they design and evaluate two system-level optimizations: (1) CPU-aware tool admission, which controls when tools run based on CPU pressure, and (2) task-aware CPU allocation, which allocates CPU resources differently across tasks according to their sensitivity and bottlenecks.

Result: The characterization reveals that agents exhibit a wide spectrum of resource behaviors and that concurrent execution surfaces distinct, task-dependent bottlenecks in CPU, disk I/O, and memory. Importantly, they find that simply using faster LLMs or adding more CPU cores does not always reduce latency because of these shifting bottlenecks. With their proposed mechanisms—CPU-aware tool admission and task-aware CPU allocation—they achieve up to about 5.4× latency improvement for CPU-sensitive agent tasks and about 32% reduction in average latency across multiple tasks relative to unoptimized/native agent setups.

Conclusion: Resource usage in LLM-based agent systems is highly heterogeneous and task-dependent, and naive scaling of LLM speed or CPU capacity is insufficient for performance optimization. By understanding and exploiting fine-grained resource dynamics, particularly via smarter CPU-aware admission control and task-aware CPU allocation, one can significantly reduce latency and improve efficiency for multi-task, multi-request AI agents. This suggests that future agent platforms should incorporate resource-aware scheduling and allocation policies rather than treating tools and containers as black boxes.

Abstract: LLM-based AI agents process user requests through iterative reasoning and tool execution, often involving the invocation of remote LLM APIs with local tool containers. This execution model can make the optimization of agent serving difficult because latency, local resource demand, and container bottlenecks inter-mix across requests. However, the current agent ecosystem runs without much consideration of resource dynamics, which results in significant waste of the precious resources. This paper analyzes the resource inter-mix of AI agents for three representative tasks: retrieval-augmented question answering, web search, and software coding. To this end, we characterize the latency with respect to the resource dynamics of processing multiple requests and tasks concurrently. Our measurements show that agents have a wide range of behaviors depending on tasks, so that even the same tool can differ substantially in resource dynamics. We also find that running multiple requests concurrently exposes task-dependent bottlenecks in resource dynamics such as CPU, disk I/O, and memory. Furthermore, we uncover that faster LLM responses or more CPU cores do not always accelerate agents. Based on these observations, we demonstrate new optimization opportunities that exploit the resource dynamics of tasks: CPU-aware tool admission and task-aware CPU allocation. Our results show that the latency of CPU-sensitive agent tasks improves $\sim$5.4$\times$, and the average latency across multiple tasks is reduced $\sim$32% compared to native agents.

</details>


### [119] [Neuro-Symbolic Agentic AI for Networked Low-Altitude UAVs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19961)
*Yuqi Ping,Tianhao Liang,Nanchi Su,Guangyu Lei,Junwei Wu,Qinyu Zhang,Tingting Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a neuro-symbolic agentic AI (NSAAI) framework to improve reliability, adaptivity, and skill reuse for networked UAV autonomy under uncertainty and intermittent connectivity, demonstrated via an urban fire-inspection case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic AI systems for UAVs struggle with hallucinations, heavy data dependence, and poor generalization when facing uncertain observations, changing environments, and unreliable networks. There is a need for an autonomy framework that can reason over explicit knowledge, ground decisions in evidence, reuse verified skills, and adapt missions while remaining data-efficient and robust in dynamic, partially observed conditions.

Method: The authors conceptualize and analyze a neuro-symbolic agentic AI (NSAAI) framework that combines neural grounding (e.g., perception models), symbolic reasoning and planning, and closed-loop agentic interaction. They identify foundational capabilities (data efficiency, compositional generalization, continual learning, zero-shot transfer) and propose a reference architecture comprising: (1) task and goal management, (2) neuro-symbolic planning, (3) verification and metacognition, (4) skill execution and network interaction, and (5) shared knowledge and memory. They implement an urban fire-inspection scenario in a low-altitude environment simulator (LAESim) to showcase how a UAV uses NSAAI to coordinate sensing and cloud access despite intermittent connectivity, reuse a previously verified image-delivery skill, and meet explicit evidence requirements for mission completion.

Result: In the simulated urban fire-inspection scenario, the NSAAI-based UAV can: (1) adapt to intermittent connectivity by coordinating on-board sensing with opportunistic cloud access, (2) reuse an already verified image-delivery skill rather than relearning or replanning from scratch, and (3) enforce explicit evidence conditions (e.g., proof of inspection) before considering the mission complete. These outcomes demonstrate improved reliability in decision-making, better skill reusability, and more adaptive mission execution compared to less structured or purely neural agent baselines (implicitly suggested, though not quantitatively detailed in the abstract).

Conclusion: The study concludes that neuro-symbolic agentic AI is a promising direction for networked UAV autonomy, enabling reusable skills, evidence-grounded decisions, and adaptive missions under uncertainty and intermittent connectivity. The authors identify future research needs in uncertainty-aware reasoning mechanisms, scalable knowledge and skill expansion, adaptive self-monitoring and metacognition, and the development of standardized evaluation benchmarks for NSAAI-driven autonomous systems.

Abstract: Networked low-altitude unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) need reliable and adaptive decision-making capabilities to operate under uncertain observations, dynamic environments, and intermittent connectivity, while many existing agentic systems remain limited by hallucination risks, data dependence, and weak generalization. This article investigates neuro-symbolic agentic AI (NSAAI) as a framework for combining neural grounding, symbolic reasoning, and closed-loop agentic interaction to support more reliable and adaptive UAV autonomy. We first examine its capability foundations in data efficiency, compositional generalization, continual learning, and zero-shot transfer, and then develop a reference architecture integrating task and goal management, neuro-symbolic planning, verification and metacognition, skill execution and network interaction, and shared knowledge and memory. An urban fire-inspection case implemented in LAESim illustrates how a UAV can coordinate sensing and cloud access under intermittent connectivity, reuse a verified image-delivery skill, and satisfy explicit evidence conditions before completing the mission. The results illustrate the potential of NSAAI to support reusable skills, evidence-grounded decision-making, and adaptive mission execution in networked UAV systems. We further discuss key research directions in uncertainty-aware reasoning, knowledge and skill expansion, adaptive self-monitoring, and standardized evaluation.

</details>


### [120] [Customizable and Jointly Optimized Route Planning: A Deep Architecture Enabling Differentiable Shortest-Path Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19996)
*Rui Zhao,Chao Chen,Longfei Xu,Chenguang Ji,Hengbin Cui,Kaikui Liu,Xiaolong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a deep learning framework that jointly learns route cost functions and a route-ranking model to generate optimal, preference-aware paths using Pareto-optimal route sets and a constrained-optimization loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing route planning relies on hand-crafted heuristic edge costs, which provide no guarantee of optimality for different user preferences. Data-driven methods that use historical trajectories as ground truth suffer from a feedback loop because trajectories are biased by existing navigation services. There is a need for a principled, preference-aware, and optimizable framework that can guarantee better route quality and customization without this bias.

Method: The authors first run a multi-objective Dijkstra algorithm offline to generate the full set of Pareto-optimal routes between origin-destination pairs, treating this as a complete candidate set of feasible routes. They then design a neural network that (1) emulates shortest-path search over this candidate set and (2) performs route ranking in an end-to-end differentiable architecture. Route preference is formulated as a constrained optimization problem over multiple route attributes (e.g., time, distance, cost), and they introduce a custom loss function that optimizes one primary objective while enforcing hard constraints on the others. The whole framework jointly learns both the cost functions and ranking from data.

Result: On real-world datasets, the proposed architecture achieves substantially better performance than existing state-of-the-art methods, both in terms of objective route quality and in its ability to flexibly adapt to different user preferences (customizability).

Conclusion: By combining multi-objective shortest-path computation with a differentiable neural architecture and a constrained-optimization loss, the method overcomes limitations of heuristic costs and feedback-loop-prone trajectory supervision. The framework can jointly learn cost functions and route ranking to produce high-quality, customizable routes aligned with diverse user preferences, demonstrating superior effectiveness on real-world data.

Abstract: With the widespread use of online navigation and ride-hailing services, achieving optimal route planning for diverse user preferences has recently attracted increasing attention. Classic graph algorithms for pathfinding use heuristic cost functions to define edge weight, thus providing no optimality guarantee of route quality. Prior data-driven approaches equating ground truth of the optimal route with user trajectory, which is however moderately influenced by the navigation service, suffers from the feedback loop problem. To address these issues, we propose a deep architecture that is able to jointly optimize cost functions and route-ranking model towards any route preference. First, we run a multi-objective Dijkstra algorithm offline to collect the set of Pareto optimal routes, deeming it as the complete candidate set. Exploiting the property of such a set, we design a neural network structure that emulates shortest-path search and route ranking in an end-to-end differentiable manner. Second, we define route preference as a task of constrained optimization of route attributes, and propose a novel loss function that optimizes a single-objective variable, with other variables strictly under constraints. We conduct extensive experiments on real-world datasets. The results show that our architecture significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods in route quality and customizability.

</details>


### [121] [E-AVI: Evidence-Grounded Multimodal Assessment for Automated Video Interviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20001)
*Haoshen Wang,Dongbo Che,Zeyi Xie,Yuanjie Du,Shicheng Hua,Xingyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: E-AVI is a multimodal, evidence-grounded framework for automated video interview assessment that improves prediction accuracy and provides inspectable, timestamped evidence for scoring and feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated video interview systems combine verbal, acoustic, and visual cues but typically output only numerical scores, offering little transparent, inspectable evidence to justify assessments or support feedback and follow-up interaction. There is a need for models that both perform well and make their decision process more interpretable and practically useful.

Method: The authors propose E-AVI, a framework that: (1) extracts timestamped multimodal evidence segments from video interviews (covering verbal content, audio delivery, and visual behavior); (2) uses dimension-conditioned evidence attention with source-level embeddings to compute assessment scores; and (3) maintains a shared evidence pool that can be repurposed to generate natural-language feedback and answer follow-up questions. They evaluate this framework against fine-tuned multimodal baselines and conduct ablation, evidence-deletion, bootstrap, human-audit, and QA analyses to examine the contribution and reliability of the evidence pathway.

Result: On the RecruitView dataset and a private hospitality dataset, E-AVI consistently achieves higher rank correlation with ground-truth assessments than strong fine-tuned multimodal baselines. Analysis via ablations, evidence deletion, bootstrapping, human audits, and QA shows that the extracted evidence is predictive, grounded in the input, and useful for practical assessment and interactive analysis tasks.

Conclusion: E-AVI improves automated video interview assessment by jointly enhancing predictive performance and interpretability. Its evidence-grounded design yields better ranking accuracy while providing timestamped, multimodal evidence that supports transparent scoring, richer feedback to candidates, and interactive question answering over the assessment process.

Abstract: Automated video interview assessment integrates verbal content, acoustic delivery, and visual behavior, yet numerical predictions alone provide limited inspectable support. We present E-AVI, an evidence-grounded framework that extracts timestamped multimodal evidence and integrates dimension-conditioned evidence attention with source-level embeddings for scoring. A shared evidence pool further supports natural-language feedback and follow-up question answering. On RecruitView and a private hospitality dataset, E-AVI consistently outperforms fine-tuned multimodal baselines in rank correlation. Ablation, evidence-deletion, bootstrap, human-audit, and QA analyses characterize the predictive contribution, grounding, and practical utility of the evidence pathway. Together, these results demonstrate that our proposed E-AVI framework improves predictive performance while providing inspectable support for assessment, feedback, and interactive analysis.

</details>


### [122] [Geopolitical Divisions Across Languages in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20005)
*Maxim Chupilkin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies whether AI chatbots give politically different answers about the war in Ukraine depending on the language of the query, showing systematic geopolitical bias patterns across 112 languages and three major models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As people increasingly use AI chatbots for news and political explanations, it is important to know whether these systems provide consistent information across languages or inadvertently encode geopolitical biases that differ by linguistic or national context. The war in Ukraine is a salient, polarized issue that allows the authors to test how language choice may shape AI-generated political content and whether this maps onto real-world political divisions and information environments.

Method: The authors query three large AI chatbots—GPT, Claude, and Gemini—with twenty evaluative statements about the war in Ukraine, translated into 112 languages. They collect 67,200 model responses and classify them as more Russia-leaning or Ukraine-leaning. They then compare the distribution of leanings across languages and aggregate them by countries’ official languages. Statistical analyses relate these aggregated leanings to external indicators: public favorability toward Russia, UN voting behavior on Ukraine-related resolutions, and levels of aid to Ukraine. Robustness checks include repeating analyses across all three models and re-running tests while omitting individual statement pairs.

Result: The language in which questions are asked systematically affects how the AI systems evaluate the war in Ukraine. Some languages elicit relatively more Russia-leaning responses, others more Ukraine-leaning ones. When response patterns are grouped by official languages of countries, they align with existing geopolitical cleavages: more Russia-leaning model outputs are associated with societies that exhibit more favorable attitudes toward Russia, provide less support for Ukraine in UN votes, and offer less material aid to Ukraine. These correlations appear consistently across GPT, Claude, and Gemini and remain even when particular statement pairs are excluded, suggesting that the effect is not driven by a small subset of prompts.

Conclusion: AI chatbots do not provide politically uniform answers across languages; instead, their responses about the war in Ukraine exhibit systematic geopolitical biases that mirror real-world political alignments. This implies that language-conditioned training data and information warfare can shape how models discuss international conflicts. Consequently, multilingual users may receive politically skewed narratives depending on their language, raising concerns for global information equity, propaganda diffusion, and the governance of foundation models in sensitive geopolitical contexts.

Abstract: People increasingly turn to AI chatbots for news and explanations of world events. But do they receive the same political answers when they ask in different languages? Here we show that the language of a question can change how the same AI systems assess the war in Ukraine. We ask GPT, Claude and Gemini to evaluate twenty statements about the war in 112 languages, collecting 67,200 responses. The balance between Russia-leaning and Ukraine-leaning responses differs across languages. When we group responses by countries' official languages, they follow a pattern resembling worldwide political divisions: relatively more Russia-leaning answers correspond to more favourable public views of Russia, less support for Ukraine in United Nations votes, and less aid to Ukraine. The broad pattern recurs across all three models and remains when individual statement pairs are removed. Our findings suggest a possible route through which information warfare may shape the text used to train AI models, which may in turn spread geopolitical biases.

</details>


### [123] [FedeRICo: Federated Region-Influenced Coupling for Traffic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20026)
*Fermin Orozco,Man Luo,Johan Wahlström*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes FedeRICo, a federated spatial-temporal traffic forecasting framework that addresses cross-client heterogeneity and missing cross-boundary dependencies via gradient-level collaboration and boundary-aware residual communication, achieving better accuracy than prior federated baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In urban traffic forecasting, data is often siloed across stakeholders (e.g., different agencies or companies) that cannot share raw data due to privacy or commercial constraints. Federated learning offers a way to collaborate without centralizing data, but existing federated spatial-temporal methods assume homogeneous graph structures and rely on parameter aggregation. This leads to two issues: (1) aggregating parameters over heterogeneous sensor graphs harms client-specific representations; and (2) partitioning the road network across clients breaks the modeling of traffic propagation across boundaries. The paper is motivated by the need for a federated method that can retain client-specific strengths while still learning transferable forecasting patterns and recovering traffic dependencies across client boundaries.

Method: The authors introduce FedeRICo, a federated traffic forecasting framework with two main innovations. First, it uses a dual-branch forecasting model for each client: a globally guided branch that captures transferable forecasting structure and a private residual branch that captures client-specific corrections. The global branch is trained via gradient-level collaboration, aligning gradients from multiple clients instead of directly averaging parameters, thereby reducing destructive interference between heterogeneous models. Second, to restore cross-client spatial dependencies, FedeRICo designs a boundary-aware residual communication mechanism. It performs trend-residual decomposition on time series observed at boundary sensors, filtering out periodic trends and extracting transient residual signals. These residual boundary messages are then communicated between physically adjacent clients, allowing the model to capture traffic dynamics that propagate across different client subgraphs without sharing raw data or full model parameters.

Result: The framework is evaluated on four real-world traffic forecasting benchmarks. Across these datasets, FedeRICo consistently outperforms state-of-the-art federated spatial-temporal learning baselines in forecasting accuracy. Additionally, its training runtime remains competitive, indicating that the added mechanisms for gradient alignment and boundary residual communication do not incur prohibitive computational overhead.

Conclusion: FedeRICo demonstrates that separating global transferable structure from local residual corrections and enabling boundary-aware residual communication can effectively address heterogeneity and broken spatial dependencies in federated traffic forecasting. The approach provides better predictive performance than existing federated spatial-temporal methods while respecting data-sharing constraints and maintaining practical training efficiency, suggesting a promising direction for federated learning on distributed, heterogeneous graph-structured time series.

Abstract: Urban traffic forecasting often relies on information distributed across stakeholders who may be unable to share raw data due to privacy or commercial constraints, motivating federated spatial-temporal approaches. In such federated settings, each client observes traffic over a distinct sensor subgraph with its own spatial topology and temporal dynamics, leading to significant heterogeneity across clients. Existing federated spatial-temporal methods typically rely on model parameter aggregation and provide limited mechanisms for recovering spatial dependencies across client boundaries. This introduces two key limitations. Specifically, parameter aggregation across heterogeneous graph domains tends to dilute client-specific representations, while road network partitioning breaks the propagation of traffic dynamics across client boundaries. To address these challenges, we propose FedeRICo, a federated traffic forecasting framework that combines gradient-level collaboration with boundary-aware residual communication. FedeRICo employs a dual-branch forecasting architecture in which a globally guided branch captures transferable forecasting structure, while a private residual branch preserves client-specific corrections and incorporates boundary residual signals. The global branch is coordinated through gradient alignment across all clients, enabling collaborative optimisation without destructive parameter interference. To recover cross-client spatial dependencies, boundary messages are extracted through a trend-residual decomposition that suppresses periodic structure and communicates only transient spatial-temporal residual signals between physically adjacent clients. Experiments across four real-world traffic forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that FedeRICo consistently outperforms state-of-the-art federated spatial-temporal baselines while maintaining competitive training runtime.

</details>


### [124] [Can Data Attribution Filter Out Subliminal Learning? Not Reliably](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20027)
*Moritz Weckbecker,Sweta Jena,Jonas Müller,Ponnurangam Kumaraguru,Sebastian Lapuschkin,Wojciech Samek,Louis Jaburi,Gonçalo Paulo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether gradient-based training data attribution methods can identify and filter the data that causes subliminally learned behaviors in language models, and compares them to a strong baseline (divergence tokens).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subliminal learning lets models pick up undesirable behavioral traits from training data that have no obvious semantic connection to those traits, which defeats content-based filtering. There is a need for methods that can identify and remove harmful training examples even when their semantics look benign. Training data attribution is a candidate solution, but its effectiveness in this specific subliminal learning context is not well understood.

Method: The authors study three gradient-based attribution methods—GradCos, a contrastive variant of GradCos, and EK-FAC—across three language models. They compare these methods to divergence tokens, a baseline technique that requires access to counterfactual teacher models and has been shown to localize subliminal learning sources. They test two interventions: filtering specific tokens versus filtering entire training samples, and measure how much these interventions mitigate subliminal learning effects for different model–preference combinations.

Result: When filtering at the token level, EK-FAC meaningfully reduces the subliminal learning effect, whereas GradCos and its contrastive variant provide little benefit. However, even EK-FAC usually performs worse than the divergence token baseline. When filtering at the sample level, all methods are less effective overall, though EK-FAC sometimes produces a stronger signal than divergence tokens. Performance varies unpredictably across models, preferences, and attribution variants, and the authors do not find a clear, unified reason for this inconsistency.

Conclusion: Gradient-based training data attribution can sometimes identify the training data responsible for subliminal learning, but its reliability is limited and method-dependent. EK-FAC appears to be the most promising of the tested approaches, especially for token-level filtering, yet it still does not consistently match the performance of divergence tokens. The findings indicate that while attribution-based filtering has potential for mitigating subliminal learning in settings where semantic inspection fails, current approximations are not robust enough to serve as a standalone safety mechanism.

Abstract: Subliminal learning allows language models to transmit behavioral traits through training data with no obvious semantic relationship to those traits, undermining content-based data filtering as a safety intervention. Training data attribution offers an alternative: it identifies the training examples responsible for a given model behavior, independent of their semantic content, and so may apply in exactly the cases where semantic inspection fails. We evaluate three gradient-based attribution methods (GradCos, a contrastive GradCos variant, and EK-FAC) across three models, comparing them against divergence tokens, a strong baseline previously shown to localize subliminal learning (albeit one that requires access to counterfactual teacher models). Filtering at the token level, EK-FAC mitigates a significant part of the effect, the other methods provide little benefit, and all mostly fall short of divergence tokens. Filtering entire samples is less effective for every method, though EK-FAC often gives a stronger signal than divergence tokens in this setting. Success is inconsistent across methods and settings: variants that work well for some model-preference combinations fail for others, and we do not identify a consistent explanation for these differences. Our results suggest that gradient-based attribution can identify data responsible for subliminal learning in some settings, but that some approximations are more reliable than others.

</details>


### [125] [DART: Distillation-Aware Reparameterization for Training-Free LoRA Reuse in Few-Step Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20051)
*Shihong Li,Juntao Xu,JinCao,Maowen Tang,Jun Huang,Jintao Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce DART, a training-free technique to make LoRA adapters work better when a video diffusion model is distilled to fewer denoising steps, improving both output quality and functional behavior without using the original training videos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Step-distilled video generators use fewer denoising steps for efficiency, but LoRA adapters trained on longer schedules often behave incorrectly or lose their intended effect when reused on these distilled models. Existing checks of parameter/geometry similarity don’t fully explain why adapters fail after distillation, and there is a need for a way to recover adapter performance without expensive retraining or access to source training data.

Method: They analyze how LoRA adapters behave under shortened denoising schedules and observe that similar static parameter geometry can produce different functional behavior. They then propose DART, a training-free method with two main components: (1) low-rank coordinate transport, which maps adapter parameters into a representation better aligned with the distilled model’s denoising trajectory; and (2) target-schedule response calibration, which uses forward evaluations on the distilled model (without training videos) to adjust the adapter’s responses for the new shorter schedule. They evaluate DART and its variants, including a calibration-only version (DART-F), across multiple distilled targets.

Result: On a four-step Wan2.2 distilled target, the calibration-focused variant DART-F increases a joint quality metric from 0.9029 to 0.9227 and improves macro functional retention from a strongly negative value (-0.4644), indicating harmful transfer, to a positive one (+0.1349), indicating better preservation of intended behavior. Ablations show that most of the quality gains come from the response calibration, while the coordinate transport adds further but smaller benefits. Adapter-wise analysis reveals that some adapters gain positive functional effects, while others exhibit attenuated behaviors with reduced negative side effects after applying DART. Similar aggregate improvements appear on two additional distilled targets.

Conclusion: DART demonstrates that training-free adjustment of LoRA adapters can substantially improve both generation quality and functional preservation when reusing them on step-distilled video diffusion models. The findings suggest that evaluations of LoRA reuse on distilled models should jointly consider how well desired functions are preserved and how much negative transfer is avoided, rather than assuming uniform recovery for all adapters. The work highlights that static parameter similarity is insufficient to guarantee functional compatibility after schedule changes and that lightweight calibration can be an effective remedy.

Abstract: Step distillation reduces the cost of video generation, but reusing a LoRA trained for a longer trajectory can alter its functional effect or degrade target quality. Static parameter compatibility offers one perspective on this problem; our observations show that similar measured geometry can coexist with different adapter behavior under a shortened denoising schedule. We propose DART, a training-free method that combines low-rank coordinate transport with target-schedule response calibration using forward evaluations and no source training videos. On a four-step Wan2.2 target, DART-F improves the joint quality score from 0.9029 to 0.9227 and changes macro functional retention from -0.4644 to +0.1349. Component analysis shows that calibration accounts for most of the quality improvement, while coordinate transport provides complementary gains when combined with calibration. Adapter-level results reveal positive functional effects for some adapters and strong attenuation with reduced negative functional effects for others. Evaluations on two additional targets show the same aggregate trend. These results motivate evaluating distilled-model LoRA reuse jointly through functional preservation and negative-transfer avoidance, without assuming recovery for every adapter.

</details>


### [126] [MAGMA-GEN: Validated Recovery Supervision from Ambiguous Failures via Counterfactual Re-Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20056)
*Loan Bernat,Matthieu Grard,Ariane Herbulot,Florent Lamiraux*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAGMA-GEN is a data-generation pipeline for hierarchical robots that turns ambiguous failed rollouts into useful supervised recovery data, improving long-horizon manipulation performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In hierarchical long-horizon manipulation, high-level decisions control stochastic low-level skills, making failures ambiguous: they could stem from bad decisions, partial observability, or execution noise. Supervised learning lacks labeled recovery data from such failure states, while reinforcement learning has difficulty due to sparse rewards and credit assignment issues. A method is needed to automatically mine informative recovery supervision from the robot’s own failures without extensive human labeling.

Method: MAGMA-GEN is an on-policy data-generation pipeline. When a rollout fails, a privileged coach module hypothesizes where in the sequence a high-level decision went wrong and proposes localized corrective or recovery actions. Because these hypotheses may be wrong, each candidate correction is validated by re-executing from the same state under matched conditions. Only those corrections that demonstrably improve downstream progress are retained, turning ambiguous failures into labeled recovery examples for supervised training of the high-level policy.

Result: On interactive long-horizon manipulation tasks, MAGMA-GEN yields better task success rates and stronger recovery behavior than baselines such as policy distillation and trajectory-repair approaches. These gains hold under changing task constraints and are demonstrated both in simulation and on real robots.

Conclusion: Automatically converting ambiguous failed rollouts into validated recovery supervision allows hierarchical robotic systems to learn robust high-level decision-making without dense human demonstrations. MAGMA-GEN effectively leverages a privileged coach and re-execution-based validation to improve long-horizon manipulation performance and adaptability in evolving task settings.

Abstract: Hierarchical robotic systems executing long-horizon manipulation tasks must make high-level semantic decisions that orchestrate stochastic low-level skills. In this setting, failed rollouts are ambiguous: a poor downstream state may reflect an invalid high-level decision, partial observation, or a valid decision whose physical execution failed. Traditional supervised learning lacks data for such recovery states, while reinforcement learning struggles with sparse rewards and non-local credit assignment. We propose MAGMA-GEN, an on-policy data-generation pipeline that converts ambiguous failed rollouts into validated recovery supervision. MAGMA-GEN first uses a privileged coach to hypothesize an early decision-level error and propose localized correction or recovery actions. Because this diagnosis is fallible, candidates are retained only if re-execution from the same state under matched conditions improves downstream progress. This produces supervised examples from the agent's own failure distribution without per-step human demonstrations. Evaluated on interactive long-horizon manipulation tasks, MAGMA-GEN improves task success and recovery capabilities, against distillation and trajectory-repair baselines under evolving task constraints in both simulation and real-robot execution.

</details>


### [127] [WiCleanData: Guaranteeing the Type Consistency of Wikidata by Taxonomy Refinement and Constraint Enforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20057)
*Yiwen Peng,Marc Jeanmougin,Thomas Bonald*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents WiCleanData, an automatically cleaned version of Wikidata with a consistent taxonomy and no type constraint violations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wikidata, being collaboratively edited, accumulates errors and structural issues such as redundant or ambiguous classes, incorrect taxonomic paths, and violations of type constraints. Manual curation cannot scale to the size and dynamism of Wikidata, so there is a need for an automated way to obtain a cleaner, structurally consistent version suitable for exploration and downstream tasks.

Method: The authors design an automated pipeline with three main stages: (1) taxonomy cleaning assisted by a language model to fix issues like instance/class ambiguity and wrong subclass paths; (2) simplification of type constraints via hierarchical aggregation so that constraints become more manageable and coherent; and (3) filtering of Wikidata facts so that the resulting knowledge graph respects all the simplified type constraints. This yields a refined graph that is structurally consistent by construction.

Result: They obtain WiCleanData, a refined knowledge graph derived from Wikidata that has a consistent taxonomy and contains no type constraint violations. This resource is published via a publicly accessible web interface, allowing users to browse and query the cleaned graph and use it as input for other applications.

Conclusion: Automated, LLM-assisted cleaning of Wikidata’s taxonomy and type constraints can produce a large-scale knowledge graph that is much more consistent and reliable than the original. WiCleanData serves as both a practical resource for downstream tasks and a proof of concept that systematic, pipeline-based curation can address structural quality problems in collaboratively maintained knowledge bases.

Abstract: Because of its collaborative nature, Wikidata suffers from errors, in- consistencies, and excessive complexity, such as redundant classes, ambiguity between instances and classes, wrong taxonomic paths, and type constraint violations. The manual curation of these issues is infeasible at scale. To address these challenges, we introduce WiCleanData, a refined version of Wikidata with a consistent tax- onomy and free from type constraint violations. Specifically, we have designed an automated pipeline that first cleans the taxonomy with language model assistance, then simplifies type constraints by hierarchical aggregation, and finally filters facts accordingly. The resulting knowledge graph, free from any type violation, is made publicly available via a Web interface, enabling easy exploration and downstream applications.

</details>


### [128] [FCA-Guided Counterfactual Explanations for Multi-Modal Breast Cancer Diagnosis: A Framework Achieving Perfect Validity with Emergent Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20067)
*Abdullahi Isa,Souley Boukari,Muhammad Aliyu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an FCA-guided counterfactual framework that produces valid, sparse, and proximate counterfactual explanations for multi-modal breast cancer diagnosis models, outperforming existing counterfactual methods and introducing a structurally novel source of sparsity from lattice topology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although deep learning models for multi-modal breast cancer diagnosis are highly accurate, they lack clinically acceptable, actionable explanations. Existing attribution-based explanation methods like LIME and SHAP cannot generate counterfactuals and therefore cannot be assessed on counterfactual quality metrics, limiting their clinical usefulness. There is a need for counterfactual explanations that are valid, sparse, proximal, and grounded in a principled structure to support clinical decision-making.

Method: The authors introduce the FCA-Guided Counterfactual (FCA-CF) framework, which uses a Formal Concept Analysis (FCA) concept lattice as a hard structural constraint for searching counterfactuals in a multi-modal TCGA-BRCA dataset. The framework generates counterfactuals that flip model predictions while restricting changes to those consistent with the lattice structure. It is benchmarked against four genuine counterfactual methods—Wachter-style CF, DiCE, FACE, and NICE—on 60 benign-predicted cases. An ablation study evaluates the contribution of the FCA lattice constraint and a Phase C greedy refinement step to sparsity and other metrics.

Result: FCA-CF achieves perfect validity (Validity = 1.0000), changing the model’s prediction in 100% of evaluated instances. It reaches the best sparsity among all valid methods with an average of 2.37 features changed, and achieves a proximity score of 0.900 (normalized L2), tying with NICE as the best. The underlying classifier is strong, with Accuracy = 0.980, F1 = 0.976, and ROC-AUC = 0.9947. Ablation analysis shows that removing the FCA lattice constraint increases sparsity by 40% (p < 0.001, Cohen’s d = 0.78), and disabling the Phase C greedy refinement increases sparsity by 113% (p < 0.001, d = 5.01), indicating their major contributions to explanation compactness.

Conclusion: The FCA-CF framework yields counterfactual explanations that are simultaneously perfectly valid, highly sparse, and highly proximate, achieving a Pareto-dominant trade-off over competing counterfactual methods. Its key novelty is that sparsity emerges from the structural properties of an FCA lattice rather than from manually tuned numerical penalty terms. This represents a new, structurally grounded approach to generating high-quality counterfactual explanations in medical AI, with strong potential for clinically actionable interpretability.

Abstract: Deep learning models for multi-modal breast cancer diagnosis achieve high predictive accuracy but remain clinically unacceptable without actionable, counterfactual explanations. Attribution-based methods (LIME, SHAP) are categorically inapplicable to this purpose, as they generate no alternative instances and thus cannot be evaluated on counterfactual quality metrics. This investigation provides empirical evidence that FCA-Guided Counterfactual (FCA-CF) framework that uses a Formal Concept Analysis (FCA) concept lattice as a hard structural constraint on counterfactual search, operating over a multi-modal TCGA-BRCA dataset. We benchmark against four genuine counterfactual methods: Wachter-style CF, DiCE, FACE, and NICE, evaluated on 60 benign-predicted TCGA-BRCA instances. The FCA-CF framework achieves Validity = 1.0000 (100% of counterfactuals successfully flip the prediction), Sparsity = 2.37 features changed (best among all valid methods), and Proximity = 0.900 (normalised L2-based, matching NICE as joint best). The classifier achieves Accuracy = 0.980, F1 = 0.976, ROC-AUC = 0.9947. Ablation analysis confirms that the FCA lattice constraint is the primary sparsity driver (removing it increases sparsity by +40%, p < 0.001, Cohen's d = 0.78), while Phase C greedy refinement accounts for the largest individual contribution (+113% sparsity increase when disabled, p < 0.001, d = 5.01). FCA-guided counterfactual generation achieves a clinically important Pareto-dominant outcome; it is simultaneously the sparsest and among the most proximate of all valid methods, with perfect validity. The emergent sparsity property arising from lattice topology rather than numerical penalty terms constitutes a structurally novel contribution to the counterfactual explanation literature.

</details>


### [129] [Marginal utility, matrix factorization, and the Key-Value (KV) cache: a unified information-economic framework for sovereign geo-mining inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20068)
*Caroline Gans Combe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Connects marginal utility from economics with matrix factorization and transformer KV-cache management, and applies this to information extraction from geo-mining documents with both theoretical and empirical results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a unified theoretical framework that explains how model capacity (latent factors, representation dimensions, cache entries) should be allocated under resource constraints, and to use this understanding to design efficient, accurate information-extraction systems for specialized corpora like geo-mining documents.

Method: Derive a mapping between economic marginal utility and (1) singular value spectra of rating matrices, (2) eigenvalue spectra of projected covariance operators for learned representations, and (3) cache eviction/low-rank compression decisions in KV-caches. Show that all correspond to a common allocation rule based on eigenvalues and a shadow price. Then instantiate this framework in a geo-mining information-extraction pipeline, proposing a multi-pass inference protocol, a layer-wise TIES (model-merging) procedure, and a selection policy that optimizes a scalarized objective including extraction quality, localization drift, energy, and a CVaR term on drift. Evaluate with a compact hierarchical classifier and diagnostics of TIES merging behavior.

Result: (1) Theoretical result: matrix spectra and cache management can be interpreted as marginal utility schedules, yielding a single eigenvalue-threshold allocation rule. (2) Empirical result: a small (11.2M parameter) hierarchical classifier, trained in ~5 minutes on one GPU, achieves 90% level-1 accuracy on a 973-document uranium-exploration corpus, close to a 92% proprietary model, but at drastically lower latency (2.62 ms vs ~2000 ms) and cost. (3) Diagnostic result: uniform-density TIES merging shows a degenerate mode where outputs collapse to identical tokens across distinct districts with high confidence; this pathology disappears when redoing TIES merging with layer-wise calibrated densities. The full LoRA-based extraction benchmark is outlined but not fully executed.

Conclusion: Marginal utility offers a unifying lens for understanding factor selection in matrix factorization, representation learning, and transformer cache management, all governed by an eigenvalue-threshold rule under constraints. This theoretical bridge informs practical design choices in structured information extraction from specialized corpora, enabling efficient, low-latency models that nearly match proprietary systems and revealing subtle failure modes in model merging that can be mitigated via better density calibration. Some proposed large-scale experiments remain prospective, indicating directions for further empirical validation.

Abstract: This paper builds a theoretical bridge between the economic notion of marginal utility and two machine-learning constructs, matrix factorization and the Key--Value cache of transformer language models. The singular value spectrum of a rating matrix is shown to be a diminishing marginal utility schedule for latent factors, the eigenvalue spectrum of the projected covariance operator to be the marginal utility schedule of a model's learned representation, and cache eviction and low-rank cache compression to be instances of constrained utility maximization under a memory budget. The three collapse into a single allocation rule: retain the top dimensions whose eigenvalue exceeds the shadow price of the binding constraint. The framework is applied to the automated extraction of structured information from geo-mining documents, where it motivates a multi-pass inference protocol, a layer-wise TIES model merging procedure, and a selection policy combining extraction quality, localization drift and energy, scalarized with a Conditional Value-at-Risk term on drift. Two empirical contributions are reported. An 11.2-million-parameter hierarchical classifier, trained in about five minutes on a single GPU, reaches 90.0 per cent level-1 accuracy on a held-out test set from a 973-document uranium-exploration corpus, against 92.0 per cent for a proprietary model on a fifty-document human audit of the same corpus, at a latency of 2.62 ms per card against approximately 2,000 ms for the API and at negligible cost. A diagnostic of uniform-density TIES merging exposes a reproducible degenerate mode in which the merged model returns token-identical outputs across five geographically distinct districts while declaring high confidence; re-executing the merge under layer-wise calibrated densities removes that signature on the diagnostic sample. The full-scale extraction benchmark, including LoRA fine-tuning, is reported as projected rather than measured and remains an empirical extension of this work.

</details>


### [130] [Tailored to you: longitudinal effects of personalising language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20077)
*Canfer Akbulut,Justine Breuch,Arianna Manzini,Lujain Ibrahim,Matija Franklin,Roma Patel,Iason Gabriel,Kristian Lum,Laura Weidinger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper experimentally studies how different approaches to personalising language models affect users’ attitudes and behaviours over repeated advice-seeking interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As personalised language models become more common, designers assume they will better meet diverse user needs. However, there is little empirical evidence about how sustained use of personalised models changes users’ perceptions of AI, their self-perception, and their relationships with others, especially beyond the immediate interaction. This gap raises questions about potential unintended psychological and social side effects of personalisation.

Method: The authors conducted a five-day user study with 992 participants who engaged in daily advice-seeking conversations with language models. They compared three conditions: (1) a non-personalised baseline, (2) a memory-based personalised model conditioned on prior conversational history, and (3) a survey-based personalised model conditioned on pre-study intake survey data. They measured changes over time in interaction patterns, attitudes, and behaviours (e.g., self-disclosure, perceived creepiness, regret over sharing information).

Result: The study shows that many temporal changes in how people interact with language models are primarily due to repeated exposure rather than personalisation. Nonetheless, personalisation types have distinct effects: memory-based personalisation increased users’ self-disclosure and reduced perceived creepiness of the model, whereas survey-based personalisation led participants to feel more regret about having shared personal information. These effects differentiate not only personalised vs. non-personalised models but also between forms of personalisation.

Conclusion: Personalisation is not a monolithic concept; different implementation strategies have nuanced and sometimes opposing effects on user attitudes and behaviours. Designers should not assume that any form of personalisation is universally beneficial or benign. Responsible deployment of personalised AI requires careful consideration of how specific personalisation mechanisms influence trust, comfort, self-disclosure, and potential regret, particularly over longer-term use.

Abstract: Interest in developing personalised language models is rapidly growing. While personalisation is often viewed as a mechanism to better serve diverse user needs, the effects of sustained interactions with personalised models on people's perception of and behaviour toward AI remain poorly understood. Most critically, downstream consequences outside the immediate human--AI interaction loop, such as effects on users' self-perceptions and interpersonal relationships, remain largely unexamined. In this study, we recruited 992 participants to complete daily advice-seeking interactions with language models over the course of five days, comparing outcomes from a non-personalised baseline against two personalisation approaches: memory-based (conditioned on prior conversational history) and survey-based (conditioned on information collected through a pre-study intake survey). We find that several changes in human-AI interaction over time are driven primarily by repeated exposure rather than personalisation itself. However, participants interacting with personalised models experienced differences in advice-seeking and information-sharing attitudes and behaviours: participants in the memory-based condition engaged in greater self-disclosure and rated the model as less creepy, while participants in the survey-based condition reported higher regret about having shared personal information with the AI. We conclude by highlighting the nuanced effects of different personalisation approaches on interaction outcomes, and discussing the implications of these findings for the responsible design and deployment of personalised AI systems.

</details>


### [131] [A Proposal for an Agentic AI Architecture to Support Multi-Domain Decision-Making in the Brazilian Armed Forces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20080)
*Gioliano de Oliveira Braga,Sidnei Barbieri,Ágney Lopes Roth Ferraz,Wagner Comin Sonaglio,Henrique Curi de Miranda e Lourenço Alves Pereira*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a conceptual Agentic AI architecture to autonomously support decision-making in complex multi-domain military operations, specifically for the Brazilian Armed Forces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI systems in defense are reactive, isolated, and heavily dependent on humans to integrate information and decide on actions, which is inadequate in high-volume, high-velocity multi-domain environments that strain the OODA loop.

Method: The authors design and describe a conceptual Agentic AI architecture capable of planning, accessing heterogeneous data sources and sensors, executing tools, and acting autonomously while remaining auditable. They outline how this architecture can be applied across different military branches and command levels, specifying data, sensor access, and security/permission requirements.

Result: The paper identifies four main application areas—decision support, situational analysis, feasibility studies, and countermeasure suggestion—and maps how the proposed architecture could operate in each, including required integrations and safeguards. As a conceptual work, it does not report empirical performance metrics but rather a structured framework and requirements.

Conclusion: The proposed Agentic AI architecture could enhance the speed, quality, and autonomy of decision-making across Brazil’s Navy, Army, and Air Force, provided that robust data access, security controls, and permission mechanisms are implemented to ensure responsible and auditable use in administrative through tactical contexts.

Abstract: The growing complexity of multi-domain operational environments (land, aerospace, naval, cyber, and electromagnetic spectrum) has increased the volume and velocity of data reaching command-and-control (C2) centers, straining the observe-orient-decide-act (OODA) decision cycle. Artificial Intelligence (AI) systems currently employed in defense are, in general, reactive and isolated tools that still rely heavily on human operators to integrate information, assess scenarios, and formulate courses of action. This paper proposes a conceptual Agentic AI architecture for AI systems that can plan, access data sources, execute tools, and act autonomously and audibly, aimed at supporting decision-making across the three Brazilian Armed Forces (Navy, Army, and Air Force). Four application fronts are discussed (decision support, situational analysis, feasibility studies, and countermeasure suggestion), as well as the data and sensor access requirements and the security and permission safeguards necessary for responsible employment across administrative, strategic, operational, and tactical contexts.

</details>


### [132] [UnifiedPlayers: Enhance Tool-Integrated Reasoning in Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20089)
*Wenjie Liao,Liangjie Zhao,Zehong Cao*

Main category: cs.AI
