TL;DR: The paper proposes UnifiedPlayers, a cooperative multi-agent framework that jointly optimizes planning, execution, and evaluation to build self-improving tool-using agents, achieving better reasoning and verification than prior self-evolving approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-evolving/tool-using agents reduce reliance on human-labeled data but usually decouple trajectory generation from evaluation. They depend on static verifiers that cannot adapt to new failure modes, or on self-consistency signals that can amplify shared mistakes. Jointly adapting planning, execution, and evaluation could address these limitations, but creates coordination problems because each component’s learning signal keeps changing. The paper aims to solve this coordination issue so that all components can co-evolve productively.

Method: The authors introduce UnifiedPlayers, a framework with three cooperative agents (players): (1) a Planning Player that generates tasks; (2) an Execution Player that performs multi-turn reasoning and issues Python tool calls; and (3) an Evaluation Player that designs executable verifiers to assess trajectories. They define role-specific rewards aligned under a shared objective and train the players jointly using GRPO (a policy-gradient-style reinforcement learning method). The evaluation player’s learned verifiers act as adaptive critics that guide the other players’ learning, replacing static or purely self-consistent evaluation schemes.

Result: On twelve reasoning benchmarks across two model backbones, UnifiedPlayers improves performance over strong prior self-evolving baselines, with at least +3.5% on mathematical reasoning tasks and +3.9% on general reasoning benchmarks. The learned verifier detects adversarial cases with 84.2% accuracy and produces reward signals with 2.03× higher per-question variance than a self-consistency baseline, indicating more discriminative and informative feedback for learning.

Conclusion: Cooperative co-training of specialized planning, execution, and evaluation agents yields more effective self-improving tool-integrated systems than approaches that separate these components or rely on static/self-consistency-based evaluation. Adaptive learned verifiers not only enhance robustness to adversarial inputs but also provide richer, more informative reward signals, pointing to multi-player cooperation as a fruitful direction for building more capable self-evolving reasoning agents.

Abstract: Self-evolving methods reduce the need for human-annotated trajectories by allowing tool-using agents to generate their own training data. Yet existing methods typically separate trajectory generation from evaluation, relying on static verifiers that cannot adapt to emerging failure modes or self-consistency signals that may reinforce errors shared across trajectories. Jointly adapting planning, execution, and evaluation offers a promising alternative, but introduces a fundamental coordination challenge: each component continuously changes the data or feedback used to train the others. We address this challenge with \textbf{UnifiedPlayers}, a cooperative framework comprising a Planning Player that generates tasks, an Execution Player that produces multi-turn trajectories with Python tool calls, and an Evaluation Player that constructs executable verifiers. We design role-specific rewards that coordinate the three players toward a shared learning objective under GRPO. Across two model backbones and twelve reasoning benchmarks, UnifiedPlayers outperforms the strongest prior baseline by at least 3.5\% on mathematical reasoning and 3.9\% on general reasoning tasks. Moreover, the learned verifier achieves 84.2\% adversarial detection accuracy, while its reward signal exhibits 2.03$\times$ higher per-question variance than a self-consistency baseline, providing more discriminative verifications. These results highlight cooperation among specialized players as a promising path toward self-enhanced tool-integrated agents.

</details>


### [133] [Solving Minimum Span Antibandwidth and Cyclic Antibandwidth Labeling Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20091)
*Hieu Truong Xuan,Khanh To Van*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces and studies the minimum-span versions of the antibandwidth and cyclic antibandwidth labeling problems and proposes SAT-based exact algorithms that are highly competitive with leading MIP/CP solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical antibandwidth and cyclic antibandwidth problems focus on maximizing the minimum distance between labels on adjacent vertices, but the complementary perspective—fixing a required minimum distance and minimizing the overall label span—has been largely unexplored. There is a need for effective exact methods for these minimum-span variants and for understanding how they compare with state-of-the-art optimization tools like CPLEX and Gurobi, especially on standard benchmark instances.

Method: The authors define the Minimum Span Antibandwidth Labeling (MSABL) and Minimum Span Cyclic Antibandwidth Labeling (MSCABL) problems, where a minimum (cyclic) distance between labels is prescribed and the objective is to minimize the label span. They develop a unified Boolean SAT-based framework that encodes these problems as a sequence of decision SAT instances and exploits the monotonicity of feasibility with respect to the span to guide search. Two SAT strategies are studied: (1) parallel SAT solving, where multiple candidate spans are checked simultaneously; and (2) incremental SAT solving, where a single SAT instance is progressively tightened by restricting the label domain while reusing learned information. Both formulations are implemented and run on benchmark graphs from the Harwell-Boeing collection.

Result: On these benchmarks, the SAT-based approaches produce solutions that are highly competitive in quality with those obtained by CPLEX Constraint Programming (CPLEXCP), CPLEX Mixed Integer Programming (CPLEXMIP), and Gurobi MIP. For MSCABL, the parallel SAT strategy achieves the best overall performance, while for MSABL, the incremental SAT strategy performs best. When a no-hole constraint on labels is added, the SAT methods remain competitive with CPLEXCP and significantly outperform CPLEXMIP and Gurobi, particularly for the cyclic variant MSCABL.

Conclusion: The study shows that SAT solving is an effective and competitive exact optimization approach for the newly defined MSABL and MSCABL problems. By leveraging monotonicity and advanced SAT strategies (parallel and incremental), the framework can match or surpass leading general-purpose MIP and CP solvers on standard benchmarks, indicating that SAT-based formulations are a promising direction for tackling minimum-span graph labeling problems.

Abstract: The Antibandwidth and Cyclic Antibandwidth problems are NP-hard graph labeling problems that aim to maximize the minimum (cyclic) distance between labels assigned to adjacent vertices. Extensive research on these problems has resulted in a variety of mathematical formulations and computational approaches. However, their minimum span perspective, in which a prescribed minimum (cyclic) distance is fixed and the objective is to minimize the label span, has received comparatively little attention. In this paper, we consider this complementary perspective by introducing the Minimum Span Antibandwidth/Cyclic Antibandwidth Labeling (MSABL/MSCABL) problems and developing a unified Boolean Satisfiability (SAT)-based framework for solving them. The SAT-based framework formulates MSABL/MSCABL as a sequence of decision problems and exploits their monotonicity to accelerate the search process. We also consider two SAT solving strategies, parallel and incremental SAT solving: the former examines multiple candidate spans concurrently, while the latter reuses a single SAT instance while progressively restricting the label domain. The proposed approaches are evaluated on benchmark instances from the Harwell-Boeing Sparse Matrix Collection and compared with CPLEXCP, CPLEXMIP, and Gurobi. The results show that SAT-based approaches are highly competitive in solution quality, with the parallel approach performing best overall for MSCABL and the incremental approach for MSABL. With the no-hole constraint, they remain competitive with CPLEXCP and significantly outperform CPLEXMIP and Gurobi, particularly for MSCABL. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of SAT solving as an exact approach for MSABL and MSCABL.

</details>


### [134] [Perception, Layout, and Validation: Calibrated Confidence for Reliable Straight-Through Processing of Financial Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20110)
*Yichao Jin,Yushuo Wang,Yuxuan Han,Kwan Ching Yee Sonia,Weiyang Song,Chiu Jin-Chun Kent,Wong Chong Hwee,Wong Tiong Kiat,Kenneth Zhu Ke,Jingyuan Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new confidence-scoring layer for vision-language models (VLMs) that enables safe straight-through processing (STP) of financial document field extraction, greatly improving error control and auto-approval rates over native VLM confidence signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In financial document processing, organizations want to automatically approve extracted key-value fields (straight-through processing) without human review, but this requires well-calibrated confidence estimates and strict control over residual errors. Modern VLMs can extract fields from documents but their verbalized or built-in confidence scores are poorly correlated with actual correctness, making them unreliable for risk-sensitive, production-grade STP. There is thus a need for a more interpretable, accurate, and reliably calibrated confidence mechanism tailored to document understanding tasks.

Method: The paper adds a decomposed confidence layer on top of existing VLM field extraction. This layer separately models three interpretable confidence channels: (1) perception (how visually certain the model is about the content it sees), (2) layout (how consistent the prediction is with the document’s structural/layout cues), and (3) validation (how well the extracted values conform to expected business rules or semantic constraints). These channel scores are then combined and passed through a conformal risk control procedure to obtain a final calibrated confidence score that offers a guaranteed bound on the error rate of auto-approved fields. The approach is tested with two different VLM families (Qwen3.6-27B and Gemini-3.1-Flash-Lite) across three datasets of real and synthetic invoices and ad-buy forms.

Result: Across all tested datasets and VLMs, the decomposed confidence score shows a much stronger separation between correct and incorrect extractions than native VLM verbalized confidence. The AUROC of confidence-vs-correctness increases from 0.54–0.74 with VLM native signals to 0.90–0.99 when using the proposed three-channel decomposition. Under a target residual error rate below 10% enforced via conformal risk control, native VLM confidence can safely auto-approve only 0.1%–7.0% of fields. In contrast, the proposed method can auto-approve 49%–72% of fields while empirically keeping the error rate of the approved tier at or below the target threshold.

Conclusion: A decomposed, interpretable confidence layer combined with conformal risk control can transform generic VLMs into practical, risk-controlled components for financial document straight-through processing. By leveraging perception, layout, and validation signals, the method yields highly calibrated confidence scores that significantly increase the proportion of fields that can be auto-approved at a fixed low error rate, making industrial deployment of VLM-based document extraction more viable.

Abstract: Straight-through processing (STP) on extracted key-value fields from financial documents without human review requires a calibrated probability together with a bounded guarantee on the residual error of the auto-approved tier. The emergence of modern Vision Language Models (VLMs) provides an out-of-the-box capability for extracting the key-values, but their verbalized confidence signals are unreliable and weakly track field correctness. This paper introduces a decomposed confidence layer along three interpretable channels, including perception, layout, and validation. Together with a final conformal risk control, the score can be used for reliable STP of financial documents. The method is validated on three public datasets covering real invoices, synthetic invoices, and ad-buy forms, using two different VLM families (Qwen3.6-27B and Gemini-3.1-Flash-Lite). Our decomposed score consistently improves the separation of correct from incorrect extractions, substantially raising the AUROC from 0.54-0.74 for VLM verbalized signals to 0.90-0.99 with contributions from all three designed channels. Crucially for industrial deployment, this enables usable STP. The native VLM confidence signals could clear only 0.1%-7.0% of fields under risk control at a target error of <10%. In contrast, the proposed method auto-approves 49-72% of fields while holding the empirical error of the accepted tier at or below the target.

</details>


### [135] [MTVA-Bench: Evaluating the Language Model Inside Cascaded Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20152)
*Pritish Mishra,Ishaan Kumar,Akshat Mandoli,Sudarshan Kamath*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MTVA-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating language models acting as the decision-making core of multi-turn, phone-style voice agents in realistic cascaded settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations for voice agents are misaligned with how these systems actually work. End-to-end voice benchmarks conflate ASR, LLM, and TTS errors into a single metric, while standard LLM benchmarks ignore characteristics that make real phone calls difficult (e.g., noisy or imperfect transcripts, speaker turns split across messages, strict language/script requirements). There is no dedicated way to measure how well the language model itself behaves as the core of a multi-turn voice agent in realistic conditions.

Method: The authors build MTVA-Bench, where the LLM under test operates in a simulated cascaded voice-agent environment. Callers are simulated by an LLM that follows detailed rubrics, and all backend tool calls are processed by a mock backend that returns responses based on the exact arguments sent. The benchmark comprises 49 distinct agents, 490 human-reviewed scenarios, and covers 7 languages. Evaluation uses deterministic checks on the correctness of tool calls plus two separate LLM-based judges: one checks adherence to scenario-specific rules; the other evaluates conversation quality without access to the underlying task. Both judges must justify their scores by citing specific transcript messages. Task success and conversation quality are weighted equally in the final score.

Result: Across seven evaluated models, six perform similarly in choosing the correct tools (within 6.4 points), but overall scores differ by 24.4 points. The large spread stems from weaknesses not captured by simple tool-selection accuracy: incorrect or suboptimal tool arguments, wrong ordering of actions, violations of scenario rules, and problematic free-form responses before, between, or after tool calls.

Conclusion: MTVA-Bench captures the true complexities of deploying LLMs as decision-making cores in cascaded voice agents more faithfully than prior benchmarks. It reveals that models with comparable tool-selection ability can still differ substantially in real call quality due to how they structure actions, fill arguments, follow rules, and converse. Thus, evaluation of voice agents must go beyond end-to-end scores or isolated LLM tests, and should jointly measure task completion and conversational quality under realistic conditions.

Abstract: Generally, most voice agents are cascaded systems, i.e., an ASR model transcribes the caller's audio, a language model reads the transcript and decides what to say and which backend tools to call, and a TTS model speaks the reply. Nearly all of the decision making happens in the language model, but existing evaluations measure it either too broadly or too narrowly. End-to-end voice benchmarks score the full pipeline, so recognition errors and model errors mix into a single number. LLM benchmarks isolate the model but they do not evaluate what makes real phone calls hard, such as transcription issues, caller's voice being split across messages and the requirement that replies follow the language and script specified. We introduce the Multi-Turn Voice Agent Benchmark (MTVA-Bench), which evaluates the language model on the same conditions it faces inside a cascaded system. The caller is played by an LLM following a set of rubrics and tool calls are answered by a mock backend which responds to the arguments the model actually sent. The benchmark contains 49 agents working across 490 reviewed scenarios and supports 7 languages. Scoring is a combination of deterministic checks on tool calls with two LLM judges, one that scores scenario specific rules and one that grades conversation quality without access to the task. Both judges must cite specific messages from the transcript. Task and conversation scores are weighted equally, since a call can complete its task and still go badly for the caller. In a seven-model study, six of the models select the correct tool within 6.4 points of one another, but their overall scores span 24.4 points. Most of the gap comes from argument values, action ordering, rule compliance, and what the model says around its tool calls.

</details>


### [136] [PaGNet: A Panel-Aware GBDT--Neural Network for Multi-Target Corporate Tax Avoidance Proxy Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20177)
*Wonho Song,Hyungjoon Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces PaGNet, a hybrid LightGBM–neural network model for forecasting corporate tax avoidance proxies from firm–year panel data, achieving higher explained variance than strong baselines while providing transparent branch-reliance diagnostics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting corporate tax avoidance proxies from panel data is hard because useful information is spread over short firm histories and correlated targets, and practical users need models whose behavior is transparent for screening and diagnostics rather than just black-box accuracy. Existing tabular methods often ignore panel structure or lack clear interpretability in a multi-target, temporal setting.

Method: The authors propose PaGNet, a two-branch model combining: (1) a LightGBM branch that uses panel-temporal summary features, and (2) a Panel-MLP branch that employs attention-based temporal aggregation and multi-task learning with a shared trunk. A non-trainable per-target blender chooses between branches based on validation performance, yielding both final predictions and a diagnostic of which branch each target relies on. The model is evaluated on the KoTaP panel of Korean listed firms (2011–2024) under a leakage-free, shared-hyperparameter protocol across four feature sets, including regimes that exclude direct lags of the target and ones that augment with tax history. They also run a panel-flatten control and rolling-origin experiments to analyze temporal robustness and horizon effects.

Result: On the KoTaP dataset, PaGNet improves explained variance for accrual-based tax avoidance proxies (TSTA, TSDA) by about 0.08–0.11 over the strongest of six baseline models in key feature regimes (FS1, FS2), with those targets consistently routed to the LightGBM branch. For GETR, the model often prefers the neural branch, while CETR behaves inconsistently, showing a mismatch between validation-based branch selection and test-time performance. The panel-flatten control reveals that most gains on accrual targets derive from multi-year base-panel information, with PaGNet’s panel-aware representation adding a smaller but consistent improvement. Rolling-origin analysis shows stable branch routing for accrual measures, indicates that ETR diagnostics are split-specific, and reveals a distant forecast split where even sophisticated models underperform a naive persistence benchmark.

Conclusion: PaGNet should be viewed as a proxy-aware panel modeling framework rather than a universally superior tabular learner. It delivers competitive forecasting performance for corporate tax avoidance proxies—especially accrual-based measures—while providing explicit, per-target reporting of whether predictions rely more on tree-based or neural representations. The analysis underscores that most benefits come from leveraging multi-year panel data, with PaGNet’s architecture offering incremental, interpretable refinements and clarifying where and when its advantages hold or break down across targets and forecast horizons.

Abstract: Forecasting corporate tax avoidance proxies from firm--year panel data is challenging because predictive signals are distributed across short firm histories and related targets, while screening-oriented use requires transparent model behavior. We propose PaGNet (Panel-Aware GBDT--Neural Network), a two-branch hybrid that combines a LightGBM branch using panel-temporal summaries with a Panel-MLP branch using attention-pooled temporal aggregation and shared-trunk multi-task learning. A per-target validation-optimal blender produces both the final prediction and a compact branch-reliance diagnostic without trainable fusion parameters. On the KoTaP panel of 1{,}754 Korean listed firms from 2011--2024, PaGNet is evaluated under a leakage-free, shared-hyperparameter protocol across four feature regimes. In the direct-proxy-lag-excluded FS1 regime and the tax-history-augmented FS2 regime, accrual targets (TSTA, TSDA) route stably to the LightGBM branch, where PaGNet raises explained variance over the strongest of six baselines by roughly $0.08$--$0.11$ on the primary split. GETR often leans toward the neural branch, while CETR exposes a validation--test branch-selection mismatch rather than a stable branch assignment. A panel-flatten control shows that most accrual gains come from observed multi-year base-panel values, with PaGNet's panel-aware representation adding a smaller but directionally consistent refinement. Rolling-origin analysis confirms stable accrual routing, bounds ETR diagnostics to split-specific behavior, and identifies a far-horizon split where supervised models underperform naive persistence. PaGNet is therefore best viewed not as a universally superior tabular learner, but as a proxy-aware panel model that combines competitive forecasting with explicit per-target branch-reliance reporting.

</details>


### [137] [Sequential Contextual Fit Predicts Human Behavioural and Neural Dynamics Across Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20179)
*Kun Sun,Rong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces an embedding-based metric, Sequential Contextual Fit (SCF), to quantify how well the current state matches recent context across diverse sequential domains, and shows it predicts processing difficulty, affective and behavioral changes, and neural state transitions beyond standard predictors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human perception, action, and decision making are inherently sequential, but existing computational predictors of processing and behavior (e.g., surprisal, prediction error) are typically domain-specific and may not generalize well across modalities such as language, music, emotion, decisions, actions, and neural dynamics. There is a need for a unified, domain-general measure that captures how compatible a current state is with its recent context, to better explain and compare behavioral latency, affective shifts, and brain state changes in different sequential tasks.

Method: The paper defines Sequential Contextual Fit (SCF), an embedding-based measure that quantifies how well the current information state geometrically matches a recency-weighted representation of its recent context. SCF is computed using a simple similarity kernel over embeddings, with more recent elements weighted more heavily. The authors apply this metric to multiple domains by embedding words, sounds, visual scenes, affective states, choices, actions, and neural representations in appropriate representational spaces. They then correlate SCF with behavioral and neural measures across a set of datasets: language reading times, music-evoked emotion ratings, subsets of audiovisual emotion EEG data, gambling choices, human activity recognition, and decision-related EEG. Regression and model-comparison analyses control for established predictors such as surprisal, reinforcement-learning prediction error, acoustic, visual, and sensor changes to test SCF’s incremental predictive power.

Result: Across all tested domains—language processing, music-induced emotion, audiovisual emotion EEG, gambling decisions, human activity recognition, and decision-related EEG—lower SCF (i.e., poorer fit between current state and recent context) systematically predicted longer behavioral processing times, larger affective or behavioral transitions, and stronger neural state changes. These relationships persisted even after statistically controlling for traditional domain-specific predictors including surprisal, RL prediction error, and low-level sensory or sensor change measures. This indicates that SCF captures additional variance in behavioral and neural data that is not explained by standard predictors.

Conclusion: Sequential Contextual Fit (SCF) functions as a domain-general computational layer for quantifying contextual compatibility across diverse sequential phenomena. By reliably predicting processing difficulty, affective/behavioral shifts, and neural transition dynamics beyond established metrics, SCF offers a unified formalism that can bridge findings across language, music, decision making, action recognition, and brain activity. The work suggests that contextual fit, as measured in an embedding space with simple recency-weighting, is a fundamental organizing principle of human sequential processing and could provide a useful tool for future cognitive, affective, and neuroscientific modeling.

Abstract: Human perception, action and decision making unfold in sequences, but computational predictors are often domain-specific. This study computes and tests sequential contextual fit (SCF), an embedding-based measure of how well a current information state matches its recent context. The metric uses a simple recency-weighted similarity kernel and can be applied to words, sounds, visual scenes, affective states, choices, actions and neural representations. Across language processing, music-evoked emotion, a subset of audiovisual emotion EEG data, gambling decisions, human activity recognition and decision-related EEG, lower contextual fit predicted longer processing times, larger affective or behavioural transitions and stronger neural-state changes. These effects remained after controlling for established predictors including surprisal, reinforcement-learning prediction error, acoustic change, visual change and sensor change. SCF therefore provides a computational measurement layer for relating contextual compatibility to behavioural processing and cognitive/neural state-transition dynamics.

</details>


### [138] [JointMatch: A Unified Heterogeneous Graph Neural Solver for Large-Scale Ride-Sharing Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20200)
*Kun Zhao,Xu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces JointMatch, a learning-based framework that jointly matches ride requests and vehicles on a single sparsified graph, improving revenue, scalability, and speed over traditional two-stage matching methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ride-sharing platforms typically decompose matching into two sequential stages: pairing requests into shared trips and then assigning vehicles to these bundles. This is computationally convenient but suboptimal because it commits to request bundles without full knowledge of available vehicles, leading to revenue loss and poor scalability, especially in large urban environments.

Method: The authors propose JointMatch, which formulates request pairing and vehicle assignment jointly on a single graph. The graph is sparsified using spatial proximity so its size grows linearly rather than quadratically with the number of vehicles and requests. A graph neural network (GNN) is used to score all candidate joint decisions in a single forward pass. The framework is trained first with supervised learning to approximate good solutions and then further fine-tuned with policy-gradient reinforcement learning to better align with realised revenue.

Result: On New York City Yellow Taxi data, JointMatch outperforms both a classical Blossom-based heuristic and a carefully implemented two-stage GNN baseline, often by a substantial margin in revenue. At city scale with a fleet size of 10,000 vehicles, JointMatch runs more than 20 times faster per dispatch epoch than either baseline. The supervised training phase already recovers most of the revenue gap to an ideal solution, and policy-gradient fine-tuning further improves revenue alignment.

Conclusion: Jointly optimizing request pairing and vehicle assignment on a sparsified graph with a GNN leads to better revenue and scalability than traditional two-stage approaches. JointMatch can handle city-scale ride-sharing scenarios efficiently, making it a promising approach for real-world deployment on large ride-sharing platforms.

Abstract: Ride-sharing platforms must continuously decide which open requests to bundle into shared trips and which idle vehicles should serve them. The dominant academic approach decomposes this into two sequential matching problems -- request pairing first, then vehicle assignment -- and applies a separate solver to each. This decomposition is convenient computationally but loses revenue and scales poorly because the first stage commits to ride bundles before the available vehicles are known. We propose JointMatch, a learning-based framework that handles request pairing and vehicle assignment together on a single graph. The graph is sparsified by spatial proximity so that its size grows linearly rather than quadratically with the number of vehicles and requests, and a graph neural network scores all candidate decisions in one forward pass. On the New York City Yellow Taxi data, the framework already exceeds both the classical Blossom heuristic and a faithfully-trained two-stage GNN baseline -- often by a wide margin -- and at city scale (fleet 10000) it runs more than $20\times$ faster per dispatch epoch than either. A supervised training stage closes most of the remaining revenue gap, and a policy-gradient fine-tune aligns the trained model with realised revenue.

</details>


### [139] [When AI Agents Commit: Cognitive Serializability Across Data, Evidence, Policy, and Authority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20261)
*Jun He,Deying Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a transaction model for autonomous agents that ensures their actions remain logically consistent with the data and policies used to derive them, even as those inputs change concurrently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents compute proposed state changes (mutations) based on data reads, evidence, policies, beliefs, and delegated authorities. In concurrent systems, these inputs may change while an agent is still reasoning, so standard database isolation and transaction serializability are not sufficient to guarantee that the committed effects match what the agent actually justified. The authors want a principled way to align committed effects with the cognitive process (derivation) that produced them, ensuring soundness and progress for agentic transactions.

Method: They introduce a new correctness criterion called Cognitive Serializability and a weaker one called Effect-Compatible Cognitive Admission. These are enforced using a protocol termed TCT that combines several mechanisms: immutable, versioned executable contract definitions; authority plans derived from a registry; sealed envelopes that capture the values and assumptions exposed to reasoning; guard-first commit transactions; grants that are bound to sealed envelopes and witnesses; co-committed receipts; idempotent grant finalization; and receipt-driven epistemic reconciliation. They also constrain the execution with complete registered footprints and a single growing phase to induce an acyclic lock-point order over local guards and conflicting external reservations, yielding serializability-like guarantees for cognitive processes.

Result: They derive formal conditions under which their protocol guarantees serializability and an observational equivalence boundary that characterizes when executions are zero-error sound (no incorrect effects relative to the derivations) and still allow positive progress (avoid deadlock/starvation). They implement a prototype with a falsification (stress/anomaly-injection) test suite, which showed that the system prevented all injected anomalies and incurred an average commit latency overhead of 3.22 milliseconds.

Conclusion: The proposed TCT protocol and its associated notions of Cognitive Serializability and Effect-Compatible Cognitive Admission provide a way to make autonomous-agent transaction effects provably consistent with the data, policy, and authority used during reasoning, despite concurrent changes. Their design gives rigorous serializability-style semantics tailored to cognitive derivations, enforces soundness and progress via structured guards and envelopes, and is practical enough to be implemented with modest performance overhead as validated by their anomaly-prevention experiments.

Abstract: Autonomous agents derive concrete mutations from database reads, retrieved evidence, policy, beliefs, and delegated authority. Those inputs may change while reasoning is in progress. Database isolation orders the submitted transaction; agentic transaction processing determines whether a proposal satisfies an executable contract. Neither guarantee establishes a common valid point for the mutation and its derivation inputs unless the contract represents the relevant predicates. Typed dependency tokens distinguish content integrity from applicability, and trusted mediation captures the values exposed to reasoning. Under strict Cognitive Serializability, committed effects admit a serial order and a logical event at which every value exposed to derivation is unchanged. The fences last until the runtime event that realizes the sealed durability domain. The weaker Effect-Compatible Cognitive Admission recertifies an effect against a simultaneously held current dependency vector and current policy without claiming to serialize the original stochastic derivation. TCT combines immutable versioned executable definitions, registry-derived authority plans, sealed envelopes, guard-first commit transactions, post-seal envelope- and witness-bound grants, co-committed receipts, idempotent grant finalization, and receipt-driven epistemic reconciliation. Complete registered footprints and a single growing phase induce an acyclic lock-point order over local guards and incompatible external reservations. The corresponding results give serializability conditions and an observational-equivalence boundary for zero-error soundness and positive progress. A falsification suite tests the implementation obligations: the prototype prevented all injected anomalies and added 3.22 ms mean commit overhead.

</details>


### [140] [AI-Driven Real-Time Relay Optimisation in Smart Urban NR-V2X Networks via Learning-to-Optimise Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20271)
*Giambattista Amati,Federica Mangiatordi,Emiliano Pallotti,Simone Angelini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents an AI-based, graph-neural-network relay selection scheme for NR-V2X that closely matches MILP-optimal connectivity while being orders of magnitude faster, enabling real-time control in smart-city and Industry 4.0 vehicular networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NR-V2X networks that support smart city and Industry 4.0 applications require highly reliable, low-latency connectivity. In practice, sparse RSU deployment and challenging urban propagation (blockages, non-line-of-sight, fast topology changes) cause frequent disconnections for CAVs. Optimal multi-hop relay selection can mitigate this, but exact optimisation via MILP is too slow and computationally heavy for real-time operation in large, dynamic networks. There is a need for a scalable, fast, and near-optimal method to control connectivity in such vehicular networks.

Method: The authors model the vehicular communication scenario as a graph in which nodes represent CAVs and RSUs, and edges encode radio link properties (e.g., channel quality, distance, or other link metrics). They formulate an offline Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) problem that yields optimal multi-hop relay selection decisions. These optimal solutions serve as labelled supervision to train a Graph Neural Network, specifically an edge-aware Graph Isomorphism Network with Edge Features (GINE). The trained GNN thus learns a mapping from graph-structured network states to relay-selection decisions, approximating the MILP solver but with far lower inference latency. The framework is evaluated on realistic urban datasets to test performance and scalability.

Result: On realistic urban scenarios, the proposed L2O-GNN approach reaches connectivity performance close to that of the MILP optimum, achieving up to 11.3% gain in connectivity compared with baselines while cutting decision-making execution time by up to a factor of 100 compared to running MILP online. This demonstrates that the GNN successfully learns the structure of the optimal solutions and can generalise across network states, delivering near-optimal multi-hop relay selection in real time.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that an AI-driven Learning-to-Optimise framework based on GNNs is an effective way to implement scalable, real-time multi-hop relay selection in NR-V2X networks. By learning from offline MILP solutions, the proposed GINE-based model attains near-optimal connectivity with orders-of-magnitude lower computation time, making it suitable for deployment in smart city and Industry 4.0 scenarios where fast network control is critical.

Abstract: Reliable and low-latency communication is a fundamental requirement for smart city services and Industry 4.0 applications enabled by NR-V2X networks. However, limited Road-Side Unit (RSU) deployment and complex urban propagation conditions often prevent Connected and Automated Vehicles (CAVs) from maintaining stable connectivity. This paper proposes an AI-driven Learning-to-Optimise (L2O) framework based on Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) for real-time multi-hop relay selection in NR-V2X systems. The vehicular network is modelled as a graph, where nodes represent CAVs and RSUs, and edges encode radio-link characteristics. An offline Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) formulation provides optimal relay decisions used as supervision for training an edge-aware Graph Isomorphism Network with Edge Features (GINE). Extensive experiments on realistic urban datasets demonstrate that the proposed approach achieves near-optimal connectivity performance, recovering up to 11.3% connectivity gain, while reducing execution time by orders of magnitude (up to 100 x speed-up) compared to MILP. The framework enables scalable and real-time network control, making it suitable for smart city and Industry 4.0 deployments.

</details>


### [141] [JEPA-WAM: Connecting Generated Visual Instructions to World Action Models through JEPA Latent Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20277)
*Tianbin Liu,Jian Zhu,Taiyi Su,Jianjun Zhang,Chong Ma,Zitai Huang,Yi Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes JEPA-WAM, a world action model that improves instruction-following in robot manipulation by augmenting textual commands with multiple, semantically aligned visual goal images encoded via a frozen V-JEPA, yielding substantial performance gains on a new real-robot benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing World Action Models can perform complex manipulation but follow natural-language instructions poorly when only text is provided. This is attributed to a data imbalance: trajectories have rich visual-action information but sparse, repetitive language, so policies learn to infer tasks from visual patterns instead of grounding the text. The authors seek a way to strengthen instruction grounding without collecting expensive new language-annotated robot data or retraining large generative models.

Method: JEPA-WAM augments each text instruction with a set of stochastically sampled, task-completion images generated by an off-the-shelf text-to-image model conditioned on the instruction. These images, while visually different from the current scene, are semantically aligned and treated as visual goals. A frozen V-JEPA 2.1 encoder converts them into dense goal representations that capture task-level semantics; these are further compressed into compact goal tokens. The goal tokens condition both the video generative model and the action expert through cross-attention, effectively injecting rich, visualized task intent into the policy without training the image generator or the JEPA encoder.

Result: On a newly constructed real-robot instruction-following benchmark with three regimes—in-distribution scenes, out-of-distribution scenes, and out-of-distribution instructions—JEPA-WAM achieves success rates of 87.3%, 74.5%, and 80.9%, respectively. These results outperform existing baselines π0 and Fast-WAM by at least 10.0, 27.3, and 14.5 percentage points in the three corresponding settings.

Conclusion: Augmenting textual instructions with a bank of semantically consistent, generated visual goals and encoding them using a frozen JEPA-style vision model substantially improves instruction-following for world action models. This approach addresses the structural language-vision imbalance in robot-learning datasets without retraining large generators, and yields large gains in both in-distribution and generalization scenarios, suggesting that visualized goals are an effective way to better ground language for robotic manipulation.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) have demonstrated strong robotic manipulation capabilities by augmenting pretrained video generative models with action experts. However, current WAMs still show limited instruction-following ability when conditioned solely on text instructions. We argue that this limitation stems in part from a structural imbalance in robot-learning data: rich visual-action trajectories are often paired with sparse and repetitive language annotations, allowing policies to identify tasks from visual context and motion regularities rather than grounding the instruction itself. To address this limitation, we introduce JEPA-WAM, which augments each text instruction with a bank of stochastically generated visual instructions, providing diverse visual cues for instruction following. Specifically, JEPA-WAM uses an off-the-shelf text-to-image generator to sample multiple task-completion images conditioned on the text instruction, without training the generator. Although these generated images may differ from the current visual scene in appearance and layout, they remain semantically aligned with the instruction and serve as visual goal references. To focus on task-level semantics beyond appearance, we encode these references with a frozen V-JEPA 2.1 encoder. The resulting dense goal representations are compressed into compact goal tokens that condition both the video and action experts through cross-attention. We further construct a real-robot instruction-following benchmark covering in-distribution, out-of-distribution scene, and out-of-distribution instruction settings. On this benchmark, JEPA-WAM achieves success rates of 87.3%, 74.5%, and 80.9% in these three settings, outperforming π0 and Fast-WAM by at least 10.0, 27.3, and 14.5 percentage points, respectively.

</details>


### [142] [AgentPProf: Semantic Profiler for Long Horizon AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20301)
*Yusheng Zheng,Chaokun Chang,Yu Mao,Tianyuan Wu,Yuxi Huang,Tao Ma,Wenan Mao,Shuyi Cheng,Andi Quinn,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces AgentPProf, a profiling framework for long-running AI agents that attributes resource use and failures to semantic task structures instead of code paths, producing pprof-compatible profiles that improve problem localization and cost optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents now run complex, long-duration workflows involving users, tools, and system resources. Developers need to understand where failures occur, what causes unsafe behavior, and which parts of agent behavior are most resource-intensive to improve quality, safety, and cost. Existing observability focuses on per-run debugging and tracing, which does not scale to cross-run, long-term profiling. Traditional profilers attribute resources to code paths, but for agents, the key entities are task intents and semantic activities without stable identifiers, creating a gap in tooling.

Method: The paper introduces a semantic operation stack model that maps the idea of software profiling to agent trajectories. It defines uniform operations to represent all agent activities and replaces a traditional runtime call stack with operation stacks to support hierarchical attribution at various granularities. The authors observe that an agent’s task typically forms a contiguous span and can be decomposed into subtasks, and leverage this by proposing recursive operation segmentation to split trajectories along task boundaries. They implement these ideas in AgentPProf, which converts agent trajectories into pprof-compatible profiles suitable for flame graph visualization and analysis.

Result: AgentPProf achieves a B^3 F1 score of 0.764 on CodeTraceBench when evaluated against human annotations, indicating accurate recovery of semantic task structure from trajectories. On three problem-localization benchmarks, using the generated profiles improves mean average precision (MAP) by up to 56%, showing that the profiler helps attribute resource usage correctly, locate issues in agent behavior, and target high-cost tasks for optimization at a reasonable profiling overhead.

Conclusion: Profiling for AI agents must operate at the level of semantic tasks rather than code, and the proposed semantic operation stack with recursive operation segmentation effectively adapts classical profiling concepts to this setting. AgentPProf demonstrates that such profiles can be generated in a pprof-compatible format, enabling standard visualization tools like flame graphs to be applied to agent behavior. Empirical results show that AgentPProf accurately captures task structure, significantly aids problem localization, and supports practical optimization of token and resource usage, offering a scalable observability solution beyond traditional debugging and tracing.

Abstract: AI agents increasingly orchestrate long-running activities with users, tools, and system resources for days and weeks. To improve agent quality, safety, and cost efficiency, developers need to determine where failures happen, what triggers unsafe effects, and which tasks consume the most budget, then optimize those tasks. In systems software, profiling answers similar questions by aggregating resource consumption and attributing it to responsible code paths to identify hotspots. Yet existing agent observability tools focus on per-execution debugging and tracing rather than cross-run, long term profiling, making these questions difficult to answer at scale. Agent observability needs profiling, not only debugging, but profiling agents is challenging: the responsible entities are task intent like diagnose authentication, compare branches rather than code paths, and lack stable identifiers for aggregation. We propose a semantic operation stack model that adapts profiling to agent trajectories. Uniform operations represent all activities, and operation stacks replace the runtime call stack, enabling hierarchical attribution at different granularities. We observe that an agent's task occupies a contiguous span and decomposes into subtasks, so we introduce recursive operation segmentation, which recursively splits trajectories at task boundaries. AgentPProf is a profiler that aggregates agent trajectories into pprof-compatible profiles, enabling flame graph visualization and analysis. AgentPProf reaches 0.764 $B^3$ F1 against human annotations on CodeTraceBench. On three problem-localization benchmarks, the profile raises MAP by up to 56%, demonstrating that it effectively attributes resources, locates problems, and helps optimize token cost at practical profiling cost. AgentPProf is available at https://github.com/eunomia-bpf/agentsight.

</details>


### [143] [Diagnose, Recover, Certify: Task Readiness under Hidden Dynamics Changes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20304)
*Nguyen Viet Tuan Kiet,Huynh Thi Thanh Binh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to keep control systems ready for unknown future tasks when actuators may have changed in hidden ways, by diagnosing and quantifying actuator faults with minimal, task-agnostic interactions and using this information to decide safe deployment or fallback for future tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In deployed control systems, an actuator can degrade or fail in ways that do not visibly affect the current task because the deployed policy barely uses that actuator. However, the same hidden change could be catastrophic for a different, future task that heavily relies on it. Existing approaches tend to either assume a known downstream task, or focus only on fault detection without explicitly connecting diagnosis to how well future tasks can still be performed. There is a need for a framework that, under limited interaction with the environment and without knowing future tasks in advance, can (1) detect and localize dynamics changes, (2) quantify how they affect control capabilities, and (3) certify readiness or trigger safe fallback for a range of potential downstream tasks.

Method: The authors define a new decision problem, task readiness under dormant dynamics drift, which unifies active fault diagnosis and post-change control recovery under a single, task-agnostic interaction budget. They propose an intervention-based Bayesian procedure called Evidence-Gated Matched-Pulse Transport. This method injects targeted excitation signals (“interventions”) to probe actuators, uses a shared matched-response representation to jointly localize which actuators/dynamics have changed and estimate their effectiveness, and maintains uncertainty over these estimates. The localized diagnostic evidence is then “transported” to the space of candidate tasks: for each task, they condition policy selection and readiness certification on the inferred actuator effectiveness, trading off expected performance, confidence, and when to abstain in favor of a safe fallback. The evaluation protocol separates the diagnosis phase from the capability recovery phase and measures readiness coverage, selective risk, interaction cost, and return across benchmarks with dormant actuators in multiple simulators.

Result: Experiments on multiple dormant-actuator benchmark environments show that the proposed framework can accurately localize actuator faults and estimate their effectiveness using a limited number of task-agnostic interactions. The Bayesian matched-pulse transport mechanism yields calibrated uncertainty that informs downstream policy selection, leading to improved readiness coverage (fraction of tasks for which the system can safely commit to a policy), lower selective risk (fewer high-cost failures conditional on deployment), and competitive returns, all while respecting interaction budgets. The analysis also characterizes which fault regimes—such as particular magnitudes or patterns of actuator degradation—benefit most from transported diagnostic evidence versus where it becomes insufficient or ambiguous.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that treating task readiness under dormant dynamics drift as a unified decision problem and solving it with intervention-based Bayesian diagnosis can bridge the gap between change detection and actionable post-change control. Their Evidence-Gated Matched-Pulse Transport method effectively converts sparse diagnostic interactions into recovery-relevant uncertainty over actuator effectiveness, enabling principled deployment decisions across unknown future tasks. This yields safer and more reliable operation of control systems in the presence of hidden dynamics changes, and establishes a benchmarked protocol for evaluating such methods in terms of readiness, risk, interaction cost, and performance.

Abstract: A deployed control policy can conceal consequential dynamics changes: an actuator may lose effectiveness without affecting the current task when the policy rarely excites it, despite being critical for a future task that has not yet been specified. We introduce task readiness under dormant dynamics drift, a decision problem that unifies active change diagnosis and post-change control recovery under a limited, task-agnostic interaction budget. An agent must identify whether and where local dynamics have changed, use a small number of informative interactions to characterize the change before downstream task identity is revealed, and subsequently provide each candidate task with either a recovered policy and a calibrated lower bound on its achievable return or an abstention decision to a safe fallback. We propose Evidence-Gated Matched-Pulse Transport, an intervention-based Bayesian procedure that couples fault localization with estimation of actuator effectiveness through a shared matched-response representation, thereby preserving diagnostic reliability while converting localized evidence into recovery-relevant uncertainty. This uncertainty is propagated to task-conditioned policy selection and readiness certification, enabling deployment decisions that explicitly trade off expected performance, confidence, and fallback use. We evaluate the resulting framework on a diverse suite of dormant-actuator benchmarks spanning multiple simulators, under a protocol that separates diagnosis from capability recovery, scores deployment by readiness coverage, selective risk, and interaction cost as well as return, and identifies the fault regimes in which transported evidence is decisive.

</details>


### [144] [NeuSOGA3D: A Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Explainable 3D Geometric Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20323)
*Qingde Li,Qingqi Hong,Zihan Li,Jie Tian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeuSOGA3D is a hybrid neuro-symbolic framework that reconstructs 3D CAD-like geometry from unorganized point clouds by combining neural perception with explicit spline- and CSG-based geometric reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D reconstruction from point clouds, especially neural implicit methods, achieves high accuracy but stores geometry as opaque latent representations. These are hard to interpret, edit, or integrate into engineering and CAD workflows, which typically require explicit, structured geometric entities. The authors aim to bridge the gap between powerful learned 3D perception and explicit, symbolic, CAD-compatible modeling.

Method: The method, NeuSOGA3D, first projects the input unorganized point cloud onto principal orthographic planes (e.g., front, side, top). From these 2D projections, it builds symbolic implicit spline representations on each plane. These splines are then combined using shape-preserving constructive solid geometry (CSG) operations to form a coarse 3D visual hull. To refine this into a detailed shape, the framework performs cross-sectional decomposition and then uses Partial Shape-Preserving Splines to reconstruct volumetric geometry via lofting between cross-sections. Throughout, the system gradually converts raw observations into explicit symbolic constructs such as control polygons, implicit spline fields, cross-sections, and volumetric lofts, rather than keeping everything as a neural latent field.

Result: On all forty categories of the ModelNet40 dataset, NeuSOGA3D reconstructs shapes that are both visually accurate and structurally meaningful, producing CAD-compatible geometric structures. The experiments indicate that the reconstructed models capture high-level structural organization, suitable for engineering-style manipulation, not just pointwise surface fit.

Conclusion: By integrating learned perceptual priors with explicit symbolic geometric reasoning, NeuSOGA3D provides an interpretable and CAD-friendly alternative to black-box neural implicit reconstruction. The work suggests a promising direction for explainable geometric intelligence, where neural networks handle perception and inference while symbolic geometry provides structure, interpretability, and compatibility with downstream design workflows.

Abstract: Three-dimensional reconstruction from unorganized point clouds remains a challenging problem in computer vision, geometric modeling, and computer-aided design. While neural implicit methods achieve impressive reconstruction accuracy, geometry is typically encoded in latent representations that limit interpretability and reuse within engineering workflows.
  We present NeuSOGA3D (Neuro-Symbolic Geometric Abstraction in 3D), a hybrid framework that combines learned perceptual priors inherited from NeuSOGA with explicit symbolic geometric reasoning. The method projects point clouds onto principal orthographic planes, constructs symbolic implicit spline representations from the resulting observations, and fuses them through shape-preserving constructive solid geometry operations to generate a coarse visual hull. Additional geometric detail is recovered through cross-sectional decomposition and volumetric reconstruction using Partial Shape-Preserving Splines.
  Unlike conventional neural implicit approaches, NeuSOGA3D progressively transforms observations into explicit symbolic entities, including control polygons, implicit spline fields, cross-sections, and volumetric lofts. Experiments on all forty categories of the ModelNet40 benchmark demonstrate the ability of the framework to recover structurally meaningful and CAD-compatible geometric representations from diverse point-cloud observations. The results highlight the potential of combining learned perception with symbolic geometric reasoning for explainable geometric intelligence.

</details>


### [145] [Structured Four-Stage Legal Translation: From Natural-Language Traffic Rules to PROLOG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20334)
*May Myo Zin,Wachara Fungwacharakorn,Ken Satoh,Katsumi Nitta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a structured, multi-step prompting framework (S4L→Prolog) that translates natural-language traffic regulations into executable Prolog rules more accurately than two baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traffic regulations are written for humans and are inherently ambiguous, context-dependent, and linguistically underspecified. Computational reasoning engines like Prolog, however, require precise, explicit logical forms. Existing direct translation approaches from natural language or semi-structured legal English often fail to capture implicit relations, modalities, and exceptions, limiting the reliability of automated legal reasoning, especially in safety-critical domains like traffic law.

Method: The authors compare three approaches for translating traffic rules into Prolog: (1) a direct Natural Language to Prolog (NL→Prolog) baseline, (2) a Logical English to Prolog (LE→Prolog) baseline, and (3) their proposed Structured Four-Stage Legal Translation (S4L→Prolog) framework. S4L is implemented as a single guided prompt that orchestrates four internal steps: semantic role extraction, scene completion (filling in implicitly understood elements), logical mapping of the enriched representation, and final Prolog rule generation. They construct a benchmark of twenty real-world traffic rules and evaluate each method on syntactic validity of the generated Prolog, semantic correctness with respect to the original rule, and logical completeness of captured conditions and exceptions. They also perform qualitative analysis of how well each method models causal relations, deontic modality (obligations, permissions, prohibitions), and exception structures.

Result: On the benchmark of twenty traffic rules, the S4L→Prolog framework correctly formalizes 75% of the rules, outperforming the NL→Prolog baseline at 60% and the LE→Prolog baseline at 55%. S4L produces syntactically valid and semantically faithful Prolog programs more frequently and more completely captures the conditions, causal dependencies, and exception patterns stated or implied in the rules. Qualitative analysis indicates that S4L more reliably encodes implicit causal relations between events, correctly handles deontic modalities such as obligations and prohibitions, and preserves exception structures present in the original regulations.

Conclusion: The study concludes that incorporating explicit, structured reasoning stages into the prompting process substantially improves the translation of natural-language legal texts into executable logic. The S4L framework, by guiding semantic role identification, implicit scene completion, and systematic logical mapping before code generation, yields higher accuracy and richer formalizations than more direct translation baselines. This suggests that reasoning-guided prompting is a promising direction for building dependable automated reasoning systems for legal and other safety-critical domains where precision and completeness of logical representation are vital.

Abstract: Traffic regulations are written for human interpretation and therefore rely on shared background knowledge and flexible phrasing, which inherently introduce ambiguity, context dependence, and semantic underspecification. These linguistic characteristics conflict with the precision required by computational reasoning engines such as Prolog, which demand explicit logical structure. This study evaluates two baseline translation approaches, Natural Language to Prolog ($NL\rightarrow Prolog$) and Logical English to Prolog ($LE\rightarrow Prolog$), and introduces a new reasoning-guided translation framework called Structured Four-Stage Legal Translation ($S4L\rightarrow Prolog$). The proposed S4L framework performs semantic role extraction, scene completion, logical mapping, and Prolog rule generation within a single guided prompt, enabling direct translation of raw traffic rules into executable logic without human intervention. A benchmark consisting of twenty real-world traffic rules was used to evaluate each approach in terms of syntactic validity, semantic correctness, and logical completeness. $S4L\rightarrow Prolog$ achieves the highest accuracy, correctly formalizing 75 percent of the rules, while $NL\rightarrow Prolog$ reaches 60 percent and $LE\rightarrow Prolog$ reaches 55 percent. Qualitative analysis further shows that S4L captures implicit causal relations, deontic modality, and exception structure more reliably than the baselines. These results demonstrate that structured reasoning prompts can substantially improve the reliability of natural-language-to-logic translation for legal and safety-critical applications.

</details>


### [146] [A Qualitative Model for Reasoning about Path and Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20349)
*Abhishek Jaiswal,Zoe Falomir*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a hybrid qualitative reasoning model for an educational block-puzzle game (Camelot Jr.) that uses symbolic reasoning plus center-of-mass physics to provide explainable, human-like guidance for training children’s spatial skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatial reasoning is crucial for success in STEM, and children can effectively train these skills through games. To turn such games into intelligent tutors, we need AI agents that can interpret spatial game events and give commonsense, human-understandable feedback, which current systems lack.

Method: The authors design a hybrid qualitative model for the Camelot Jr. puzzle game. They use qualitative reasoning with symbolic representations to capture game states and constraints, and augment it with a quantitative, mathematical center-of-mass stability logic so that the system can reason about structural stability while remaining interpretable. This model powers a qualitative solver that analyzes potential moves and puzzle solutions.

Result: The integrated model is able to handle the precise physical requirements (such as platform stability) within Camelot Jr. while still operating in an interpretable, symbolic framework. It supports assessing solutions, planning paths, and ensuring all blocks are used, thereby enabling the system to guide players through the puzzle tasks.

Conclusion: The proposed hybrid qualitative + center-of-mass model enables explainable, human-centric AI guidance in the Camelot Jr. game and supports spatial skill training. More broadly, it demonstrates how qualitative reasoning frameworks, augmented with targeted quantitative physics, can underpin interpretable game-playing agents for educational domains.

Abstract: Spatial reasoning abilities correlate strongly with performance in STEM fields. Games offer a compelling medium for training these critical skills in developing children who have a natural proclivity for play. However, to facilitate human-like tutoring and player guidance, these games require an AI agent capable of making commonsense inferences from spatial events. Qualitative reasoning (QR) models appear to be a suitable framework for these application domains. As these models reason in symbolic representations, they can seamlessly translate game states into interpretable feedback for human-like player guidance. This paper introduces a hybrid qualitative model designed for Camelot Jr., a block-puzzle game that requires constructing multi-level bridges to connect two avatars stationed on separate towers. The game poses a challenge for the player, who must make platforms stable, plan their path, and ensure they use all the provided blocks. To handle the precise physics required by the domain, we integrate a mathematical center-of-mass stability logic to guide our qualitative solver. Our work facilitates spatial skill training in Camelot Jr. and contributes to the development of human-centric, explainable game-playing agents.

</details>


### [147] [Generating Heterogeneous 3D Geological Microstructures from 2D Images via a Stable Diffusion-Adversarial Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20358)
*Ali Aouf,Eric Laloy,Bart Rogiers,Christophe De Vleeschouwer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a new hybrid deep generative model that uses diffusion-style denoising with an adversarial loss to reconstruct 3D microstructures of clay and cementitious materials from 2D images, improving detail and reducing artefacts compared with SliceGAN.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D physical property simulations for clay and cementitious materials require 3D microstructure data, but 3D imaging is costly, hard to access, and technically inadequate for some materials. Existing GAN-based methods like SliceGAN work for simple, mostly homogeneous microstructures but fail to capture fine details in more complex heterogeneous systems. This limitation motivates developing a more powerful generative framework that can infer realistic 3D volumes from readily available 2D slices.

Method: They design a hybrid generative approach built on denoising diffusion models but modify the training objective because no true 3D ground-truth volumes are available. Instead of the usual diffusion denoising loss, they employ an adversarial loss, i.e., a discriminator-based objective, to guide the diffusion-style generator. The system is trained only from 2D slice data while learning to generate consistent 3D microstructures. The architecture is tailored to reduce slice artefacts and faithfully reproduce statistically relevant structural descriptors.

Result: The proposed model successfully generates 3D microstructures across a range of complexity levels. Compared to SliceGAN, it produces volumes with fewer slice artefacts, better visual and structural fidelity, and phase fractions and structural descriptors that agree closely with ground truth. Training remains stable under the adversarial loss formulation in the authors’ experiments, and the generated samples match target statistics well even without 3D ground-truth supervision.

Conclusion: A diffusion-inspired, adversarially trained generative model can overcome limitations of existing GAN-based approaches like SliceGAN for 3D microstructure reconstruction from 2D data. It enables realistic generation of complex heterogeneous microstructures, with accurate phase fractions and structural statistics and minimal artefacts, while remaining stable to train despite the lack of 3D ground-truth volumes. This provides a more practical and accurate route to 3D microstructure data for property simulations in clay and cementitious materials and potentially other similar systems.

Abstract: Characterizing the physical properties of clay and cementitious materials matters across many fields, from materials science to geological waste disposal. Property simulation typically calls for 3D imaging, which is expensive, not always accessible, and technically limited for certain materials. Recent progress in deep generative models offers a way around this, reconstructing 3D volumes from the more easily acquired 2D images.
  Among GAN-based methods for 3D microstructure generation, SliceGAN has shown strong results for homogeneous isotropic and anisotropic systems. It struggles, however, to capture the finer detail of more complex heterogeneous microstructures, which motivates alternative generative frameworks.
  We introduce a hybrid approach that draws on the stability and generation quality of denoising diffusion models. Since no 3D ground truth is available, we replace the standard denoising loss with an adversarial loss, which yields a stable training process in our experiments. We show that the resulting model generates microstructures of varying complexity with minimal slice artefacts and close agreement with ground-truth phase fractions and structural descriptors.

</details>


### [148] [The Organization of Inference: Information, Resource Constraints, and AI Production](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20449)
*Yukun Zhang,Kemu Xu,Yishen Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how the organization of AI workflows (direct execution vs. planning + execution) and token limits affect the economic value of AI inference on software-engineering tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems are deployed in real production settings, their economic value depends not only on raw model capability but also on how tasks are structured, how much context they see, and how inference capacity is allocated across stages like planning and execution. The authors want to understand, in a controlled way, how workflow design and information access affect task success rates and resource use.

Method: They run controlled workflow experiments on externally verified software-engineering tasks using two matched resource panels with different logical-token ceilings (12k and 24k). They compare (1) direct execution of tasks by the model and (2) information-constrained planning workflows where a planning stage and an execution stage share limited tokens. They further run a read-only planning experiment varying whether the planner can see the original task issue or not. They analyze success rates, token binding rates, and how total token use changes across conditions using bootstrap confidence intervals over task clusters.

Result: Direct execution achieves 59.6% success under both 12k and 24k token ceilings, implying that it does not fully use the larger capacity. Under information-constrained planning, success increases from 36.2% at 12k tokens to 51.2% at 24k tokens, shrinking the performance gap with direct execution by 15 percentage points (CI: 4.2–25.8). In a strict read-only planning setup at 12k tokens, allowing the planner to see the task issue raises success by about 16 percentage points versus hiding the issue. Relative to direct execution, task-informed planning is about 10 points worse at 12k tokens but 29.6 points better at 24k tokens. In the resource panels, direct execution uses significantly fewer tokens than either ceiling, while in the planning workflow the rate of hitting the token limit drops from 46.2% to 0.8% as the ceiling increases, and downstream execution accounts for 89.9% of the added token use.

Conclusion: The productive value of AI inference is shaped not only by model scale (token capacity) but also by workflow design and information structure. Direct execution can be efficient at lower token ceilings, but planning-based workflows become substantially more effective as token capacity increases, especially when planners are given access to the task description. Token limits bind planning workflows much more than direct execution, and relaxing those limits primarily benefits the downstream execution stage. Therefore, to maximize economic value, organizations need to jointly optimize model scale, workflow architecture, and how information is allocated across stages of AI-assisted work.

Abstract: The economic value of inference depends on how capacity and task information are distributed across stages of AI production. We study these organizational margins using controlled workflow experiments on externally verified software-engineering tasks. In two matched resource panels, direct execution records the same success rate of 59.6 percent at logical-token ceilings of 12,000 and 24,000, while success under information-constrained planning rises from 36.2 to 51.2 percent. The planning disadvantage narrows by 15.0 percentage points (95 percent task-cluster bootstrap interval: 4.2 to 25.8). A strict read-only planning campaign varies whether the planner sees the task issue. At 12,000 tokens, issue access raises success by about 16 percentage points over issue-hidden planning. Compared with direct execution, task-informed planning is about 10 points lower at 12,000 tokens; at 24,000 tokens, it shows a 29.6-point advantage. In the resource panels, direct execution uses substantially less than either ceiling, while the planning workflow's binding rate falls from 46.2 to 0.8 percent and downstream execution accounts for 89.9 percent of the increase in total use. Scale determines the capacity available to a system; workflow and information structure shape the productive value

</details>


### [149] [SkillAA: Attribution-Guided Skill-Graph Updating with Targeted Validation and Rollback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20455)
*Ziqiao Shang,Ling-Yue Ge,Lan-Zhe Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillAA is a graph-based framework that repairs and optimizes external skills for frozen language models by attributing failures to specific skill graph components and validating local edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing external-skill and skill-graph methods lack a structured way to connect observed failures to precise, editable parts of a skill library, and they underuse semantic and structural information (object addresses, topology) for retrieval, updating, and validation. The authors want a more principled, fine-grained mechanism to improve skills without retraining the base language model.

Method: They propose SkillAA (Skill Abductive Attribution), which represents skill applicability, execution, and composition as a unified graph. The system contrasts successful and failed executions to infer which graph objects (skills, edges, conditions) are responsible for failure, routes candidate repairs to those locations, and then performs local graph edits only there. Two types of gates—Local Gates and Big Gates—filter and validate candidate changes before final commitment, using the same graph structure for skill selection, repair, and validation.

Result: Using the frozen model gpt-5.6-sol plus SkillAA, they achieve accuracies of 81.5% on SearchQA, 66.7% on LiveMath, and 91.2% on DocVQA, and report the best mean performance across all main experimental settings compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Attribution-guided editing of a unified skill graph, together with graph-scoped validation via gating, provides an effective way to improve external skills for frozen language models, yielding consistent performance gains and demonstrating the value of structured skill attribution and local repair.

Abstract: External skills provide domain procedures without parameter updates, but existing methods often edit skills directly from failed rollouts without structured routing from an observed failure to an editable location; existing skill graphs also underuse semantic boundaries, object addresses, and topological dependencies for skill retrieval, targeted updating, and scoped validation. We introduce SkillAA (Skill Abductive Attribution), a structured skill-optimization framework for frozen language models. It represents skill applicability, execution, and composition in a unified graph, allowing the same structure to support skill selection, attribution-guided repair, and update validation. SkillAA contrasts successful and failed executions to route candidate repairs to specific graph objects, updates only the selected local structure, and uses Local and Big Gates to screen candidate changes before commitment. With gpt-5.6-sol, SkillAA reaches 81.5%, 66.7%, and 91.2% on SearchQA, LiveMath, and DocVQA, respectively, and attains the highest observed mean in every main setting. These results support the utility of attribution-guided graph editing and graph-scoped validation.

</details>


### [150] [How Do Agent Harnesses Create Value? Planning Information and Release Control in Stateful LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20474)
*Yukun Zhang,Kemu Xu,Yishen Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how planning guidance and verification affect task success, error rates, and cost in agent-based supply planning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand which parts of an agent system—task-specific plans or verification components—most improve reliability and cost-effectiveness in practical domains like retail and airlines.
