Method: Run two Retail experiments and an Airline pilot in the τ^2-bench framework, comparing prewritten fixed task-specific plans to sham plans with shuffled text, and evaluating a read-only terminal verifier’s ability to detect incorrect episodes. Use oracle verification and task-clustered bootstrap confidence intervals for statistical analysis.

Result: Fixed task-specific plans increase oracle-verified success by about 7.17 percentage points over sham plans, especially on more complex tasks. The verifier rejects 61% of oracle-invalid episodes while incorrectly rejecting 17% of valid episodes, adding less than one cent per episode. The relative value of planning versus verification depends on the cost of false acceptances; at low liability, planning benefits dominate, while at high liability, avoided false passes from verification dominate. A standalone verifier captures most of the false-pass reduction of the full stack at much lower cost.

Conclusion: Both planning guidance and verification improve agent performance, but their relative importance depends on the application’s tolerance for erroneous acceptance. Fixed plans help overall success, especially on complex tasks, whereas cheap verification is critical when false acceptances are costly, with a simple verifier capturing nearly all the safety benefits of a more complex planning-plus-verification system.

Abstract: Agent harnesses supply planning guidance, organize execution, and check completion. We study how these components affect success, erroneous acceptance, and cost in two Retail experiments and an Airline pilot in $τ^2$-bench. The primary comparison pairs prewritten task-specific plans (Fixed) with shuffled policy text matched in word count (Sham), isolating the contribution of guidance content. Across 265 matched cells, Fixed improves oracle-verified success by 7.17 percentage points (90\% task-clustered bootstrap interval, 1.15--13.36 points), with gains concentrated in higher-complexity tasks. A read-only terminal verifier rejects 61\% of Retail oracle-invalid episodes while withholding 17\% of correct ones, at less than one cent of additional cost per episode. Which component matters more depends on the loss assigned to erroneous acceptance: at low liability the planning gain dominates; at high liability the verifier's avoided false passes dominate---and a standalone verifier captures nearly all the false-pass benefit of the full planning-plus-verification stack at a fraction of its cost.

</details>


### [151] [Language-model groups overstate consensus when replaying human deliberation on a reasoning task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20543)
*Tengfei Shao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares consensus rates between human discussion groups and groups of belief-anchored LLM agents on a Wason task, showing that LLM groups reach much higher consensus than humans but that this simulated consensus does not predict correctness or accurately mirror human group outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers increasingly use LLM agent groups to simulate human deliberation and infer likely human group decisions. However, consensus rates are often taken as indicators of collective cognition without carefully aligning how participation and final states are defined for humans vs. agents. There is a need to rigorously test whether LLM-based simulated groups are good estimators of actual human group outcomes and whether their consensus behaves similarly to human consensus.

Method: The authors took 100 held-out human Wason task discussion groups and recreated them with matched groups of LLM agents. Each agent was belief-anchored to a human participant’s pre-discussion answer. Both agents and humans were scored using the same coding scheme. They examined multiple ways to define and measure consensus, performed sensitivity analyses to reduce different measurement asymmetries (e.g., submit-based vs. participation-matched comparisons), tested robustness by removing early stopping and reparameterizing the task to avoid a trivially memorizable answer, and compared chat vs. reasoning modes for agents.

Result: Human full-consensus estimates varied widely (24.0%–57.0%) depending on how participation and final group states were defined, partly because ~20% of human participants never posted whereas LLM agents almost always did. Across harmonized scoring definitions, LLM agent groups consistently showed substantially higher consensus than human groups, with gaps of roughly 34–44 percentage points, and these gaps were stable across different comparison schemes and robustness checks. When the answer could not be trivially memorized, reasoning-mode agent groups still reached near-unanimous consensus but often on incorrect answers. Overall, simulated consensus failed to align reliably with human consensus or with collective accuracy.

Conclusion: LLM agent groups can substantially overestimate consensus relative to human groups and do not provide an unbiased estimate of human group-outcome distributions in this Wason task setting. High simulated consensus is not a reliable proxy for either human-like deliberative behavior or collective correctness. The paper argues that evaluations of simulated groups must be explicit and careful about how participation and final states are defined and scored, and it provides a framework for more rigorous, scoring-explicit comparison between human and simulated group decisions.

Abstract: Full-consensus rates are often treated as indicators of collective cognition, yet depend on how participation and final states are operationalized. We replayed 100 held-out human Wason groups with matched large language model (LLM) agent groups, seeding one belief-anchored agent per participant's pre-discussion answer and scoring agents and people with the same code. Across human scoring definitions, estimates ranged from 24.0% to 57.0%; about one fifth of participants never posted, whereas agents almost always did. Agent groups remained more consensual in two post-unblinding sensitivity analyses: the submit-based comparison (n = 98) yielded gaps of 34.0 and 43.9 percentage points for chat and reasoning modes, and the participation-matched comparison (n = 45) yielded gaps of 34.1 and 44.4 points. These complementary routes reduced different measurement asymmetries yet converged within 0.5 percentage points. The gap persisted without early stopping and under a reparameterization removing the memorizable answer; reasoning-mode groups then agreed nearly unanimously, mostly on incorrect answers. Simulated consensus did not track collective accuracy, and belief-anchored agent groups were biased estimators of the human group-outcome distribution in this setting. These analyses provide a scoring-explicit basis for assessing simulated-group estimates of human deliberative outcomes.

</details>


### [152] [SoL-Pi: Recursively Scaling Auto-Research Loops for Efficient Agent Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20519)
*Haozhe Liu,Tian Ye,Sensen Gao,Qihang Cao,Yitong Li,Mingchen Zhuge,Duomin Wang,Ruihua Zhang,Ping Luo,Jiawang Bian,Lei Zhu,Ligeng Zhu,Enze Xie,Song Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SoL-Pi, a set of mechanisms that make autonomous coding agents much more token- and cost-efficient while preserving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous coding agents are moving from single code completions to long-running, unattended exploration and self-improvement loops. In this setting, they consume many tokens over long trajectories of reasoning, tool use, and feedback, making token efficiency a critical bottleneck for scaling recursive self-improvement in practical, cost-sensitive environments.

Method: The authors design and scale an "RSI-inspired" auto-research loop at the harness layer, running large numbers of harness rollouts across many diverse environments. Through this large-scale search and selection process, they identify mechanisms that consistently improve token efficiency without harming performance. These mechanisms are consolidated into SoL-Pi, covering four key aspects of agent behavior: how actions are executed, how context is compacted, how observations are handled, and how reading-heavy tasks are delegated.

Result: On the 51-task EdgeBench benchmark, SoL-Pi matches the performance of the Pi harness across models (GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5) while significantly reducing token usage, cutting recorded token traffic by roughly 45–49% and API costs by about one third. They also translate these savings into concrete hourly dollar reductions relative to existing harnesses (Codex, Claude Code, and Pi).

Conclusion: The paper shows that automated harness discovery at scale can yield reusable, production-ready improvements. SoL-Pi demonstrates that substantial token and cost savings are possible for autonomous coding agents without sacrificing benchmark performance, supporting more scalable and economical recursive self-improvement workflows.

Abstract: As coding agents move from supervised code completion to unattended, around-the-clock exploration, their work expands from isolated predictions into long trajectories of reasoning, tool use, and feedback. Token efficiency therefore becomes important for scaling recursive self-improvement. We take an RSI-inspired approach at the harness layer, scaling auto-research loops across increasingly numerous and diverse environments for harness rollouts. At this scale, the process yields reusable improvements that transfer beyond their development setting, moving automated harness discovery toward production-level outcomes. Four mechanisms survive selection and form SoL-Pi, spanning action execution, context compaction, observation handling, and delegated reading. On the 51-task EdgeBench evaluation, SoL-Pi achieves performance comparable to Pi across GPT-5.6 Sol and Opus 5 while reducing recorded token traffic by 44.7-49.0% and API cost by about one third. In other words, estimated hourly savings are \$8.75-\$13.50 relative to native Codex and Claude Code harnesses, and \$4.36-\$5.71 relative to Pi.

</details>


### [153] [PAA: The Probabilistic Allen Algebra: A Generative and Complete Probabilistic Extension of Allen's Interval Relations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20634)
*Julian Eggert*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper extends Allen’s interval algebra into a probabilistic framework where temporal relations have graded probabilities derived from Gaussian models of time intervals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Allen’s interval algebra treats temporal relations as crisp, exact predicates over interval boundaries, which is insufficient for real-world data from language, perception, databases, or uncertain histories where times and durations are imprecise and expressions like “just before” or “roughly during” require graded, probabilistic interpretation.

Method: Introduce the probabilistic Allen algebra (PAA), a generative model where time points are Gaussian variables and intervals are defined by Gaussian midpoints and truncated-Gaussian durations. Relation probabilities are computed as probabilities of boundary orderings in a shared probabilistic space, reducing point-point, point-interval, and interval-interval relations to (multivariate) Gaussian orthant probability computations under linear inequalities. Contact relations are assigned positive measure via a tolerance band, and a single tolerance produces a partition of the 13 Allen relations that converges to the crisp case as tolerance goes to zero. The taxonomy is constructed as unions of leaf relations, and decomposed into finer temporal primitives, with all computations validated via Monte Carlo and implemented in an open-source Python package.

Result: A complete probabilistic extension of Allen’s algebra where all 13 base relations (and their coarser groupings) are assigned well-defined probabilities derived from Gaussian models of time intervals, with a principled handling of contact relations, scale invariance, and preservation of Allen’s relational hierarchy as intervals degenerate to points. The model yields closed-form expressions in terms of error functions and multivariate Gaussian orthant probabilities, is empirically validated via Monte Carlo simulation, and provided as a practical Python implementation.

Conclusion: Probabilistic Allen algebra provides a mathematically coherent, generative, and scale-invariant framework for reasoning about uncertain temporal relations, recovering classical Allen algebra in the limit while enabling graded interpretations of vague temporal expressions and correlation-aware temporal primitives suitable for applications in language understanding, historical databases, and knowledge representation.

Abstract: Allen's interval algebra is a qualitative calculus for temporal relations, but its thirteen base relations are crisp predicates over exact interval boundaries. This is inadequate for temporal information from language, perception, databases, or uncertain histories, where times, durations, and boundaries are uncertain and expressions such as "just before" or "roughly during" have graded meaning. We develop the probabilistic Allen algebra (PAA): a generative and complete extension in which relation probabilities are derived from distributions over interval boundaries rather than assigned as scores. Time points are Gaussian; intervals have Gaussian midpoints and truncated-Gaussian durations. Every relation is a boundary-ordering predicate in one common probability space: point-point relations reduce to error functions, and point-interval and interval-interval relations to multivariate Gaussian orthant probabilities induced by linear inequalities. Contact relations (meets, starts, finishes, equals) receive positive measure through a tolerance band, and under a single tolerance the thirteen relations form a true partition that recovers crisp Allen as the tolerance vanishes. The construction derives Allen's taxonomy rather than positing it: coarse predicates such as precedence, overlap, and containment are unions of leaves whose probabilities are leaf sums, and this hierarchy is preserved as intervals collapse to points and thirteen relations reduce to five and then three. Each relation further decomposes into correlation-aware temporal primitives in the spirit of CIDOC CRM. The algebra is scale-invariant and separates graded expressions such as "shortly before" from contact relations. All results are Monte-Carlo validated and shipped as an open, tested Python package.

</details>


### [154] [FreqCondNorm: Towards Cross-domain Predictive Maintenance through a Frequency-Conditioned Transformer Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20535)
*Zaynab Raounak,Camille LHermine,Zhiguo Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce FreqCondNorm, a Transformer with frequency-conditioned normalization to handle multi-frequency predictive maintenance time-series, achieving strong cross-domain fault diagnosis but limited gains for remaining useful life prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive maintenance models using deep learning typically don’t transfer well across different machines, operating conditions, and especially widely varying sampling frequencies, and they struggle when labeled data are scarce. There is a need for a unified architecture that can ingest heterogeneous time-series data spanning several orders of magnitude in sampling frequency and still perform accurate diagnosis and transfer learning.

Method: They design FreqCondNorm, a Transformer-based architecture that incorporates a FiLM-style frequency-conditioned normalization layer to standardize representations across different sampling frequencies. The model is pretrained using a combination of masked auto-encoding and contrastive learning, with a balanced domain sampling strategy, on five public predictive maintenance datasets (CWRU, MFPT, UOC18, PRONOSTIA, CMAPSS) covering a wide range of conditions and sampling rates. After pretraining, it is evaluated for downstream tasks like fault diagnosis and remaining useful life prediction.

Result: For fault diagnosis, FreqCondNorm achieves 99.2% accuracy on the CWRU dataset, an improvement of 6.4 percentage points over a CNN baseline, and obtains 82.1% zero-shot accuracy on MFPT, indicating effective transfer across domains and sampling frequencies. However, when applied to remaining useful life prediction, the method does not yield performance improvements, implying that the learned representations from the chosen pretraining tasks are not well aligned with RUL estimation needs.

Conclusion: FreqCondNorm successfully unifies heterogeneous, multi-frequency time-series into a single predictive maintenance model and demonstrates strong cross-frequency transfer for fault diagnosis, particularly under limited labeled data. Nonetheless, its failure to enhance remaining useful life prediction reveals a limitation of the current pretraining strategy and highlights the need to better align representation learning objectives with RUL tasks in future work.

Abstract: Deep learning predictive maintenance models suffer from poor transferability across machines and operating conditions, especially when labelled data are scarce and signals span five orders of magnitude in sampling frequency (1 Hz to ~100 kHz). We propose FreqCondNorm, a Transformer-based architecture that introduces a FiLM-style frequency-conditioned normalization layer to unify heterogeneous time-series within a single model. The architecture is pretrained on five public predictive maintenance datasets (CWRU, MFPT, UOC18, PRONOSTIA, CMAPSS) using masked auto-encoding and contrastive learning with balanced domain sampling. On fault diagnosis, the model achieves 99.2% accuracy on CWRU (+6.4 pp over CNN) and 82.1% zero-shot accuracy on MFPT, demonstrating strong transfer across sampling frequencies. However, the approach does not improve remaining useful life prediction, suggesting a mismatch between pretraining and RUL objectives that warrants future investigation.

</details>


### [155] [An Empirical Study of Harness Design for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20804)
*Run-Ze Fan,Zihao Zhang,Simin Ma,Yebowen Hu,Shouju Wang,Kaiqiang Song,Fei Liu,Hamed Zamani,Xiaoyang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how individual components of lightweight coding harnesses—planning, action space, and context management—affect the performance, cost, and behavior of autonomous coding agents on long-horizon software-engineering tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of autonomous coding agents typically treat the coding harness as a single, monolithic system, making it unclear which specific components (e.g., planning, tools, context management) actually drive performance. As models get stronger and tasks more complex, practitioners need principled, component-level guidance to design harnesses that are both effective and cost-efficient under different context-window and budget constraints. This paper addresses this gap by decomposing the harness and systematically comparing variants.

Method: The authors construct a lightweight coding harness where the main execution loop is held fixed while three components are systematically varied: (1) planning (presence/absence and its role), (2) action space (bash-only versus predefined tools plus bash), and (3) context management (five strategies under four context-window budgets). They evaluate four different LLMs on SWE-Bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1, resulting in 176 matched harness configurations. They then perform both quantitative benchmarking (accuracy, cost, failures) and trajectory-level qualitative analysis to understand how each component changes agent behavior, trajectory length, and failure modes.

Result: The study finds that: (1) Context management grows more important as context windows shrink, with most benefits coming from avoiding context-overflow failures rather than improving reasoning quality; a pipeline that first applies rule-based elision and then LLM summarization offers the best efficiency, while making elided content recoverable adds complexity without accuracy gains. (2) Planning benefits weaker models primarily by improving accuracy, but for stronger models it mainly reduces cost without much effect on accuracy. (3) Predefined tools help models that are weak at bash, but bash-proficient models perform well with a minimal bash-only interface and achieve substantially lower costs, particularly on command-line-heavy tasks. (4) Trajectory analysis shows that context management mainly prolongs viable trajectories, planning changes where trajectories terminate, and action-space design alters the granularity of code edits rather than the overall strategy.

Conclusion: Component-level analysis of coding harnesses reveals that the effectiveness of planning, action space, and context management is highly dependent on model strength, context budget, and task type. Efficient context management is most critical under tight context budgets, planning should be tuned to the model’s capabilities (accuracy support for weaker models, cost control for stronger ones), and action spaces should match the model’s bash proficiency and task characteristics. The authors propose that future harness design should be model- and budget-aware and use their modular evaluation framework to systematically test new harness components rather than relying on monolithic, hard-to-interpret agent stacks.

Abstract: Coding harnesses shape how autonomous coding agents translate model capabilities into long-horizon software-engineering performance, yet existing work typically evaluates harnesses as monolithic systems, leaving the effectiveness of individual components unclear. To enable component-level comparisons, we study this question with a lightweight coding harness whose execution loop is fixed while three components are varied: planning, action space, and context management. Across four models evaluated on SWE-Bench Verified and Terminal-Bench 2.1, we evaluate 176 matched settings spanning five context-management strategies, four context-window budgets, and targeted ablations of planning and action space. We find that: (1) Context management becomes increasingly valuable as the context-window budget tightens, with most of its benefit coming from preventing context-overflow failures. (2) Staging rule-based elision before LLM-based summarization provides the strongest overall efficiency among the context-management strategies, whereas making elided content recoverable adds machinery that models rarely use and yields no accuracy gain. (3) Planning shifts from an accuracy scaffold for weaker models to a cost saver for stronger models, with little change in accuracy. (4) Predefined tools improve performance for models with weaker bash proficiency, whereas bash-capable models can operate effectively with a bash-only interface and achieve substantially lower cost, especially on command-line-centric tasks. Trajectory-level analysis explains these effects: context management extends execution trajectories without substantially altering agent behavior, planning changes where trajectories stop, and the action space changes the granularity at which code is written. These findings inform model- and budget-aware harness design and provide a modular framework for evaluating future harness components.

</details>


### [156] [Refuse, Decompose, Refresh: A Claim-Safe Protocol for Closed-Loop AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20538)
*Peiying Zhu,Sidi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a protocol (“claim-safe” evaluation) to ensure AI evaluations don’t support unjustified claims, even when they are perfectly reproducible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reproducible AI evaluations can still be misleading because the policy under evaluation shapes which states are visited, what is observable, and which failures are even detectable. This is especially risky in closed-loop systems, where the evaluation may systematically miss important failure modes, yet still report confident, reproducible metrics and pass/fail labels.

Method: The authors define a three-action “claim-safe” evaluation protocol: (1) Refuse – abstain from making claims when there is no clean reference stream or aligned runtime comparison; (2) Decompose – separate reporting of protocol execution, operational false admissions, and structural hypotheses instead of collapsing everything into a single PASS/FAIL; (3) Refresh – treat distribution shift alarms as signs that the reference needs to be recomputed, not as direct evidence of system faults. They implement this protocol in an aggregate-only simulator with 24 policy components, three demand regimes, two families of fault masks, and separate development vs. heldout random seeds. They preregister a heldout set with 1,440 cases and 21,600 partition rows and analyze admission rates, false admission, and likelihood-based calibration metrics across partitioned units and component clusters.

Result: Of 72 regime–component units, only 55 had sufficient support to be “reference-admitted,” and 54 of those remained admissible at runtime, so abstention is explicitly part of the reported results. For the 20 represented components, the stable false-admission count was 0, giving a one-sided exact 95% upper bound of 0.1391 under a fixed 0.20 decision rule. Within the admitted units, the observed clean-traffic behavior predicted faults better than the nominal cell-level fault fractions: across 540 unit–arm rows in 20 component clusters, the negative-log-likelihood difference (cell fraction minus traffic-based) was 0.1264 nats per row, with a 95% interval [0.0593, 0.1918] over clusters. Drift logs show that “null” behavior is relative to the chosen reference: in clean, fault-free streams, the detector raised 15/15, 0/15, and 14/15 alarms in three regimes, and only the middle regime matched the frozen detector reference distribution.

Conclusion: AI evaluations should be framed as executable contracts that tie together what is observably supported in the data, how the statistics are calibrated, and exactly which claims are justified. Instead of a universal threshold or a single pass/fail verdict, evaluations must explicitly allow abstention, distinguish operational from structural conclusions, and dynamically refresh their reference maps under distribution shift. This structure reduces the risk that perfectly reproducible evaluations nonetheless endorse incorrect or overstrong claims about system safety or performance.

Abstract: An AI evaluation can be perfectly reproducible and still support the wrong claim. This risk is acute in closed-loop systems: policy determines visited states, observable components, and which failures leave a measurable trace. We propose a claim-safe protocol with three actions. Refuse: abstain when a clean reference stream or matched runtime comparison lacks support. Decompose: report protocol execution, operational false admission, and structural hypotheses separately rather than as one PASS/FAIL label. Refresh: treat distribution-shift alarms as requests to invalidate and recompute a reference map, not as fault evidence. We instantiate the protocol in an aggregate-only simulator with 24 policy components, three demand regimes, two fault-mask families, and independent development and heldout seeds. The preregistered heldout contains 1,440 cases and 21,600 partition rows. Only 55/72 regime-component units were reference-admitted and 54/55 remained runtime-admitted, making abstention part of the result. Stable false admission was 0/20 represented components, with a one-sided exact 95% upper bound of 0.1391 under a frozen 0.20 rule. Within admitted units, affected clean traffic outpredicted nominal fault-cell fraction: across 540 unit-arm rows nested in 20 component clusters, the cell-minus-traffic negative-log-likelihood difference was 0.1264 nats per row, with a 95% component-cluster interval of [0.0593, 0.1918]. A drift log shows why "null" must be reference-relative: clean fault-null streams triggered 15/15, 0/15, and 14/15 alarms across three regimes, while only the middle regime matched the frozen detector reference. Rather than a universal threshold, we contribute an executable contract linking observable support, statistical calibration, and justified claims.

</details>


### [157] [Limits of Confidence in Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20581)
*Russ Webb,Amitis Shidani,Alice Bizeul,Dan Busbridge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes discrete diffusion methods that update multiple token positions per step, showing they inherently fail to match true joint distributions when there are dependencies between tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether and when discrete diffusion methods like remasking and uniform-state samplers can faithfully model data domains (pixels, phonemes, words) where tokens are not independent, and to characterize the resulting distributional mismatch.

Method: The authors theoretically analyze the conditions under which a discrete diffusion step can match the training distribution, focusing on per-position sampling and selection. They then construct a synthetic task, ScanAndAdd, with a known closed-form joint distribution, to empirically test dependence structures and measure divergence between generated and true distributions.

Result: They prove that a diffusion step only matches the training distribution when the positions it updates are conditionally independent given already-fixed tokens, and that no product of per-position distributions can represent a dependent group. They further show that identical per-position marginals can correspond to different joint dependencies. Empirically, on ScanAndAdd, they confirm that every group of two or more undecided positions chosen by a confidence-based ranking is dependent, and the generated distribution deviates from the true one by 29× the sampling-noise-floor in total variation distance, even though per-sample quality metrics remain perfect (1.0).

Conclusion: Discrete diffusion methods that update multiple positions using per-position distributions cannot faithfully model joint distributions with dependencies, even if per-sample metrics look perfect. Dependence between token positions causes significant hidden distributional mismatch, which is not detectable by standard per-sample evaluation and must be addressed in model design.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion, including remasking and uniform-state samplers, generate a sequence by writing multiple token positions per step, drawing each from a per-position distribution and choosing which positions to write from those same distributions. For domains of general interest (pixels, phonemes, or words) there are inherent dependencies between tokens. We show that a step matches the training distribution only when the positions it writes are conditionally independent given the tokens already fixed, that no product of per-position distributions can match a dependent group, and that per-position distributions do not determine whether a group is dependent: two joint distributions can have identical per-position marginals while differing in which combinations of values occur. On ScanAndAdd, a synthetic task whose joint distribution is available in closed form, we verify that every group of two or more undetermined positions a confidence ranking writes is dependent, and measure the generated distribution to be $29\times$ the sampling-noise floor total variation while per-sample metrics are $1.0$.

</details>


### [158] [Ownership in AI-Assisted Everyday Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20658)
*Megan Wei,Melanie Subbiah,Audrey Lee,Annya Dahmani,Dave Edwards,Helen Edwards,Ellie Pavlick*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Exploratory survey on when AI-assisted work still feels like one’s own, highlighting how collaboration processes shape felt ownership, voice, and disclosure norms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI tools integrate into everyday tasks, people increasingly co-create with machines. This raises a key question: under what conditions do people still feel that the outcomes are genuinely theirs? Understanding this is important for designing AI systems that respect and support users’ sense of authorship, agency, and identity, and for informing social norms and policies around credit, disclosure, and responsibility.

Method: The authors conducted an exploratory qualitative survey. Participants were asked to recall and describe two recent, self-chosen tasks they had completed with AI: one task that felt like their own work and one that did not feel like their own. The researchers then qualitatively analyzed these narratives to identify patterns in how people talk about ownership, collaboration, and authorship when using AI tools.

Result: They found that perceived ownership hinges strongly on the collaboration process. Participants tended to disown work when their role was limited to passively approving AI suggestions. In contrast, they felt ownership when they actively led the task, iterated on, or substantially rewrote AI outputs. Ownership could remain high even when people relied on AI for execution or could not have completed the task alone, as long as they felt they owned the vision or direction. Conversely, loss of personal voice in the output and poor understanding of how or why the AI produced the result reduced felt ownership. They also observed that willingness to disclose AI use did not necessarily track pride or ownership; instead, disclosure decisions were influenced by community norms and worries that acknowledging AI might undermine perceived credit for the work.

Conclusion: The study suggests that design of AI systems should prioritize modes of interaction that allow users to lead, revise, and imprint their own voice on AI-assisted work, rather than simply approving suggestions. It highlights that authorship can meaningfully persist even in strongly AI-dependent tasks when users control the vision. The paper calls for future research into interaction designs, explanation mechanisms, and social norms that reinforce users’ sense of authorship and agency, and that decouple constructive AI assistance from the fear of losing credit or ownership over one’s work.

Abstract: When does work done with AI still feel like ours? As AI becomes woven into everyday tasks, we must examine what happens to our sense of ownership and contribution when a machine shares in producing what we make. We report an exploratory qualitative survey in which participants were asked to describe two recent, self-selected tasks completed with AI: one that felt like their own and one that did not. We find that felt ownership depends on the process of collaboration: people disown work when they merely approve AI's suggestions, but retain ownership when they lead, iterate, or rewrite. Ownership can also extend to settings where people own the vision for a project but not the execution; respondents reported high ownership on tasks they could not have completed without AI. Loss of personal voice and a lack of comprehension of the output both erode ownership. Finally, willingness to disclose AI use is often decoupled from actual pride or ownership, and instead shaped by community norms and fear of credit erasure. We propose several research directions as a result of these findings to promote AI development that supports people's sense of authorship over their own lives.

</details>


### [159] [Deep Noir: Autonomous Steering Discovery via Architectural Chronometry in Transformer Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20722)
*Frank E. Bobe,Gregory D. Vetaw,Darshan W. Bryner,Matthew G. Cook,Jose L. Salas-Vernis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deep Noir is an automated framework for finding where and how much to apply activation steering in LLMs, improving spam and sentiment classification performance while revealing a new prompt-injection attack surface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Activation steering can control LLM behavior at inference time but currently requires manual, ad hoc choices of layers, heads, and steering strengths. This makes it hard to scale, reproduce, and deploy steering across different models, tasks, and sizes. The paper aims to mechanistically ground and automate these decisions so that steering becomes more reliable, effective, and transferable, and to understand its implications for model security.

Method: The authors propose Deep Noir, which combines (1) Logit Lens convergence analysis to locate layers where model representations are sufficiently stable and predictive, and (2) causal head-level attribution to identify specific attention heads that causally drive the target behavior (e.g., spam or sentiment). Using these mechanistic signals, Deep Noir automatically selects steering locations and magnitudes, then applies activation steering during inference without changing model weights or code. They benchmark across multiple model sizes and architectures, and compare against RepE and baselines, including ablations like no head masking. They also evaluate how varying steering strength affects vulnerability to prompt-injection-style attacks in steered classifier setups.

Result: Across six models at three parameter scales, Deep Noir yields large performance gains: around +16.7 percentage points on a spam classification task for 1B-parameter models (std 4.7 across 39 runs), and larger gains of +21 to +42 percentage points for 7–9B models across four architectures. On SST-2 sentiment, it gives a +13.1 percentage-point improvement without modifying code. Unlike RepE without head masking, which does not outperform baseline, Deep Noir improves all models with statistically significant gains (p < 0.01). The discovered intervention points transfer across tasks and architectures. Additionally, they quantify that stronger steering monotonically increases susceptibility to prompt-injection-like attacks.

Conclusion: Mechanistically informed, automated activation steering is both feasible and effective: Deep Noir can autonomously choose where and how strongly to steer, yielding substantial and statistically robust improvements in classification tasks across diverse LLMs without architectural changes. The framework’s use of Logit Lens convergence and causal head attribution produces intervention points that generalize beyond the specific training setup. However, steering also systematically opens up and enlarges a prompt-injection attack surface, with vulnerability scaling with steering magnitude. This highlights a trade-off between performance gains and robustness, and signals that steered agents require dedicated security analyses and defenses.

Abstract: Activation steering modifies LLM behavior at inference time, but identifying where and how strongly to steer remains manual. We introduce Deep Noir, a framework that uses Logit Lens convergence and causal head-level attribution to autonomously discover optimal steering parameters. Across three scales (1B x 3, 2-3B x 2, and 7-9B x 4), our engine achieves 16.7 percentage-point improvement on spam at 1B (standard deviation 4.7; 39 runs), with gains increasing to 21 to 42 percentage points at 7-9B across four architectures. On SST-2 sentiment, it achieves a 13.1 percentage-point improvement with zero code changes. Mechanistic grounding enables automated discovery of intervention points that generalize across tasks and architectures. On sentiment, RepE without head masking fails to improve over baseline, while Deep Noir improves all models (p less than 0.01). We further show that steering creates a predictable prompt-injection attack surface whose vulnerability increases monotonically with steering magnitude. This finding is relevant to agent systems deploying steered classifiers.

</details>


### [160] [Q&A on Any Spreadsheet Requires Interpreting Its Grid Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20732)
*Zofia Smoleń*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a semantic cell-role annotation framework to create more interpretable spreadsheet chunks for LLM-based RAG, improving answer generation via richer context but revealing a fundamental ceiling of discrete cell classification and motivating new dimensionality-reduction methods to linearize 2D spreadsheets into 1D text.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-driven RAG systems struggle with spreadsheets because their 2D, loosely structured nature makes standard chunking and retrieval less interpretable and less useful for answer generation. Existing methods treat spreadsheets as tables or flat text without capturing the nuanced roles of cells (e.g., headers, labels, values, notes), limiting the contextual quality of chunks presented to the LLM. The authors aim to improve how spreadsheets are transformed and fed into RAG pipelines so that LLMs can reason over them more effectively.

Method: They introduce a framework that first semantically annotates each spreadsheet cell with its functional role (such as header, data value, metadata, comment, etc.) and then uses these annotations to split the spreadsheet into semantically coherent, interpretable chunks. These role-aware chunks are then used in a RAG setup to supply contextual information to an LLM for downstream question answering. They compare their approach against prior chunking and spreadsheet-processing baselines and analyze both retrieval performance and answer quality, highlighting that their gains come from better contextualization rather than improved retrieval metrics.

Result: Their annotated, role-aware chunking framework outperforms existing state-of-the-art spreadsheet chunking methods in LLM-based QA tasks. The gains manifest mainly in the interpretability and usefulness of the retrieved chunks for answer generation, not in raw retrieval accuracy. However, they empirically observe a performance ceiling, arguing that even perfect cell role classification cannot fully encode the rich, continuous structural relationships in 2D spreadsheets because any classifier is limited to a finite set of pre-defined roles.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that semantic cell role annotation is a meaningful step forward for making spreadsheets more interpretable and useful in LLM-driven RAG pipelines, but it also exposes fundamental limitations of discrete cell classification. The authors conclude that to truly overcome the spreadsheet-to-LLM bottleneck, future research should move beyond finite role labels and instead develop dimensionality-reduction techniques that directly transform 2D unstructured spreadsheets into 1D unstructured text representations. Such flattened text chunks would be easier for LLMs to interpret and reason over than current cell-based structures.

Abstract: Semantic cell annotation improves chunking interpretability for spreadsheets in LLM-driven RAG systems, aiding answer generation through enriched context rather than improved retrieval accuracy. We propose a novel framework of splitting any spreadsheet into interpretable chunks using cell role annotation. Our framework beats the state of the art, yet it faces a hard ceiling. Spreadsheets are fundamentally two-dimensional unstructured data with continuous relationships and infinite potential cell roles. Because classification models are restricted to finite, pre-defined classes, they cannot perfectly capture this structural nuance, even with human-level annotation. We show that addressing the spreadsheet-to-LLM bottleneck requires moving beyond discrete cell classification. Instead, the field must develop dimensionality-reduction techniques to directly flatten 2D unstructured spreadsheets into 1D unstructured text. Text chunks would be easier for downstream RAG to interpret and generate from.

</details>


### [161] [RAFT: A Stateful Retrieval-Augmented Framework for Troubleshooting Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20754)
*Mingxuan Zhang,Xiaowen Wang,Anupma Sharan,Zhengyi Chen,Chenyu Diana Zhang,Shanshan Yang,Chittibabu Pacharu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces RAFT, a stateful retrieval-augmented framework for troubleshooting agents that retrieves at the level of intermediate case states instead of whole documents, improving case matching across stages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise troubleshooting support cases unfold over multiple stages, but existing RAG approaches treat them as static documents, failing to exploit their stateful, temporal structure for better case retrieval and guidance. There is also a lack of suitable public, multi-stage troubleshooting datasets to evaluate such systems realistically.

Method: Model each historical support case as a directed chain of timeline entries (states) and perform retrieval at the entry level, returning the trajectory of the matched case anchored at the most relevant intermediate state. Optionally, connect cases in a higher-level graph via configurable similarity measures. Evaluate only the retrieval layer, avoiding full agent deployment, using a synthetic multi-stage benchmark derived from Microsoft Learn Windows Server docs and a real-world Apache Jira dataset labeled with duplicates. Compare RAFT against vanilla RAG and GraphRAG baselines using metrics such as Case Hit across case progress stages.

Result: RAFT consistently increases Case Hit compared to vanilla RAG and GraphRAG at every stage of a troubleshooting case, with improvements over the strongest baseline being statistically significant. Experiments on Apache Jira duplicate-labeled issues indicate that these gains likely extend to real historical case data.

Conclusion: State-aware, timeline-level retrieval via RAFT more effectively matches in-progress troubleshooting cases to helpful historical trajectories than document-level or graph-only RAG approaches. The framework offers a deployable retrieval layer, validated on both synthetic and real data, and the released benchmark, code, and evaluation set can facilitate further research on multi-stage troubleshooting agents.

Abstract: Effective troubleshooting agents in enterprise customer support depend on retrieving actionable guidance from similar historical cases, yet existing retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems treat support cases as static documents and overlook their multi-stage, stateful nature. We introduce RAFT (Retrieval-Augmented Framework for Troubleshooting Agents), a stateful RAG framework that abstracts each closed historical case into a directed chain of timeline entries and retrieves at the entry level, surfacing cases whose intermediate states match the active case and returning the parent-case trajectory anchored at the matched state; an optional case-level graph links cases through a configurable similarity representation. We evaluate this retrieval layer directly, which, unlike evaluating a full agent system, requires no production deployment. Because public multi-stage troubleshooting data is extremely rare, we pair a synthetic benchmark built from Microsoft Learn Windows Server documentation with real Apache Jira issues carrying human-created duplicate labels. RAFT improves Case Hit over vanilla RAG and GraphRAG baselines at every stage of case progress, with statistically significant gains over the strongest baseline; the Jira results provide directional evidence that the advantage transfers to real case histories. We release our benchmark, implementation, and the Apache Jira evaluation set.

</details>
